THE LAW OF CONTRACTS I. IMPORTANCE OF LAW OF CONTRACTS 

The importance of Contracts in the study of law is evidenced by the fact that most of the principal subjects of the law are based in large part upon the law of Contracts, the five chief exceptions being the subjects of Criminal Law, Evidence, Practice, Torts, and Wills. 

II. DEFINITIONS AND NATURE 

A.

CONTRACT 

A contract is a business agreement between two or more persons, arising from mutual promises, or an accepted promise, the legal effect of which is to impose a binding obligation upon each party to the extent of the terms of the agreement. 

Montgomery Co. v Johnson, (1911), 209 Mass. 89, 95 N. E. 290 

Ashcroft v Butterworth, (1884), 136 Mass. 511; 13 C. J. 237

A contract has also been defined as a prome or set of promises for breach of which the law gives a remedy, or the performance of which the law in some indirect way recognizes as a duty. The word "contract" is derived from the Latin word "contrahere", meaning to draw together, and signifies a meeting of the minds, either actual or legal. 

Hudson v Columbian Co., (1904), 137 Mich.255,100 NW 402, Cost.43

It follows that there must be at least two parties to every contract, and a man cannot make a contract with himself. 

Burditt v Colburn, (1891), 63 Vt.231,22 AL..572 

EXAMPLE: A, as trustee, signs and seals a promise to himself as an individual or as an executor. The instrument is void.

It is another question whether a contract may be formed in which the same person is one of several on one side of a bargain, and either alone or with others is also a party on the other side. Such a transaction is a valid contract. 

EXAMPLE: A becomes a member of an unincorporated society and, by so doing, promises to pay dues to the society. He is bound by the contract.

The intention or understanding of the parties must be mutual to create a legal obligation. 

EXAMPLE: A contractor who was furnishing labor and materials on a building told the owner that it would cost about $600, and the ovner supposed that the work was to be done for that sum, while the contractor understood that he was to be paid a definite price per hour and the price of the materials used. There was no meeting of the minds on any express contract. 

Lonnqvist v Lammi, (1922), 240 Mass. 371; 134 N. E. 255

A contract further imports that there shall be a mutual communication between the parties of their intentions to agree for, without this, neither could know the state of the other's

mind. The law, therefore, judges of a contract between two persons exclusively from those expressions of their intentions which are communicated between them. It is not merely mutual assent but an expression indicating such assent which the law requires. The understanding or unexpressed intention of one party is not binding on the other party to the contract. Conduct which imports assent is assent in the view of the law whatever may have been the actual state of mind of the parties. 

Embry v Hagaräine Co., (1907), 127 Mo. App. 38 105 S.W. 777 

Hobbs v Massasoit Whip Co., (1893)158 Mass.194;33 NE 495,C.246 

Luckey v St.Louis R.R.(1908),135 Mo.App.589,113 S.W.703 

Nickel v Zeitz, (1927), 258 Mass. 282; 154 N. E. 769

EXAMPLE 1: A offers to sell his library to B at a stated price, forgetting that his family Bible, which he actually did not intend to sell, was in the library. If B accepts the offer, he is entitled to have the entire library, including the Bible. EXAMPLE 2: A vrites an offer to B which he encloses in an envelope, and stamps. Shortly afterwards, he decides not to send the offer and determines to throw the letter into his waste-basket. Absent-mindedly, he picks it up with the other letters, and deposits it in a mail chute, and it is delivered to B who accepts the offer. There is a contract. 

EXAMPLE 3: A sold goods to B who sold them to C. A supposed that he was selling the goods to C through B, as agent for C, and never actually intended to sell them to B on 

B's sole credit. There is a contract. 

Stoddard v Ham, (1880), 129 Mass. 383

The unexpressed views of the parties, as to the legal relations which their words or acts give rise to, are immaterial. 

EXAMPLE: A orally promises to scil a book to B in return for B's promise to pay $5. A and B both think that such promises are not binding unless in writing. There is a contract.

It is essential, however, that the acts expressing assent should be done either with an actual or legal intention to be bound thereby. There must be a conscious will to perform such acts, although it is not material what induces the will. Thus, an insane person may express such assent, but a somnambulist cannot.

Contracts may be classified in various ways, such as express, implied, simple or parol, specialties, bilateral, unilateral, executed, executory, valid, void, and voidable contracts.

R AGREEMENT 

!

The word "agreement" is ordinarily used in practice as meaning the same thing as the word "contract". 

In reality, it is a broader word, however, and includes not only what are contracts in the strictest sense of that term but also acts which do not create any obligation, such as gifts, executed

deeds of real estate which contain no covenants of warranty, and sales of goods for cash, with immediate delivery, and without warranty. There is the highest authority for speaking of such deeds and sales as executed contracts. 

Fletcher v Peck, (1810), 6 Cranch (U. S.) 87

On the other hand, the propriety of calling such deeds and sales "contracts" has been questioned on the ground that it is of the essence of a contract, as a legal conception, that it shall impose an obligation on one or both of the parties to do or to forbear doing some act. An agreement whereby a person binds himself to convey land would, therefore, be called a contract but, it is asked, how can a conveyance be called a contract since, wher it has been entirely executed, it creates no obligation whatever but, at the very moment the parties agree, the agreement is carried out. 

Clark on Contracts 8, note.

C. EXPRESS CONTRACTS 

An express contract is one where the intention of the parties and the terms of the agreement are declared or expressed by the parties, in writing or orally, at the time it is entered into. 

Yawger v Joseph, (1915), 184 Ind. 228, 108 N. E. 774.

Express contracts are of two classes: (1) Contracts under seal, which are called specialties; and (2) Contracts not under seal, which are called single or parol contracts, and include all express contracts not under seal, whether oral or in writing. D.

IMPLIED CONTRACTS 1. IN GENERAL 

An implied contract is one in which the terms are not expressly stated or declared by the parties. It is a general rule that when the parties have made an express contract, the law will not imply a contract. 

Worthen v Stevens, (1808), 4 Mass. 447

EXAMPLE: An agreement to serve as director of a musical festival and to receive a certain compensation if the festival made a specified profit, but to serve gratuitously if the festival resulted in a loss, is binding. The festival having resulted in a loss, the agreement to serve gratuitously prevented any implication of a promise to pay a reasonable compensation. 

Zerrehn v Ditson, (1875), 117 Mass. 553 

Implied contracts may be divided inte two classes: (1)

Contracts implied in fact; and (2) Contracts implied in law. 

People v Dummer, (1916), 274 111. 637, 113 N. E. 934 

2. CONTRACTS IMPLIED IN FACT 

A contract implied in fact is a true contract, in which the agreement of the parties is inferred from the circumstances. There must be an assent of the parties, as in express contracts. There is an actual or an apparent meeting of the minds. A contract need not be an actual meeting of the minds. If the relation of the parties and the circumstances

VIOV 

0113 この ​To abscb 

092 Hone

-- 
--  are such that they would imply an agreement, and imply that the minds of the parties met on the proposition, there is a valid contract. 

Hobbs v Massasoit Whip Co.,158 Mass.194,33 N.E.495, Cost.246 

Fogg v Portsmouth Atheneum, 44 N. H. 115 

Newmarket Mfg. Co. v. Coon, (1890), 150 Mass.566,23 N.E.380 

Vickery v Ritchie, 202 Mass.247, 88 N.E.835,26 LRA(NS) 810 

Sturges v Robbins, (1811). 7 Mass. 300 

Austin v Burge, (1911), iss Mo. App. 286, 137 S. W. 618 

See McKenna v Twombly, (1910), 206 Mass.62,91 N.E.1023 

Distinguish Klug v Sheriffs, (1906),129 Wis, 468,109 N.W.656 

Miller v McClelland, (Iowa, 1919), 173 N.W.910,10 A.L.R.1317 

Cavanaugh v Ranlet, (1918),229 Mass.366, 118 N. E. 650 

EXAMPLE 1: A telephones to his grocer: "Send me a barrel of flour". The grocer sends it. A has contracted to pay a reasonable price for the flour. 

EXAMPLE 2: A, on passing a market where he has a charge account, sees a box of apples marked "5 cents each". A picks up an apple, holds it up so that the clerk sees the act, the clerk nods, and A passes on. A has contracted to pay 5 cents for the apple.

EXAMPLE 3: A master, to whom an apprentice was bound, died and the apprentice continued to live with and work for his widow, both believing themselves to be bound by the articles of apprenticeship. The law will not imply a contract by the widow to pay the compensation fixed by the articles, but will imply an obligation to pay what the services are fairly worth. 

Hennessey v Deland, (1872), 110 Mass. 145

EXAMPLE 4: A contract by a vendee of land that the vendor might take ice needed for his own use from the vendee's icehouses as long as the vendor lived contained no express promise by the vendee to cut and store any ice. Such co-operation, being essential to the carrying out of the agreement, is implied. 

Proctor v Union Coal Co., (1923),243 Mass. 428, 137 N.E.659

EXAMPLE 5: D sold the wood growing on his land to X with a proviso that it should be taken from the land within two years. X conveyed his interest to P who had no notice as to the time within which the wood must be removed. P cut the wood but did not remove it within the two years. The property in the wood reverted to D and P cannot recover either the value of the wood, or pay for cutting it. 

Kemble v Dresser, (1840), 1 Met. 271 

EXAMPLE 6: D'accepted a devise of land on condition that he should within two years after the testator's decease pay to

A a certain sum of money. Such conduct implies a promise on the part of D to pay that sum tc A, upon which an action may be maintained by A's administrator, although A died within the two years. 

Adams v Adams, (1867), 14 Allen 65

TILG OL ? TO)

- 

EXAMI LE 7: Ordinarily, the superintendent or foreman of an employer has no implied authority to employ a surgeon or physician at the expense of the employer to attend an injured employee. 

Sheehan v Elliott Co. (1929)83 NH 542,145 Atl.139, 71 ALR 633 

King v Forbes Lithograph Co., (1903), 183 Mass. 301, 67 N.E. 330 

Distinguish Weinberg v St. Louis, 135 Mo.App.553, 116 N.W. 461 Contracts implied from the conduct of the parties are implied as a matter of fact, and not as a matter of law. There is an agreement in fact, evidenced by acts. To lead a person to suppose that you assent to an oral agreement is to assent to it, aside from the element of fraud. 

Calkins v Wire Hardware Co. (1929), 267 Mass.52,68,165 N.E.889

The law implies a promise to pay for the reasonable value of benefits received only when there is no evidence that they were conferred upon other grounds than that of contract. One who relies on a contract must prove its existence and this he does not do by merely showing a set of circumstances that can be accounted for by another relation appearing to exist between the parties. When the relations of the parties are such as to warrant the inference that the benefit was bestowed gratuitously, by way of hospitality, or by reason of any obligation, legal or moral, it becomes a question of fact to determine whether it was in reality gratuitous or upon the basis of contract. 

Spring v Hulett, (1870), 104 Mass. 591 

French v Bray, (1928), 263 Mass. 121, 160 N.E. 424 

Plate v Durst, (1896) 42.Va. €3, 24 S.. 530, 32 L.R.A.404 

Cicotte v Church of St. Anne, (1886), 60 Milch. 552, 27 N.W.682 

True v Lebovach, (1925), 245 Hass: 369, 137 1. E. €71 

Spencer v Spencer, (1902), 181 Mass. 471, 63 N.E. 947, Cost.45 

Graham v Stanton, (1901), 177 Mass. 321, 58 N.E. 1023 

Loring v Bacon, (1808), 4 Mass. 575 

EXAMPLE 1: If a son is in the employment of his father, a contract of hiring is not to be inferred from the fact of employment alone, because the principle of family affection is sufficient to account for the family association and does not demand the inference of a contract. 

Hertzog v Hertzog, 29 Fa. 265

EXAMPLE 2: 

Although a man is under no obligation to support the mother of his wife yet, if at the request of his wife's brother, he receives his mother-in-law into his family, and supports her there, the law will not imply a contract that he is to be paid by such brother-in-law for her support in the absence of evidence that the brother-in-law knew or reasonably ought to have understood that he was to be charged for such maintenance. 

Lyons v Jackson, (1919), 232 Mass. 275, 122 .E. 304 

EXAMPLE 3: If a lessor, who takes possession of leased premises upon the tenant's failure to pay rent, notifies the owner of an engine put on the premises for the tenant's use that, if he does not remove it, he will charge him for each day

it continues to remain there, a promise on such owner's part to pay reasonable storage for the time thereafter elapsing may be implied on these facts if the owner did not intend to abandon the engine to the lessor. 

Taylor v Dexter Engine Co., (1888), 146 Mass.613, 16 N.E. 462 

EXAMPLE 4: Where a woman married a man and lived with him till his death but afterwards learned that he had a lawful wife living, it has been held in Massachusetts that she cannot recover in an action of contract against his administrator for her services in keeping house. 

Thurston v Perry, (1881), 130 Mass. 240 

Cooper v Cooper, (1888), 147 Mass. 370, 17 N. E. 892 

Ogden v McHugh, (1897), 167 Mass. 276; 45 N. E. 731 (Dictum)

But the majority rule on this question in other states is contra. 

Re Fox, (1922), 178 Wis. 369, 190 N. W. 90; 31 A.L.R. 420

EXAMPLE 5: There is no presumption that services rendered by a woman in caring for her brother's children are gratuitcus, although they are rendered in the family home of the children's grandparents, where both the brother and sister reside. 

Snyder v Guthrie (1922)193 Iowa 624,187 N.E.953,24 A.L.R.950

Silence may be interpreted as assent where a proposition is made to one under circumstances whereby he is fairly called upon to deny or admit his liability, that is to say, if he is silent in the face of facts which fairly place upon him an obligation to speak. Mere silence is not enough unless there is also an obligation to speak. 

Bowley v Fuller, (Me.,1921), 115 Atl. 466, 24 A. L. R. 964 

Fogg v Portsmouth Atheneum, 44 N. H. 115 

Felthouse v Bindley, (1862),11 C.B.N.S.869; Costigan 244 

Preston v American Linen Co., (1876), 119 Mass. 400 

Connor v Hackley, (1841), 2 Met. 613 

Austin v Burge, (1911), 156 Mo. App. 286; 137 S. W. 618 

Carnahan v Boebe-Bowles(1916)0 Ore.124,156 Pac:584;Cost.250 

Taylor v Dexter Engine Co.,(1658),146 Mass. 613, 16 N.E. 462 

O'Donnell v Clinton, (1887),145 Mass. 461; 14 N. E. 747 

See Wheeler v Klahclt, (1901)178 Mass.141,59 N.E. 756, Cost.247 

Ostman v Lee, (1917),91 Conn.731,101 Atl.23, Costigan 251 

EXAMPLE 1: If D sees a laborer day after day at work in his field, doing services which must of necessity enure to his benefit, knowing that the laborer expected pay for his work, if it were perfectly easy to notify him that his services were not wanted, but D remained silent, such a request, either previous to or contemporaneous with the services, might fairly be inferred, even if no request were expressly proved. But if the fact was merely brought to his attention on a single occasion, and casually, and D had little opportunity to notify the laborer that he did not desire the work and would not pay for it, or if he could only notify him at the expense of much time and trouble, the same inference might not be made. The circumstances of each case would necessarily determine whether silence with a knowledge that another was doing valuable work

100

--  for his benefit, and with the expectation of payment, indicated an assent which would give rise to the inference of a contract. 

The question would be one for the jury. 

Day v Caton, (1876), 119 Mass. 513 

EXAMPLE 2: If a man's house takes fire, the law does not presume or imply a contract to pay his neighbors for their services in saving his property. 

EXAMFLE 3: Where a buyer of goods did not reject an offer by the seller to insure them before delivery at the joint expense of both parties, but remained silent and accepted and retained policies taken out by the seller in the belief that the proposition had been accepted, he will be considered as having accepted the offer, notwithstanding a secret intention not to accept. 

Bohn Mfg. Co.v Sawyer, (1897),169 Mass.471,48 N. E. 620 

EXAMPLE 4: A gave several lessons on the violin to B's child, intending to give a course of twenty lessons and to charge B the price. B never requested A to give this instruction but silently allowed the lessons to be given to their end, having a good reason to know A's intention. B is liable for the price of the course.

EXAMPLE 5: B frequently solicited orders for goods from A through salesmen, the orders to be subject to B's personal approval. B shipped the goods in every case within a week and without notice to A. B's salesman solicited and received another order from A which was delivered to B who did not approve of it. He did not notify A of his disapproval but remained silent. A, relying on the order, forbore to buy elsewhere for a week. B is bound to fill the order. 

EXAMPLE 6: If one person lends his horse to another, without more, he does not authorize the latter to make him answerable for its keep and improvement. 

Cahill v Hall, (1894), 161 Mass. 51, 237 N. E. 573 

EXAMFLE 7: P, when selling a patent medicine to D, read to D a contract which recited that, in consideration of a discount, the purchaser agreed not to sell the medicine for less than a certain price and D accepted the goods without expressing any dissent. D impliedly contracted not to sell the medicine, for less than the agreed price. 

Garst v Harris, (1900), 177 Mass.72, 58 N. E. 174

Comparing Example 1 with Example 2, the common principles of human conduct mark self-interest as the motive of action in the first example and kindness in the second; and, therefore, by common custom, compensation is mutually counted on in the one case, and in the other not. 

3. CONTRACTS IMPLIED IN LAW 

Contracts implied in law, or more properly quasi or constructive contracts, are a class of obligation which are imposed or created by law without regard to the assent of the

308

- 

party bound, on the ground that they are dictated by reason and justice, and which are allowed to be enforced by an action ex contractu. They rest solely on a legal fiction, and are not contract obligations at all in the true sense, for there is no agreement; but they are clothed with the semblance of a contract for the purpose of the remedy, and the obligation arises not from consent, as in the case of true contracts, but from the law or natural equity. So, when the party to be bound is under a legal obligation to perform the duty from which his promise is inferred, the law may infer a promise even as against his intention. 

13 C. J. 244

In a contract implied in fact, the contract defines the duty but, in a contract implied in law, the duty defines the contract.

Leonard v State (1909) 56 Tex. Crim. Rep.307,120 S.W.183 

Earle v Coburn, (1881), 130 Mass. 596 

Loring v Bacon, (1308), 4 Mass. 575 

Keith v De Bussigney, (1901), 179 Mass.255, 60 N.E.614 

N.Y.Central Ry.v Sturtevant (1920)236 Mass.16,127 N.E.509

Examples of quasi or constructive contracts include money received from another by mistake of fact, or through fraud, duress, or undue influence, or on a consideration which has failed, or under a contract rescinded, terminated, or unenforceable, or as the proceeds of another's property; money necessarily paid in discharge of legal liability primarily on another; infants' or insane persons' liability for necessaries; and a husband's liability for necessaries furnished to his wife if he has, without cause, refused to provide for her, Vaughan v Mansfield, (1916), 229 Mass.352,118 N.E.652

There is also an implied promise to discharge an obligation created by statute. 

Bath v Freeport, (1809), 5 Mass. 325 E.

BILATERAL AND UNILATERAL CONTRACTS 

A bilateral contract is one in which there are mutual promises between the parties to the agreement, cach party being both a promisor and a promisec. It is a promise for a promise. 

Thus, if A promises to sell his horse to B for a certain price, and B promises to buy it for that price, there is a bilateral contract. A unilateral contract is one in which there is a promise on one side only, the consideration of the other side being executed. It is a promisc for an act, or an act for a promise. 

Great Northern Ry.vWitham (1873) L.R.9C.P.16, Costigan 206 

Thayer v Burchard, (1868), 99 Mass. 508, 519 

Cohen v Bailly, (1928) (1928), 266 Mass. 39, 45, 165 N.E. 7 

Buffington v McNally, (1906),192 Mass. 198, 78 N. E. 309 

Des Brisay v Foss, (1928),264 Mass. 102,111,162 N.E. 4 

See Hopkins v Racine Co.,(1909), 137 Wis.583,119 N.W.301

Thus, if B promises to buy A's horse if A will deliver it on a certain day, and A delivers the horse on the day, there is a unilateral contract.

The statement is often made that, unless both partics are bound by an agreement, neither is bound, and that there must always be mutuality of obligation in every contract. is truc only of bilateral contracte but never of unilateral contracts.

This 

---

GOT TU

Bernstein v W.B.Mfg. Co. (1920) 235 Mass.425, 126 N.E.796 

Bernstein v W.B.Mfg. Co. (1921) 238 Mass.589,131 N.E.200 

EXAMPLE: An offer to sell goods for cash, or an offer of reward, becomes a contract when what is requested is given or done, though no obligation to give or to do anything ever exists. 

Train v Gold, (1827), 5 Pick. 380, 384

In every unilateral contract, at the very instant it is created, it is also fully performed. 

Every contract is either bilateral or unilateral. 

Professor Costigan supposes, for examples, the following cases: 

EXAMPLE 1: A desires to have his safe moved from his old office to a new one. He asks B to do this act, and says he will pay him 25. When the safe has been carried to the door of the new building, A appears and tells B that he withdraws his offer, directing him to leave the safe there. Nevertheless 

B proceeds and completes the moving. Under such circumstances, how can a contract be found, or how can A be held to any liability? 

EXAMPLE 2: A merchant is anxious to have a cart-load of goods placed upon an outgoing steamer, and there is barely time to accomplish this. He asks a cartman to take the goods to the steamer and offers 25 for the act. The cartman loads the goods and proceeds towards the wharf. On the road, another merchant hails him and offers 100 for the immediate cartage of his goods. The cartman thereupon places the first merchant's goods in a safe place and proceeds to carry the second load. As the cartman has never obligated himself to take the first load, he cannot be held for failure to perform, and the first merchant suffers his loss without remedy. 

See Biggers v Owen (1887) 79 Ga. 658, 5 S.E.193, Costigan 174 

Williston on Contracts, section 60

F. EXECUTED AND EXECUTORY CONTRACTS 

An executed contract is one where nothing remains to be done by either party. 

Farrington v Tennessee (1877) 95 U. S. 679, 683

An executory contract is one in which a party binds himself to do or not to do a particular thing in the future. A contract may be partly executed and partly executory. Such a contract must be classified as executory because there is still something remaining to be done by one or both of the parties. G.

VALID, VOID, AND VOIDABLE CONTRACTS 

A valid contract is one which is recognized by law. 

A void contract is one which is not recognized by law. 

The phrase "void contract" is a convenient one notwithstanding it is a contradiction in terms since a void contract is, in reality, no contract.

A voidable contract is a valid contract until it is avoided. It is one in which one or both parties thereto have the power to avoid the legal relations created by the contract. Typical examples are the contracts of infants and

- insane persons; where a contract was induced by fraud, mistake, or duress; or where a breach of warranty justifies the injured party in rescinding the bargain. Contracts within the statute of frauds, or barred by the statute of limitations, or by a discharge in bankruptcy likewise are voidable in the sense that they are valid contracts which cannot be enforced by one party against the will of the other who abandons or repudiates them. 

27 C. J. 310 

Williston on Contracts, section 16

There are six essentials of every valid, enforceable contract: (1) Offer and acceptance; (2) The formalities, if any, must be complied with; (3) Definiteness of terms; (4) Consideration; (5) Capacity of parties; (6) Legality of object.

If any one of the above six essentials is not met in any contract, the contract is not enforceable at law.

III. 

OFFER AND ACCEPTANCE A. IN GENERAL 

It is theoretically possible for a third person to state a suggested contract to the parties and for them to say simultaneously that they assent thereto, thereby forming a contract, but such a possibility is so rare as to be practically negligible. For all practical purposes, a contract may be said to be formed by an offer made by one party and an acceptance by the other. A contract implies the assent of two minds. This idea is often expressed by the phrase, It takes two to make a bargain or, to state it in other words, it must be understood between the parties that the one party has made an offer and that the other has accepted it. If one party should make an offer and the other party should not accept it there would be no contract. There is sometimes an exception to this rule but it is only apparent. Thus, if a person should put forth an advertisement offering a reward to any one who would recover lost property, this offer is to no one in particular and no one accepts it at the time it is made. But the meaning of the offer is that it is made to whomsoever will act upon it; and it is an implied part of the offer that time shall be afforded to any one who chooses to accept it; and, if a person, before the offer is withdrawn, does that which by the terms of the offer will entitle him to the reward, his so acting upon the offer constitutes an acceptance of it, and the party making the offer is bound to fulfill his promise. But when the parties are face to face, to constitute a contract, the one must offer and the other accept; unless where it is a part of the agreement that time shall be given to the person to whom the offer is made to determine whether he will accept it or not, in which case, the time given makes a part of the offer. 

Thruston v Thornton, (1848), 1 Cush. 89 

Bornstein v Lans, (1870), 104 Mass. 214 

A contract may arise (1) by a promise for a promise; (2) by a promise for an act; (3) by an act for a promise; or (4) by an act for an act. Unless some particular requirement as to the form of a contract is prescribed by law, a contract is equally binding whether expressed in writing, orally, by acts,

JULODCE MOTO GOR

-or partly in one of these ways and partly in others. 

An offer is a promise which is, in its terms, conditional upon an act, a forbearance, or a counterpromise being given in exchange for the promise. 

EXAMPLE: A says to B: "This book is yours if you promise to pay me 5 for it." A's offer is a promise in terms conditional upon receiving a promise of $5 from B. Upon B's making such promise, and receiving delivery of the book, it belongs to 

B although he may never pay for it.

B. THE OFFER 

1. 

CHARACTER OF OFFER 

An offer may invite an acceptance to be made (1) merely by an affirmative answer; (2) by performing or refraining from performing a specified act; or (3) it may contain a choice of terms from which the offeree is given the power to make a selection. 

Elastic Tip Co.v Graham (1899) 174 Mass.507,55 N.E.315

EXAMPLE 1: A offers B 100 tons of coal at $15 a ton, payable in thirty days. B's mere assent creates a contract. 

EXAMPLE 2: A offers B any amount of coal, up to 100 tons, for which B will promise to pay $15 a ton. In order to accept this offer, B must specify the amount he desires. 

Ramey Lumber Co. v Schroeder Co. (1916) 237 Fed.39, Costigan 454 

Keller v Ybarru (1853) 3 Cal. 147, Costigan 209 

Minnesota v Whitebreast (1895)160 111.85,43 NE 775,31 LRA 529 

Wells v Alexandre (1891) 130 N.Y. 642, 29 N.E. 142

An offer may propose the formation of a single contract by a single acceptance, or the formation of a number of contracts by successive acceptances from time to time. 

EXAMPLE 1: A offers to sell and deliver to B during the following year any quantity of goods between 4000 and 6000 pounds in amount which B may specify. In order to accept this offer, B must specify, within a reasonable time, a particular amount of not less than 4000 pounds and not more than 6000 pounds; and there can be but one acceptance and one contract. 

EXAMPLE 2: A offers a railway company such quantities of certain goods as the company's storekeeper may order from time to time during the next twelve months. Each order of the storekeeper during that period creates a unilateral contract for the quantity ordered. A might withdraw his offer at any time within the twelve months and thus absolve himself of the obligation to fill any orders received thereafter. 

Great Northern Ry.v Witham (1870)L.R.9 C.P.16, Costigan 206 2. COMMUNICATION OF OFFER 

An offer is made when it is communicated to the offeree. Two expressions of willingness to make the same bargain do not constitute a contract unless he is made in reference to the other. An offeree, therefore, cannot accept

This may be done through the medium of an agent, but mere information indirectly received by one party that another is willing to enter into a certain bargain is not an offer by the latter. 

Morris v Brightman, (1887), 143 Mass. 149, 9 N. E. 512 

Felthouse v Bindley, (1862), 11 C.B.N.S. 868, Costigan 244 

EXAMPLE 1: A mails an offer to B to pay 500 for B's horse. While this offer is in the mail, B, in ignorance thereof, mails to A an offer to sell him the horse for 500. There is no communication of A's offer and there is no contract. 

Thruston v Thornton, (1848), 1 Cush. 89 

See Tinn v Hoffman, (1873), 29 L.T.N.S. 271, Costigan 157 

EXAMPLE 2: A advertises that he will give a specified reward for certain information; or writes to B a similar proposal. B gives the information in ignorance of the advertisement or without having received the letter. There is no communication of the offer and there is no contract. 

EXAMPLE 5: An offer by mail which gives eight days within which to accept or reject it runs from the time the offer is received through the mail and not from the time it is posted. 

Caldwell v Cline, (W.Va.,1930), 156 S.E.55, 72 A.L.R.1211

In general, each party to a contract has a right to assume that the other can read and that he understands what he has signed, and so assents to the terms of the writing as a contract. But where one party tells the other that he cannot read or that fact is otherwise brought home to the knowledge of the other party, as where the instrument is executed by one of the parties making his mark, then the question whether a contract has been made depends upon the fact of the illiterate person's having in fact known and agreed to it. 

McKinney v B. & M.R.R., (1914), 274 Mass. 217, 104 N.E. 446

If a man signs a lawful contract and the other side is not privy to any improper motive for his signing it, such as may be created by fraud, duress, or mistake as to its contents he is bound, whatever his voluntary ignorance or his involuntary misinterpretation of the words. 

Clark v Boston, (1901), 179 Mass. 409, 60 N. E. 793 

O'Reilly's Case, (1927), 258 Mass. 205, 154 N. E. 851

It is not essential that the expression accurately conveys the thought in the offerer's mind for he is bound by what he says, and not by what he intended to say. In the absence of fraud, the tendency of the cases is to hold that if one accepts a document which purports to contain the terms of an offer, he is bound by all the terms, although he may not choose to inform himself of their tenor, or even of their existence. 

Fonseca v Cunard Co. (1891) 153 Mass.553,27 NE 665,12 LRA 340 

Atlas Shoe Co. v Blooin, (1911), 209 Mass.563, 95 N.E. 952 

Grace v Adams, (1868), 100 Mass. 505 

Rice v Dwight Mfg. Co., (1848), 2 Cush. 80 

See Jones v R.R. (1923)68 Mont.231,217 Pac.673,37 ALR 754

Thus, if one takes, without dissent, a bill of lading or

3. OFFERS AT AUCTION 

Auctions are of two classes: (1) Ordinary auctions; and (2) Auctions announced to be "to the highest bidder", or "without reserve".

Ordinarily, an auctioneer merely invites offers from successive bidders and his language is not an offer but a mere invitation to deal. The bidder is the offerer, and the auctioneer is the offeree. At the ordinary auction, the auctioneer, by beginning to auction property, does not impliedly say, "I offer to sell this property to whoever makes the highest bid", but rather requests that the bidders make offers to him. Where the auctioneer has merely invited offers, a bidder may withdraw his bid or the auctioneer may withdraw the goods at any time before the fall of the hammer. 

Payne v Cave, (1788), 3 T.R. 148, Costigan 73 

See Spencer v Harding, (1870), L.R.5 C.P.561, Costigan 51 

EXAMPLE 1: A publishes a notice stating that he will sell his household goods at public auction at a specific time and place. This in no way affects his legal relations. It is a mere invitation to deal. 

EXAMPLE 2: A's auctioneer, in Example 1, at the specified time and place, holds up a chattel and says, "How much am I bid for this?" After each bid is made, he urges others to bid higher. Each bidder makes an offer to the auctioneer, but he makes no offer to them. 

But if an auction is announced to be "without reserve", or "to the highest bidder", this is not a mere invitation to deal, but a genuine offer made by the auctioneer to sell at any price bid by the highest bidder. The auctioneer is the offerer and the bidder is the offeree. Each bid is an acceptance making a contract which is conditional upon no higher bid being received. After the first bid has been made, the bidder cannot withdraw it, nor can the auctioneer withdraw the goods so offered from the sale. 

EXAMPLE 1: A advertises "I offer my farm Blackacre for sale to the highest bidder and undertake to make conveyance to such person as submits the highest bid within the next thirty days."

This is an offer and each bid operates as an acceptance creating rights and duties conditional upon no higher bid being received within thirty days.

EXAMPLE 2: A advertises a sale of his household furniture 

"without reserve". An article is put up for sale and B is the highest bona fide bidder; but A, being dissatisfied with the bidding, either accepts a higher fictitious bid from an agent

1 employed for the purpose, or openly withdraws the article from the sale. In either case, A is bound by his contract with B to sell the article to B. But no one has any legal ground for complaint if A should withdraw the remainder of the furniture from the sale because a mere declaration of intention to sell goods before they have been actually put up for sale cannot, of itself, be construed as a binding contract to sell the goods. 

Anderson v Ry.107 Minn. 296,120 NW 39,20 LRANS 1133, Cost. 71 

4. OFFERS OF GUARANTY 

Ordinarily, in the formation of a unilateral contract, the offerer is the party making the promise, a compliance with the request in the offer fulfills the double function of a manifestation of acceptance and of giving consideration, and there is no occasion to notify the offerer of the acceptance of the offer, for the doing of the act is sufficient acceptance and the offerer is bound thereby. 

Bishop v Eaton (1894) 161 Mass. 496, 37 N.E.665, Costigan 171

When a party stipulates to do a certain thing in a certain specific event which may become known to him, or with which he can make himself acquainted, he is not entitled to any notice unless he stipulates for it; but when it is to do a thing which lies within the peculiar knowledge of the opposite party, then notice ought to be given him. 

Vinal v Richardson, (1866), 13 Allen 521 

Lascelles v Clark, (1910), 204 Mass. 362, 90 N. E. 875

Thus, where a guaranty is in the nature of an offer to make a unilateral contract and not pursuant to some previous understanding or arrangement, and no consideration is acknowledged in the instrument and none moves directly to the guarantor, and the circumstances of the parties and the transaction are not such as to indicate that knowledge of acceptance quickly will come to the guarantor, notice of acceptance must be given within a reasonable time in order that the guarantor may be held. 

Lane Bros.Co.v Sheinwald, (Mass., 1931), 175 N. E. 148

In continuing guaranties, and guaranties of future credits of uncertain amount, with or without a fixed limit, it is held that, when the transactions are effected, the guarantor is entitled to be informed of the extent to which the credit has been availed of. 

Vinal v Richardson, (1866), 13 Allen 521

Even then, it is not the notice which creates the contract but the lack of notice which ends the obligation. 

EXAMPLE 1: A writes to B: "Let C have 100 and I 

guarantee its repayment". Imediately upon receiving this letter, B lets C have $100 but fails to notify A of the fact, although B knows that A otherwise is not likely to learn of it. B cannot enforce the contract against A if C fails to pay the debt. 

Black v Grabow, (1913), 216 Mass. 516, 104 N. E. 346 

EXAMPLE 2: Suppose B, in Example 1, one hour after advanc-

TOQ M 

ти 

COTCHTY ALIJ 

090 TLA

ing the money, receives a letter from A revoking the offer of guaranty, and B subsequently, but promptly, notifies A of the advance. There is a binding contract of guaranty. A unilateral contract was formed by the advance of the money and since B sent notice thereof within a reasonable time thereafter, the contract is not discharged. 

Lennox v Murphy, (1898), 171 Mass. 570, 50 N. E. 644 

Jordan v Dobbins, (1877), 122 Mass. 168, Costigan 167 

5. OFFERS UNDER SEAL OR FOR CONSIDERATION 

An offer not under seal and not given for some consideration can be revoked at any time before acceptance, even though it is expressly stipulated that it shall remain open for a definite time. But an offer which is under seal, or given for a consideration, cannot be terminated during the time fixed in the offer itself or, if no time is fixed, during a reasonable time, either by revocation or by the offerer's death or insanity.

EXAMPLE: A offers to sell Blackacre to B for $5000 at any time within thirty days, and receives 100 for a promise to hold the offer open for that period. Nevertheless, he Sub- notifies B a few days later that the offer is withdrawn. sequently, but within the thirty days, B accepts the offer, and tenders 5000, which A refuses. There is a contract. The result would be the same if the offer were under seal, though not given for any consideration. 

Thomason v Bescher (1918)176 NC 622,97 SE 654,2 ALR 626, Cost.81

The offer in the above example is called an option. An option to purchase real estate is a unilateral contract by which the owner of the property agrees with the holder of the option that the latter has the right to buy the property according to the terms and conditions of the contract. By such an agreement, the owner does not sell the land, nor does he, at the time, contract to sell it. He does, however, agree that the person to whom the option is given shall have the right, at his election or option, to demand the conveyance in the manner specified. It is merely the right to an clection which has been sold and the owner of this right is not bound to complete the sale. 

Morgan v Forbes, (1920), 236 Mass. 480, 485, 128 N. E. 792 

Strong v Moore, (Ore., 1922), 207 Pac. 179, 25 A.L.R. 1217 

O'Brien v Boland, (1896), 166 ass. 481, 44 N. E. 602 

Mansfield v Hodgdon, (1888), 147 Mass. 304, 17 N. E. 544 

Chadsey v Condley, (1900), 62 Kan. 853, 62 Pac. 663 

EXAMPLE: In a state, like Massachusetts, where seals retain their common law effect, A offers Blackacre to B at a stated price in writing under seal. Subsequently, within a reasonable time for acceptance, A informs B that the offer is revoked. The offer is a contract, and the attempted revocation is ineffectual until a reasonable time has elapsed, at the end of which time, it is terminated automatically, unless previously accepted.

OFFERS TO THE PUBLIC 

An offer may be made to a specified person or persons, or to a class of persons, or to the public generally. 

Reif v Paige, (1882), 55 Wis. 496, 13 N. W. 473 

Spencer v Harding, (1870), L.R.5 C.P. 561, Costigan 51

If made to the public generally, it is made only to those of the public to whom it becomes known prior to their acceptance. 

Sears v Eastern R.R. (1867) 14 Allen 433, Costigan 53

In some offers to the public, the exercise of the power of acceptance by one person will extinguish the power of every other person to accept. In other cases, this will not be true. 

This question depends upon the nature and interpretation of the offer. 

EXAMPLE 1: A publishes an offer of reward to whoever will give certain information. This creates a power of acceptance in any person learning of the offer. B is the first of such persons to give the information. He has accepted the offer. 

Since the nature and reasonable interpretation of the offer is that the information is to be paid for only once, the giving of the information by B terminates the power of every other person to accept the offer. 

Symmes v Frazier, (1810), 6 ass. 344, Costigan 182 

Shuey v United States, (1875), 92 U.S. 73, Costigan 178 

Biggers v Owen, (1887), 79 Ga. 658, 5 S. W. 193, Costigan 174 

Besse v Dyer, (1864), 9 Allen 151 

Loring v Boston, (1844), 7 let. 409 

In re Kelly, (1872), 39 Conn. 159, Costigan 126

EXAMPLE 2: A offers 100 to any one who contracts a certain disease after using specified medicine as directed. 

B, C, and D use the medicine as directed and afterwards contract the disease. A has contracted to pay $100 to each of them. The acceptance by B will have no effect upon the power of C and D also to accept because they reasonably understand this to accord with the intention of A from the nature of the offer.

Carlill v Carbolic Smoke Ball Co., (1893),1 Q.B.256, Cost. 118 

According to the better rule, an offer of reward cannot be accepted, or the reward recovered, by one who performs the requested services in ignorance of the offer. 

Broadnax v Ledbetter (1907)100 Tex.575,99 SW 1111, 9 LRANS 1057 

Fitch v Snedaker, (1868), 38 N.Y. 248, Costigan 186, note 

Contra: Williams v Carwardine (1833)4 B.& Ad.621, Costigan 183 

See Crawshaw v Roxbury, (1856), 7 Gray 374 

7. OFFERS AS REFERRING TO LEGAL RELATIONS 

Where words appropriate to an offer are evidently used merely as a boast, or as an explosion of wrath, or in any other manner so that no reasonable person would be justified in taking them literally, no contract will result from an acceptance. But if a reasonable person would understand the words used as meaning that the speaker promised to do something if iven a requested exchange therefor, it is immaterial what intention the offerer may have had.

bodo OJ JUG 189

MITI

Higgins v Lessig, (1893), 49 Ill. App. 459, Costigan 48 

Reif v Paige, (1882), 55 Wis. 496, 13 N. W. 473 

Yale v Curtiss, (1897), 151 N.Y. 598, 45 N.E. 1125, Costigan 47 

Plate v Durst, (1896), 42 W.Va.63,24 S.E.580,32 L.R.A. 404

Ordinarily, the question whether a contract has been made is for the jury but if the evidence consists only of a writing, or is uncontradicted, it is for the court. 

Bresky v Rosenberg, (1926), 256 Mass. 66, 152 N.E. 347 

Brown v Grow, (1924), 249 Mass. 495, 144 N. E. 403

An offer, although formal and complete, cannot be the foundation of an agreement where it is made and accepted, not with the intention to contract, but as a mere jest or joke. 

But a person cannot set up that he was merely jesting when his conduct and words would warrant a reasonable person in believing that he intended a real agreement. 

McClurg v Terry, (1870), 21 N. J. Eq. 225 

Keller v Holderman, 11 Mich. 248 

Crouch v Wartenberg, (W.Va., 1920) 104 S.E.117, 11 A.L.R. 212 

13 C. J. 287 

8. INVITATIONS TO DEAL 

If a proposal is nothing more than an invitation to the person to whom it is made to make an offer to the proposer, it is not such an offer as can be turned into an agreement by acceptance. Proposals of this kind, although made to definite persons and not to the public generally, are merely invitations to trade; they go no further than what occurs when one asks another what he will give or take for certain goods. Such inquiries may lead to bargains but they do not make them. They ask for offers which the proposer has a right to accept or reject as he pleases. But where there is more than a mere quotation of prices, and the elements of an offer are present, the contract may be completed by acceptance. 

13 C. J. 288

If, from a promise, or expression of intention, or from the circumstances existing at the time, it should be reasonably apparent to the person to whom the statement or inquiry is addressed that the person making it does not intend it as an expression of his fixed purpose until he has given a further expression of assent, he has not made an offer. A mere reply to an inquiry as to the price of goods is not an offer to sell the goods to the inquirer at that price. 

Ashcroft v Butterworth, (1884), 136 Mass. 511, 514 

Thruston v Thornton, (1948), 1 Cush. 89 

Governors v M'Manamy, (1916) 2 Irish kep. 161, Costigan 218 

Perkins v Westcoat, (1893),3 Colo. App. 358, 33 Pac. 139 

Stamper v Temple, (1845), & Humphrey (Tenn.)113, Cost. 49, note 

Montgomery Ward v Johnson, (1911), 209 ass. 89, 95 N.E. 290

Invitations to deal occur frequently in circular letters and auvertisements of business houses seeking to sell goods, and should be distinguished from genuine offers to the public, such as are to be found in advertised offers of reward and in railroad time tables. A mere courtship, or even an intention to marry, is not sufficient to constitute an agreement of marriage. Acquaintance is essential to an intelligent contract to marry. The parties, therefore, may form an ac-

.

2005

quaintance and have a courtship without any necessary inference therefrom of a promise to marry. 

Yale v Curtiss, (1897), 151 N.Y.598,45 N.E.1125, Costigan 47

It is often difficult to draw an exact line between offers and negotiations preliminary thereto. It is common for one who wishes to make a bargain to try to induce the other party to the intended transaction to make a definite offer, he himself suggesting with more or less definiteness the nature of the contract he is willing to enter into. Besides any direct language indicating an intent to defer the formation of a cortract, the definiteness or indefiniteness of the words used in opening the negotiations must be considered and also the customs of business and, indeed, all surrounding circumstances. 

Moulton v Kershaw, (1884), 59 Wis.316,18 N.W.172, Costigan 57 

Smith v Gowdy, (1864),Ɛ Allen 566 

Cherokee Co.v W.U.Tel.Co.,(1906),143 NC 376,55 SE 777, Cost.56 

Boyers v Duke, (1905), 2 Irish R.617,3 B.R.C.220, Costigan 59 

Anderson v Ry.,107 Minn.296,120 NW 39,20 LRANS 1133, Cost.71 

Spencer v Harding, (1870), L.R.5 C.P. 561, Costigan 51 

Edge Moor Works v Bristol, (1898),170 Mass.528,49 NE 918 

Wheaton Co.v Boston, (1910), 204 Mass. 218,90 NE 598, Cost.202 

Lyman v Robinson, (1867), 14 Allen 242, 254 

Benton v Springfield, (1898),170 Mass. 534,49 NE 928, Cost.66 

DeVito v Boehme & Rauch Co. (1921), 239 Mass. 290,132 N.E. 35 

Harsh v Nebraska Seed Co.,98 Neb.89,152 NW 310, LRA 1915F 824 

See Horne v Niver, (1897),168 Mass.4,46 N.E.393, Costigan 113

EXAMPLE 1: A merchant advertises overcoats of a certain kind for sale at 50. This is not an offer to sell but an invitation to the public to make offers to buy. 

EXAMPLE 2: A advertises that he will pay 5 for every copy of a certain book that may be sent to him. This is an offer, and A is bound to pay $5 for every copy sent to him while the offer is unrevoked.

EXAMPLE 3: A corporation or municipality bids, or tenders, for certain specified work. offer but a request for offers. advertises for 

This is not an 

EXAMPLE 4: A writes to B: "I can quote you flour at 35 a barrel in carload lots." This is not an offer, and A is under no obligation to reserve flour for B. The word "quote" and the incompleteness of the terms indicate that the writer is simply naming a current price which he is demanding. 

EXAMPLE 5: A writes to B: "I am eager to sell my house. 

I wish to get $20,000 for it". B answers promptly: "I will buy your house at the price you name in your letter." There is no contract. A's letter is a mere suggestion or request that an offer be made to him. The only offer is from B, which was never accepted.

C. THE ACCEPTANCE 

1. 

NECESSITY AND EFFECT OF ACCEPTANCE 

An offer, before it will become a binding promise

I" 31

and result in a contract, must be accepted. 

Tucker v Woods, (1815),12 Johnson (N.Y.) 190, Costigan 836 

Ball v Newton, (1851), 7 Cush. 599 

Real Estate Ins. Co. v Roessle, (1854), 1 Gray 336 

Thruston v Thornton, (1848), 1 Cush. 91 

See Tinn v Hoffman, (1873), 29 L.T.N.S. 271, Costigan 157

EXAMPLE: D an insurance agent, wrote to P that he would renew P's fire insurance policy when it expired, unless notified to the contrary. P, in reliance thereon, did not reply, and D neglected to renew. D is not liable upon P's suffering a loss since the alleged agreement lacks mutuality. 

D's letter was a mere offer which had not been accepted. 

Prescott v Jones, (1898), 69 N. H. 305, 41 Atl. 352

Acceptance changes the character of an offer and makes it a promise. Upon performance of the overt act of acceptance, the contract springs into immediate existence. 

Earle v Angell, (1892), 157 Mass. 294, 32 N. E. 164 

Brackenbury v Hodgkin, (1917),116 Ife.399,102 Atl.106, Cost.176 

The Satanita, (1895), Court of Appeals (Eng.)248,Costigan 68 

Cary Library v Bliss, (1890), 151 ¡lass.364,25 NE 92,27 LRA 765 

Cf. Post v Frank, (1912), 152 N.Y.Supp.807, Costigan 125

The person or persons in whom are created a power of acceptance are to be determined by the reasonable interpretation of the offer. The offer can be accepted only by the person or persons to whom it is made. 

Boston Ice Co.v Potter, (1877), 125 Muss.28, Costigan 574 

Boulton v Jones, (1857), 2 H. & N. 564, Costigan 222 

Orcutt v Nelson, (1854), 1 Gray 536 

2. COMMUNICATION OF ACCEPTANCE 

Acceptance must be more than mere mental assent or mere determination to accept. Such determination must be manifested by the conduct of the offeree. 

Felthouse v Bindley, (1862),11 C.B.N.S.868, Costigan 244 

Ferrier v Storer, (1884), 63 Iowa 484,19 N.W.288, Costigan 128 

Mactier v Frith, (1830), 6 Wendell (N.Y.) 103, Costigan 130

The unexpressed or uncommunicated intention of one party to a contract is not binding upon the other party. 

Farnum v Whitman, (1904), (1904), 187 Mass. 381, 73 N. E. 473 

3. ACCEPTANCE IN BILATERAL CONTRACTS 

A bilateral contract, by definition, consists of mutual promises. In general, if a promise is requested, no contract exists until that promise is expressly or impliedly given. But this statement must be qualified in a single case, namely: if an offer requests a promise from the offeree and the offeree, without making the promise, actually does what he was requested to promise to do, there is a contract, subject to the requirement of notice in cases of offers of guaranty, provided such performance is completed or tendered within the time allowed for accepting by the making of a promise. 

The reason for this rule is that if, within the time allowed for promising to accept offer, full performance has been given, the offerer has received something better than he asked for, he is bound, since the only object of requiring a promise is

12

ultimately to obtain performance of it. Merely beginning to perform within the time allowed for accepting will not amount to an acceptance because the offerer has not manifested a willingness to be bound unless given assurance that performance will be completed. 

McCrillis v White Shoe Co., (1928),264 Mass.32,161 N.E. 902 

Springfield v Harris, (1871), 107 Lass. 532 

Cottage Street Church v Kendall, (1877),121 Mass.528,530 

Wellington v Apthorp, (1887),145 Mass.69,74,13 N.E. 10 

EXAMPLE 1: A writes to B: "I will pay you $100 for plowing Flodden Field if you will promise by next Monday to finish the work before the following Saturday." B makes no promise but notifies A on Monday that he has completed the work. There is a binding unilateral contract.

EXAMPLE 2: D, desiring to have his offices fitted up, submitted certain specifications to P, who gave D an estimate of the cost which D took under advisement, no contract having as yet been entered into. D left a note for P: "Upon ar agreement to fit up my offices in two weeks from date, you can begin at once. The writer will call again between 5 and 6 this afternoon." P did not reply to this note but began performance immediately by the purchase of lumber and by commencing work thereon. The next day, D countermanded the note. 

There was no contract. D's offer requested an agreement or promise and the act of beginning to perform was not an acceptance of the offer. 

White v Corlies, (1871), 46 N.Y. 467, Costigan 212 

Williston on Contracts, section 75

Where an offer is made by one party to another, where they are not together, it is not necessary that the acceptance of the offer shall come to the knowledge of the offerer before he will be bound. But, although this is true, yet the offerer is not bound unless the offer is accepted in the mode either expressly or impliedly required by the offerer. If the offerer requires or suggests a mode of acceptance which turns out, so far as giving actual notice to the offerer, to be insufficient or entirely nugatory, it is the fault of the offerer, and the agreement is complete. 

13 C. J. 285

Thus, a letter by mail containing an offer may be answered by mailing a letter of acceptance unless a different method of acceptance is required by the terms of the offer; and, in general, if the mail is the authorized method of communication the contract is concluded as soon as the letter of acceptance is mailed. 

White v Corlies, (1871), 46 N. Y. 467, Costigan 212 

4. ACCEPTANCE BY MAIL 

An acceptance may be transmitted by any means which the offerer has authorized the offeree to use and, if so transmitted, becomes a valid acceptance and completes the contract as soon as put out of the offeree's possession without regard to whether it ever reaches the offerer. And it is not important that the offeree has the power to withdraw his acceptance from the mail.

no 100.00

- 

Henthorn v Fraser, (1892), 2 Ch. 27, Costigan 106 

Wheat v Cross, (1869), 31 Ma. 99, Costigan 252 

Myers v Moore-Kile Co., (1922), 279 Fed. 233, 25 A.L.R. 1 

Federal Co.v Zimmermann, 171 Wis.594, 177 NW 881,25 ALR 5 

Swampscott Co. v Rice, (1893), 159 lass. 404, 34 N. E. 520 

See Traders' Bank v Bank, 142 Tenn.229,217 SW 977,9 ALR 382 

EXAMPLE 1: A makes an offer by mail, adding "telegraph your answer". B promptly telegraphs an acceptance. The telegram never reaches A. There is a contract as soon as the telegram is delivered to the telegraph company by the offeree. 

EXAMPLE 2: A makes an offer by mail to B, not specifying how the acceptance may be. sent, and B promptly sends an acceptance by his own servant, or by special messenger. There is no contract until the acceptance is delivered to the offerer or to some person by him authorized to receive it in his behalf. 

EXAMPLE 3: P, desiring to insure his home in Maryland for 

8000, inquired the cost of D's agent. On Dec. 2nd, D's agent wrote to P in Alabama, where P then was, giving the information requested, and adding, "Should you desire to effect the above insurance, send me your check for 57 and the business is concluded". This letter, having been måsdirected, did not reach P until Dec. 20th and, on the next day (Dec. 21st), P sent his check for $57 to D's agent as requested. This letter reached D's agent on Dec. 31st but, in the meantime, on Dec. 

22nd, P's home was destroyed by fire. There was a binding contract made on Dec. 21st, when the letter of acceptance was mailed. 

Tayloe v erchants' Ins. Co., (1850), 9 Howard 390, Costigan 253

It is almost, if not quite, the universal rule, in offer and acceptance by mail, that the contract is created when the acceptance is sent and not when it is received. The argument against this rule is that the offerer ought not to be bound by his offer until he has received notice that his offer has been accepted. But if this argument were sound, then the offeree equally ought not to be bound until he has received notice that the offerer has received the acceptance and assented to it.

And so it might go on ad infinitum with the result that no contract ever could be completed by the post. The offerer must be considered in law as making, during every instant of time his letter is travelling, the same identical offer to the offeree and the contract is completed by the sending of the acceptance by the latter.

Household Ins. Co.v Grant(1879)L.R.4 Exche.Div.216, Costigan 90 

Averill v Hedge, (1838), 12 Conn. 424, Costigan 142 

Dunlop v Higgins, (1848), 1 H. L. Cas. 381, Costigan 86 

Lactier v Frith, (1830), 6 Wendell (N.Y.) 103, Costigan 130

EXAMPLE: D wrote a letter to P offering to sell certain wool to P at a specified price, "receiving your answer in course of post". P accepted the offer by mail on the day it was received. The letter containing the offer was misdirected so that it was not received until two days later than it would have been received in the usual course of post. D waited

M until the usual time for a reply and, receiving none, sold the wool to another. There was a binding contract with P. As to the delay in the receipt of the acceptance, that arose entirely from the mistake of the offerer and it, therefore, must be taken as against him that the acceptance was sent in due course of post, and it is inmaterial when it was received or whether it was ever received. 

Adams v Lindsell, (1818), B. & Anderson 651, Costigan 85

This rule, of course, does not apply where the offer expressly or by implication stipulates that the contract is to be complete when the acceptance is received. The person making the offer may always, if he chooses, make the formation of the contract dependent upon the actual communication to himself of the acceptance. 

Haldane v United States, (1895), 69 Fed. 819, Costigan 111 

Union Bank v Miller, (1890), 106 NC 347, 11 SE 321, Costigan 115

EXAMPLE: P made an offer to D by mail, requesting a reply by telegraph either "yes" or "no" and adding: "If I do not hear from you by the 18th or 20th of July, I shall conclude 'no".

D sent a telegram to P on July 17th, accepting the offer but, due to the fault of the telegraph company, it was never delivered to P. There was no contract. 

Lewis v Browning, (1881), 130 ass. 173 5.

ACCEPTANCE BY TELEGRAPH AND TELEPHONE 

It is now well established that the same rule applies to a contract by telegraph as to a contract by mail and that a contract is completed as soon as a telegram of acceptance, in response to an offer by telegram, is deposited with the telegraph company for transmission to the offerer. 

Lucas v W.U.Tel.Co. (1906) 131 Iowa 669,109 N.W.191,6 LRANS 1016 

See Ayer v W.U.Tel.Co.(1887)79 Me.493,10 Atl.495, Costigan 238

Acceptance by telephone is governed by the principles applicable to contracts between parties who are in the presence of each other. If the parties are in different states, the contract is made in the state where the offeree is at the time he makes his acceptance. 

Carow Co.v "Ed. McWilliams" (Can.1919)49 Dom.L.R.506, Cost.117 6.

SUFFICIENCY OF ACCEPTANCE a. IN GENERAL 

There is no doubt that, unless the offer stipulates to the contrary, the person making the offer authorizes the use of the same medium for transmitting the acceptance that was employed in transmitting the offer. 

EXAMPLE: À sent, by mail, an offer dated from his house, to B, stating that the acceptance must be received within three days. B mailed an acceptance which reached A's house and was delivered to a servant, or was deposited in a mail box at A's door within three days, but A had been called away from home and did not receive the letter for a week. There is a contract.

3015

It has also been held that no other medium is authorized under these circumstances. 

Lucas v W.U.Tel.Co. (1906) 131 Iowa 669,109 NW 191,6 LRANS 1016

But this theory has been opposed by several cases. 

Henthorn v Fraser, (1892), 2 Ch. 27, Costigan 106 

EXAMPLE: P, when in Boston, received an oral offer from D to purchase certain goods, and requested that the offer be held open until the next day to which D assented. The next day, P telegraphed an acceptance from Providence. It was held that the contract was made in Rhode Island as soon as the telegram was intrusted to the telegraph company for transmission. The court said that if there be any question that the telegraph is a natural and ordinary method of transmitting such an acceptance, that is a question of fact for the jury. 

Perry v Mt. Hope Iron Co., (1886), 15 R. I. 380, 5 Atl. 632

An acceptance which is inoperative when sent because the offeree has used a means of transmission which he was not authorized to employ by the terms of the offer will be operative when received, if it was received by the offerer within the time in which an acceptance sent in an authorized manner probably would have been received by him. 

Trounstine v Sellers, (1886), 35 Kan. 447, 11 Pac. 441 

Williston on Contracts, section 87 

See Eliason v Henshaw (1819)4 Wheaton (U.S.)225, Cost.116, note

An acceptance sent by mail, or otherwise from a distance, is not valid when dispatched unless it is properly addressed and any other precaution taken which is ordinarily observed to insure the safe transmission of similar messages. In order to be properly mailed, a letter must be properly addressed, with postage fully prepaid, and deposited in the post-offece, or in a post-office box or mail chute, or delivered to a proper pastoffice agent or official. 

Maclay v Harvey, (1878), 90 111. 525, Costigan 114, note 

Johnson v Brown, (1891), 154 Mass. 105, 27 N. E. 994 

Pearce v Langfit, 101 Pa. 507

If this is done, it is not only a sufficient sending by mail but also prima facie evidence of the receipt of the letter by the offerer because of the presumption of fact founded on the probability that the officers of government will do their duty and that the letter will be duly delivered. 

Tobin v Taintor, (1917), 229 Mass. 174, 118 N. E. 247 

Bank v Harding Co.(S.D.,1929)225 N.W. 721, 63 A.L.R. 925 

Briggs v Hervey, (1881), 130 Mass. 186 

Huntley v Whittier, (1870), 105 Mass. 391 

Swampscott Co. v Rice, (1893), 159 Mass. 404) 34 N. E. 520

In view of such evidence and presumption, it cannot be said as a matter of law that the letter did not reach the offerer, notwithstanding his testimony that he did not receive it. It is then a question of fact for the jury. 

Shea v N.Y.N.H.& H.R.R., (1899), 173 Mass. 177, 53 N. E. 396 

Elliott v Baker, (1907), 194 Mass. 518, 80 N. E. 450

TOMU OF SCIE

b.

ACCEPTANCE LUST BE UNEQUIVOCAL 

An acceptance must be unequivocal in order to create a contract. An offerer is entitled to know in clear and unambiguous terms whether the offeree accepts his proposal. 

It is not enough that the words of the reply justify a probable inference of assent. 

EXAMPLE 1: A sent an order for goods to B. B replied that the order would receive his prompt attention. There is no contract. 

Krohn-Fechheimer v Palmer, (Mo.1920), 221 SW 353,10 A.L.R.673

EXAMPLE 2: A wrote to B, offering to sell a lot of land. 

B replied, "I shall meet you with the money in a few days and be ready to arrange particulars". There is no contract. 

Thurber v Smith, (1903), 25 R. I. 60,54 At1.790, Costigan 155 

Tinn v Hoffman, (1873), 29 L.T.N.S. 271, Costigan 157 

See Pickering v Piekering, (1833), 6 N. H. 120, Costigan 254

EXAMPLE 3: A debtor, shortly before his debt became payable, wrote to the creditor, asking for an extension of twelve months, and received the reply, "I am satisfied with the determination you express to secure and to pay your indebtedness at a stated time. But so small an amount as mine I should feel willing to extend twelve months. If you have many creditors here, there ought to be a concert of action between you and them". This was held to be an equivocal reply which did not show an assent to the proposed extension. 

Palmer v Williams, (1859), 13 Gray 338 C.

ACCEPTANCE VARYING FROM OFFER 

A valid acceptance must comply exactly with the terms of the offer, omitting nothing from the promise or performance requested. 

EXAMPLE 1: A published an offer of reward for the "apprehension and conviction" of a certain person. B gave information leading to the apprehension and, subsequently, C gave information necessary for the conviction. Neither B nor C is entitled to the reward; nor are they entitled to it jointly unless they acted jointly. 

EXAMPLE 2: A offered B 5 for a book. B promptly promised A that he would deliver the book. There is no contract, since A requested delivery of the book, not a mere promise to deliver it. 

Mactier v Frith, (1830), 6 Wendell 103, Costigan 130 

Sterling v Watson Co., (1916), 193 Mich. 11, 159 N. W. 381 

Shady Hill Co. v Waterer, (1901), 179 Mass. 318, 60 N. E. 789

Thus, an acceptance by mail is insufficient if the telegraph is the mode prescribed. 

Horne v Niver, (1897), 168 Mass. 4,46 N.E.393, Costigan 113

Frequently an offeree, while making a positive acceptance of the offer, adds as a request or suggestion that some addition or modification be made. So long as it is clear that the meaning of the acceptance is positively and unequivocally to

accept the offer whether such request is granted or not, a contract is formed. 

Nelson v Hamlin, (1927), 258 Mass. 31; 155 N. E. 18 

EXAMPLE: D had been negotiating for the purchase of P's hay. Finally, D wrote to F, offering $9.50 per ton. P wrote in reply, "You can take the hay at your offer and when you have got it hauled in, if you can pay $10 a ton, I would like to have you do it if the hay proves good enough for the price." 

There is an absolute and unconditional acceptance and a binding contract. 

Phillips v Moor, (1880), 71 Me. 78, Williston's Cases 173 d.

LATE ACCEPTANCE 

A late acceptance is no acceptance. An offerer who receives an acceptance which is too late, or which is otherwise defective, cannot, at his election, regard it as valid. The late or defective acceptance is a counter-offer which, in order to create a contract, must in turn be accepted by the original offerer who has now become the offeree as to the counter offer.

An acceptance after the time limited by the express terms of the offer or, in the absence of an express limitation, after the lapse of a reasonable time, imposes no obligation on the offerer. The offer, unless sooner withdrawn, stands during the time limited or, if there is no express limitation, during a reasonable time. Until the end of that time, the offer is regarded as being constantly repeated. 

Adams v Lindsell, (1818), B.& Anderson 681, Costigan 85

After that, there is no offer and nothing to withdraw. 

The time having expired, there is nothing which the offeree can do to revive the offer or produce an extension of time. 

Ferrier v Storer, (1884), 63 Iowa 484,19 N.W.288, Costigan 128 

Union Bank v Miller, (1890), 106 NC 347,11 SE 321, Costigan 115 

Averill v Hedge, (1828), 12 Conn. 424, Costigan 142 

Minneapolis Ry. v Columbus Mills, (1886), 119 U.S. 149 

Maclay v Harvey, (1878), 90 Ill. 525, Costigan 114, note 

EXAMPLE: An offer, made May 16, 1884, to buy certain stock "at any time after Jan. 1, 1886, if at that time you desire to have me do so, not because I want the stock but solely from a feeling of friendship," and an acceptance July 9, 1886, constitute no contract. The acceptance should have been made known by January 1, 1886 or at least within a reasonable time thereafter. 

Park v Whitney, (1889), 148 Hass.278, 19 N. E. 161 •。 

COUNTER OFFERS 

An offer must be accepted in the terms in which it is made in order to become a binding contract, and a conditional acceptance or one that varies from the offer in any substantial respect is in effect a rejection and is the equivalent of a new proposition. 

Moss v Old Colony Trust Co., (1923),246 Mass.139,140 N.E. 803

There is no counter-offer unless the expression is such as to create a power of acceptance in the original offerer. 

This distinguishes a counter-offer from a mere inquiry regard-

- ing the possibility of different terms, a request for a better offer, or a comment upon the terms of the offer. There is also no counter-offer if, at the time of making the new proposition, the offeree states in express terms that he is keeping the original offer under advisement. 

Stevenson v McLean, (1880), 5 Q. B. D. 346, Costigan 150, note 

EXAMPLE 1: A offered to sell Blackacre to B for $5000, stating that the offer will remain oren for thirty days. B replies, "I will pay 4800 for Blackacre", and, on A's declining, B again writes, "I accept your offer to sell for $5000".

There is no contract although B's second reply was made within the time limit fixed in A's offer. 

Hyde v Wrench, (1840), 3 Beavan 334, Costigan 148 

McManus v Fortescue, (1907), 2 K. B. 1, Costigan 165 

Baker v Holt, (1882), 56 Wis. 100, 14 N. W. 8

EXAMPLE 2: A makes the same offer as in Example 1, and B 

replies, "Won't you take less?" to which A answers, "No". An acceptance thereafter by B will make a binding contract, if made while A's offer is still outstanding.

EXAMPLE 3: A makes the same offer as in Example 1, and B 

replies, "I am keeping your offer under advisement but, if you wish to close the mutter at once, I will give you 4800". A does not reply and B then accepts the offer of A within the thirty days before A has revoked it. There is a contract. f. 

QUALIFIED OR CONDITIONAL ACCEPTANCE 

Where a person offers to do a definite thing and another accepts conditionally or introduces a new term into the acceptance, his answer is either a mere expression of willingness to treat or it is a counter proposal, and in neither case is there an agreement. 

13 C. J. 281 

Putnam v Grace, (1894), 161 Mass. 237, 37 N. E. 166 

Harlow v Curtis, (1876), 121 Mass. 320 

Metropolitan Coal v Boutell Co., (1904), 185 Mass. 391,70 N.E. 421

EXAMPLE: A made an offer to B which B accepted but added, "Prompt acknowledgment must be made of receipt of this letter". 

There is no contract but a qualified acceptance.

But an acceptance which merely states as an express condition of the acceptance, that which would be implied as a matter of law from the terms of the offer, is not a qualified acceptance. 

EXAMPLE: A offered in writing tɔ sell Blackacre to B for a certain price. B replies, "I accept your offer if you can convey me a good title." This is an absolute and not a conditional acceptance.

An acceptance which merely suggests a change or addition to the terms of the offer is not invalidated thereby unless the acceptance is made to depend upon the changed or added terms.

Dunlop v Higgins, (1848), 1 H. L. Cases 381, Costigan 86

EXAMPLE: A offered to sell to B 100 tons of steel at a certain price. B replied, "I accept your offer. I hope that if you can arrange to deliver the steel in weekly installments of 25 tons, you will do so". There is a contract and A is not bound to deliver in installments.

Thus, if an offer prescribes the place, time, or manner of its acceptance, its terms must be complied with in order to create a contract. But if an offer merely suggests the place, time, or manner of acceptance, another method of acceptance is not precluded. 

Stevenson v McLean, (1880), 5 Q. B. D. 346, Costigan 150, note 

EXAMPLE 1: A wrote an offer to B, adding, "I must receive your acceptance by return mail". An acceptance sent by any other means which reached A as soon as a letter sent by return mail will create a contract.

EXAMPLE 3: A offered to sell his land to B on certain terms, saying, "You must accept this, if at all, in person at my office at 10 o'clock tomorrow". B's power of acceptance is strictly limited to a single method. 

EXAMPLE 4: A offered to sell his land to B on certain terms, saying, "You may accept by leaving word at my house".

This indicates one operative mode of acceptance but B's power to accept is not limited to that method alone. A personal notice would be just as well.
