ACCEPTANCE IN UNILATERAL CONTRACTS 

An acceptance of an offer may be by act, as where an offer is made that the offerer will pay or do something else if the offeree shall do a particular thing. In such a case performance of the overt act is the only thing needful to complete the agreement and to create a binding promise. To make performance of a thing proposed sufficient as an acceptance of the proposal, performance must have been induced by the proposal, and must be in accordance therewith. 

13 C. J. 275 

Mott v Jackson, (1911), 172 Ala. 448, 55 So. 528 

Bornstein v Lans, (1892), 104 Mass. 214 

Smith v Edwards, (1892), 156 Mass. 221, 30 N. E. 1017

Carlill v Carbolic Smoke-Ball Co., (1893),1 Q.B.256, Cost. 118 

First Nat. Bank v Watkins, (1891), 154 Mass. 385, 28 N.E. 275 

Lennox v Murphy, (1898), 171 Mass. 370, 50 N. E. 644

Mauger v Crosby, (1875), 117 Mass. 330 

See Wright v Sonoma County, (1909),156 Cal.475,105 Pac.469 

EXAMPLE 1: A offered a reward for information leading to the conviction of a criminal. B gave the information under circumstances compelling the conclusion that he was not induced to do so by the offer of reward but was actuated solely by motives of fear or public duty. There is no contract. 

EXAMPLE 2: If B, in Example 1, while actuated by motives of fear or public duty, would have given the information without any hope of reward, yet, knowing that the offer was outstanding, gave the information with intent to accept the offer, there is a contract. 

Broadnax v Ledbetter (1907) 100 Tex.375,99SW 1111,9 LRANS 1057 

Fitch v Snedaker, (1868), 38 N.Y. 248, Costigan 186, note 

Contra: Williams v Carwardine (1833) 4 B.& Ad.621, Cos tigan 183 

See Crawshaw v Roxbury, (1856), 7 Gray 374

Where the offeree exercises dominion over property which has been offered to him for purchase, such dominion is an acceptance, in the absence of other circumstances showing a contrary intention. If the circums tances show that the exercise of dominion is tortious, then the offerer, at his option, may treat such dominion as an accepta nce whatever may have been the intenti on manifested by the offeree. 

Carnahan v Beebe-Bowles (1916) 80 Or.124,156 Pac.584,Cost.250 

See Felthouse v Bindley(1862) 11 C.B.N.S.868,Costigan 244 

Bowley v Fuller, (Me.,1921), 115 Atl. 466, 24 A. L. R. 964 

Taylor v Dexter Engine Co., (1888), 146 Mass. 613,16 N.E.462 'Donnell v Clinton, (1888), 145 Mass. 461, 14 N. E. 747 

Ostman v Lee,(1917),91 Conn. 731, 101 Atl. 23, Costigan 251 

See Wheeler v Klaholt, (1901),178 Mass.141,59 N.E.756,Cost.247

EXAMPLE: A sends to B a one-volume edition of Shakespeare with a letter, saying, "If you wish to buy this book, send me $6.50 within one week after receipt hereof, otherwise notify me and I will forward postage for return". (1) B examines the book and, without replying, makes a gift of it to his wife. He owes A $6.50. (2) B examines the book and, without replying, carefully lays it on a shelf to await A's messenger. 

There is no contract. (3) B examines the book and uses it or gives it to his wife, and writes to A at the same time that he has taken the book but that it is worth only $5 and that he will pay no more. A may, at his option, treat B as a tort-feasor and sue him in tort for conversion, or he may sue him in contract for $6.50.

D. THE REVOCATION 1. IN GENERAL 

In ordinary cases, a party making an offer may withdraw or modify it before it is accepted.

Cevacation!! еси batorex9 930 AC 20120 0114

Foster v Boston, (1839), 22 Pick.33, 38

Craig v Harper, (1849), 3 Cush. 158 

He may do this even though he has expressly declared that he will not withdraw it, or has, by the very terms of his offer, allowed the offeree a certain number of days within which to accept it, as in the case of options or refusals, unless the offer is under seal, or unless the agreement to hold it open is supported by a consideration. But the offer is a continuing one until it is withdrawn, and the withdrawal communicated; and if it is accepted, and the acceptance is communicated before the offer is withdrawn and notice thereof given, and within the time expressly or impliedly limited, the agreement is complete, and the offer is no longer revocable. 

Wheat v Cross, (1869),31 Md. 99, Costigan 252 

Elliot v Kasajian, (1926), 255 Mass. 459, 152 N. E. 351 

Adams v Lindsell, (1818), B & Anderson 681, Costigan 85 

Boston & M. R. R. v Bartlett, (1849), 3 Cush. 244, Costigan 79 over-ruling Cooke v Oxley, (1790), 3 T. R. 653, Costigan 77 

2. COMMUNICATION OF REVOCATION 

It is almost universally settled that a revocation requires communication and that, therefore, an acceptance made prior to a communicated revocation will make a binding contract.

A state of mind not notified cannot be regarded and an uncommunicated revocation is, for all practical pruposes, and in the law generally, no revocation at all. If the contrar y were true, no person who had received an offer by post and had mailed his acceptance would know his position until he had waited such a time as to be quite sure that a letter withdrawing the offer had not been posted before his acceptance was mailed. 

Byrne v Van Tienhoven, (1880),L.R.5 C.P.D.344,Costigan 103

EXAMPLE: A made by mail an offer to B and subsequently by mail revoked the offer. Before receiving the revocation, however, B mailed an acceptance. The revocation is ineffectual and, although, on receiving the revocation, B should assume that there was no contract and change his position in reliance on that assumption, A is not precluded from asserting a contract for, when B changed his position, he know all the facts and his mistake is a mistake of law which does not excuse him. 

Henthorn v Fraser, (1892), 2 Ch. 27, Costigan 106 

Wheat v Cross, (1869), 31 Md. 99, Costigan 252 

Farmers' Co. v Newcomb (1916) 192 Mich.634,159 N.W.152,Cost.164

In Massachusetts, however, the law is unsettled, since the only contrary decision which has not been overruled, seems to be a Massachusetts case. 

Williston on Contracts, section 81, note 98

M'Cullock v Eagle Ins. Co., (1882), 1 Pick. 278 

This was an action of contract on an insurance policy. 

On Jan. 1, 1820, D offered by mail to insure P's brig for a certain sum. On Jan. 2, D mailed a revocation of the offer.

On Jan. 3, P received the offer, and mailed his acceptance. 

Subsequently, the revocation was received by P. The court

MCullock Cars 

Ins ببند 10 さす ​00 LIDDI LOIJ Abou 11.00

Das Jedt no

held that there was no contract because the revocation was sent before the acceptance was sent.

This is a very early case, decided over one hundred years ago, and apparently the court was governed in its decision by the actual state of mind of the offerer at the time he received the acceptance but this is held to be immaterial in all other jurisdictions. hether this case would now be followed in 

Massachusetts may be doubted. 

See Commonwealth Ins. Co.v Knabe (1898)171 Mass. 265,50 N.E.516 

Another Massachusetts case should be distinguished carefully from the M'Culloch decision. 

Brauer v Shaw, (1897), 168 Mass. 198, 4€ N.E. €17 

At 11:30, L telegraphed an offer to P. At 12:10, the offer was received. At 12:28, F telegraphed an acceptance. 

At 1:00, D telegraphed a revocation. At 1:20, the acceptance was received. At 1:43, the revocation was received. The court held this to be a valid contract. 

It should be noted that Brauer v Shaw does not and cannot overrule the M'Culloch Case because the facts in the two cases are not the same. There is no modern case in Massachusetts which decides the question whether an acceptance, which is sent after the revocation has been sent but before the revocation has been received, makes a binding contract. The M'Culloch case holds that it does not, contrary to the rule in all other jurisdictions. Erauer v Chaw does not decide that point because the facts of that case did not permit of a decision on that point, since there the acceptance was sent before the revocation was sent. It is true that Judge Holmes stated in 

Brauer v Shaw that a revocation ought to be received before it could become operative and that an acceptance sent at any time previous to the receipt of the revocation should make a binding contract, but that statement is a mere dictum which, however strongly one may agree with it in principle, cannot be relied on as the law of the case, since it was not the question there in issue. Brauer v Shaw is authority merely for the principle that if an acceptance is sent before a revocation is sent, there is a binding contract.

Formal notice of revocation, however, is not always necessary. It is sufficient that the person making the offer does some act inconsistent with it, as for example, selling the property to another, and that the person to whom the offer was made has knowledge of such transaction. 

13 C. J. 29€

EXAMPLE: A offers Blackacre to B at a stated price and gives B a week in which to consider the proposal. Two days later, E learns that A has contracted to sell Blackacre to C but, nevertheless, sends a formal acceptance to A thereafter within the week. There is no contract. 

Watters v Lincoln, (1912), 29 S.E.98, 135 S..712, Costigan 188 

Dickinson v Dodds, (1876),2 Ch.D. 463, Williston's Cases €1 

Coleman v Applegarth, (1887), 68 d. 21, 11 Atl. 284

School Directors v Trefethren, 101 Ill. App. 127

​OTA

- 3. 

RECEIPT OF REVOCATION 

A written revocation, rejection, or acceptance is received when the writing comes into the possession of the person addressed, or of some person authorized to receive it for him, or is deposited in some place which he has authorized the place for this or similar communications to be deposited for him. Under this definition, a written communication may be received though it is not read or though it does not even reach the hands of the person to whom it is addressed. 

EXAMPLE: A sent an offer by mail to B, but later telegraphed a revocation. The messenger boy carrying the telegram from the receiving office met C, a neighbor of D. C volunteered to give the telegram to B but forgot to deliver it to B until the following morning. If B should mail an acceptance before the telegram of revocation is delivered to him, there would be a binding contract, under the general rule. 

4. REVOCATION OF OFFERS TO THE ULIC 

A general offer to the public may be revoked and the revocation be effective without actual notice to the party who may afterward attempt to accept it without knowing of its revocation or withdrawal. It is only essential that the revocation be made in the same way as the offer was made and the latter thereupon comes to an end. 

13 C. J. 29€

EXAMPLE: A posts a notice in a post-office, offering a reward for the apprehension of D, a criminal. Before the offer has been accepted, A posts another notice in the same place, stating that he withdraws the offer. This is a sufficient revocation. 

Shuey v United States, (1875), 92 U. S. 75, Costigan 178 

Sears v Eastern R. I. (1867), 14 Allen 433, Costigan 53 

Biggers v Owen, (1887), 79 Ga. €58, 5 S. . 193, Costigan 174 

5. OFFERS CONTEMPLATING SEVERAL ACCE. TANCES 

An offer contemplating a series of independent contracts by separate acceptances may be revoked as to the future though one or more of the contracts have been formed already by the offeree's acceptance. An offer may propose several contracts to arise at separate times. Such an offer is divisible and the power to revoke continues pari passu with the continuing power of the offeree to accept. 

Chicago R. I. v Dane, (1870), 43 N. Y. 240, Costigan 209 

EXAMPLE 1: A makes an offer to to buy ten tons of steel from him daily. The amount is furnished daily for a number of days. A unilateral contract is formed thereby each day. A revocation of the offer by A would prevent the formation of any contracts thereafter. But if A's proposal had been to buy ten tons daily during the ensuing month and it was promptly accepted by B, there would be a valid bilateral contract for one month and there could be no revocation of the offer during that period. 

Great Northern Ry. vitham, (1973),L.R. 9 C. F. 16, Costigan 20€ 

Williston on Contracts, section 58

EXAMPLE 2: A offers C to guarantee the payment of all bills of exchange drawn by B and discounted by C. C discounts one such bill. A is bound to pay it. A then notifies C that the guaranty is withdrawn. A is not bound to pay bills subsequently discounted by C. offord v Davies, (1862), 12 C.B.N.S. 748, Costigan 168, note 

Jordan v Dobbins, (1877), 122 Mass. 168, Costigan 167 

Grob v Gross, (1912), 83 N.J.L. 430, 84 Atl. 1064 

Wm. Filene's Sons Co. v. Lothrop (1922) 243 Mass. 214, 137

E. OTHER METHODS OF TERMINATING OFFERS 1. IN GENERAL 

An offer may be terminated not only by a revocation by the offerer but also (a) by its rejection by the offeree, (b) by lapse of time, (c) by death, or (d) insanity of either party. Where an offer is terminated in any one of these ways, a contract cannot be created by a subsequent acceptance of the offer. 2.

REJECTION OF OFFER 

If an offer is rejected, either by an absolute refusal or by an acceptance conditionally, or not identical with the terms of the offer, or by a counter proposal, the party making the original offer is relieved from liability on that offer. 

Hyde v French (1840), 3 Beavan 334, Costigan 148 

Neer v Lang, (1918), 252 Fed. 575, Costigan 150 

Tinn v Hoffman, (1873), 29 L.T.N.Š. 271, Costigan 157· 

Minneapolis Ry. v Columbus Mill, (1886), 119 U.S. 149, Cost. 149, note 

Clay v Ricketts, (1885), 66 Iowa 362, 23 N.W. 755 

Carr v Duval, (1840), 14 Peters (U. Š.) 77 

Phillips v Moor, (1880), 71 Me. 78, Costigan 130, note

EXAMPLE: A makes an offer to B and adds "This offer will remain open for one week". B rejects the offer the next day but later, and within the week, accepts it. There is no contract. 

3. LAPSE OF TIME 

An offer comes to an end at the expiration of the time given for its acceptance. Where no time is fixed in the offer it expires at the end of a reasonable time. What is a reasonable time depends largely on the nature of the particular offer and the circumstances of the case. An offer to buy or sell land would not require so prompt an acceptance as an offer to buy or sell chattels or goods of a perishable character or of fluctuating value. Where an oral offer is made to another and he goes away without accepting it, it would seem that ordinarily the offer is to be considered as having lapsed. 

13 C. J. 297 

Averill v Hedge, (1838), 12 Conn. 424, Costigan 142 

In re Kelly, (1872), 39 Conn. 159, Costigan 126 

Loring v Boston, (1844), 7 Met. 409 

Boston & M.R.R. v Bartlett, (1849), 3 Cush. 224, Costigan 79 overruling Cooke v Oxley, (1790), 3 T. R. 653, Costigan 77

A contract is not necessarily incomplete because some of its terms must be fixed at a future time or by subsequent events.

EXAMPLE 1: A promises to construct a building according to stated plans and specifications and B promises to pay 30,000 therefor. EXAMPLE 2: A promises to serve. B as chauffeur and B 

promises to pay him $100 a month. The full period for which the service is expected to continue is not stated. There is at once a bilateral contract for a month's service. It is . sometimes a difficult question of interpretation whether an agreement specifies merely a rate of compensation or indicates, at least impliedly, an understanding that the employment shall continue for not less than one of the periods for which the rate is stated, in which case there is a contract for one period and, at its expiration, an offer of another, in the absence of revocation. 

EXAMPLE 3: A promises to sell and deliver certain goods to B and B promises to pay a specified price therefor. 

Though no time for performance is fixed, there is a contract, the presumption being that the parties intended performance to be made within a reasonable time. What is a reasonable time is a question of fact in each case, depending on the character of the goods and all the surrounding circumstances. 

CONSIDERATION DEFINITION 

Consideration is a promise, an act, or a forbearance to act bargained for and given in exchange for the promise. 

Williston on Contracts, section 111 B.

NECESSITY OF CONSIDERATION 

Consideration has been treated as the very life and essence of a contract. The term "nudum pactum" is now commonly used to describe a contract made without consideration. 

Wilson v Clements (1807), 3.Mass. 1 

Lyon v Williams, (1856), 5 Gray 557

A consideration is necessary to the validity of every simple contract. 

Cooke v Oxley, (1790), 3 T. R. 653, Costigan 77 

Morris v Norton, (1896) 75 Fed. 912, Costigan 319 

Luing v Peterson, (1919),143 Minn.6,172 N.W.692,Costigan 330

This is true whether the agreement is written or oral, provided it is not a specialty. 

Thacher v Dinsmore, (1809), 5 Mass. 299

C. WHAT CONSTITUTES A CONSIDERATION 1. GENERAL RULE AND NATURE 

There is a sufficient consideration for a promise if there is any benefit to the promisor or any loss or detriment to the promisee. 

Briskin 'v Fackard Motor; (1929),269 Mass.394, 169 N.E.148

The performance of gratuitous promises depends wholly upon the good will which prompted them and will not be enforced by the law. 

Cottage Street Church v Kendall, (1877), 121 Mass. 528

The law undoubtedly allows men, who have inconsiderately undertaken to bind themselves in contracts for which they have received no equivalent, to avoid such engagements by showing

bianoo A

- that they have received nothing for them, or that the party rith whom such contract was made would lose nothing by the non-performance of it. 

Amherst Academy v Cowls, (1828) 6 Pick. 427, 432 as a con- The detriment to the promisee which suffices sideration for a contract must be a detriment on entering into the contract, not from the breach of it. 

13 C. J. 318 

EXAMPLE: A, an administrator, promises to pay personally 

The promise is not a debt which he owes as administrator. binding without a new consideration. 

Rann v Hughes, (1778), 3 T. R. 346, Costigan 280

A detriment or damage to the promisee, as well as a benefit to the promisor, is a sufficient consideration te support a promise. 

Forster v Fuller, (1809), 6 Mass. 58

For example, a promise by one to pay the debt or perform the duty of another, or where the party in whose favor the contract is made foregoes some advantage, or incurs an expense, or suffers some loss in consequence of placing his confidence in another's promise, all such contracts possess consideration. 

Sumner v Williams, (1811), 8 Mass. 162,200 

EXAMPLE 1: An uncle promised his nephew that, if the nephew would take a trip to Europe, the uncle would reimburse and repay to the nephew all money expended by him on the trip.

The nephew took the trip. There is ample consideration for the uncle's promise and it is immaterial that the nephew was benefited by the expenditure of his own money, for he was induced to suffer a legal detriment in reliance on the uncle's promise. It is an entirely different thing from a promise to make a present to another. 

Deveemon v Shaw(1888) 69 Ma.199,14 Atl.464, Costigan 354

EXAMPLE 2: A's aunt promised him during her lifetime that she would leave him 500 in hervill if he would promise to attend her funeral. A promised his aunt that he would attenă, and he did attend. A's promise is consideration for the promise of the aunt. 

Earle v Angell, (1892), 157 Mass. 294, 32 N. E. 164 

EXAMPLE 3: A requests B to give him a book, promising B 

10 in exchange therefor; or B offers the book in exchange for 

A's promise to pay 10. In either case, if the book is given and received, there is consideration for A's promise. 

EXAMPLE 4: A says to B, the owner of a garage, "I will pay you 100 if you will make my ear run properly". The acts necessary to produce this result are the requested consideration. 

Consideration may be given to the promisor or to some other person. It may be given by the promisee or by some

3010

- other person.

It is a legal detriment to the party furnishing it. It may or may not be a benefit to the party receiving it. 

Smith v Mayo, (1861), 1 Allen 160

It is usually furnished at the express or implied. request of the party receiving it. 

Lent v Padelford, (1813), 10 Mass. 230 

Arnold v Lyman, (1821), 17 Mass. 400 

Tucker v Haughton, (1852), 9 Cush. 350

It is often said that consideration must move from the plaintiff, the promisee, to the promisor; 

Fink v Cox, 18 Johnson (N. Y.) 145 

Thomas v Thomas, (1842), 2.. B. 851, Costigan 461 and this is usually the case. 

Cottage Street Church v Kendall, (1877), 121 Mass. 528

But it is not, in all cases, essential to the validity of the contract that the consideration move from the promisee to the promisor. it v Commercial Hotel Co.(1925)253 Mass.564,149 N.E.609 

EXAMPLE 1: A promissory note made to a payee in return for a consideration received by the maker from a third person is binding. 

Mellen v Whipple, (1854), 1 Gray 321 

Williston on Contracts, section 114

EXAMPLE 2: A promises 3 to guarantee payment for a bill of goods if B will sell the goods to C. 

C is consideration for A's promise te B. 1 

Selling the goods to 

EXAMPLE 3: A makes a promissory note to 3 in return for a payment by B to C. The payment by B to C is consideration for 

A's note to B. 

EXAMPLE 4: A, at C's request, and in exchange for 1 paid by C, promises B to give him a book. The payment is consideration for A's promise.

EXAMPLE 5: The privilege of naming a child is sufficient consideration for a promise to pay money; 

Gardner v Denison, 217 Mass.492,105 N.E.359,51 L.R.A. (NS)1108 and the action on such a promise may be brought either in the name of the child; 

Eaton v Libbey, (1896), 165 Mass. 218, 42 N. 3. 1127 ur in the name of the parents to whom the promise was made. 

Wolford v Powers, (1982), 85 Ind. 294, Costigan 323

It is not necessary the t the consideration exist at the time of the promise if it arises afterwards in consequence of such promise, as in the case of offers of reward and in subscriptions to charities. 

Cottage Street Church v Kendall, (1877), 121 Mass. 528

Thus, if A promises B to pay him a sum of money if he will do a particular act, and B does the act, the promise becomes binding although B, at the time of the promise, does not engage to do the act. In the intermediate time, the obligation of the promise is suspended; for, until the performance of

I.

-M 

the condition of the promise, there is no consideration, and the promise is nudum pactum; but, on the performance of the condition by the promisee, it is clothed with a valid consideration which relates back to the promise and it then becomes obligatory. 

Gardner v Webber, (1835), 17 Pick. 407, 413 

Goward v Waters, (1868), 98 Mass. 596

But the act which constitutes the consideration must be at the instance of the promisor. 

13 C. J. 318

EXAMPLE: An agreement between A and B by which A agreed to forbear to sue В would not be a consideration for a promise of C if not made at C's express request or communicated to C at or before the time of the making of the promise by C. 

Ellis v Clark, (1872), 110 Mass. 389, 392

A contingent benefit, or hope of benefit, is sufficient consideration for a promise. If a man acts in consideration of a conditional promise, if he gets the promise, he gets all that he is entitled to by his act and if, as events turn out, the condition is not satisfied, and the promise calls for no performance, there is no failure of consideration. 

Gution v Marcus, (1896), 165 Mass. 385, 43 N. E. 125 

Richardson v Hooper, (1835), 13 Pick. 446 

Newhall v Paige, (1858), 10 Gray 366

EXAMPLE: The release of an inchoate right of dower of a wife in the real estate of her husband is consideration for a promise. 

Fitcher v Griffiths, (1913)., 216 Mass. 174, 103 N. E. 471 

2. ADEQUACY OF CONSIDERATION 2 IN GENERAL 

All executory contracts, whether oral or written, if not under seal, are void as between the contracting parties unless they are made on a legal consideration. 

But if a contract is deliberately made, without fraud, and with full knowledge of all the circumstances, the least consideration will be sufficient. It is the existence of a consideration, not its quantity nor quality nor appropriateness that gives validity to a contract. It need not be an equivalent. 

Vinal v Richardson, (1806); 13 Allen 521

While consideration must be either some benefit to the promisor or some loss or detriment to the promisee, it need not be adequate in point of actual value. 

Nash v Lull, (1869), 102 Mass. 60 

Hall Co. v Gale, (1924), 248 Mass. 299, 142 N. E. 813

The benefit conferred by the plaintiff as promisee on the defendant as promisor, or on a third person; or the loss or detriment suffered by the plaintiff as promisee or by some third person, at the request of the defendant as promisor,

may be slight. fact, be real.

The detriment to the promisee need not, in It means giving up something which he had a right to keep, or doing something which he had a right not to do. It would be a detriment to the promisee in a legal sense if he, at the request of the promisor, and upon the strength of that promise, had performed any act which occasioned him the slightest trouble or inconvenience, and which he was not obliged to perform. 

Wit v Commercial Hotel Co., (1925), 253 Mass.564,149 N.E.609

It is hard to imagine any change of position, not made in pursuance of a previous duty, which may not be sufficient as a consideration or which is not a detriment in a legal sense. 

March v Culpepper, (1627), Cro. Car. 70, Costigan 283 

Hunt v Brown, (1888), 146 Mass. 253,15 N.E.587, Costigan 435 

Bainbridge v Firmstone, 8 A.& E.743, Corbin's Cases 211, note

With the exception of an exchange of unequal sums of money, the relative values of a promise and the consideration for it do not affect the sufficiency of consideration. 

Manson v Flanagan, (1919), 233 Mass. 150, 123 N. E. 614 

Gunther v Gunther, (1902), 181 Mass. 212, 63 N. E. 402

Bret v J. S. & Wife, (1600), Cro. Eliz. 756, Costigan 277 

Gardner v Denison, 217 Mass. 492, 105 N.E.559,51 LRA (NS)1108

The law does not undertake to determine the adequacy of consideration. That is left to the parties who are the sole judges of the benefits or advantages to be derived from their contracts. It is sufficient if the consideration be of some value, though slight, or of a nature which may enure to the benefit of the party making the promise.

If a legal contract is made, the parties are bound by its terms and cannot be relieved from a bad bargain because the agreement may be foolish and improvident. 

Chamberlain v Cohn, (1927), 261 Mass. 322, 158 N. E. 846

The court cannot estimate, as the parties can, the benefit to one or the inconvenience to the other. The law does not nicely weigh the amount of benefit to one party or injury or loss to the other which will make a legal consideration. 

Hubbard v Coolidge, (1840), 1 Met. 84 

"The value of all things is measured by the appetite of the contractors and therefore the just value is that which they be contented to give." When a party gets all the consideration he honestly contracted for, he cannot say that he gets no consideration or that it has failed. If this doctrine is not correct, then it is not true that parties are at liberty to make their own contracts. 

Wolford v Powers, (1882), 85 Ind. 294, Costigan 323 

EXAMPLE 1: A, from motives of pity for animal suffering, promises B 10 for a broken-legged horse which B had thought worthless and would have gladly paid 10 to have A remove. 

The transfer of the horse to A is a sufficient consideration for A's promise.

EXAMPLE 2: D believes a letter in P's possession will enable him to establish a claim against X. D offers P $1000 for

ITA"

the letter. P accepts the offer and gives to D the letter which proves of no value to D in establishing his claim. letter is sufficient consideration for D's promise.. 

Wilkinson v Oliveira, (1835), 1 Bingham (N. C.) 490

EXAMPLE 3:

EXAMPLE 4: The finder of lost goods has a lawful possession which none but the owner can question and, if the owner cannot be found, the finder may lawfully sell them, and such sale is sufficient consideration for a promise to pay for them. 

Ellery v Cunningham, (1840), i Met. 112

EXAMPLE 5: If P leases lands to X at a certain rental and 

X assigns the lease to D, and rent becomes due which D promises to pay to P if he will show him a deed by which it may appear that rent is due and P shows it accordingly, the promise is binding, the showing the deed being a sufficient consideration. 

Train v Gold, (1827), 5 Pick. 280, 384 

EXAMPLE 6: If D promises to pay P a certain sum of money if he will call for it at a particular time and P calls accordingly, the promise is binding, the calling for the money being a sufficient consideration. 

Train v Gold, (1827), 5 Pick. 320, 384

In general, a waiver of any legal right at the request of another is a sufficient consideration for a promise. 

Hamer v Sidway, (1891),14 N.Y.538,27 N.E.256,12 L.R.A.463

EXAMPLE 1: If P incurs a legal liability, as by entering into a contract with a third person at D's request, the liability thereby incurred is a sufficient consideration to support a promise made by D to P. 

Steele v Steele, (1892),75 Ma.477,23 Atl.959, Costigan 351 

EXAMPLE 2: The execution of a release of all one's right title and interest in any property, real or personal, fairly made, for the purpose of quieting a doubtful title, is valið consideration for the price agreed to be paid and it would make no difference if the release should afterwards be found to be of no use to the party obtaining it. For though a consideration is necessary, yet the doing of anything that creates trouble or inconvenience to the party doing it is sufficient. The compromise of a doubtful claim is sufficient, and there is not a tutal failure of consideration. 

Kerr v Lucas, (1861), 1 Allen 279 

EXAMPLE 3: If A promises to buy a house for his nephew, that is nothing; but if A promises to buy a house for his nephew and requests the nephew to enter into a contract of purchase in the nephew's name, and the nephew does so, the law implies a promise on the part of A to reimburse the nephew

Skidmore v Bradford, L.R.8Eq.134, Costigan 352, note 

EXAMPLE 4: If D says to P, "I will give you $5,000", and

P, in reliance thereon, spends $5,000 in building a house there is no consideration for D's promise, since P did not build the house at D's request. 

In re Hudson, (1885), 54 L.J.Ch. 811, Costigan 352, note 

EXAMPLE 5: The surrender of a note, whether at the time it was capable of being enforced or not, is sufficient consideration for the giving of a new note. 

Wilton v Eaton, (1879), 127 Mass. 174 

Whitney v Clary, (1887), 145 Mass. 156, 13 N. E. 393

A contract is void where there is a complete failure of consideration. For example, when a plaintiff pays a defendant for property sold to him in which the latter has no title; or when money is paid by a plaintiff to a defendant for an agreement to perform acts which he totally fails to perform; and doubtless there may be other classes of total failure of consideration. 

Palmer v Guillow, (1916), 224 Mass. 1, 112 N. E. 493 b.

EXCEPTION TO THE RULE 

There is one exception to the general rule that consideration need not be adequate, namely, in the exchange of unequal sums of money. Ordinarily, courts do not go into the question of equality or inequality of consideration and they grant relief only when the inequality is shown to have arisen from mistake, misrepresentation or fraud. A different rule would, in every case, impose upon the court the necessity of inquiring into and determining the value of the property received by the party giving the promise. But the reason for the rule ceases, and hence the rule itself ceases, when applied to contracts to pay money founded solely upon a money.consideration. 

Shepard v Rhodes, (1863), 7 R. I. 470, Costigan 311, 314

In other words, the rule does not apply to a mere exchange of sums of money, of coin whose value is exactly fixed, but to the exchange of something of, in itself, indeterminate value for money or perhaps for some other thing of indeterminate value. Thus a promise to pay 4600 in consideration of a promise to pay one cent is not a binding contract unless the one cent mentioned were some particular one cent, a family piece, or ancient remarkable coin possessing an indeterminate value, extrinsic) from its simple money value. Otherwise, such a promise is simply one to make a gift and is unenforceable. 

Schnell v Nell, (1861), 17 Ind. 29, Costigan 315 

Presbyterian Church v Cooper, (1889)112 NY 517,20 NE 352,0 3.

See G. L. Chapter 4, Section 7, Clause 29 4. MOTIVE AND CONSIDERATION DISTINGUISHED 

Motive is not the same thing as consideration.

It does not take the place of consideration. There may be a motive for a promise which is without consideration. 

EXAMPLE: A promise by one of two brothers to share with the other the property left to the promisor by their mother is not binding without a consideration. 

Chase v Chase, (1906), 191 Mass. 556, 78 N. E. 115

There may even be a promise for a consideration for which it is difficult to ascribe any motive. 

Dendy v Russell, (1903), 67 Kan. 721, 74 Pac. 248 When an act has been done to the knowledge of the other party which purports expressly to invite certain conduct on his part and that conduct on his part follows it is only under exceptional and peculiar circumstances that it will be inquired into how far the act in truth was the motive for the conduct whether in case of consideration or fraud. consideration in the one case, and the fraud in the other, exists regardless of the motive which prompted the act. 

Martin v Moles, (1901), 179 Mass. 114, 60 N.E. 397

The 

But if one has benefited by some act or forbearance in time past without thereby incurring any legal liability and has afterward, whether from good feeling or interested motives, made a promise to the person by whose act or forbearance he has benefited and that promise is made on no other consideration than the past benefit, it is gratuitous and cannot be enforced; it is based on motive and not on consideration. 

13 C. J. 360 

Conant v Evans, (1909), 202 Mass. 34, 88 N. E. 438 

Moore v Elmer, (1901), 180 Mass. 15, 61 N. E. 259 

Consideration is a present exchange for a promise; 

Williston on Contracts, section 111 and it means something which is of value in the eye of the law. 

Thomas v Thomas, (1842), 2 . B. 851, Costigan 401 €.

--  ·Ome 

Wright v Threatt, (1917),146 Ga.778,92 SE 640,LRA 1918C 541 

Schnell v Nell, (1861), '17 Ind. 29, Costigan 315 

Parkman v Brewster, (1860), 15 Gray 271 

Parish v Stone, (1833), 14 Pick. 198

Puterbaugh v Puterbaugh, 131 Ind. 288, 30 NE 519,15 LRA 341 5. GOOD AND VALUABLE CONSIDERATION 

Good consideration is the consideration of blood, or love and affection. Good consideration is a sufficient consideration in a deed against all persons except creditors and bona fide purchasers, but it is not sufficient in any personal action founded on an executory contract. In executory contracts, the consideration must be a valuable consideration for the bencfit of the promisor, or to the trouble, loss or prejudice of the promisee, as previously defined. 

Fink v Cox, (1830),18 Johnson (N.Y.)145, Costigan 318, note 

Wright v Threatt, (1917),146 Ga.778,92 S.E.640, LRA 1918C 541 

Bret v J. S. & Wife, (1600), Co. Eliz. 756, Costigan 277 

Behanan v Maxwell, Iowa, 1921), 181 N.W.683, 14 A.L.R.1004 

Phillips v Fryc 1867), 14 Allen 36 

Dodge v Adams, (1837), 19 Pick. 429 

Loomis v Newhall, (1863), 15 Pick. 159 

Mills v Wyman, (1825), 3 Pick. 207

The distinction in those terms, however, is frequently ignored, and a valuable consideration is often referred to as a good consideration. 

13 C. J. 325

But a good consideration is not a valuable consideration and if the question before the court calls for the making of this distinction, it does so end holds that a consideration which is merely good, and not valuable, will not support an executory contract. 

Thomas v Thomas, (1842), 2 Q. B. 851, Costigan 461 

6. MORAL OBLIGATION AS CONSIDERATION 

According to the great weight of authority, a moral obligation which has been at no time a legal duty will not afford a consideration for a promise; and where services are rendered to another without any roquest and under circumstances which do not raise an implied contract on his part to pay for them, a promise founded on motives of honor or gratitude is not on a sufficient consideration. 

13 C. J. 358, 359 

Mills v Wyman, (1825), 3 Pick. 207 

Eastwood v Kenyon, (1840),11 A. E.438, Costigan 292 

Kent v Rand, (1886), 64 N.H. 45, 5 Atl. 760, Costigan 295 

Thomas v Thomas, (1842), 2 Q. B. 851, Costigan 461 

Morris v Norton, (1396), 75 Fed. 912, Costigan 319 

Loomis v Newhall, (1833), 15 Pick. 159 

Rask v Norman (1918)141 Minn.198,169 N.W.704,17 A.L.R.1296

Peters v Poro, (Vt.,1922),117 Ati.244,25 A.L.R.615 

Bartholomew v Jackson, 20 Johnson (N. Y.) 28 

Muir v Kane, (1909), 55 Wash.131, 104 Pac.153, 26 LRANS 519 

Distinguish Chase v Corcoran, (1871), 106 Mass. 286

G 

EXAMPLE: A promise by a husband to his wife, made at her death-bed, that their son should have certain property, is not given for consideration and is unenforceable. 

Peek v Peek, (1888), 77 Cal. 106, 19 Pac.227, 1 L.R.A.185

But a moral obligation for which there has been some preexisting obligation, which has become inoperative by positive law, will form the basis for an effective promise. 

The cases of debts barred by the statute of limitations, cf debts incurred by infants, of debts of bankrupts, illustrate this rule. Express promises founded on such preexisting equitable obligations may be enforced; there is sufficient consideration for them; they merely remove an impediment created by law to the recovery of debts honestly due but which public policy protects the debtors from being compelled to pay. 

In all these cases, there was originally a consideration. 

There is a moral obligation to pay them, founded upon an antecedent valuable consideration. 

Mills v Wyman, (1825), 3 Pick. 207 

Dodge v Adams, (1837), 19 Pick. 429

On the other hand, where a debt has been voluntarily released, an express promise does not revive it, there being no consideration to support the new promise. 

Valentine v Foster, 11840), 1 Met. 520 

7. MUTUAL PROMISES 

Mutual promises, made at the same time, are consideration for each other: 

Earle v Angell, (1892), 157 Mass. 294, 32 N. E. 164 

Nichols v Raynbred, (1615), Hobart 88, Costigan 683 

Baker v Smith, (1651), Style 295, Costigan 284 

Phelps v Townsend, (1829), 8 Pick. 392 

Stearns v Barrett, (1323), 1 Pick. 443 provided always that they impose some legal obligation on the persons making them. 

13 C. J. 329 

Mowbray Pearson v Stanton, 109 Wash.601,187 Pac.370, Cost.445

Pocatello v Fidelity Co.,(1920), 207 Fed.181, Costigan 460 

Wood v Lucy, (1917), 222 N.Y.88, 118 N.E.1082, Costigan 467 

Dement v Coon, (1919), 104 Wash.503, 177 Pac.354, Costigan 469 

Tucker v Woods, (1615), 12 Johnson (N.Y.)190, Costigan 836 

Thayer v Burchard, (1868), 99 Mass. 508 

Mills v Smith, (1906), 193 Mass.11,78 N.E.765,6 L.R.A.(NS) 865 

EXAMPLE 1: A son promised to cease complaining to his father because the son had not been given so much money or so many advantages as the father's other children, in consideration for which the father promised to surrender a note which he held against the son. There is no legal consideration for the father's promise, as the son had no legal right to complain.

White v Bluett, (1853), 23 L.J.EX. (N.S.)36, Costigan 355 

Jamieson v Renwick, (1891), 17 Vict.L.R. 124, Costigan 558 

EXAMPLE 2: A note given by a father to a son for a release by the son of all the interest which he would have in the father's estate after the father's death is invalid. 

A son

- has no legal interest in his father's estate during the lifetime of the father. 

Loring v Sumner, (1839), 23 Pick. 98 

EXAMPLE 3: An agreement to convoy, at a future time property belonging to a third person at the time of the agreement, is supported by a sufficient consideration. 

Trask v Vinson, (1838) 20 Pick. 105 

Stearns v Foote, (1838), 20 Pick. 432 Thereafter

EXAMPLE 4: P deeded to D a farm by metcs and bounds, described as containing 521 acres, at 16 an acre. doubt arose as to the quantity of land in the tract and they agreed to have the land surveyed and if there was less than 521 acres, P agreed to refund at the rate of 16 an acre but if there was more than 521 acres, D agreed to pay for the excess at the same rate. There was an excess of 57 acres. D is liable to pay for the excess, there being sufficient consideration for his promise. 

Seward v Mitchell, (1860), 1 Cold. (Tenn.) 87, Costigan 497 

Distinguish Williams v Hathaway, (1837), 19 Pick. 387

It seems, however, that the liability need not be perfect. 

Thus a acfenso given by the law, such as insanity, infancy, fraud, statute of frauus, statute of limitations, or dischargein bankruptcy, which makes the promise voidable or unenforceable, will not render the consideration insufficient. 

Atwell v Jenkins, (1895) 163 Mass. 362, 40 N. E. 178 

Holt v Ward Clarencieux, (1732), 2 Strange 937, Costigan 471 

EXAMPLE 1: P, an unmarried woman, and D, a married man, make mutual promises to marry, P not knowing or having reason to know that D is marrioā. P's promise is sufficient consideration for the promise of D though D's fraud would be a defense to any action on the contract brought by D. 

EXAMPLE 2: D promises P100 for P's promise to cut timber on Blackacre upon which D is a trespasser, P not knowing of D's fraud. P's promise is consideration for the promise of D). 8.

MUTUALITY OF OBLIGATION a. IN GENERAL 

Mutuality of obligation is the very essence of all bilateral contracts. Nothing can be more contradictory to such an obligation than an entire liberty in either of the parties making a promise, to perform it or not as he may plcase. An agreement permitting such liberty would be absolutely void for want of obligation and is sometimes called an "illusory promise". naha good contract. 

Loudenback v Tennessee,121 Fed.298,61 L.R.A.402,Costigan 446

Thus, a contract of sale is mutual where it contains a promise to sell on one side and e. promise to purchase on the other; but it is not mutual where there is an obligation to sell and no obligation to purchase, or an obligation to pur-

GOD 

DILS

- chase and no obligation to sell. 

EXAMPLE 1: A offers to deliver curtain stono to B in such quantities as may be desired and B accepts the offer. There is no mutuality because B is not bound to desire any stone. 

Hoffman v Maffioli, (1899),104 Wis 630,80 NW 1032,47 LR: 427 

Wickham v Farmers' Co. (Iowa, 1920), 179 NW 417, Costigan 438

EXAMPLE 2: offers B to receive and transport railroad iron from New York to Chicago, not to exceed a certain number of tons during a specified period at a definite rate and B accepts the offer. If B has not agreed to deliver any iron for transportation, there is no contract for went of mutuality. 

Chicago Ry. v Dane, (1870), 45 N. Y. 240, Costigan 209 

Davie v Mining Co., (1892),93 Mich.491, 53 N..625,24 LRA 357 

Petroleum Co. v Coal Co., (1890), 89 Tenn.381, 18 S.W. 65 

American Cotton Oil Co. v Kirk, 68 Fed. 791, Costigan 449 

Crane v Crane, 105 Fed. 869, Costigan 452

But a contract to buy all of a certain article that one may require for one's own use in his business during a certain period of time is binding if it is certain that he will require some quantity of the article even though he does not agree to require any given amount. Though the quantity to be bought and sold is indefinite, it is ascertainable by the terms of the agreement, and therefore curtain. "Certum est quod certum reddi potest". The promise to take all that one can consume would be broken by buying from another and it is this obligation to take one's entire supply which saves the mutual character of the promise. 

Loudenback v Tennessee, 121 Fed.298,61 LRA 402, Costigan 446 

Texas Co. v Pensacola (1922), 279 Fed. 19, 24 .. L. R. 1536 

Great Northern Ky.vWitham, (1873),L.R.9 C.P.16, Costigan 206 

Wells v Alexandre, (1891),130 N.Y.642,29 N.E.142,15 L.R.1.218

Brawley v United States, (1877) 96 U. S. 168

See Dickinson, (1896), 63 Minn. 405, 63 Minn. 405, 65 N.W. 661/ no temper lavil

VOLUNTARY SUBSCRIPTIONS 

Some courts have held that the promise of each subscriber is the consideration for the promises of the others. 

Furman University v Waller, 124 SC 68,117 SE 356,33 IR 615 

Eastern League v Vail, 97 Vt.495, 124 Atl.568, 38 .L.R.845

But generally, a gratuitous subscription for a charitable purpose is considered only as a continuing offer to make a gift and until accepted or acted upon by the promisee in such e manner as to raise a consideration it is unenforceable and may be withdrawn, since until then it is without consideration. 

Sherwin y Fletcher, (1897), 168 Mass. 413, 47 N. E. 147 

First Universalist Society v Currier, (1841), 3 Met. 417

Phillips Limerick Academy v Davis, (1814), ii Mass. 113 

Robinson v Nutt, (1904), 185 Mass. 345, 70 N. E. 198 

Distinguish Martin v Melos,179 Mass.114,60 NE 397, Cost.347

EXAMPLE: The duty assumed by a college which accepts a partial payment of a subscription to a scholarship fund me de on condition that the gift shall bear the donor's name and serve to perpetuate her memory is a sufficient consideration to support the donor's promise and creates a bilateral agrcement. 

Allegheny College v Bank, (1927) 246 NY 369,159 NE 173,57 ALR 98C

In the later Massachusetts casos, more weight has been laid on the incurring of other liabilities and making expenditures on the faith of the defendant's promise than on the counter promisc of the plaintiff and it is held not to be a sufficicnt consideration thet others were led or induced to subscribe by the subscription of the dcfendant. 

Cottage Street Church v Kendall, (1876), 121 Mass. 528

If, on the faith of the subscription and before its withdrawal, the promisco performs some act - expends money, incurs enforceable liabilities, etc. in furtherance of the enterprise the promisor intended to promotc, consideration for the subscription is supplied and it is thereby rendered valid, binding and enforceable although, until so acted upon, it stands as a mere offor, revocable at the will of the donor. 

Re Griswold, (Neb.,1925),202 N.7.609,58 4.L.R.858,881 

Amherst Academy v Coris, (1828), 6 Pick. 427

Thompson v Pago, (1840), 1 Mot. 565 

Ives v Sterling, (1843), 6 Met. 310 

Mirick v French, (1854), 2 Gray 420 

Brokaw v McElroy,162 Iowa 288,143 NE 1087,50 LRANS 835, Cost.338 

See Ricketts v Secthorn, 57 Neb.51,77 NW 365,42 LRA 794, Cost.344 

EXAMPLE: X signed a subscription paper, promising to pay $3500 towards 45,000 due on a church mortgage provided the whole sum of 45,000 should be raised within one year. 

X paid $2000 on account and died before the year was up and before the full sum of 45,000 had been raised. There was no consideration for X's promisc and his estate is not liable for the balance of his subscription. 

Presbyterian Church v Cooper, 112 NY 517,20 NE 352, Costigan 334

EXAMPLE: A and B were jointly indebted to C. Apaid onehalf of the debt and was released and discharged from all further liability by C. B gave his note for the balance of the debt. There was no consideration for the note of B. 

Herring v Dorrell, (1840), 8 Dowling 604, Costigan 480

It is well settled in this Commonwealth that forbearance to sue constitutes or may be found to constitute a sufficient consideration for a promise to an intending litigant and it is not necessary in an action on such promise that it should appear that there was in fact a good cause of action or a fair and reasonable ground for success in the threatened suit. 

Forbearance to sue on a claim known to be frivolous or vexatious would not be a sufficient consideration for the reason that the promotion of such a suit could be found either to be fraudulent or wanting in good faith. But, short of that, forbearance to sue would seem to be a sufficient consideration for a promise founded thereon. 

Kennedy v Welch, (1907), 196 Mass. 592, 83 N. E. 11 

Prout v Pittsfield District, (1891),154 Mass.450,28 N.E.679 

Wilton v Eston, (1879), 127 Mass. 174

Kerr v Lucas, (1861), 1 Allen 279 

Codman v Dumbing (1924), 249 Mass.451,144 N. E. 408 

Reed v Kansas Co., (1928),125 Ken.603,264 Pac.1065,57 ALR 275

EXAMPLE: P's threat to contest a will, merely for the purpose of compelling D to settle with P and buy his peace, without any intention on P's part of actually contesting the will if no such settlement were made, would not be sufficient and would not constitute a valid consideration for D's promise. 

Mackin v Dwyer, (1910), 205 Mass. 472, 91 N. E. 893 

Silver v Graves, (1911), 210 Mass. 26, 95 N. E. 948

The authorities are grued that the promisee must believe in his claim or in his action; and that forbearance to sue on a demand known by him to be unenforceable, or to proceed in an action knowingly brought without cause, is no consideration. 

Wade v Simeon, (1846), 2 C. B. 548, Costigan 478

Barlow v Ocean Insurance Co., (1842), 4 Met. 270 

It is also woll settled that the more fact that the claim is invalid or unenforceable does not prevent its surrender from constituting a sufficient considcration if the claim was a doubtful one in regard to the validity if which there was reasonable ground for a difference of opinion. Beyond this point, the cases are in much conflict. 

Smith v Ferre, (1891), 21 Or.395,28 Pac.241, Costigan 484

The present rule in Massachusetts is that if a claim is made bona fide and is not frivolous or vexatious, a compromise agreement is supported by a sufficient consideration oven though the court should ultimately declare that the claim was without legal foundation. If an intending litigant bona fide forbears a right to litigate ho does give up something of value. The reality of the claim must bc measured, not by the state of the law as it is ultimately discovered to be but by the state of the knowledge of the person who at the time has

If the validity of a compromise depended upon the validity of the claim on which the compromise is founded, as ultimately adjudicated by the courts, no compromisc of a disputed claim would bc binding and a defundant would be under the necessity of insisting at all hazards upon a judicial determination of all his controverted rights. 

Blount v Dillaway 199 Mass.3530,17 LRANS 1036, Costigan 491 

Silver v Graves, (1911), 210 Mass. 26, 95 N. E. 948 

Cook v Wright, (1861), 1 Best & Smith 559, Costigan 481 

Cobb v Arnold, (1844), 8 Met. 403 

Allis v Billings, (1848), 2 Cush. 19

The settlement of a controversy is valid and binding, not because it is the settlement of 3 valid claim but because it is the settlement of a valid controversy. 

Tuttle v Tuttle, (1847), 12 Met. 551 

Barlow v Ocean Insurance Co., (1842), 4 Met. 270 

Of course, an agrooment not to su upon a debt already due and pay ble, for no other consideration than a payment of a part of the debt. is without legal consideration. 

Warren v Hodge, (1876), 121 Mass. 106 ?

But the compromise of a claim, disputed wholly or in part, is a sufficient consideration for an express promise to pay; 

Dunbar v Dunbar, (1901), 180 Mss. 170, 62 N. E. 248 

Clark v Gamwell, (1878), 125 Moss. 428 and, if the money is paid es promised, such a settlcmcnt and compromise of a matter in dispute is binding as a valid accord and satisfaction. 

McKay v Myers, (1897), 168 Mass. 312, 47 N. E. 98

It seems to have been assumed in this Commonwealth that an express agreement to forbear bringing suit for a debt duc, cven though for an indefinite time, and even though it cannot be construed to be an agreement for perpetual forbearance, if followed by forbearance for a reasonable time, is a sufficient consideration for a promise. 

Howe v Taggart, (1882), 133 Mass. 284 

Prouty v Wilson, (1877, 123 Mass. 297 

Ellis v Clark, (1872), 110 Mass. 389 

Pratt v Heddon, (1876), 121 Mass. 116

Where there is a promise to forbear suit by the creditor, there is no consideration for such promise unless there is a corresponding promise on the part of the debtor not to pay during the time of the promised forbearance. 

EXAMPLE: A creditor promises to extend a note for one week and the creditor promises to pay within the week. Here the debtor does not promise that he will not pay until the end of the stipulated time or in case he does pay before that time, that he will also pay the interest for the entire time of the extension and hence there is no consideration for the promise of the creditor to extend the note and to forbear suit. 

Austin Co. v Bahn, (1895), 87 Tex.582,29 S.W.646, Costigan 427

16. TO TR

- A covenant, or promise under seal, not to sue an obligor, Without any limitation of time, may be pleaded as a release, No avoid circuity of action. The same is true of a promise hot under seal which is given for consideration. It is equally well settled that a covenant or promise not to sue an obligor within a limited time cannot be so pleaded but that the obligor's only remedy is upon the covenant or promise if he should be sued by the covenantor or promisor within the time limited. 

Foster v Purdy, (1845), 5 Met. 442

Rodocanachi v Buttrick, (1878), 125 Mass. 134 b. NECESSITY OF PROMISE TO FOR BEAR 

Actual forbearance without a promise to forbear is generally sufficient in most States if such forbearance is at the request of the promisor and in reliance on the promise. 

Strong v Sheffield, (1895),144 N.Y.392,39 N.E.330,Costigan 464

In a few States, however, including Massachusetts, an agreement to forbear is sometimes necessary, it being held that a mere forbearance to sue, without any agreement to that effect, is not a sufficient consideration for the promise of another to pay the debt of the person liable, although the act was induced by such promise. These States hold that there must be also an express promise to forbear, unless a promise to forbear is implied.

Manter v Churchill, (1878), 127 Mass. 31 

Atlas Shoe Co. v Bloom, (1911), 209 Mass. 563, 95 N. E. 952 

Robinson v Gould, (1853), 11 Cush. 55 

Queal & Co.v Peterson, (Iowa, 1908),116 N..593,19 L.R.A.(NS)842

The reason given is that if the creditor makes no agreement to forbear, he is under no obligation, legal or moral, not to bring suit and that there is no damage to the creditor or benefit to the debtor upon which the consideration of a promise can rest.

Mecorney v Stanley, (1851), 8 Cush. 85 

Professor Williston criticises the Massachusetts doctrine as failing to recognize the validity of unilateral contracts, and that it assumes the necessity of mutuality of obligation in every contract whereas such mutuality never exists in a unilateral contract. 

Williston on Contracts, section 136, note 91

Even in Massachusetts, the doctrine is recognized that the agreement to forbesr need not be express but may be implied from the circumstances although it is not always so implied. 

Parson v Clark, (1882), 132 Mass. 569 

Boyd v Freize, (1856), 5 Gray 553 

Pelton v Baker, (1893), 158 Mass. 349, 33 N. E. 394 

Codman v Dumaine, (1924), 249 Mass. 451, 144 N. E. 408

EXAMPLE: The maker of a note and the payee agreed upon an extension of the note for twelve months. Here a promise is implied on the part of the payce not to suc and upon the maker not to pay within the stipulated time. The promise of the debtor to forego his right to pay at any time after the note

Benson v Phipps, (1895), 87 Tex. 578,29 S.W.1061, Costigan 425 

10. PROMISING OR DOING WHAT ONE IS BOUND TO DO 2. IN GENERAL 

A promise to do what the promisor is already bound to do cannot be a consideration, for if a person gets nothing in return for his promise but that to which he is already legally entitled, the consideration is illusory and unreal. Therefore, as a general rule, the performance of or promise to perform an existing legal obligation is not a valid consideration. This legal obligation may arise from the law independent of contract, or it may arise from a subsisting contract. 

13 C. J. 351 

b. SUBSISTING OBLIGATION IN LAW 

Where it is the legal duty of the promisee to do, without reward, the act induced by the promise sought to be enforced, the doing of the act is no consideration for the promise. 

EXAMPLE 1: A minor ward, having left his guardian without right, returned to live with him in reliance on the guardian's promise to charge him nothing for his board or education. 

There is no consideration for the guardian's promise. 

Keith v Miles, (1860), 39 Miss. 442, Costigan 363 

EXAMPLE 2: P, a watchman of Boston, while in the discha: arge of his official duties, discovered a person setting iire to a building and prosecuted him to conviction. The city had offered a reward for the detection and conviction of incendiaries which P knew. P is not entitled to the reward. 

Pool v Boston, (1849), 5 Cush. 219 

Lees v Colgan, 120 Cal. 262, Costigan 361

EXAMPLE 3: If a finder of lost goods, who is unable to discover the owner after due endeavor, surrenders them to a person not the owner in return for his promise to pay for them, such surrender is consideration for the promise. 

Eliery v Cunningham, (1840), 1 Met. 112 

The claim that extra services have been rendered by an officer while in discharge of his official duties furnishes no warrant for the charge of extra compensation. The pretense that it is for extra services would cover any conceivable corruption or extortion. If a constable, for making extraordinary efforts to perform an ordinary official act, may not only receive but may also collect by law a compensation beyond what the statute allows for the act, any other officer may do the same and put his "extraordinary efforts" in the market, to be had by the highest bidder. 

Hogan v Stophlet, (1899),179 111.150,53 N.E.604, Costigan 360

Brophy v Marble, (1875), 118 Mass. 548 

Bryan v kers, (Ark., 1928), 7 S. W. (2nd) 325, 58 A.L.R.1124 

EXAMPLE: P, an officer of the United States Customs found smuggled goods while assisting the inspectors in char ge of examining a vessel, although he was not in discharge of the specific duty assigned to him. He could not recover a reward offered by the owners of the vessel to any person giving information concerning smuggled goods. 

Davies v Burns, (1862), 5 Allon 349, Costigan 361

There are cases which seem to hold to the contrary but these will be found to be distinguishable either because the officer arrested the offender beyond his territorial jurisdiction or under particular statutes which did not make it the duty of the officer to make the arrest. 

Studley v Ballard, (1897), 169 Mass. 295, 47 N. E. 1000 

In re Russell's Application, 51 Conn. 577, Costigan 360

EXAMPLE: A constable of a country town spent a substantial amount of time in the performance of purcly detective work in the investigation and collection of evidence in consequence of an offer of reward for such services made by his home town. 

Although a close case, ho was held entitled to the reward on the ground that such services were not within the scope of his official duties. 

Hartley v Granville, (1913), 216 Mass. 38, 102 N. E. 942 But

A promise to pay a witness more than the legal fees for his attendance in court to which he has been duly summoned is void because he is simply performing his legal duty. if a witness agrees with a party that he will attend and testify without being summoned and he is not summoned and so is not brought under the order of the court, doubtless any reasonable promise for compensation is good and may be enforced, bucause the proceeding or service is not under nor in pursuance of the statute. 

Massachusetts Bar Examination, December, 1905, Morning #8

An action to recover extra compensation as an expert witness may be maintained if there is consideration therefor. 

Barrus v Phaneuf, (1896), lóo Mass. 123, 44 N. E. 141

Attendance as a witness in an action pending in another State is a sufficient consideration for a promise to pay extra witness fees. 

Armstrong v Prentice, (1893), 86 Wis. 210, 56 N. W. 742

A promise by the master of a ship to pay extra wages when the ship is in distress, as an inducement to extraordinary exertion, is without consideration. 

Stilk v Myrick, (1809), 2 Campbell 317, Costigan 373

-- . contract obligation, affords no consideration for a new promise on the part of the party to whom the existing obligation is duc, on the ground that the other party is doing no more than he was already obliged to do. 

The weight of American authority is in favor of this view. 

The reasoning is that while it is true that the law would not interpose to compel the performance of the contract, this is not because the promisee has a right to violate his contract but because the law presumes that such violation can be sufficiently compensated for by money damages. A man may commit a trespass for which the law would merely give a right of action against him to recover damages but it does not follow that he had a right to commit the trespass. 

Johnson v Sellers, (1858), 33 Ala. 265, Costigan 369

Of course it is competent for the parties to a contract to modify or to waive their rights under it and engraft new terms upon it and, in such a case, the promise of the one is the consideration for the promise of the other. 

Morrison v Slonzynski, (1920),145 Minn.485,175 NW 992, Cost.393

EXAMPLE: D is employed by P at a certain compensation, but for an indefinite term of service. D promises P a bonus if he will continue in his service until a certain job is finished and P does so, in reliance on the promise. There is consideration for D's promise. 

Scott v Duthie, (Wash., 1923), 216 Pac. 855, 28 4.L.R. 328 

(2) REFUSAL TO PROCEED WITH PERFORMANCE (a) IN GENERAL 

The general rule is that an agreement made in substitution of an executory contract of prior date annuls the former contract and 'is itself a sufficient consideration for a release of its obligations provided. the substituted obligation contains a change of the obligations of each party; and, if the obligation of one party only is affected by the new arrangement and the other party receives nothing additional and is relieved of no duty, there is a want of consideration. 

Raggow v Scougall, (1915), 31 T. L. R. 564, Costigan 423

Thus, under the general rule, a promise to pay a contractor an additional sum of money to complete his work upon his representation that he is unable to carry through the contract at the agreed price is without consideration and unenforceable. 

Leggett v Vincent, (Miss., 1929), 124 Sc. 472 

Lingenfelder v Brewing Co., (1891), 103 Mo. 578, 15 S.W. 844 

McDevitt v Stokes, (1917),174 Ky.515,192 S.W.681, Costigan 388

EXAMPLE: D employed P for one year at $40 a month. After 

P had worked for several months, he received an offer from X of 65 a month, to begin work immediately, whereupon D told P that if he would stay cut the balance, of the year with D that he should receive $120 additional at the end of the year. P worked out the year. There is no consideration for D's promise

Davis v Morgan, (1903), 117 Ga. 504, 43 S.E. 732, Costigan 367

In other words, where the promise of one party is simply a repetition of a subsisting legal promise, most states hold that there can be no consideration for an additional promise of the other party. 

(b) MASSACHUSETTS RULE 

There is some authority to the contrary, notably in Massachusetts, New York, Indiana, and

Illinois. As a general proposition, it is settled in this 

Commonwealth that a promise to pay one for doing that which he was under a prior legal duty to do is not binding for want of a valid consideration. 

Smith v Bartholomew, (1840), 1 Met. 276 

Com. v Johnson, (1849), 3 Cush. 454 

Pool v Boston, (1849), 5 Cush. 219 

Warren v Hodge, (1876), 121 Mass. 106

EXAMPLE: An agreement by a creditor to receive in installments, at future times, money, all of which is due and payable presently, is without consideration. 

Walker v Russell, (1835), 17 Pick. 280

It has often been said that the principle involved is the same that lies at the foundation of the doctrine that a promise to accept or pay a less sum in discharge for a debt for a greater amount is not binding. Nevertheless, a limitation of the general proposition has been established in Massachusetts in cases where a plaintiff having entered into a contract with the defendant to do a certain act or perform certain work, refuses to proceed with it, and the defendant in order to secure to himself the actual performance of the act or work in place of a right to collect damages from the plaintiff, promises to pay him an additional sum. This limitation is not intended to affect the rule that a contract cannot be binding without a consideration; but it rests upon the doctrine that, under these circumstances, there is a new consideration for the promise. 

N.E.590,34 LRA(NS) 261 

Parrott v Mexican Ry., 207 Mass.184,9 

Devine v Murphy, (1897), 168 Mass. 249, 45 N. E. 1066 

Munroe v Perkins, (1830), 9 Tick. 298 

Holmes v Doane, (1851), 9 Cush. 135 

Tobin v Kells, (1911), 207 Mass. 304, 311, 93 N. E. 596 

Lattimore v Harsen, (1817), 14 Johnson 330, Costigan 364 

See Blakeslce v Com'rs,106 Conn.642,139 Atl.106,55 A.L.R.1319 

Distinguish McGovern v New York, (1923), 234 NY 377,25 ALR 1442

EXAMPLE: P, prior to his election as President of a bank, agreed with D, for a valuable consideration, to resign the office on D's demand. He refused to resign at D's request unless given a promissory note which D gave to P, whereupon P resigned. The consideration for the note is not merely the original agrocment to resign the office but so P's waiver of his refusal to resign.. D could not have cobelled P to resign

Pock v Requa, (1859), 13 Gray 407

The reasoning of the Massachusetts court is that an executory bilateral agreement may be varied by a subsequent oral agreement between the parties, that the release of one from the stipulations of the original agreement is the consideration for the release of the other, and that the mutual releases are the consideration for the new contract and are sufficient to give it full legal effect. 

Rollins v Marsh, (1880), 128 Mass. 116, 120 

Cutter v Cochrane, (1874), 116 Mass. 408 

Thomas v Barnes, (1892), 156 Mass. 585, 31 N. E. 683

Byington v Simpson, (1883), 134 Mass. 145 

If, while a party is hesitating about performing his contract, other parties contract with him for more money to do the same thing, such as to build an archway, or paint a picture, etc. there is no good reason why that is not a binding contract. If that is so, the Massachusetts court asks why cannot the original parties also make a new agreement. 

Allen v Leonard, (1860), 16 Gray 202

The new agreement is substituted for the old and thus operates as a discharge, rescission, or waiver of the old agreement. 

Rogers v Rogers, (1885), 139 Mass. 440, 1 N. E. 122

In other words, the party who refuses to perform his contract to do the work he has agreed to do thereby subjects himself to an action for damages and the opposite party has his election, either to bring an action for the recovery of such damages, or to accede to the demands of his adversary and make the promise and, if he does so if he does so, it is a relinquishment of the original contract and the substitution of a new one. 

The cases in this Commonwealth whore an agreement to modify a contract has been held to be supported by the original consideration have rested either on the ground that the first contract has been waived or rescinded and a new contract with modifying terms substituted for it, or on the ground that, one of the parties having refused to perform his part of an executory contract, the other party, instead of resorting to an action for damages, has made a new agreement to pay additional compensation for its performance. While this doctrine is well established in Massachusetts, it should not be extended beyond the cases to which it is applicable upon the recognized reasons that have been given for it. 

Torrey v Adams, (1925), 254 Mass. 22, 27, 28, 149 N. E. 618 

Feinberg v Adelman, (1927), 260 Mass. 143, 156 N. E. 896

It should be distinguished from the doctrine that recognition of mutual mistake by both parties and the rescission of the old contract for that reason affords consideration for a contract for labor and materials. 

Martiniello v Bemel, (1926), 255 Mass. 25, 150 N. E. 838

(c) OBLIGATION TO THIRD PERSON 

In most jurisdictions, a promise to perform an existing contract with a third person or the performance of it does not constitute a valuable consideration. 

The contrary is the law in England, and in Massachusetts. 

13 C. J. 356 

Abbott v Doane, (1895),163 Mass.433,40 N.E.197,34 L.R.A.33 

Scotson v Pegg, (1861), 6 H. & N. 295, Costigan 379 

EXAMPLE: An uncle promised his nephew an annuity of 150 pounäs upon the marriage of the nephew to a girl to whom the nephew was already engaged at the time of the uncle's promise. 

The subsequent marriage of the nephew was held to be consideration for the uncle's promise.

Shadwell v Shadwell, (1860), 30 L.J.C.P.145, Costigan 374 

See DeCicco v Schweizer, 221 NY 431, LRA 1918E 1004, Costigan 381 

a. PART PAYMENT IN SATISFACTION OF DEBT (1) IN GENERAL 

The payment of a smaller sum in satisfaction of a larger is not a good discharge of a debt for it is doing no more than the debtor is already bound to do and is therefore no consideration for the creditor's promise to forego the residue. 

13 C. J. 357 

Parrott v Mexican Ry., 207 Mass.184,93 N.E.590,34 LRA (NS) 261 

Tyler v Odd Fellows, (1887), 145 Mass. 154, 13 N. E. 360 

Rowland v Hackel, (1922), 243 Mass. 160, 137 N. E. 265 

Bender v Been, (1889),78 Iowa 283,43 N.W.216, Costigan 397 

EXAMPLE: A promise by the holder of a joint and several note, made to one of the makers who had made a part payment thereof, that he would look to the other maker for payment of the residue is without consideration. 

Smith v Bartholomew, (1840), 1 Met. 276

But a gift of a horse or other property, no matter how slight its value, given in satisfaction of a claim or debt, is sufficient, for it will be presumed that such property might be more beneficial to the creditor than the money or he would not have accepted it in satisfaction. 

Pinnel's Case, (1602), 5 Coke' 117 

Costigan 404 

Ellsworth v Fogg, (1862), 35 Vt. 355, Costigan 1457

This principle has been severely criticised as being a rule that a bar of gold worth 100 will discharge a debt of $500 while four hundred gold dollars in current coin will not."

Nevertheless the rule remains and its foundation is that there

-is no consideration for the promise and the creditor may violate his promise with logal impunity, however freely and understandingly made. This rule, which obviously may be urged in violation of good faith, is not to extend beyond its precise import and, whenever the technical reason for its application does not exist, the rule itself is not to be applied. This general principle that part payment of a liquidated indebtedness is no consideration for the discharge of the entire debt is a deduction of strict scholastic logic and has always been regarded as technical and unjust, and the modern tendency of the courts has been to enlargo the exceptions to the rule in order to avoid its harshness and to carry into effect settlements, adjustments and compromises. 

EXAMPLE 1: The check of a firm of which the judgment debtor is a member is consideration for an agreement to accept less than the amount due in full satisfaction of the judgment. 

Vaughn v Robbins, (1925),254 Mass.35,149 NE 677,41 ALR 1488 

EXAMPLE 2: The payment and acceptance of a less sum before the day of payment has arrived is sufficient consideration for a promise to release and discharge the residue of the debt. 

Brooks v White, (1841), 2 Met. 283 

EXAMPLE 3: The giving of a promissory note for a less sum, whether it is the note of a third person or of the debtor, is sufficient consideration for a promise to relcase the whole debt. 

Jaffray v Davis, (1891),124 N.Y.164, 26 N.E.351,11 L.R.A.710 

Melroy v Kemmerer, (1907), 218 Pa.381,67 Atl.699,11 LRA (NS)1018

EXAMPLE 4: A debt may be validly discharged, without a release under scal, in consideration of payment of a part of the amount thereof by the check of a third person. 

Guild v Butler, (1879), 127 Mass. 386

The fact that the debtor borrowed the money from a third person with which to make a part payment does not make this a payment by the third person so as to afford consideration for the promise of the creditor to accept it in full satisfaction. 

Barnett v Rosen, (1920), 235 Mass. 244, 126 N. E. 386

But if it is a part of the agreement that, if the stranger will lend the debtor the money, the creditor will accept such payment in full satisfaction, there is a sufficient consideration for the creditor's promise.. 

Harriman v Harriman, (1859), 12 Gray 341

A contract under seal requires no consideration and, therefore, a creditor, on receiving a part payment of his overdue debt, may release the residue by an instrument under seal. 

Bender v Sampson, (1814), 11 Mass. 42 

Jackson v Ins. Co. (1908), 233 Ill. 161, 84 N. E. 198 

Moss v Goldstein, (1925), 254 Mass. 334, 336, 150 N. E. 91 

Parrott v Mexican Ry., 207 Mass. 184, 93 N.E.590,34 LRANS 261

Specialty Glass Co. v Dalcy, (1899),172 Mass. 460, 52 N.E.633

(2) COMPOSITION WITH CREDITORS 

A composition with creditors is an cxccption to the general rule. An agreement to accept, in satisfaction and discharge of a liquidated debt, a sum less than the full amount due, when such agreement forms part of a composition in which two or more creditors join, mutually stipulating to withdraw or withhold suits and that they will release to their common debtor a part of their claims upon payment of a certain other part, becomes binding upon cach creditor who is a party thereto and upon the debtor. The reason is that the rights and interests of other parties become involved in the arrangement and this affords a new and legal consideration for the promise. 

Perkins v Lockwood, (1868), 100 Mass. 249 

Eaton v Lincoln, (1816), 13 Mass. 424

EXAMPLE: A makes a composition agreement with B, C, and D, three of his creditors, whereby each of them, in consideration of a payment of forty cents on the dollar and of A's promise to treat all assenting creditors alike, promises to accept the said payment as full satisfaction. The composition is valid. 

It is immaterial to its validity that A may have other creditors who do not assent unless B, C and D made their agreement on the condition or expressed assumption that the se creditors should also become parties to the agreement. 

Ordinarily, agreements of composition contain a seal opposite the signature of each creditor and, where this is done, the agreement would be valid even if there were no consideration except in those jurisdictions where the common law significance of the seal has been abolished. 

11.ACCORD AND SATISFACTION a. IN GENERAL 

An accord is an agreement whereby one of the parties undertakes to give or perform and the other to accept in satisfaction of a claim, liquidated or in dispute, and arising either from contract or from tort, something other than or different from what he is or considers himself entitled to. A satisfaction is the execution of such an agreement. 1 C. J. 523 

Forbes v Allen, (1922), 240 Mass. 363, 134 N. E. 244 

Kromer v Heim, (1879), 75 N. Y. 574, Costigan 430

Where the debt or demand is liquidated or certain and is due, the payment by the debtor and the receipt by the creditor of a less sum is not, at common law, an accord and satisfaction, although the creditor agrees to accept it as such, unless under seal or supported by an independent consideration. 

Riggs v Hawley, (1875), 116 Mass. 596 

McKay v Meyers, (1887), 168 Mass. 312 47 N. E. 98 

Tompkins v Hill, (1887), 145 Mass. 379, 14 N. E. 177 

Gilman v Carey, (1908), 198 Mass. 318, 84 N. E. 312 

Attorney Gen. v Am. Legion, (1907),196 Mass.151,163,81 N.E.966 

Gilson v Nesson, (1908),198 Mass.598,84 N.E.854,17 LRA (NS)1208

Tuttle v Metz Co., (1917), 229 Mass. 292, 118 N. E. 291 

EXAMPLE: Whether or not a written release is given cr agreed to be given, the transfer of a business and its acceptance in lieu of any claim for damages will constitute an accord and satisfaction which, as matter of law, will put an end to liability.

b. ACCEPTANCE OF CHECK IN SATISFACTION 

The oregoing principles find frequent application in the case of checks and drafts. Nevertheless, cases in which debtors have undertaken to force a settlement upon their creditors by sending a check in full discharge of a debt or claim which is unliquidated and in dispute have given rise to more than one question on which there is a conflict in the authorities.

In England, a creditor who cashes a check sent in full settlement of an unliquidated or disputed claim is not barred from contending that he

Ion which it was sent did not agree, to take it on the terms if, at the he takes it, he says that he takes it on account. 

Day v licLea, (1889), 22 Q. B. F. 610, Costigan 1405

In Massachusetts, the true rule as to disputed claims is asserted to be the contrary and that the creditor can only accept the check on the terms on which it was offered, namely, in full satisfaction of his demand. He cannot accept the benefit and reject the condition and, if he accepts the check at all, it must be cum onere. When he indorses and collects the check, such acceptance is a conclusive election to be bound by the condition upon which it is offered. rule is supported by the great weight of authority. 

Worcester Co. v Wood, (1911), 209 Mess. 105, 95 N. E. 392 

Whittaker Co. v Standard Co., (1913), 216 Mass.204,103 N.E.695 

Nassoiy v Tomlinson, (1896), 148 N.Y.326,42 N.E.715 This

But in such cases, if there is a dispute as to the amount due but the debtor admits that a portion of the amount is due and sends a check for the amount which he admits to be due on
