The question whether a creditor who, under these circumstances, accepts such a chock, protosting that he takes it on account, is or is not barred from suing for the balance of the claim is onc on which the authorities are again in conflict. 

Schnell v Purlmon, (1924), 238 NY 362,144 NE 641,34 ALR 1023

Some Status hold that the creditor is barred in such a case on the ground that when it is admitted that one of two specific sums is due, but there is a genuine dispute as to which is the proper amount, the demand is regarded as being unliquidated. 

Tanner v Morrill, (1895), 108 Mich.58,65 N.W.664, Costigan 416 

Thompson v Southern Co., (Colo.,1919),178 Pac.577,4 A.L.R.471 

Nassoiy v Tomlinson, (1896),148 N.Y.326,42 N.E.715

The complete rule in Massachusetts is that where a check is sent in discharge of a claim, no part of which is admitted to be duc, the creditor must accept the chuck, if at all, on the condition on which it is sent and, by cashing the check, ho clucts to accept the condition on which it is scnt. But the doctring of election does not obtain where the debtor undertakes to make payment ca that which he admits to be due conditioned on its being accepted in payment of that which is in dispute. Such a condition, under these circumstances, is onc which the debtor has no right to impose and it is void for that reason. In such a caso, the creditor is not put to en election to refuse the payment or clse to take it on the condition on which it is ciforud, but he can take it free of the void condition which the debtor has sought to impose. 

EXAMPLE: P sold and delivered goods to D to the value of $80. D returned part of the goods, valued at $30, which he claimed to be defective but P denica this and refused to accept them. Thile the matter was still in dispute, D sunt a check for $50 to P which was stipulated to be in full settlement of P's claim. P cashed the check and sued D for the balance. By cashing the check, P did not lose his right to sue 

D for the balance. 

Whittaker Co. v Standard Co., (1913),216 Mass.204,103 N.E.695

The law on this point is contra in some jurisdictions. 

Goddard v O'Brien, 1882), 9 .B.D. 37, Costigan 401, note 

Bidder v Bridges, (1887), 37 Ch. Div. 406, Costigan 398 

Nassoiy v Tomlinson, (1896), 148 N. Y. 326, 42 N. E. 715 12.

PAST CONSIDERATION a. IN GENERAL 

By the great weight of authority, a past consideration, if it imposed no legal obligation at the time it was furnished, will support no promise whatever; or, as the rule has been stated otherwise, an executed consideration is no consideration for any promise other than that which the law would imply.

Morse v Mason, (1870), 103 Mass. 560

EXAMPLE 1: A subsequent promise by a vendor of land, sold for an entire sum, to make a deduction from the purchase money if there was any deficiency in the estimated quantity of land, is void as without consideration.

There is consideration for the promise, though it was made after the medicine was furnished and without any express previous request by the 

Overseers.

In other words, the present day value of a subsequent express promise is that a service or act rendered on request. raises a presumption by implication to pay what it is worth and the subsequent express promise of a sum certain is evidence for the jury to fix the amount. Williston on Contracts, Section 144 EXAMPLE: P performed a surgical operation on D's minor son at D's request and later sent a bill for $1000 for his services which D promised to pay. After the election, D gave his note for the amount of the bill. There is no consideration for the note. 

Dearborn v Bowman, (1841), 3. Met. 155

There is an exception to the rule that a subsequent promise to do anything beyond what the law would imply is ineffectual in cases of past liability where the right of action is barred by a positive rule of law, such as debts discharged in bankruptcy or barred by the statute of limitations, and the contracts of infants and insane persons. insane persons. Likewise, a payment made or act done in behalf of a person may subsequently be ratified by him and he will become liable.

MORAL OBLIGATION 

By the general rulc, a more moral consideration is no consideration for an express promise. 

Dearborn v Bowman, (1841), 3 Met. 155 

Chamberlin v Whitford, (1869), 102 Mass. 448 

Schnell v Nell, (1861), 17 Ind. 29, Costigan 315 

Rask v Norman, (1918), 141 Minn. 198, 169 N.W.704,17 A.L.R.1296

It has been said that such a doctrine is not creditable to the common law but the rule has its origin in the widely diversified character of moral duties and the consequent difficulty of measuring them with exactness and actcrmining which arc so high and obligatory in their nature as to demand, in their performance, the payment of money. It is truc that gencral rules of law established for the protection and security of honest and fair minded men who may inconsiderately make promises without any equivalent will sometimes screen men of different charactor from engagements which they are bound in the forum of conscience to perform. This is a defect inheront in all human systems of legislation. The rule that a more verbal promisc without any consideration cannot be enforced by action cannot be departed from to suit particular cases in which a refusal to perform may be disgraceful. 

Williams v Hathaway, (1037), 19 Pick. 387

EXAMPLE: P rendered medical services to D's adult son of which D learned after the services were performed and promised to pay for them. There was no consideration for the promise. 

Mills v Wyman, (1825), 2 Pick. 207 

a. PROMISE UNENFORCEABLE BY ACT OF LAW (1) IN GENERAL 

Under certain circumstances, a consideration insufficient to raise an implied promise will support an express one. 

Eastwood v Kenyon, (1840), 11 A. & E. 438, Costigan 292

Thus, while a moral cbligation alone will not support an express promise yet, if it is coupled with some preexisting obligation which has become incperative by positive law, it will form the basis for an cffcetive express promise, as in the case of debts discharged in bankruptcy, or barred by the statute of limitations, or the contracts of infants or insane persons.

Express promises founded on such preexisting equitable obligations may be enforced. There is consideration for them. 

They merely remove an impediment created by law to the recovery of debts honestly due but which public policy protects the debtor from being compelled to pay. In all these cases, there was originally a quid pro quo and, according to the principles of natural justice, the party receiving ought to pay but the legislature has said that he shall not be coerced. all these cases, there is a moral obligation founded upon an antecedent valuable consideration. These promises, therefore, have a sound legal basis. They are not promises to pay some- In

- thing for nothing, not mere naked pacts, but the voluntary revival or creation of an obligation which before existed in natural law but which had been dispensed with, not for the benefit of the party obliged solely, but principally for the public convenience. The general position, therefore, that moral obligation is a sufficient consideration for an express promise is to be limited in its application to cases where at some time cr other a sufficient consideration has existed. 

Mills v Wyman, (1825), 3 Pick. 207 

(2) STATUTE OF LIMITATIONS 

The Massachusetts statute of limitations provides: "No acknowledgment or promise shall be evidence of a new or continuing contract whereby to take an action of contract cut of the operation of this chapter or to deprive a party of the benefit thereof unless such acknowledgment or promise has been made or is contained in a writing signed by the party chargeable thereby."

G. L. chapter 260, section 13 

"The preceding section shall not alter or impair the effect of a payment of principal or interest made by any person. 

G. L. chapter 260, section 14 

See Peterson v Abbe, (1920) (1920), 234 Mass. 467, 125 N. E. 611 

State Bank v Beacon Trust Co., (1929),267 Mess.355,166 N.E.837 

Old Dominion Co. v Bigelow, (1909), 203 Mass.159,201,89 N.E.193 

Bogart v Porter, (Cal., 1924), 223 Pac. 959, 31 A.Ĺ.R.1045

A debt barred by the statute of limitations may be revived by a new promise to pay it by the debtor but not by a third person who was not morally bound to pay it. 

13 C. J. 364 

Maxim v Morse, (1811), 8 Mass. 127 

Little v Blunt, (1830) 9 Pick. 488 

Ilsley v Jewett, (1841), 3 Met. 439

The statute applies only to actions of contract, and actions of tort arc not revived by a new promise. 

Nelson v Petterson, (1907),299 111.240,82 NE 229,13 LRANS 912

But a promise to pay damages for a tort, if made before the statute has run against the right of action, and in consideration that the injured party will not sue, will toll the statute and cause it to run from the date of the promise. 

Armstrong v Levan, (1885),109 2a.St.177,1 Atl.204, Costigan 509

The rule that a part payment will take a debt out of the statute of limitations is founded on the theory that the part payment is an acknowledgment that the debt exists from which the law may imply a new promise to pay the balance. The part payment must be made under such circumstances as reasonably and by fair implicetion lued to the inference that the debtor intended to renew his promise of payment. 

Roscoc v Hale, (1856), 7 Gray 274 

Pond v Williams, (1854), 1 Gray 630 

Gillingham v Brown, (1900), 178 Mass. 417, 421, 60 N. E. 122

If the identity of the debt sued on with the debt on which the payment is made is established, a part payment will take the whole debt cut of the statute whether it is represented by one

Taylor v Foster, (1881), 132 Mass. 30

If the right of action on a debt is not merely barred by an act of the law but is extinguished by an act of the parties, it cannot be revived either by a part payment or by a subsequent promise to pay it. Thus, after the voluntary release of a debt by a creditor, there is no obligation, legal or moral, to pay the debt, and no consideration to support a new promise. 

Hale v Rice, (1878), 124 Mass. 292, 299 

Valentine v Foster (1840), 1 Met. 520 

Shepard v Rhodes, 1863), R. I. 470, Costigan 311 

(3) BANKRUPTCY AND INSOLVENCY LAWS 

A Massachusetts statute provides: "No promise for the payment of a debt made by an insolvent debtor who has obtained his discharge from such debt under proceedings in bankruptcy or insclvendy shall be evidence of a new or continuing contract whereby to deprive the debtor of the benefit of relying upon such discharge in bar of the recovery of a judgment upon such debt unless such promise is made by or contained in some writing signed by him or by some person thereunto by him lawfully authorized."

G. L. chapter 259, section 3 

In a few states, including Massachusetts, the promise to pay a debt discharged in bankruptcy or insolvency must be in writing. The statute modifies in this respect the rule in 

Way v Sperry, (1850), 6 Cush. 238

The statute requires an express promise. An acknowledgment is not enough. Thus, an admission that a debt was incurred and has not been paid is not sufficient. 

Pratt v Russell, (1851), 7 Cush. 462

A part payment is an acknowledgment of the debt but it will not revive a debt discharged in bankruptcy or insolvency. 

Institution for Savings v Littlefield, (1850), 6 Cush. 210

A promise to pay "some thing on account" will not revive the whole debt on the ground that there is no reason why a promise to pay a sum on account should do what an actual payment cf it would not do. The words "on account" simply admit or acknowledge the existence of the original debt unsatisfied and apply the payment to it. They do not in terms waive any defense and the implication of a now promise to pay the whole debt is excluded by the promise which is expressed. The promise is not to pay the whole debt but to pay "something", that is to say, a sum, the amount of which the debtor reserves the right to determine himself. It cannot do more than revive a nominal portion of the debt. 

Bigelow v Norris, (1885), 139 Mass. 12, 29 N. E. 61

The statute does not extend beyond the requirement of a continuing promise in some writing signed by the debtor or by his authority. The new promise must be distinct and unequivocal. 

Merriam v Bayley, (1848), 1 Cush. 77 

Elwell v Cumner, (1883), 136 Mass. 102

yd. oben si deinon

MI SIJA TROOTAC 

40 

TOR 

DEI. 061 10

But no preciso form of statement is necessary. Tho intention and obligation of the debtor must be gathered from the phraseology which he chooses to employ. 

EXAMPLE: A bankrupt wrote a letter to a creditor, saying: "You are not to regard yourself as in any danger of losing the amount as long as I hold my present position because I have promised in time to take care of it but I must have time to do it, as it will be done as fast as my resources will allow. 

This is a sufficient promise to revive the debt. 

Nathan v Leland, (1906), 193 Mass. 576, 581, 79 N. E. 793 

Massachusetts Bar Examination, December, 1918, afternoon #4

A written promise given after the petition in bankruptcy is filed but before the discharge is cbtained will revive the debt although a discharge is subsequently obtained; but a promise made before the petition is filed is not enforceable. 

Nathan v Leland, (1906), 193 Mass. 576, 581, 79 N. E. 793 

Jacobs v Carpenter, (1894), 161 Mass. 1o, 36 N. E. 676 

Heim v Chapman, (1898), 171 Mass. 347, 50 N. 

United Society v Winkley, (1856), 7 Gray 460 

Lerow v Vilmarth, (1863); 7 Allen 463 lö, . 529 E.

There are similarities and differences in the statutory methods of reviving debts which are barred by the statute of limitations and debts discharged in bankruptcy. Both statutes apply to contract debts only and both require the promise to be in writing. Debts barred by the statute of limitations can be revived only by a promise signed by the debtor personally or by his written acknowledgment, or by a part payment. Debts discharged in bankruptcy may be revived by a promise signed either by the debtor or by his agent, but an acknowledgment by hier a part payment is not sufficient. 

Owen v Young, (1873), 112 Mass. 403 

(4) INFANTS AND INSANE PERSONS 

Another exception to the rule that a subsequent promise to do anything beyond what the law would imply is ineffectual exists in the cases of contracts of infants and insane persons whose contracts, though voidable, may be ratified by a subsequent express promise or by subsequent conduct after they have become sui juris. This topic is elsewhere treated.

Although there is much conflict in the cases, it is a well established rule in Massachusetts and generally that, in actions brought on promises made by infants and insane persons and ratified after they have become sui juris; on unconditional promises by debtors after their discharge in bankruptcy or insolvency; and on promises which have been revived after the statute of limitations has furnished a bar, the plaintiff may declare on the original promise and the new promise may be relied on as a waiver of the defense which the debtor originally possessed. The law only suspends the remedy. It does not extinguish the debt. 

Way v Sperry, (1850), 6 Cush. 238

Reed v Batchelder, (1840), 1 Met. 559 

Wolffe v Eberlein, (1885), 74 la. 99, Costigan 305

State Trust Co. v Sheldon, (1896), 68 Vt. 259, 35 Atl. 177

VI. 

CAPACITY OF PARTIES 

A. 

INFANTS 1. IN GENERAL 

At common law, an infant is a person of either sex under the age of twenty-one years. This is also the rule in Massachusetts. An infant reaches his majority the first moment of the day before the twenty-first anniversary of his birthday, because the law takes no notice of fractions of a day, under the rule "de minimis non curat lex". 

Bardwell v Purrington, (1871), 107 Mass. 419 

Ross v Morror, (1892), 85 Tex. 172, 16 L. R. A. 542

An infant who has neither parent nor guardian is, nevertheless, an infant, and his capacity to contract is not thereby enlarged. 

Wickham v Borley, (1911), 136 Ga. 594, 71 SE 881,36 LRA (NS) 57

Emancipation, which is the renunciation by the father of his right to the services of his minor son, does not enlarge or affect the infant's legal capacity to contract. 

Mason v Wright, (1847), 13 Met. 306 

If a minor is emancipated, and an express promise is made, with the consent of the father, to pay the infant for his labor, no other notice of his emancipation is necessary to entitle the infant to maintain an action on the promise. 

Wood v Corcoran, (1861), 1 Allen 405

If a minor who has been emancipated is employed by another, but without any express contract of employment, the law will imply a promise by the employer to the infant, and not to the father. 

Cory v Corey, (1837), 19 Pick. 29 

2. WHETHER VOID OR VOIDABLE 

The contract of an infant is considered in law as different from the contracts of all other persons. The general rule is that his contracts are voidable, and not void, the law so far protecting him as to give him an opportunity to avoid them at his election. 

Earle v Reed, (1845), 10 Met. 387 

Owen v Long, (1873), 112 Mass. 403 

Parker v Lincoln, (1815), 12 Mass. 16 

Reed v Batchelder, (1840), 1 Met. 559 

Willis v Twambly, (1816), 13 Mass. 204 

Thompson v Hamilton, (1832), 12 Pick 425 

Nightingale v Withington, (1818), 15 Mass. 272

An adult may dispose of his property without any valuable consideration, or give away his money, and cannot get it back; but a transfer by an infant, whether with or without consideration, is voidable. 

Vent v Osgood, (1837), 19 Pick. 372

- 3. 

RATIFICATION AND AVOIDANCE a. IN GENERAL 

An executory contract, while voidable, is presumed to be invalid until ratified, and the burden of overcoming this presumption is upon him who seeks to enforce its terms.

An executed contract, while voidable, is presumed to be valid, and the burden is upon him who seeks to avoid it to establish his right to do so. 

31 C. J. 1061

Therefore, the executed contracts of an infant, if voidable, bind him unless he disaffirms them but his executory contracts do not bind him unless he ratifies them. 

Whitney v Dutch, (1817), 14 Mass. 457

The former principle applies when an interest in property of a fixed and permanent nature is vested either in the infant or the other party to the contract under an executed contract, as by a conveyance of real estate. 

Clemmer v Price, (1910), 59 Tex. Civ. App. 84, 125 S. W. 604

RATIFICATION OF CONTRACTS 

b. 

(1) TIME FOR RATIFICATION 

The time for ratification of the contract or undertaking of an infant is after his arrival at majority. A ratification during infancy is voidable as well as the contract. 

31 C. J. 1062 

Owen v Long, (1873), 112 Mass. 403 

Jackson v Mayo, (1814), 11 Mass. 147 

Ford v Phillips, (1822), 1 Pick. 202 

(2) WHO MAY RATIFY The ratification of a contract is a personal right to an infant. Hence the ratification can be only by the infant himself, his agent, or his personal representatives. 

31 C. J. 1062 

(3) KNOWLEDGE OF NON-LIBILITY 

Although the cases are in conflict it has been held in Massachusetts and in many states that it is not necessary, at the time of ratification, that one should know that the transaction was not binding on him at the time it was made by reason of his infancy. Such knowledge is presumed. 

Morse v Wheeler, (1862), 4 Allen 570

Owen v Long, (1873), 112 Mass. 403 (dictum to contrary) or conditional. 

(4) CONDITIONAL RATIFICATION 

Ratification may be either absolute

If conditional, the terms of the condition must have happened or been complied with before an action can be sustained. 

Proctor v Sears, (1862), 4 Allen 95

- EXAMPLE: A promise to pay "when able" is a conditional promise and the plaintiff, to avail himself of it, must prove the ability of the defendant to pay. It is not necessary to show an ability to pay without inconvenience. Evidence that there is property from which the debt might be paid, or an income from some source which would enable the party to pay, is sufficient.

Thompson v Lay, (1826), 4 Pick. 47 (5) WHAT CONSTITUTES RTIFICATION (a) IN GENERAL 

In the absence of statute, ratification may be by an express promis, either written or orai, or it may be implied by such conduct, after the infant comes of age, as fairly and justly leads to the inference that he intended to ratify the contract. 

Whitney v Dutch, (1817), 14 Mass. 457 

Martin v Mayo, (1813), 9 Mass. 62 

Tobey v Wood, (1877), 123 Mass. 88

Peirce v Tobey (1842), 5 Met. 168 Dana v Stearns, (1849), Cush. 372 

Keegan v Cox, (1874), 116 Mass. 289

Barnaby v Barnaby, (1822) 

(1822), 1 Pick. 221 ? 

Boston Bank v Chamberlin, (1818), 15 Mass. 220 

McDonald v Sargent, (1898), 171 Mass. 492, 51 N. E. 17

EXECUTORY CONTRACTS 

An executory contract requires a greater degree of confirmatory conduct than one executed. 

31 C. J. 1064

It has been said that a direct promise, when of age, is necessary to establish a contract made during minority; 

Proctor v Scars, (1862), 4 Allen 95 

Ford v Phillips, (1822), 1 Pick. 202 

Smith v Kelley, (1847), 13 Met. 309

Peirce v Tobey (1842), 5 Met. 168 

Smith v Mayo, (1812), 9 Mass. 62 or a direct confirmation, although not in words amounting to a direct promise. Thus, if an infant, after coming of age, should say "I do ratify and confirm", or "I do agree to pay! the debt, either is a sufficient ratification. 

Thompson v Lay, (1826), 4 Pick. 47 

But it is also said that, in the absence of an express promise to pay, an affirmance can be shown by unequivocal acts of the defendant, after he became capable of contracting, which show his intention to pay the debt. Ratification must be clear and unequivocal, whether made by words or conduct. 

Tobey v Wood, (1877), 123 Mass. 88 

Todd v Clapp, (1875), 118 Mass. 495

There seems to be a difference between contracts simply for the payment of money, or the performance of any personal duty, and those which are connected with land, or grow out of an interest therein. In the latter case, a ratification may be implied.

EXAMPLE: If an infant takes a lease for years of land, rendering rent which is in arrears, then the infant comes of age and still continues the cccupation of the land, this is a ratification and charges him with all arrears of rent incurred during his minority.

Barnaby v Barnaby, (1822), 1 Pick. 221 

A mere acknowledgment of a debt, such as would take a case out of the statute of limitations, is not a ratification. 

The reason is that an acknowledgment merely avoids the presumption of payment which is created by the statute of limitations whereas a minor may avoid his contracts before or after coming of age, whether he acknowledges the debt or not. Hence, some positive act or declaration on his part is necessary to defeat his power of avoiding the debt. 

Ford v Phillips, (1822), 1 Pick. 202 

Tappan v Abbott, (1820), 1 Pick. 203, ncte 

EXAMPLE 1: An infant made a promissory ncte. Upon being asked to pay it after reaching his majority, he acknowledged that the money was due and promised that, on his return to his home in Eastport, he would endeavor to procure the money and send it to the payee. This is a sufficient promise to pay and ratiiication. 

Whitney v Dutcli, (1817), 14 Mass. 457 

EXAMPLE 2: An infant made a promissory note. After coming of age, he told the payce that he owed the money, was unable to pay it, but would endeavor, however, to get his brother to be bound with him. This is not a ratification. 

Ford v Phillips, (1822), 1 Pick. 202 

EXAMPLE 3: An infant make a promissory note for a valuable consideration, though not for necessaries. He died after coming of age, leaving a will in which he directed that his just debts should be paid. This is not a ratification. The note was not in law one of his just debts" and there is nothing in the language of the will to show that the testator so considered it. More specific language would be required to prove his intent to pay a voidable debt. 

Smith v Mayo, (1812), 9 Mass. 62 It

Part payment by an infant, after coming of agc, of a debt contracted during infancy, is not sufficient ratification. is, in effect, no more than an acknowledgment that the debt has not been paid. 

Whitney v Dutch, (1817), 14 Mass. 457

Barnaby v Barnaby, (1822), 1 Fick. 221 

Internat'l Sec.v Santana,166 La.671,117 So.768,59 £LR 276

31 C. J. 1065 

(b) RETENTION OR DISPOSAL OF CON- SIDERATION 

By the general rule, if the infant, after coming of age, retains the property for an unreasonable time, or sells or otherwise disposes of it, there is a ratification of the contract and he cannot avoid it thereafter. 

Boyden v Boyden, (1845) 9 Met. 519 

Ready v Pinkham, (1902), 181 Mass. 351, 63 N. E. 387 

(c) EFFECT OF RATIFICATION 

A ratification renders the contract absolutely binding ab initio and in toto. 

Dempsey v Chambers, (1891), 154 Mass. 330, 28 N. E. 279 

Winchester v Thayer, (1880), 129 Mass. 129 

Kennedy v Doyle, (1865), 10 Allen 161 

Owen v Long, (1875), 112 Mass. 403

If a contract has been ratified, it cannot be disaffirmed thereafter. 

Mc Carty v Hoodstock Co.(1890),92 Ala.463,8 So.417,12 L.R.A.136

C. AVOIDANCE OF CONTRACTS (1) WHO MAY AVOID 

Only the infant has the privilege of disaffirming the contract and, until he does so, the other party is bound by it. 

Winchester v Thayer, (1880), 129 Mass. 129

The right to disaffirm is the personal privilege of the infant and cannot be exercised by any other person except by his heirs, or his administrator or executor, who will be entitled to plead infaney in avoidance of the infant's contracts if the plea is made in good time. 

Hussey v Jewett, (1812), 9 Mass. 100 

Barnard v Eaton, (1848), 2 Cush. 294 

McCarty v Murray, (1854), 3 Gray 578 

Badger v Phinney, (1819), 15 Mass. 359 

Kendall v Lawrence, (1839), 22 Pick. 540 

Harvey v Briggs, (1890), 68 Miss. 60, 8 So. 274, 10 L.R.A. 62

The fact that the father of an infant was present when the infant entered into the contract, and approved it, does not affect the infant's right to avoid. 

Bombardier v Gocarich, (Vt., 1920), 110 Atl. 11, 9 A.L.R.1028

A guardian cannot disaffirm the contracts of his infant ward.

Oliver v Houalet, (1816), 13 Mass. 240 

Distinguish Chandler v Simmons, (1867), 97 Mass. 508

A guardian can make no contract binding upon the ward or upon his estate. The guardian has only the management and

Wallis v Bardwell, (1879), 126 Mass. 366

The right of avoidance is not assignable, hence an infant's contracts cannot be avoided by his creditors or by his assignee in insolvency. 

Kingman v Perkins, (1870), 105 Mass. 111 (1839), 22 Pick. 540 

Kendall v Lawrence 

McCarty v Murray, (1854), Gray 578

EXAMPLE: An infant executed a mortgage and later became insolvent. The mortgage cannot be avoided by the assignee in insolvency. 

Mansfield v Gordon, (1887), 144 Mass. 168, 10 N. E. 773 Coverture?does does not remove the disability of infancy. 

Walsh v Young, (1872), 110 Mass. 3961 ! 

(2) TIME FOR AVOIDANCE 

Executory contracts relating to either real or personal property may be avoided by an infant either before or after his coming of age. 

McCarthy v Henderson, (1885), 138 Mass. 310

Executed contracts relating to personal property are subject to the same rule, except implied contracts for necessaries. 

At common law, a lease of real estate is personal property; 

Shipley v Smith, (1904), 162 Ind. 526, 70 N. E. 803 but, by Massachusetts statute, a lease of real estate for one hundred years or more is real property, so long as fifty years remain unexpired.

G. L. Chapter 186, Section 1 

Executed contracts relating to real property may be avoided by the infant only after coming of age. However, after an infant has delivered a deed of real estate, he may enter upon the land during his infancy, and take the rents and profits, although he cannot disaffirm his deed until he reaches his majority. 523, 530 

Chandler v Simmons (1867), 97 Mass. 508, 511 

Scanlan v Wright, (1833), 30 Mass. 

Worcester v Eaton, (1856), 13 Mass. 370

In summary, all contracts of an infant may be avoided by him, either before or after reaching majority, except executed contracts relating to real estate and implied contracts for necessaries. 

(3) WHAT CONSTITUTES AVOIDANCE 

In general, any act showing unequivocally a renunciation of, or a disposition not to abide by the contract made during minority is sufficient to avoid it.

GOTTA & DAUNUGTECTON OF

31 C. J. 1068 

Simpson v Ins. Co., (1903),184 Mass.348,68 N.E.673,63 L.R.A.741 

Willis v Twambly, (1816), 13 Mass. 204 

EXAMPLE 1: An infant bought a bicycle which was not a necessary, paid therefor, used it several months, and then returned it. This is sufficient disaffirmance to enable him to recover the full purchase price paid. 

Pyne v Wood, (1888), 145 Mass. 558, 14 N. E. 775 

EXAMPLE 2: An infant, after his father's death, shipped as a sailor for a long whaling voyage. He deserted the ship after two years, before the completion of the voyage. This is a sufficient disaffirmance and he can recover on a quantum meruit for the fair value of his services. 

Vent v Osgood, (1837), 19 Pick. 572

If the contract is purely executory, a plea of infancy to a suit brought to enforce it is sufficient without any preceding act of avoidance. 

14 R. C. L. 238

G. L. Chapter 231, Section 147, Form 32 

Worcester v Eaton, (1816), 13 Mass. 371 

Sanders v Williams, (1909), 163 Als. 451, 50 So. 893

The defense of infancy, like that of insanity or intoxication must be pleaded or shown in evidence under the general issue in order to be taken advantage of in avoidance of an executory contract, otherwise the defense is held to be waived. 

Worcester v Eaton, (1816), 13 Mass. 371 

(4) RETURN OF PROPERTY OR CONSIDERATION 

It was said formerly in Massachusetts that there could be no recovery of property sold by an infant unless he returned the consideration received for it. 

Bartlett v Cowles, (1860), 15 Gray 445 

Badger v Phinney, (1819), 15 Mass. 359

But that statement is said now to be obiter dicta and contrary to the whole theory of the law of infants. 

Bartlett v Drake, (1868), 100 Mass. 174 

Englebert v Pritchett, (Neb., 1894), 26 L. R. A. 177

It is now well settled in this Commonwealth, whatever may be the law elsewhere that, in order to avoid a contract, it is not necessary that the other party should be put in statu quo. 

Simpson v Ins. Co., (1903),184 Mass.348,68 N.E.673,63 L.R.A.741 

Benson v Tucker, (1913), 212 Mass.60,98 N.E.589,41 LRA (NS) 1219 

Dube v Beaudry, (189)),150 Mass.448,25 N.E.222,6 L.R.A. 146 

Gillis v Goodwin, (1901), 180 Mass. 140, 61 N. E. 813 

Kelley v Halox, (1925), 256 Mass. 5, 152 N. E. 236 

Morse v Ely, (1891), 154 Mass. 458, 28 N. E. 577

The reason is that, if the right of an infant to disaffirm a contract depended on his putting the other party in statu quo, he would, in many cases, lose the protection which the law affords him by reason of his infancy. 

White v New Bedford Corp., (1900), 178 Mass. 20, 59 N. E. 642

Smith v Equitable Bank, (1914), 219 Mass. 382, 106 N. E. 1020 

Shipley v Smith, (1904), 162 Ind. 526, 70 N. É. 803

The general rule, including Massachusetts, is that all consideration which remains in the infant's possession upon his reaching majority, or at the time of his attempted disaffirmance, in case he is still under age, must be returned. 

In other words, if an infant, when he seeks to avoid a purchase of property by him, has in his possession the specific property which came to him under the contract, or any part of it, he must return it as a prerequisite to a recovery of the amount paid by him. Furthermore, his act of avoiding the contract will devest him of all right to retain such property and the other party may reclaim it. 

Chandler v Simmons, (1867), 97 Mass. 508 

Tracy v Brown, (1928), 265 Mass. 163, 163 N. E. 885 

Crowley v Mutual Corp., (1922), 242 Mass. 259, 136 N. E. 179 

McGuckian v Carpenter, (R. I.,1920),110 Lt1.402,16 A.L.R.1473 

Pelletier v Couture (1888) 148 Mass.269,19 NE 400,1 LRA 863

If he still has the property and returns it, he may recover the full purchase price paid, without allowing anything for the rent or use of the property while in his possession. 

McCarthy v Henderson, (1885), 138 Mass. 310 

Gillis v Goodwin, (1901), 180 Mass. 140, 61 N. E. 813 

Simpson v Ins. Co., (1903),184 Mass.348,68 N.E.673,63 L.R.A.741 

Contra: Rice v Butler, (1899), 160 NY 378,55 NE 275,47 LRA 303 

Pettit v Liston, 97 Or.464,191 Pac.660,11 A.L.R.487

But if the property purchased by an infant has been parted with by him, he may, on the disaffirmance of the contract, recover the amount paid without restoring the property. 

White v New Bedford Corp., (1900), 178 Mass. 20, 59 N. E. 642

Likewise, if property has been sold by an infant and the money received by him therefor has been wasted or spent during his infancy, payment or tender of the amount is not necessary to enable him to avoid the contract. 

Walsh v Young, (1872), 110 Mass. 396 

Chandler v Simmons, (1 o7), 97 Mass. 514

It is generally held that he may be required to return only such part of the consideration as remains in his hands in specie and, as to the remainder, he is not obliged to return the equivalent thereof in money because, at the most, his failure to do so can be considered only as a breach of an implied contract and this the law permits him to avoid. 

(5) EFFECT OF AVOIDANCE 

t common law, disaffirmance renders the contract absolutely void eb initio and in toto, not only as between the immediste parties, Boyden v Boyden, (1845) 9 Met. 519 

Badger v Phinney, (1819) 15 Mass. 359 

Drumhiller v Norick Co., (Okla.,1930), 289 Pac.698,69 ALR 1368 but also as against third parties, who acquire no rights under the avoided contract of an infant, irrespective of their good faith.

Brewster v Weston (1920), 235 Mass. 14, 126 N. E. 271 

Searcy v Hunter, (1891), 81 Tex. 644, 17 S. W. 372

The common law rule is modified by the Uniform Sales Act as to the rights of third parties with respect to sales of personal property only, as follows: "If the seller of goods but his title has not been has a voidable title the to avoided at the time of the sale, the buyer acquires a good title to the goods; provided that he buys them in good faith, for value, and without notice of the seller's defect in title." 

G. L. Chapter 106, Section 26 

EXAMPLE: A minor sold a Dodge car to D who resold it to 

X who bought in good faith, for value, and without notice of D's defect of title. The minor then disaffirmed the contract. 

The Sales Act has deprived the infant of any rights in the property or against X. He may,

He may, however, recover from D the value of the car as of the date of the contract. 

Carpenter v Grow, (1923), 247 Mass. 133, 141 N. E. 859

Once a contract has been disaffirmed, it cannot be ratified afterwards by the infant. 

Edgerton v Wolf, (1856), 6 Gray 453 

Massachusetts Bar Examination, July, 1909, afternoon #1

A contract cannot be disaffirmed in part. Thus, if an infant, when sued for the price of goods sold and delivered to him, pleads infancy, this is a disaffirmance. If he still has the goods in his possession, he cannot keep them without paying for them, for the privilege of pleading infancy is a shield, not a sword; and, if he refuses to pay for them or to surrender them, title to the goods revests in the seller who may maintain replevin to recover them. 

Badger v Phinney, (1819), 15 Mass. 359, 363

Dana v Stcarns, (1849), 3 Cush. 372 

Drude v Curtis, (1903), 183 Mass. 317, 67 N.E.317,62 LRA 755 

Cf.Lamkin v Ledoux, (3906),101 Me.581,64 Atl.1048,8 LRA (NS) 104

So an infant cannot repudiate a purchase-money mortgage of land without avoiding the deed to himself.

Robert v Viggin, 1 N. H. 73 

Unless it has been fully executed, a special contract by an infant for his services is voidable by him and, upon avoid-' ing it, he may maintain an action upon a quantum meruit as if such contract had never been made. 

Vent v Osgood, (1837), 19 Pick. 572 

Stcne v Dennison, (1832), 13 Pick. 1 

Moses v Stevens, (1824), 2 Pick. 332 

Gaffney v Hayden, (1872), 110 Mass. 137

PARTICULAR TRANSACTIONS 

a.

MARRIAGE 

A marriage in which either party is under the common law age of consent, which is twelve for females and fourteen for males, is void.

G. L. Chapter 207, Section 9

TRG KONTIGEN TON

A marriage in which each party has attained the common law age of consent at the time of the marriage is valid at common law and in Massachusetts, notwithstanding a Massachusetts statute which prohibits ministers and magistrates, under heavy penalties, from solemnizing marriages of males under twenty-one or of females under eighteen without the consent of their parents or guardians if there are any such in the state competent to act. The reason is that such statute is intended as directory only upon ministers and magistrates and to prevent such marriages, from being entered into, so far as possible, without holding them to be void.

G. L. Chapter 207 Sections 7, 51 Parton v Hervey, (1854), 1 Gray 119 

Browning v Browning, (1913),89 Kan.98,130 Pac.852, LRA 1916C 737 

See Levy v Downing, (1913), 213 Mass. 334, 100 N. E. 638 

See Holt v Ward Clarencieux, (1732), 2 Strange 937, Costigan 471 

See Acts of 1931, Chapters 212, 237, 341 

b. CONTRACTS PURSUANT TO LEGAL OBLIGATION 

Obligations imposed by law upon an infant are binding upon him. 

Baker v Lovett, (1309), 6 Mass. 78

Where an infant is under a legal obligation to do a certain act, he may, in general, bind himself for its performance by a contract which will be valid notwithstanding his infancy. Thus, an infant father of an illegitimate child may bind himself by contract with the mother for its support, or by a bond executed in a bastardy proceeding to indemnify the local authorities against the expense of supporting the child. 

31 C. L. 1081 

Mc Call v Parker, (1847), 13 Met. 372

At common law, a husband, though an infant, was liable for his wife's debts contracted before her marriage. 

Butler v Breck, (1843), 7 Met. 164

By statute, a husband is not liable for his wife's debts contracted before her marriage.

G. L. Chapter 209, Section 8 

Although there is no decision in this state, there seems to be no doubt that an infant is liable on any recognizance given by him when accused of crime. 

Pierce Co.v Wallace, (1925), 251 Mass.383,146 N.E.658 (dictum)

By statute, it is provided that a minor who is a material witness in a criminal matter may recognize for his appearance and that the recognizance shall be valid notwithstanding his minority.

G. L. Chapter 276, Section 48 

C.

APPOINTMENT OF AGENTS 

The authorities are in conflict but the better view appears to be that an infant's appointment of an agent or attorney, and the acts of the latter, are not void but voidable, there being no distinction in this regard between such an appointment and other acts or contracts of the infant. 

Whitney v Dutch, (1817), 14 Mass. 457 

Benson v Tucker, (1912), 212 Mass. 60, 98 N. E. 589 !

d. CONTRACTS FOR NECESSARIES (1) IN GENERAL 

An infant is liable for necessaries furnished him or his family suitable to his state and condition in life. 

31 C. J. 1075 

Vent v Osgood, (1837), 19 Pick. 572 

Angel v McLellan, (1819), 16 Mass. 28 

Bradford v French, (1872), 110 Mass. 365 

(2) LIMITS AND EXTENT OF LIABILITY (a) IN GENERAL 

An infant is liable in implied contract for the fair value of necessaries actually furnished to him at his request and on his credit. 

McCarthy v Henderson, (1885), 138 Mass. 310

To render an infant liable for necessaries, they must have been actually furnished; hence, he cannot make a binding executory agreement to purchase necessaries, or to pay for necessaries to be supplied to him in the future. 

Earle v Reed, (1845), 10 Met. 387 

Trainer v Trumbull, (1886), 141 Mass. 527, 6 N. E. 761

He may disaffirm such agreement before it has been performed and recover the unearned portion of the money paid in advance, although he will be liable for necesseries already furnished him. 

31 C. J. 1076

A contract even for necessaries supplied to an infant is not binding upon him and cannot be ratified by his express promise after coming of age to pay for them unless they were supplied to him at his request and on his credit.

EXAMPLE: P rendered necessary medical services to an infant who was living with and supported by his father to whom the son paid all his wages. The father, because of poverty, performed imperfectly his duty of supporting the son but did not refuse or neglect to support him to the best of his ability. 

The son did not request P to render the services but, after coming of age, promised P he would pay for them. There is no ratification and the son is not bound by his promise. 

Hoyt v Casey, (1874), 114 Mass. 397 

b) EXPRESS CONTRACTS; AMOUNT OF RECOVERY 

The authorities are in conflict on the question whether a minor is liable on his express contracts for necessaries and, if so, to what extent he is so

- liable. The general rule is that he is not bound to pay the amount expressly promised but the claim is to be settled just as it would have been had no specific sum been promised in payment. The law allows him to make contracts for necessaries as being essential to his own preservation but his obligation is quasi-contractual and he is liable only for the reasonable value of the necessaries furnished. 

The rule in Massachusetts and in some states is that an action will lie on an express contract of a minor for necesseries furnished as well as on an implied one where the contract is of such a nature as to leave the question of consideration open to inquiry. 

EXAMPLE: A minor may be sued on a promissory note and, if it be shown that it was given in whole or in part for necessaries, the plaintiff may recover on the note as much as the necessaries for which it was given were reasonably worth, and no more. 

Earle v Reed, (1845), 10 Met. 387 

Stone v Dennison, (1852), 13 Pick. 1 

See Askey v Williams, (1889), 74 Tex.294,11 S.W.1101,5 LRA 176

However, this conflict of authority is immaterial, since the practical result is the same. The amount of liability is always limited to the reasonable value of the necessaries furnished and not what the infant may have promised to pay. 

31 C. J. 1076 

Trainer v Trumbull, (1886), 141 Mass. 527, 6 N. E. 761 

(c) WHERE INFANT ALREADY SUPPLIED 

If an infant is already provided with necessaries, things furnished to an infant on his credit are not necessaries even though the person furnishing them did not know that the infant was already supplied. 

Davis v Caldwell, (1853), 12 Cush. 512 

Trainer v Trumbull, (1886), 141 Mass. 527, 6 N. E. 761 

(a) WHERE INFANT HAS PARENT OR GUARDIAN 

As a rule, a parent is liable for the support of his minor child and a guardian for the support of his ward. 

L. P. Hollander Co. v Porter, (1929),267 Mass.378,166 N.E.724

Thus an infant, residing with his parents who support him in accordance with his station in life, cannot bind himself or his estate for things which, under other circumstances, would be considered necessaries. 

Moskow v Marshall (1930), 271 Mass. 302, 171 N. E. 477 

Foster v Adcock, Tenn., 1930), 30 S.. (2nd) 239,70 A.L.R.569 

Hoyt v Casey, (1874), 114 Mass. 397 

Swift v Bennett, (1852), 10 Cush. 436 

Distinguish Cole v Wagner, (1929),197 NC 692,150 SE 339,71 ALR220

But the mere fact that an infant has a parent or guardian ? neither of whom does anything towards his care and support, does not prevent the infant from being bound to pay for that which

- was actually necessary when furnished. The law implies the promise to pay from the necessity of the situation. 

Trainer v Trumbull, (1886), 141 Mass. 527, 6 N. E. 761

When a child leaves his father's house voluntarily for the purpose of seeking his fortune in the world, or to avoid the discipline and restraint necessary for the due regulation of families, he carries with him no credit; and the parent is under no obligation to pay for his support. 

Angel v McLellan, (1819), 16 Mass. 288 

(3) WHAT ARE NECESSARIES (a) IN GENERAL 

Personal property which is a necessary has been defined by the Uniform Sales Act as "goods suitable to the condition in life of the infant or other person and to his actual requirements at the time of delivery 

G. L. Chapter 106, Section 4 

Necessaries comprise more than personal property, however, and include food, clothing, shelter, medical and other essential services, including a reasonable amount of education. 

The word "necessaries" is not used in its strictest sense nor limited to that which is required to sustain life. That which is proper and suitable to each particular individual, according to his circumstances and condition in life, are necessaries, if not supplied from some other scurce. From this view, it follows in most cases that the question whether the articles or services sued for were necessaries is a question of fact for the jury except in a very clear case where a judge would be warranted in directing a jury as matter of law that some articles, such as diamonds and race horses, cannot be necessaries. Such a question of fact depends on the circumstances of the particular case and the two principal circumstances are (1) whether the articles are suitable to the infant's estate and condition in life and (2) whether he is without other means of supply. 

Davis v Caldwell, (1853), 12 Cush. 512 

Pyne v Wood, (1888), 145 Mass. 558, 14 N. E. 775 

Cf. Tupper v Cadwell, (1847), 12 Met. 559

EXAMPLE: D, an infant an infant, lived in an almshouse, his father in a soldier's home, and his mother in a reformatory. legacy of $10,000 was left to D, payable upon the death of his father. During the father's lifetime, D was in a sickly and diseased condition when P took him out of the almshouse and cared for him at her home, relying upon payment when D received his legacy. Looking to the advantage of D's subsequent life, as well as his present welfare, his transfer from the almshouse to the care of P, a suitable person, provided for his comfort and education, is such support as falls within the class of necessaries. 

Trainer v Trumbull, (1886), 141 Mass. 527, 6 N. E. 761

Ryan v Smith, (1895), 165 Mass. 303, 43 N. E. 109

Merriam v Cunningham, (1853), 11 Cush. 40 

Mason v Wright, (1847), 13 Met. 306 

A few states are contra by statute. 

Wickham v Turley, (1911), 136 Ga. 594, 71 S.E.881,36 LRA (NS) 57 

(c) LIFE INSURANCE 

At common law, life insurance was not deemed to be a necessery. 

Simpson v Ins. Co.,(1903),184 Mass.348,68 N.E.673,63 L.R.A.741 

O'Rourke v Ins. Co., (1902), 23 R.1.457,50 Atl.834,57 L.R.A.496

This rule is modified by a Massachusetts statute which provides: "No party to any contract of life or endowment insurance made aiter March 50, 1917, upon the life of such party, for the benefit of the insured, or for the benefit of the wife, husband, children, father, mother, brother or sister of the insured shall, because of infancy, be entitled to avoid or repudiate the contract or to avoid or repudiate any discharge or receipt given by him for any benefit accrued or accruing, or for any money paid or payable under the contract; provided that such party had attained the age of fifteen when the contract was effected."

G. L. Chapter 175, Section 128 (a) IMPROVEMENT OF INFANT'S LANDS 

Ordinarily, an infant is not bound to pay on contracts made by him for repairs or improve. ments upon his real estate, such as upon his dwelling-house, even though the repairs were necessary to prevent immediate and serious injury to the house. The fact that he has real estate which may require supervision and may need repairs, furnishes the proper occasion for the appointment of a guardian through whose agency the repairs can be made and, as the law assumes, more judiciously made, than through the agency of the minor. 

Tupper v Cadwoll, (1847), 12 Met. 559 

Wallis v Bardwell, (1879), 126 Mass. 366 

Distinguish Ragan v Williams, 220 Ala.590,127 So.190,68 ALR 1182

So one who has paid off a mortgage on an infant's land cannot maintain an action against him for money had and received, or money lent, although the mortgage was paid off at the request of the guardian. 

Bicknell v Bicknell, (1875), 111 Mass. 265 

See Libby v Todd, (1906), 194 Mass. 507 

194 Mɛ ss. 507, 80 N. E. 584 

(e) THINGS ANSWERING BODILY NEEDS 

Articles furnished to an infant, in order to be necessaries, must concern his person and not his estate. 

Trainer v Trumbull, (1886), 141 Mass. 527, 6 N. E. 761 

Swift v Bennett, (1852), 1ó Cush. 436

(f) EDUCATION 

A good common school education, at least, is ranked as a necessary. 

Stone v Dennison (1832), 13 Pick. 1 

Kilgore v Rich, (1891), 83 Me.305,22 Atl.176,12 L.R.A.859

A college education is not ranked as a necessary as matter of law, although it may be so found in some cases as a matter of fact. 

Internat'l Co.vConnelly, 206 NY 188,99 NE 722,42 LRANS 1115 

(g) SERVICES OF ATTORNEY 

Ordinarily, the liability of an infant to pay a reasonable compensation for attorney's services is limited to services mereä in connection with cases involving the infant's personal relief, protection, or liberty, for example, in suits for indecent assault by or upon an infant, seduction, bastardy proceedings, and in defense of criminal actions brought against the infant. 

Sutton v Heinzle, (1911), 84 Kan. 750,115 Pas.560,34 LRANS 238 

Askey v Williams, (1889), 74 Tex.294, 11 S.W.1101,5 L.R.A.176 

Crafts v Carr, (1902), 24 R.I.397,53 Atl.275,60 Í.R.A.128 

Hoyt v Casey, (1874), 114 Mass. 397

In general, legal services rendered to an infant in regard to ordinary property rights are not recognized as necessaries. 

McIsaac v Adams, (1906), 190 Mass. 117, 76 N. E. 654 

Conant v Burnham, (1882), 133 Mass. 503

The reason is that such rights should be protected by a guardian and not left to the care of the infant. 

G. L. Chapter 201, Section 37 ) LOANS AND ADVANCES FOR NECESSARIES 

The general rule is that money is not a necessary though lent to an infant who afterwards purchases necessaries with it. Nevertheless, one who pays money at the infant's request to a third person for necessaries can recover it from the infant. The difference is between lending and paying. The reason for the distinction is that the law will not support a contract for lending money to a minor which is to depend for its validity upon a subsequent contingency. 

Swift v Bennett, (1852), 10 Cush. 436 

Kilgore v Rich, (1891), 83 Me. 305, 22 Atl.176, 12 L.R.A. 859 

See Norwood Bank v Allston, (1929),152 S.C.199,149 S.E.593 Thus, as between husband and wife, although a husband is liable for his wife's debts incurred for necessaries, he is not liable for money sorrowed by the wife to be used in paying for necessaries. 

Newell v Hadley, (1910), 206 Mss.335,92 NE 507,29 LRANS 908 

(4) INFANT'S CONTRACTS FOR SERVICES 

Ordinarily, a contract for the services of an infant is voidable by him, the law giving him the privi-

Vent v Osgood, (1837), 19 Pick. 572

Moses v Stevens, (1824), 2 Pick. 335 

Gaffney v Hayden, (1872), 110 Mass. 137 

Dube v Beaudry, (1890), 150 Mass. 448, 23 N. E. 222

Occasionally, such a contract has been held binding on the infant on the ground that it is clearly beneficial to him. 

Breed v Judd, (1854), 1 Gray 455

Page v Morse, (1880), 128 Mass. 99 

Welch v Welch, (1870), 103 Mass. 562 

See Moley v Brine, (1876), 120 Mass. 324 

5. TORTS CONNECTED WITH CONTRACTS 

a.

IN GENERAL 

The general rule is, of course, that infants are liable for their torts.

Walker v Davis, 

(1854), 1 Gray 506 

Sikes v Johnson, (1820), 16 Mass. 289

But the rule is not an unlimited one and is to be applied with due regard to the other equally well settled rule that with certain exceptions, they are not liable on their contracts; and the dominant consideration is not that of liability for their torts but of protection from their contracts. 

Slayton v Barry, (1900),175 Mess.515,56 N.E.574,49 L.R.A.560

The test of an action of tort against an infant is whether he can be held liable in tort without directly or indirectly enforcing his promise; in other words, whether a liability can be made out without taking notice of the contract. 

Rice v Boyer, (1886), 108 Ind. 472, 9 N. E. 420,424 

Lowery v Cate, (1901), 108 Tenn. 54, 64 S. W. 1068, 57 LRA 673 

EXAMPLE 1: If an infant hires a horse and injures it through driving immoderately, he has breached his contract of bailment that he would drive with reasonable care. He cannot be held liable in tort for the tort cannot be proved without proving the contract of bailment. The facts that constitute the breach of his promise cancel all the facts which constitute the alleged tort, leaving nothing over and above the breach of the contract upon which to found a tcrt action. 

Jennings v kundall, 3 T. R. 335 

Brunhoelzl v Brandes, (1917), 90 N. J. L. 31, 100 Atl. 163 

Homer v Thwing, (1826), 3 Pick. 492 

EXAMPLE 2: If an infant hires or borrows a horse and drives him beyond the place to which he had permission to go, the infant can be held liable in tort for conversion. 

Homer v Thwing, (1826), 3 Pick. 492 

EXAMPLE 3: P, an infant, bought a horse from D, another infant. P paid the price and D spent the money. P cannot recover the money from D in contract if D sets up his infancy; nor can he hold D liable in tort for conversion because, at most, D has been guilty of a oreach of an implied promise to

Drude v Curtis, (1903), 183 Mass.317,67 N.E.317,62 L.R.A.755 1.

FRAUD AND FALSE REPRESENTATIONS 

In general, infancy is not a defense to an action of tort founded on the infant's fraud or false representations, not connected with his contracts. 

Walker v Davis, (1854), 1 Gray 506 

EXAMPLE: D, an infant, who bought goods from P on credit, availed himself of his infancy to avoid payment but kept possession of the goods. This is fraud for which P can maintain replevin or conversion. 

Badger v Phinney, (1819), 15 Mass. 359

It is a general rule of law that, where the substantial ground of action against an infant is in contract, a party cannot, by declaring in tort, make the infant liable when he would not have been in an action of contract. Any different rule would take away the shield which the law throws around the infant for his protection. 

Caswell v Parker, (1901), 96 Me. 39, 51 Atl. 238 

McCall v Parker, (1847), 13 Met. 372 

O'Leary v Brooks, (1898), 7 N. D. 554, 75 N.W.919,41 LRA 677 

EXAMPLE: An infant cannot be held liable in tort for misrepresentation in connection with a breach of warranty in the sale of a horse. 

Collins v Gifford, (1911), 203 N.Y.465,96 N.E.721,38 LRANS 202

If the tort is subsequent to the contract, and not a mere breach of it, but a distinct, wilful and positive wrong of itself, then, although it may be connected with a contract, the infant is liable. 

Fitts v Hall, 9 N. H. 441, 445 C.

MISREPRESENTATION OF AGE 

Massachusetts holds that an infant's false representation as to his age, inducing a contract, is directly connected with the contract so that he cannot be held liable in tort for deceit or conversion. 

Slayton v Berry, (1900), 175 Mass.513,56 N.E.274,49 L.R.A.560 

Knudson v Sales Co., (1918), 230 Mass. 54, 119 N. E. 359 

Drude v Curtis, (1903),183 Mass.317,67 N.2.517,62 L.R.A.755 

Brooks v Sawyer, (1906), 191 Mass. 151, 76 N. E. 953 

White v New Bedford Co., (1900), 178 Mass. 20, 59 N. E. 642

Merriam v Cunningham, (1853), 11 Cush. 40 

Baker v Stone, (1884), 136 Mass. 405 

Distinguish Baager v Phinney (1819), 15 Mass. 359

Walker v Davis, (1854), 1 Gray 506 

Homer v Thwing, (1826), 3 Pick. 492

Some other states do not regard the misrepresentation as to age as directly connected with the contract and accordingly hold the infant liable in tort. 

Wisconsin Co.vGoodnough, (Wis.,1930) 228 N.W.484,67 A.L.R.1259

La Rosa v Nichols, (1918),92 N.J.L.375,105 Atl.201,6 A.L.R.412

REPRESENTATION BY GUARDIAN OR NEXT FRIEND 

It is undoubtedly true that, in civil actions brought on behalf of infants, in theory of law the next friend is to be appointed by the court but, under our practice, no actual appointment by the court is necessary and the prochein ami, as well as the guardian, is to be admitted without any other appointment than recital in the pleadings; and the person who assumes to act as next friend in instituting the proceedings is not ousted from his position by a challenge of his authority, but only by removal by the court and, until such removal, his authority is in force. The settlement of an action brought on behalf of an infant cannot be made by his next friend unless approved by the court. 

Butler v Winchester Home, (1914), 216 Mass. 567, 104 N. E. 451 

Se Welch v Fox, (1910), 205 Mess. 113, 91 N. E. 145 B.

INSANE PERSONS 1. IN GENERAL 

The supposed maxim of the common-law that no person of full age would be allowed to stultify himself by pleading insanity and thus avoid his contracts is of doubtful origin and authority; 

Mitchell v Kingman, (1827), 5 Pick. 431 and the modern doctrine is that, in general, the contracts and deeds of an insane person are voidable by him but not by the other party. 

Carrier v Sears, (1862), 4 Allen 536 

Peaslee v Robbins (1841), 3 Met. 164 

Newton v Feeley, (1880), 130 Mass. 12 

Hix v Whittemore, (1842), 4 Met. 445 

Leggate v Clark, (1873), 111 Mass. 308 

Riley v Carter, (1893), 76 Md.581,25 Atl.667,19 L.R.4.489 

French v Theriault, (1900),107 Wis.627,83 N.W.927,51 LRA 910

The defense of insanity must be pleaded or shown in evidence under the general issue in order to avoid an executory contract, otherwise it is held to be waived. But this doctrine cannot apply to executed deeds for in such cases there may be no opportunity to plead the matter which may avoid the deed. 

Worcester v Eaton, (1816), 13 Mass. 371 

The words "insane person" and "lunatic", as used in the

Massachusetts statutes, include every, idiot, non compos, lunatic, and insane and distracted person.

G. L. Chapter 4, Section 7, Clause 15

Insanity is of various degrees and no degree of physical or mental imbecility which leaves the party legal competency to act is, of itself, sufficient to avoid a contract made by him.

Kendall v May, (1865), 10 Allen 59 

Farnam v Brooks, (1830), 9 Pick. 212, 220 

Meigs v Dexter, (1898), 172 Mass. 217, 52 N. E. 75

Insanity is a question of fact to be determined by the court upon all the evidence before it. 

Cogan v Cogan, (1909), 202 Mass. 58, 88 N. E. 662

The burden of proving it is on the party who alleges it. 

Howe v Howe, (1868), 99 Mass. 88 

See Hodgdon v Cummings, (1890),151 Mass.293,23 N.E. 836

Sanity, when once shown to exist, like any other fact of a continuing nature, is presumed to continue. 

32 C. J. 756

The same is true of insanity unless it was accidental or temporary in its nature. 

Hix v Whittemore, (1842), 4 Met. 445 EXAMPLE: The appointment by the court under the statute of a guardian ad litem for a respondent to a libel for annulment of a marriage on the ground of insanity is prima facie evidence of the respondent's insanity in any subsequent stage of the cause. 

Little v Little, (1859), 13 Gray 264 

2. NATURE AND EXTENT OF INCAPACITY TO CONTRACT 

The insanity that affects the validity of a contract must be of the time at which the transaction occurs, regardless of previous or subsequent insanity. 

32 C. J. 728

The test in cases of the execution of an instrument is whether the person executing it has sufficient mental capacity to be capable of transacting the business. If he could not understand the nature and quality of the transaction or grasp its significance, then it was not the act of a person of sound mind.

Inability to realize the true purport of the matter in hand is equivalent to mental incapacity. must be pretty for

EXAMPLE: If a person had sufficient capacity to understand that he was signing a mortgage and note, and did so understand, but was not of sufficient capacity to realize the effect it might have on his estate and to judge of the wisdom or of the transaction, and did not understand it, the transaction is idable. 

Sutcliffe v Heatley, (1918), 230 Mass. 231, 122 N. E. 317

An insane person is liable for his quasi contracts, for example, for necessaries supplied to himself or family. 

Hallett v Oakes, (1848), 1 Cush. 296 

Reando v Mosplay, (1886), 90 Mo. 251, 2 S. W. 405

EXAMPLE: A husband is liable for necessaries furnished to his wife although he is, at the time of such supply, a person non compos and is residing separately from her, in an almshouse 

Shaw v Thompson, (1854), 16 Pick. 198

But he is not liable for necessaries supplied to himself, not on his own credit but on the credit of another. 

Mass. Hospital v Fairbanks, (1880), 129 Mass. 78, 132 Mass. 414 A judgment against one who was incompetent at the time of its rendition and was not represented by a guardian or committee is not absolutely void but is, at most, merely voidable. 

Farmers Bank v Duke, (1924), 187 NC 385, 122 SE 1, 34 ALR 215 

3 EFFECT OF GUARDIANSHIP 

If a person is under guardianship for insanity, his contracts and deeds, made while under guardianship, are absolutely void and cannot be ratified after the guardianship is removed. 

Leonard v Leonard, (1833), 14 Pick. 280 

Wait v Maxwell, (1827), 5 Pick. 217 

See Warfield v Fisk, (1884), 136 Mass. 219

If food, clothing, and other articles suitable for the condition of the ward were furnished by the guardian or were supplied from some other source, the ward is not responsible for articles furnished him and, in such circumstances, the supplies are not necessaries. The burden is on the plaintiff to show that the defendant's needs were not sufficiently supplied by the guardian. 

Belluci v Foss, (1923), 244 Mass. 401, 138 N. E. 551 4. 

AVOIDANCE AND RATIFICATION &. IN GENERAL 

The contracts and deeds of an insane person, made while he was not under guardianship for insanity, may be ratified or disaffirmed by him after he becomes sane or may be disaffirmed before he becomes sane. The other party is also entitled to rely on such contracts and deeds if he can show a ratification or adoption of them by the incompetent after he was restored to sound mind. 

Sutcliffe v Heatley, (1919), 232 Mass. 231, 122 N.E. 317

Thus the deed of an insane person is ineffectual to convey a title to land which will be good against the grantor himself or against his heirs or devisees unless it is confirmed by the grantor when of sound mind, or by his guardian, or after his death by his heirs or devisees. 

Brown v Brown, (1911), 209 Mass. 388, 95 N. E. 796 

Brewster v Weston, (1920), 235 Mass. 14, 126 N. E. 271 

Gibson v Soper, (1856), 6 Gray 279 

Allis v Billings, (1843), 6 Met. 415 

Valpey v Rea, (1881), 130 Mass. 384.

EXAMPLE 1: An insane person executed and delivered a deed. After becoming sane, he demanded and received payment of the note for the purchase price. This is a ratification.

Arnold v Richmond Iron Works, (1854), 1 Gray 434 

EXAMPLE 2: A, while sane, executed and delivered a valid deed to B. Later, while insane, he delivered a voidable deed

- of the same property to C. Subsequently, B placed his deed on record. Thereafter, A became sane and ratified the deed to C. This is not a valid ratifiction. A is estopped to deny the validity of his previous deed given when he was in his right mind. 

Bond v Bond, (1863), 7 Allen 1

In Massachusetts, it is not necessary that an insane person, any more than an infant, should be obliged to put the other party in statu quo by the return of the consideration before he can avoid his contract. He must restore all of the consideration which he may still have remaining in his possession but, if he has none of it, he may still avoid the contract. To hold otherwise would be to say in effect, in a large majority of cases, that his contract cannot be avoided at all. The more insane he is, the less likely will he be to retain the fruits of his bargain so as to be able to make restitution. The same incap. city which made the contract voidable may have wasted the consideration received for it. 

Gibson v Soper, (1856), 6 Gray 279 

Allis v Billings, (1843), ô Met. 415

IGNORANCE AND GOOD FAITH OF OTHER PARTY 

In most states, a lunatic is bound by a contract wholly or partially executed and fair and reasonable in its terms, if made by him before judicial ascertainment of his lunacy with one who neither knows nor has reason to know that the lunacy exists, provided the parties cannot be restored to their original positions. 

Flach v Gottschalk, (1898),88 Ma.368,41 Atl.908,42 L.R.A.745

But, in Massachusetts, it is no defense to the sane party to a contract, either executory or executed, that he did not know of the insanity of the other party or of circumstances which ought to have put him on inquiry because one who deals with an insane person, like one who deals with an infant, does so at his peril and the fairness of conduct of the one cannot supply the want of capacity of the other. The law binds the strong and protects the weak. 

Seaver v Phelps, (1831) 11 Pick. 304 

Allen v Berryhill, (1869),27 Iowa 534, Costigan 473 

Reed v Mattapan Trust Co.,(1908),198 Mass.306,314,84 N.E.469 

Atwell v Jenkins, (1895),163 Mass.362,40 N.E.178,28 L.R.A. 694

Thus, in Massachusetts, a deed of real property by an insane person can be avoided by him even against a bona fide purchaser from his rantee who acquired title in good faith without knowledge of the insanity, before the grantee's title was avoided. 

Brewster v Weston, (1920), 235 Mass. 14, 126 N.E. 271

This rule has been modified as to sales of rersonal property by the Uniform Sales Act which provides that "if the seller of goods has a voidable title there to but his title has not been voided at the time of the sale, the buyer acquires a good title to the gccas; provided that he buys them in good faith, for value, and without notice of the seller's defect in title."

G. L. Chapter 106, Section 26

MARRIED WOMEN 

1. 

WIFE'S LEGAL NAME 

She

A wife's legal name is that of her husband. takes her husband's surname. Thus if Ethel M. Williams is the vife of John P. "illiams illiams, her legal name upon her marriage is Ethel M. Williams. But it does not follow 

But it does not follow, under a statute requiring automobiles to be registered in the names of their owners, that a motor car registered in the name of Mrs. John P. 

Williams was illegally registered. 

Koley v Williams, (1929), 265 Mass. 601, 164 N. E. 444

But, under this statute, if an automobile is registered by a married woman under her maiden name, it is illegally registered, is a nuisance upon the highway, and will preclude recovery by the owner for injury resulting from a collision. 

Bacon v Boston E.Ry., (1926), 256 Mass.30,152 N.E.35,47 ALR 1100

POWER TO MAKE CONTRACTS 

2. 

a IN GENERAL 

At common law, husband and wife are in legal contemplation but one person and the husband is that person. Upon this principle depends almost all legal rights, duties and disabilities that either of them acquires by the marriage. 

30 C. J. 507

At common law, a married woman was generally incapable of making a valid contract and her contracts were absolutely void and not merely voidable, even for necessaries. 

Tracy v Keith, (1865), 11 Allen 214 

Dean v Richmond, (1827), 5 Pick. 461 

Stevens v Beals, (1852), 10 Cush. 291 

Shaw v. Thompson, (1834), 15 Pick. 198

Hence, if she made a contract for necessaries in her own name during coverture, her promise to pay for them, made after the disability was removed, would be without consideration and void. 

Lyell v Walbach, (1910),113 Md.574,77 Atl.1111,33 LRA (NS) 741 

Gilbert v Brown, (1906),29 Ky.L.1248,97 S.W.40,7 LRA (NS) 1053 

But, by statutes, known as "Married Woman's Acts", her common law disabilities have been almost entirely removed. 

Thus, in Massachusetts "A married woman may make contracts, oral and written, sealed and unsealed, in the same manner as if she were sole, except that she shall not be authorized hereby to make contracts with her husband."

G. L. Chapter 209, Section 2 

Gahm v Gahm, (1922), 243 Mass. 374, 137 N. E. 876 

Kenyon v Vogel, (1924), 250 Mass. 341, 145 N. E. 462 

Hunt v Rhodes Bros., (1910), 207 Mass. 30, 92 N. E. 1001 

Reid v Miller, (1910), 205 Mass. 80, 91 N. 3. 223 

Patterson v Patterson, (1908), 197 Mass. 112, 83 N. E. 364

But nothing in this statute will invalidate a marriage settlement or contract.

G. L. Chapter 209, Section 13

busdzen ai ensi udo & 16 mer od Jeinur

Even under the statute, a husband cannot contract directly with his wife, in law or equity. 

Young v Young; (1925), 251 Mass. 218, 146 N. E. 574

Thus, if a wife makes loans to a partnership in which her husband is a partner, she cannot maintain proceedings either in law or equity against the husband, or his estate after his death, or the partnership, or surviving partner to compel repayment. 

Barbour v Sampson, (1929), 266 Mass. 180, 165 N. D. 14

Likewise, a business partnership between husband and wife is void. 

Voss v Sylvester, (1909), 203 Mass. 233, 89 N. E. 241

But a note given by a man for money lent him by a woman prior to their marriage is not extinguished or made vcid by their subsequent marriage; 

MacKeown v Lacey, (1909) 200 Mass. 437,86 N.E.799,21 LRANS 68 

See Stevens v Beals, (1852), 10 Cush. 291 and a contract between a supposed husband and wife, valid on account of the nullity of the marriage ceremony, is not invalidated by the subsequent valid marriage of the parties. 

Witherington v Elüredge, (1928), 264 Mass. 166, 162 N.E. 300

Statutes which confer upon arried women the power to contract generally, or even to convey their separate real estate, with or without the consent of their husbands, do not remove the disability of infancy. Such statutes merely remove the disability of coverture and the disability of infant married women is the same as if such statutes were not in force 

Shipley v Smith, (1904), 162 Ind. 520, 70 N. E. 803

But, in Massachusetts, a statute expressly provides that "the signature of a married woman under twenty-one affixed by her to any instrument relating to the conveyance of land of her husband shall have the same effect as if she were over that age.

G. L. Chapter 189, Section 6 

b.

SERVICES AND EARNINGS OF WIFE 

Unless permitted by positive legislative enactment, a married woman cannot contract with her husband with respect to domestic services which are imposed by her marital status since such contracts are not only without consideration but also are against public policy. 

Frame v Frame, (Texas, 1931), 36 .7.(2nd) 152, 73 A.L.R. 151

Under the Workmen's Compensation Act, as at common law, a wife cannot be the employee of her husband. 

Humphrey's Case, (1917),227 Mass.166,116 N.E.413, LRA 1918F 19

At common law, all a wife's earnings vest absolutely in her husband unless he relinquishes his rights in her favor or agrees that she may receive her earnings as her own. 

McKavlin v Bresslin, (1857), 8 Gray 177 

Russell v Brooks, (1828), 7 Pick. 65 

Hinckley v Phelps, (1861), 2 Allen 77

But, by statute, "work and labor performed by a married woman for a person other than her husband and children shall,

G. L. Chapter 209, Section 4 

Dorsett v Dorsett, (1922), 183 N.C. 354, 111 S.E.541,23 ALR 15 

Bohanan v Maxwell, (Iowa, 1921), 181 N.W. 683, 14.A.L.R. 1004 • 0 

CONTRACTS FOR NECESSARIES 

It is the legal duty of the husband to support the wife and family even though the wife has means or property of her own and he is at the time without means. 

Cartwright v Bate, (1861), 1 Allen 514 

Newman's Case, (1916), 222 Mass. 563,111 N.E.359, LRA 191601145

In an action against the husband to recover for goods purchased by the wife, the burden is on the plaintiff to prove an agency in fact or one implied from the inadequacy of the husband's provision. 

Groce v First Nat'l Stores, (1929), 268 Mass.210,167 N.E. 308 If the presumption that a father does not intend to charge his adult daughter for board conflicts with the presumed obligation of the husband to support his wife who was that daughter, there is strong ground for holding as an inference of fact that the father may hold the husband liable for board and laundry furnished by the father to the daughter where the father vas ignorant of the marriage at the time the services were rendersi. 

Fisher v Drew, (1924), 247 Mass.178,141 N.E.875,30 A.L.R.1100

The meaning of the term "necessaries" has been discussed under the topic of "Infants" There is no rigid test. 

Willey v Beach, (1874), 115 Mass. 559 

Eames v Sweetser, (1869), 101 Mass. 78 

Conant v Burnham, (1882), 133 Mass. 503 

Raynes v Bennett, (1874) 114 Mass. 424, 429 

Berkwitz v Dunham, (1929), 269 Mass. 65, 168 N. E. 183 

Vaughan v Mansfield, (1918), 229 Mass.352,118 N.E.652 

Auringer v Cochrane, (1916), 225 Mass. 273,114 N.E.355 

Carr v Anderson, (Minn.,1923),191 N.W.407, 26 A.L.R.557 

State v Moran, (1923),99 Conn.115,121 Atl.277,36 A.L.R.862

Labadie v Henry, (1926),132 Okla. 252,270 Pac.57,60 A.L.R.1183 

In Massachusetts, the funeral expenses of a married woman. are payable out of her separate estate, if any. 

Morrissey v Mulhern, (1897), 168 Mass. 412, 47 N. E. 407 

Constantinides v Walsh, (1888), 146 Mass. 281, 15 N. E. 631

This rule is contra in most states. 

Hall v Stewart, (1925),135 Va.384,116 S.E.469,31 A.L.R.1489

The turning over of money by the husband to the wife, to be used so far as necessary for household expenses and the surplus to be saved for the joint benefit and enjoyment of both, does not constitute a gift but joint possession or atrust for their joint benefit. 

Cram v Cram, (1928), 262 Mass. 509, 160 N. E. 337

The liability of a married woman for necessaries depends entirely upon statutes since she was not liable at common law. 

In some states, the Married Woman's Acts have no effect on the husband's common-law duty to furnish the household necessities.

Schultz v Pomplon, (1924), 227 Mich.304,198 N.W.916,36 ALR 387 

But, in Massachusetts, under statutes reserving to married women their separate estate and permitting them to contract with respect thereto, a wife may bind her separate estate even for necessaries by contracting therefor on her own credit and not on the credit of her husband. 

Labaree v Colby, (1868), 99 Mass. 559

Such a contract may be created either by express language or by conduct and words from which an intentional obligation to that effect may be inferred, and it may include the hiring of a household servant. 

Charron v Day, (1917), 228 Mass. 305, 117 N. E. 347 

Sherry v Littlefield, (1919), 232 Mass. 220, 122 N. E. 300 

Caldwell v Blanchard, (1906), 191 Mass. 489, 77 N. E. 1036

A Massachusetts statute also provides "A married woman shall not be liable for her husband's debts, nor shall her property be liable to be taken on execution against him excep as provided in section ten (relating to wife's separate business certificate). But a married woman shall be liable jointly with her husband for debts due, to the amount of $100 in each case, for necessaries furnished with her knowledge or consent to herself or her family, if she has property to the amount of $2000 or more." 

Cart 236 Moss. 353

G. L. Chapter 209, Section 7 

العرب سلیم

If a husband and wife have separated without a court decree for separate maintenance but under an agreement that she will accept a certain periodical allowance for her support and will not pledge his credit, the agreement is void. 

Shaw v Thompson, (1834), 16 Pick. 198

But if the Probate Court has decreed separate maintenand the husband has made the required periodical payments to her, she cannot pledge his credit. 

McIlroy v McIlroy, (1911), 208 Mass. 458, 94 N. E. 696 

Jordan Marsh v Cohen, (1922); 242 Mass.245,136 NE 350,24 ALR 148

The general rule is that when the wife lives apart from her husband without any fault on her part, the husband is liable for her adequate maintenance in her station in life, and in case he fails in that duty, he is liable to any third person for necessaries furnished to the wife. 

Hall v Weir, (1861), 1 Allen 261 

Mayhew v Thayer, (1857), 8 Gray 172 

Dolan v Brooks, (1897), 169 Mass. 350, 47 N. E. 408 

Caldwell v Blanchard, (1906), 191 Mass. 489, 77 N. E. 1036 

Jordan Marsh v Hedtler, (1921), 238 Mass. 43, 130 N. E. 78 

Malden Hospital v Murdock, (1914), 218 Mass.73,105 N.E.457 

Brown v Durepo, (1922),121 Me.226,116 Atl.451, 27 A.L.R.551

Newman's Case, (1916), 222 Mass.563,111 N.E.359, LRA 1916C 1145 

Skinner v Tirrell, (1893),159 Mass.474,54 N.E.692,21 LRA 673

In Massachusetts, however, the husband is not so liable if the wife has property adequate to her support. He is liable only upon ground of reasonable necessity.

3. CAPACITY TO SUE AND BE SUED 

At common law, except where the husband was never within the state or has gone beyond its jurisdiction, a married woman was unable to sue or be sued as a feme sole and this disability continues unless a statute, by express language or by construction, removes it. The husband usually must join in her suit. 

Burns v Lynde, (1863), 6 Allen 305 

Gregory v Pierce, (1842) (1842), 4 Met. 478 

Peterson v Gunnarson, (Neb., 1921), 182 N.W.505,15 A.L.R.830

By statute, "a married woman may sue and be sued in the manner as if she were sole" except that the statute does not authorize suits between husband and wife. same

G. L. Chapter 209, Section 6 

Feneff v Ry., (1909), 203 Mass.278,89 N.E.436,24 LRA (NS) 1024 

Duffee v Boston El. Ry., (1906), 191 Mass. 563, 77 N. E.1035

This statute does not prevent money lent by a woman, as administratrix of an estate, to to a man whom she afterwards marries, from being recovered from her husband by one to whom she assigned the claim after her marriage; 

Delval v Gagnon, (1912), 213 Mass. 203, 99 N. E. 1095 and, in some states, by statute, suits between husband and wife are expressly authorized. 

Notes v Snyder, (1925), 4 F. (2nd) 426, 41 A. L. R. 1052

TRANSFERS BETWEEN HUSBAND AND WIFE 

Under the common law, sales and transfers of personal property between husband and wife are void but they may be upheld in equity as vesting an equitable title. 

Holmes v Winchester, (1882), 133 Mass. 140

At common law, a married woman was incapable of conveying her lands by deed and the conveyance was absolutely void; 

Wing v Deans, (1913), 214 Mass. 546, 102 N. E. 313 and the husband could not, by deed, convey the legal title to lands directly to his wife, because of the legal fiction of unity. 

Ames v Chandler, (1829), 265 Mass. 428, 164 N. E. 616

The husband, however, could make a conveyance of land indirectly to the wife by making an absolute conveyance to a third person who in turn conveyed it to the wife. 

Crosby v Clem, (1911), 209 Mass. 193, 95 N. E. 297 

Donahue v Hubbard, (1891), 154 Mass. 537, 28 NE 909,14 LRA 123

By statute, "gifts of personal property and conveyances of real estate other than mortgages, between husband and wife, shall be valid to the same extent as if they were sole, except that no such conveyance of real estate shall have any effect" until recorded.

G. L. Chapter 209, Section 3 See G. L. Chapter 183, Section 32
