WIFE'S CONTROL OF HER SEPARATE PROPERTY 

At common law, the husband had full title to the rents and profits of the wife's real estate during coverture He had a freehold estate which he might convey. 

Nickerson v Nickerson, (1920), 235 Mass. 348, 126 N. E. 834

By statute, "the real and personal property of a woman shall, upon her marriage, remain her separate property and a married woman may receive, receipt for, hold, manage, and dispose of property, real and personal, in the same manner as if she were sole. But no conveyance by a married woman of real estate shall, except as otherwise provided in this chapter extinguish or impair her husband's tenancy by the curtesy by statute or his right to curtesy on December 31, 1901, in such property unless he joins in the conveyance or otherwise releases the same.

G. L. Chapter 209, Section 1 

Willard v Eastham, (1860), 15 Gray 328, 334 

Hey v Prime, (1908),197 M ss.474,84 N.E.141,17 L.R.A. (N.S.) 570

LIABILITY OF ONE SPOUSE FOR OTHER'S DEBTS

dabts of her husband.

In general, a wifc is not liable for the 

Barker v Thayer, (1914), 217 Mass. 13, 104 N. E. 572

It is a common case for a wife to join in a mortgage of her property for a debt of her husband. After the husband's death, she is entitled to have her real estate exonerated out of the personal and real assets of the husband, the court considering her estate only as surety for his debt. 

Browne v Bixby, (1906), 190 Mass. 69, 76 N. E. 454

At common law, a husband was liable for the debts of his wife contracted before marriage. 

Butler v Breck, (1842), 7 Met. 164

But, by statute, "a husband shall not be liable upon a cause of action which originated against his wife prior to their marriage, or to pay a judgment recovered against her, except under section ten." (relating to separate business certificate).

G. L. Chapter 209, Section 8 

A statute further provides that "contracts made by a married woman, relative to her separate property, trade, business, labor or services shall not, except as provided in the following section (section ten) bind her husband or render him or his property liable therefor; but she and her separate property shall be liable on such contract in the same manner as if she were sole.

G. L. Chapter 209, Section 9

SEPARATE BUSINESS CERTIFICATE 

By statute "if a married woman does or proposes to do business on her separate account, she shall cause to be recorded in the clerk's office of the town where she does or proposes to do such business a certificate stating her name and that of her husband, the nature of the business and the place where it is or is proposed to be carried on, giving, if practicable, the strect and number, and the name which shall not be her husband's, under which' she proposes to carry on business. If the naturc of the business or the place where or the name under which it is carried on is changed, a new certificate shall be recorded accordingly. If she fails to cause such certificates to be recorded, her husband may do so. If such certificates are not so recorded by either husband or wife, the personal property employed in such businuss shall be liable to be attached as the property of the husband and to be taken on exccution against him, and the husband shall be liable on all contracts lawfully made in the prosecution of such business in the same manner and to the same extent as if such contracts had been made by him. This section shall not, however, affect the rights of any person under any certificate filed or recorded prior to August 1, 1898.' 11 

G. L. Chapter 209, Suction 10 

Lowell Trust Co. v Wolff, (1916), 223 Mass. 168, 111 N. E. 798 

Parsons v Honry, (1908), 197 Mass. 504, 83 N. E. 1110

As to what constitutes the carrying on of a separate business by a married woman so as to require the filing of the statutory certificate in order to protect her property from attachment for her husband's acots, Proper v Cobb, (1870), 104 Mess. 589 

Dawes v Rodier, (1878), 125 Mass. 421 

Snow v Sheldon, (1879), 126 Mass. 332 

Harndon v Gould, (1879), 126 Mass. 411 

Chapman v Briggs, (1866), 11 Allen 546 

Theclcr v Raymond, (1881), 130 Mass. 247 

Lockwood v Coroy, (1889), 150 Mass. 82, 22 N. D. 440

Upon an analysis of the statute, these things appear: 

1. If the wife files o certificate, her property is not liable for the husband's dcots. 

2. If she docs not filc a certificate, the property used in the business is liable for his debts and that is so whether or not the debt has any relation to the business. 

3. In the absence of a certificate by the wife, the husband may file a certificate in which case, although the property of the wife used in the business is still liable for his debts, he is not liable for the debts of the business. 

4. If neither files a certificate, then he is liable.

It thus appears that the lisbility on the part of the vife is not personal while the t of the husband is; that the liability of the wife's property is for any acbt of the husband whether or not contracted in carrying on the business, while the liability of the husband is only for the business debts;

that the liability on the part of the wife's property ceases when the title passes from her, while the liability of the husband continues during his life and remains against his estate in the hands of his personal representatives as his own debt; and, lastly, the liability of the wife may exist although the husband, by filing a certificato, may escape liability at least for debts contracted in the business. 

Neither in nature or duration are these liabilitics identical or even similar. 

EXAMPLE: Whore a married woman, in her lifetime, without any such certificate being filed, conducted a business in her own name, but her administrator took no steps to continue the business for the benefit of her estate, her property was not liable to attachment in an action brought after her death against her husband as property subject to his debts.

INTOXICATED PERSONS 

At cerly common law, a contract of an intoxicated person could not be repudiated by him. By the modern rule, a completely intoxicated person is generally placed on the same footing with persons of unsound mind. In the absence of fraud on the part of the other contracting party, a person will not be relieved from a contract, otherwise, valid, on the ground of intoxication alone, unless it is shown that the drunkenness was so excessive that he was utterly deprived of his ruason and unücrstanding and was altogether incapable of knowing the effect of what he was doing. 

Hauge v Bye, (N.D.,1924), 201 N.. 159, 36 A.L.R. 613 

Wright v Waller, (1900), 127 Ala.557,29 So.57, 54 L.R.A.440 

Case Machine Co.v Meyers, (Neb.,1907),111 N..602,9 LRA (NS) 970

Foss v Hilaroth, (1865), 10 Alien 76 

In the latter case, the contracts of an intoxicated person are, in general, vcid bie by him or by his legal representatives whether the intoxication was voluntary cr involuntary. They are not voidable by the other party. 

Gibson v Soper, (1856), 6 Gray 279 

Harlow v Kingston, (Wis., 1919), 173 N.W. 508, 6 A.L.R. 327 

Philadelphia v Allison, (1911),108 Mc.326,80 Atl.833,39 LRANS39

Under the Uniform Salus hot, there is no right to avoid a voidable title to personal property after the property has been acquired by one who buys in good faith for value and without notice of the voidable title.

G. L. Chapter 106, Scetion 26 

If a person who enters into a voidable eontract while he is intoxicated fails to disaffirm the agreement within a reasonable time after he becomes sober and has full knowledge of what he has done, he thereby ratifics the contract and is bound thereby. 

Matz v Martinson, (1914),127 Minn. 262,149 NW 370, LRA 1915B 1121

If the contract sought to be repudiated is merely the formal execution of a prior agreement, made when the party was

さん ​t tofscotub birce besecu 540 OT ;)

not completely intoxicated, the alleged intoxication is no defense. 

EXAMPLE: Checks issued in fulfillment of a prior valid agreement will not be held invalid even though the maker was intoxicated at the time the checks were signed. 

Drefahl v Security Bank, (1906), 132 Iowa 563, 107 N.W.179 

See Reed v Mattapan Trust Co. (1907),198 Mass.306,84 NE469 !

Intoxication is not a defense when the action is not upon the express contract or promise but is on an implied contract for the value of the consideration received by the Crunkard. 

19 C. J. 813

Although in law the contracts of an intoxicated person are avoided on the ground.of incompetency, in equity they are avoided on the ground of fraud.

VII. 

19 C. J. 816

STATUTE OF FRAUDS A. IN GENERAL 

1. 

ORIGIN 

ло Has with the form Ged All contracts may be oral unless required by some statute to be evidenced by a writing. These statutes are known as "statutes of frauds". Each state has its own statute of frauds, differing in some degree from those of the other states, but all are based upon the English statute of frauds (Stat. Car. II, c. 3), enacted in 1676..

The Massachusetts statute is, in terms, so like the English statute that the English authorities are, on the question of construction, entitled to the same consideration as upon questions of common law. 2. 

OBJECT, EFFECT AND PLEADING OF STATUTE 

The object of the statute is to prevent fraud and perjuries. The effect of the statute upon contracts which come within it is not to make them void but voidable in the sense that they cannot be enforced against the will of a 3fendant who abandons or repudiates them.

A defendant who would take advantage of the statute must plead it in his answer. If he does not plead it, it is thereby waived and is no defense to him. 

Webster v Condon, (1924), 248 Mass. 268, 142 N. E. 777 

Aldrich v Carpenter, (1893), 160 Mass. 166, 35 N. E. 456

In a suit at law upon a contract within the statute, the declaration upon it need not allege that it is in writing. 

Bank v Schooner, (1928), 263 Mass. 119, 160 N. E. 790

In a bill in equity upon a contract within the statute, demurrer does not lie for mere omission to allege that the agreement was evidenced by a writing.

But if it appears on the face of the bill that an agreement is oral when, by the statute of frauds, it must be evidenced in writing, advantage may be taken of this by demurrer. 

Quinn v Quinn, (1927), 260 Mass. 494, 497, 157 N. E. 641 

3. WHERE PART OF CONTRACT IS WITHIN STATUTE 

Where part of a parol contract is within the statute, and part is not, the whole contract is unenforceable if the contract is entire and indivisible; but if the contract is divisible, the part which is not within the statute is enforceable though oral. 

Traiman v Rappaport, (1930),41 F (2nd) 336, 71 A.L.R.475 

Hurley v Donovan, (1902), 182 Mass. 64, 64 N. E. 685

Eastern R. R. v Benedict, (1860), 15 Gray 280 

McMullen v Riley, (1856), 6 Gray 500 

Haynes v Nice, (1863), 100 Mass 327 

Rand v Mather, (1853), 11 Cush. 1

The question whether a contract is divisible or indivisible is controlled by the intention of the parties and, where that intention is not clearly expressed, two principal questions have influenced the decisions of the courts: 

1. Whether the consideration for the two or more promises was entire, or divisible so as to be capable of apportionment without doing violence to the terms of the contract or without making a new contract for the parties. 

2. Whether the two or more promises were so interdependent that the parties would not have entered into the one without the other.

Page v Monks, (1885), 5 Gray 492 

4.

AGREEMENTS TO WHICH STATUTE DOES NOT APPLY 

a.

EXECUTED CONTRACTS 

The statute of frauds, by its express. terms, relates only to the enforceability of executory agreements. It does not render a contract void. It is valid for all purposes except that of suit. The parties may, if they desire, perform the contract and, when fully performed on both sides, the statute has no application to it. 

Blackwell v Blackwell, (1907), 196 Mass. 186, 81 N. E. 910 

King v Welcome, (1855), 5 Gray 41 

Stone v Dennison, (1832), 13 Fick. 1 

Brown v Bellows, (1826), 4 Pick. 179 

Pomeroy v Winship, (1815), 12 Mass. 514 

Aiken v Nogle, (1891), 47 Kan. 96, 27 Pac. 825 

Starratt v Mullen, (1889), 148 Mass.570,20 N.E.178,2 LRA 697 

b. QUASI CONTRACTS 

The statute, by its express terms, relates only to a "special promise". This means an express promise, a true contract, and not a quasi contract or contract implied by law. 

Goodwin v Gilbert, (1813), 9 Mass. 510 

Furbish v Goodnow, (1867), 98 Mass. 296

Thus if the consideration for an oral promise to pay money is a deed poll of land, and the deed is delivered to and retained by the grantee, the consideration is executed and the law will imply a promise to pay. 1 

Pike v Brown, (1851), 7 Cush. 133 fact.

But an express promise may be implied as a matter of 

27 C. J. 131

C. SUBSEQUENT ORAL AGREEMENTS 

A written contract within the statute of frauds may be varied by a subsequent oral agreement made on a new and valuable consideration before breach of the original contract. Such oral agreement may enlarge the time of performance, vary any other terms of the contract or discharge and annul it altogether. 

Stearns v Hall, (1851), 9 Cush. 31, 34 

Hurlburt v Fitzpatrick, (1900), 176 Mass. 287 

176 Mass. 287, 57 N.E. 464 

Contra: Noble v Ward, (1867), L. R. 2 Ex. 135, Costigan 1364

Bonicamp v Starbuck, (Okl.)106::Pac.383., LNA 1917B 141 

EXAMPLE: D made a written agreement, within the statute of frauds, to deliver cloth in installments to P, on eight. months' credit. Subsequently, before any breach, the parties orally agreed upon an extension of time for payment. The oral extension is not within the statute and is enforceable. 

Cummings v Arnold, (1842), 3 Met. 486 d.

STATUTE AS MEANS OF FRAUD 

The statute of frauds, designed to prevent fraud, will not be allowed to operate as a means of fraud either in permitting one guilty of fraud to shelter himself behind it or in allowing its use as a means of perpetrating fraud. But fraud must be shown. Where not shown, the rule does not operate. operate. A refusal to perform an oral contract is not fraud as the word is here used. 

27 C. J. 302 

Sprague v Kimball, (1913), 213 Mass.380,100 NE 622,54 LRANS962

The fraud most commonly treated as taking an agreement out of the statute is that which consists in setting up tho statute against its performance after the other party has been induced to make expenditures or a change of situation in regard to the subject-matter of the agreement, or upon the supposition that it was to be carried into execution, and the assumption of rights thereby to be acquired; so that tho refusal to complete the exeoution of the agreement is not merely a denial of the rights which it was intended to confer but the infliction of an unjust and unconscientious injury and loss. In such case, the party is held by force of his acts or silent acquiescence which have misled the other to his harm to be estopped from setting up the statute of frauds. 

Harrell v Sonnabend, (1906), 191 Mass. 310, 77 N. E. 764 

Mackin v Dwyer, (1909), 205 Mass. 472, 91 N. E. 893

Thus, part performance by a tenant by taking possession and making improvements on the land after the owner's oral agreement to give a lease will take the case out of the statute. 

Traveler Shoe Co.v Koch, (1914), 216 Mass.412,103 N.E.931 

5. WHEN A CONTRACT IS "WITHIN THE STATUTE" 

A contract is said to be "within the statute of frauds" when the promises of the defendant are of such a nature that they are required by the statute to be in writing. 

If such promises are in writing, the contract is enforceablo; if not, the contract cannot be enforced. 

Cummings v Arnold, (1842), 3 Met. 486 6.

TEXT OF FOURTH SECTION OF STATUTE 

There are two sections of the original English statute of frauds which affect the form of simple contracts, the Fourth and the Seventeenth. The corresponding sections in the various statutes of frauds of the several states are numbered differently. Nevertheless, in any discussion of such corresponding secticns in the statute of frauds of any American state, it is customary to refer to them as the Fourth and the Seventeenth sections, the former relating to real property and certain other kinds of contracts, the latter to sales of personal property. 

The so-called "Fourth section" of the Massachusetts as follows: 

statute of fraud. 12. 259 

"Section 1. No action shall be brought 

First, to charge an executor or administrator, or an assignee under an insolvent law of the commonwealth, upon a special promise to answer damages out of his own estate; 

Second, To charge a person upon a special promise to answer for the debt, default or misdoings of another; 

Third, Upon an agreement made upon consideration of marriage; 

Fourth, Upon a contract for the sale of lands, tenements or hereditaments or of any interest in or concerning them; or 

Fifth, Upon an agreement that is not to be performed. within one year from the making thereof; 

Unless the promise, contract or agreement upon which such action is brought, or some memorandum or note thereof is in writing and signed by the party to be charged there with or by some person thereunto by him lawfully authorized. 

Section 2. The consideration of such promise, contract or agreement need not be set forth or expressed in the writing signed by the party to be charged therewith, but may be proved by any legal evidence."

G. L. Chapter 259, sections 1 and 2. 

The language of the statute of frauds does not require any contract to be in writing. It is quite true that the statute will be satisfied by a written contract signed by both parties or their agents but it is also true that the

- statute will be completely satisfied if the contract is evidenced by any written memorandum containing all the promises of the defendant (except the consideration), signed by him or by his agent. 

Cahill v Bigelow, (1856), 18 Pick. 369 

Bugbee v Kendricken, (1880), 130 Mass. 437

B. PROMISE BY EXECUTOR OR ADMINISTRATOR 

This clause was enacted to prevent executors or administrators from being held liable fraudulently to pay out of their own pockets for the debts or liabilities of the estates which they are called upon to administer. In general, a personal representative is not liable personally for the debts of the estate which he represents and, as such representative, his obligation is limited to the payment of claims against the estate to the extent of its assets in his hands. 

But he may promise to be liable personally and, if he does, it is this class of contracts which this clause of the statute requires to be evidenced by a writ ing. 

Mackin v Dwyer, (1910), 205 Mass. 472, 91 N. E. 893 

Smith v Carroll, (1886), 112 Pa.st.590,4 Atl.24, Costigan 1233 

Dillaby v Wilcox, (1891), 60 Conn.71,22 Atl.491,13 L.R.A.643

The clause relates only to promises of the personal representative to pay out of his own estate a debt owed by the estate of the deceased. Therefore, his promise to pay out of the estate of the deceased is not within the statute. 

Hay v Green, (1855), 12 Cush. 232 

Stebbins v Smith, (1826), 4 Pick. 97

The lause is closely allied to, if not identical in principle with the next clause of the statute relating to promises to answer for the debt, default or misdoings of another. The undertaking contemplated by either clause is in the nature of a contract of guaranty or suretyship. The refore. if the principal object of the promisor is not to pay the debt of another but to subserve some purpose of his own, the promise is in reality a promise to pay his own debt and not the debt of another, and is not within the statute. 

The consideration must be not only sufficient to support the promise but it must also be of such a nature as to take the promise out of the statute; and that requisite is found if the facts show that the undertaking operates to the advantage of the promisor and places him under a personal, pecuniary obligation to the promisee, entirely independent of the original debt. 

Bellows v Sowles, 35 Vt. 591 

Gabbert v Evans, (1914), 184 Mo. App. 283, 166 S. W. 635 

EXAMPLE: A testator bequeathed a legacy of $1,000 to his daughter P, devised all his real estate to D and to D's son, and named D as executor. The testator's personal estate was sufficient to pay his debts but not the legacy to P. D orally promised P that if she would not contest the will, D himself would pay her the $1,000.

- Although D's promise is to pay a debt due to P from the estate, yet the chief object of the promise was to serve a purpose of his own. The promise is not within the statute and is enforceable though oral. If the rule were otherwise, P would be defrauded of her legal right to contest the will by D who has reaped the benefit of the transaction. 

Mackin v Dwyer, (1910), 205 Mass. 472, 91 N. E. 893 возветровит 

0. PROMISE TO ANSWER FOR DEBT, DEFAULT OR MISDOINGS OF ANOTHER 1. IN GENERAL 

The statute requires careful analysis to 

OF 

determine its meaning. The sole description of what is covered therein is "a special promise to answer for the debt, default or misdeings of another". Its phraseology is indefinite in three particulars: (a) as to the person to whom the promise is made; (b) as to the person by whom it is made; (o) as to the estate out of which the promisor is to answer for the debt.

A statute may be viewed as a body with a soul. Its phraseology is its body and its thought is its soul. Sometimes the thought of a statute and its phraseology do not; coincide. There such is the case, it is its thought which is the statute.

A full expression of the thought of the statute as to the character of the promise covered by it may be stated as follows: It.is a promise to answer for the debt of another (i.e. to pay if he does not), made to the creditor, by one who has no personal concern or interest in the debt, the answering therefor to be out of his own estate. Therefore, there are four essentials which must exist to create a promise to answer for the debt of another within the meaning of the statute: (1) The promise must be a conditional promise to pay the debt of a third person, i.e. to pay it if he does not. Therefore, an absolute promise to pay a debt of a third person is not within the statute, as where exclusive credit is given to the promisor. (2) The promise: must be made to the creditor. If made to the debtor, there is no third person to whom the promisor is answerable. (3) The promise must be made by one who is not primarily concerned in the debt. If the primary object of the promisor is to subserve some special purpose of his own, then, the promisor is regarded as, in reality, promising to pay his own debt, although a third party is the original obligor and performance of the promise will also result, incidentally, in payment of the debt of another.

LLOM STO 

sa Sedeta 20 1000 دان 37

WUGHTY A DANE 

J 401 an abionice fo bg 30 647 (0) 

SIOD OVIOLUMN OF

(4) The promise must be to pay out of the promisor's own estate and not out of the debtor's estate which may be in the promisor's hands. 

Cincinnati Traction Co.y Cole, (1919), 258 Fed.169, Costigan 1239

All these various essentials may be expressed in the form of a single test, as stated by Professor Williston: "The true test is, whether a promisor is, to the actual or presumed knowledge of the creditor, a surety; if so, his promise is within the statute". 

Williston on Contracts, section 462

The importance of keeping this test constantly in mind in the consideration of every case involving the first two clauses of the Fourth section of the statute of frauds cannot be overemphasized. 

2. NATURE OF DEBT, DEFAULT OR MISDOINGS 

The express language of the statute clearly applies to liability in tort as well as to debts in contract. 

EXAMPLE: D's infant son negligently fired a gun in the direction of P's stable, the shot penetrating the stable and killing P's mare. D subsequently orally promised to pay P the value of the mare. The promise is within the statute and is not enforceable. 

Baker v Morris, (1885), 33 Kan. 580, 7 Pan. 267 

3. EXISTENCE AND LIABILITY OF PRINCIPAL DEBTOR 

The person for whom the promise is made must be liable to the person to whom it is made for if there is no liability on the part of the promisee there would be no debt or default of another to answer for and the promise necessarily would be an original and independent promise and not within the statute. 

27 C. J. 130 

Brown v Bank, (1895), 88 Tex. 265, 31 S.7. 285, 33 L.R.A.359

There must be either a present or prospective liability of a third person for which the promisor agrees to answer. 

Furbish v Goodnow, (1867), 98 Mass. 296

EXAMPLE: D orally promised P to pay the amount due to P on the note of a corporation if P would forbear to sue the corpora tion. The corporation is the principal debtor for whose debt orally agreed to answer. The promise is within the statute and not enforceable. 7. 

Lawley Corp. v зuff, (1918), 230 Mass. 21, 119 N. . 186

In order to bring a promise within this clause of the statute or the clause relating to executors or administrators, there must be a contract of guaranty with three parties, debtor, creditor and guarantor and two promises to pay running to the creditor, one from the original debtor, the other from the guarantcr. If these conditions exist, then the contract is within the statute except where the real nature and intent

S of the transaction constitutes the third party primarily liable by reason of his having made the promise with the rimary object of subserving some purpose of his own. 

If there are only two parties to the contract, it is not within the statute and is enforceable though oral. 

Mease v Wagner, (1821), McCord (S.C.) 395, Arant's Cases 140

Thus a promise to pay if the promisee will lend money to a third person is within the statute, there being three parties to the transaction; but a promise to pay money if the promisee will pay money to a third person is not within the statute, there being only two parties to the transaction. 

Ames y Foster, (1871), 106 Mass. 400 

Swift v Pierce, (1866), 13 Allen 136 

Pierce v Kittredge, (1874), 115 Mass. 374 

Carleton v Floyd, (1306), 192 Mass. 204, 78 N. E. 128 

O'Connell v Mt. Holyoke College, (1893), 174 Mass. 511, 56 

N. E. 460 

So, if the promisor becomes primarily and not collater ally liable, the promise is not within the statute, although the goods are delivered to or for the benefit of a third person. Thus, if a promise is made by one in his own name and on his own credit to pay for goods delivered to or services done for another, that is original; it is his 6wn contract, on sufficient consideration, and is called original and is binding on him without a writing. 

Kendricken v Bugbee, (1881), 130 Mass. 437 far But if the oral language is "let him have money or goods, or do service for him, and I promise you that he will pay", or "if he does not pay, I will", this is collateral and, though made on sufficient consideration, is within the statute and not enforceable. 

Phelps v Stone, (1898), 172 Mass. 355, 52 N. E. 517 

Eastwood v Kenyon, (1840), 11 A. & E. 446, Costigan 292

Eastern R. R. v Benedict, (1860), 15 Gray 289 

Tighe v Morrison, (N. Y., 1889), 5. L. R. A. 617 

Barrett v McHugh, (1880), 128 Mass. 165 

Stone v Walker, (1859), 13 Gray 613 

Walker v Hill, (1876), 119 Mass. 249 

Dean v Tallman, (1870), 105 Mass. 443 4.

ORIGINAL CR COLLATERAL PROMISES GENERALLY 

There is a temptation for a promisee, in a case where the real debtor has proved insolvent or unable to pay, to enlarge the scope of the promise or to torture mere words of encouragement and confidence into an absolute promise. The reason for enacting this clause of the statute requiring contracts of guaranty to be evidenced by a writing is because it is so obviously just that a promisor receiving no benefits should be bound only by the exact terms of his promise. Therefore, whenever the alleged promisor is an absolute stranger to the transaction, and without interest in it, courts strictly uphold the obligations of the statute.

Collateral promises exchange 

B have consedesations

Scontractual liability

But cases sometimes arise in which, although a third party is the original obligor and primary debtor, yet the promisor has a personel, immediate and pecuniary interest in the transaction and is therefore himself a party to be benefited by the performance of the promise! In such cases, the reason which underlies and which prompted this statutory provision fails, and the courts will give effect to the promise, even though it is oral. 

Davis v Patrick, (1891), 141 U. S. 479

The language of the statute expressly applies only to "a special promise to answer for the debt, default or misdoings of another". Liabilities growing out of any other transactions are not affected by this clause of the statute. 

It is clearly intended to relieve a guarantor or surety. was never intended to relieve one who has a personal beneficial interest in the transaction. It 

Bailey v Marshall, (1896), 174 Pa.St.602,34 Atl.326, Cost.1265

For this reason, an original promise or undertaking, as distinguished from a collateral promise to answer for another person's promise or undertaking is not within the statute. 

27 C. J. 131

Contracts held to be outside the statute and therefore unaffected by it are commonly called "original" or "independent" while contracts falling within the statute are spoken of as "collateral". 

Newbern v Fisher, (1530),198 1.C.385,151 S.E.875,68 A.L.R.345

The terms original and collateral promise, although not used in the statute, are convenient to distinguish between the cases where, although the effect of the promise is to pay the debt of another, yet the leading object of the undertaker is to subserve or promote some interest or purpose of his own and those where the direct and leading object of the promise is to pay the debt of another. The latter, whether made before, or after, or at the same time with the promise of the principal, is not enforceable unless manifested by evidence in writing; the former, if made on sufficient consideration, is unaffected by the statute, although the effect of it is tɔ release or suspend the debt of another, for that is not the leading object of the promisor. 

Nelson v Boynton, (1341), 3 Met. 396 

D'Wolf v Rabaud, (1828), 1 Peters 476, 479 

Taylor v Harrington, (1922), 243 Mass.210,137 N.E.350 

Davis v Abell, (1919), 185 Ky.843,216 N.W.104, Costigan 1236 

Hartley v Sanford, (1901), 66 N.J.L.627,50 Atl.454, Cest. 1250 

Smith v Portis Bros., (1919) 140 Ark.356, 215 SW 590, Cost. 1235 

Hill v Grat, (1923), 247 Mass.25,141 N.E.593, following 

Curtis v Brown, (1850), 5 Cush. 488, and 

Ames v Foster, (1871), 106 Mass. 400

The cases are numerous, and the distinctions as to cases which are or are not within the statute are so nice and often

- so shadowy that it is difficult to reconcile them. The reason for this difficulty is that the test whether a promise is original or collateral, whether there is a contract of suretyship or not, is the intention of the parties and, in determining the section. regard must be had to the nature of the promise, the situation of the parties and all the circumstances of each particular case. This is generally a question of fact for the jury. There is seldom, if ever, any particular fact which is decisive of the question. 

Hurst v Goodman (1910)68 W.Va.462,69 S.E.898,32 LRA (NS) 598 

Com. v Hinson, (1911), 143 Ky.428,133 S.W.912, LRA 19178 139

Mankin v Jones, (7.Va.,1908)60 S.E.248,15 L.R.A.(N.S.) 214 

Berry v Central Trust Co., (1923), 247 Mass. 241,142 N.E.58 

Alexander v Dove (1918), 231 Mass. 362, 121 N. E. 74 

Phelps v Stone, (1898), 172 Mass. 355

The mere verbal form of expression is not controlling, although the language is of great importance in determining the nature and intent of the transaction. 

27 C. J. 155 

Thus, the words "I will see you paid" may mean either that "I will pay you", or "You shall be paid". The true meaning must depend on the other facts of the case. 

Mount stephen v Lakeman, L. R. 7 Q. B. 196 

Allen v Leonard, (1560), 16 Gray 202 

Stone v Symmes, (1836), 18 Pick. 467 

Rand v Mather, (1853), 11 Cush. 1 

EXAMPLE: D, who had a dissipated brother, orally stated to P: "I don't want my brother to go ragged and hungry; for any necessaries of life you may furnish him, I will see you paid". P boarded the brother in reliance on D's promise and, after attempting to collect from the brother therefor, sues D on his promise. The evidence shows that P held the brother primarily responsible, hence D's promise is collateral, within the statute, and not enforceable. 

Hill v Raymond, (1862), 3 Allen 540

The following cases have been held to create original, independent promises, not within the statute, and hence enforceable though oral: 

Manning v Anthony (1911) 208 Mass.399,94 NE 466,32 LRA(NS)1179

Jahnson v Huffaker (1917)99 Kan.466,162 Pac.1150, LRA 1917D 872 

Wahl v Cunningham (1928)320 Mo.57,6 S.W. (2nd)576,67 ALR 489 

Cf. Burr v Mazer, 2 Pa. Super. Ct. as quoted in

Howell v Harvey (1909) 65 W.Va.310,64 S.E.249,22 LRA (NS) 1077note 

EXAMPLE 1: D said to P: "If you will take care of my brother William, I will pay you when I can. 

Downs v Perkin, (1911), 207 Mass. 409, 93 N. E. 645 

Ellis v Felt, (1910), 206 Mass. 472, 92 N. E. 702

EXAMPLE 2: D was having a house built for him by X but 

X could obtain no credit and D orally promised P that if P would supply materials to K on X's credit, D would not pay

The following cases have been held to be collateral promises, within the statute and hence not enforceable because oral: 

Trustees of Andover v Flint, (1847), 13 Met. 539 

Edwards v Bryan, (1926), 214 Ala.441,108 So.9,52 A.L.R.784

EXAMPLE: P had a lien on X's boat for repairs made thereon. D orally promised P that if P would relinquish the lien and would not sue X, D would pay X's debt to P. The lien was relinquished but X was still the primary debtor to P. 

Mallory v Gillett, (1860), 21 .Y.412, Arant's Cases 206 5.

PROMISE TO PAY PROMISOR'S OWN DEBT 

a.

IN GENERAL 

An oral promise which, under the circumstances, is in effect a promise to pay a debt due from the promisor himself is not within the statute although the incidental result of the performance may be the discharge of the debt of another. 

27 C. J. 136

It is, in principle, like the case of a debtor giving money to another to pay his debt and he neglecting to do it. 

It is not a promise to pay the debt of another within the meaning of the statute. 

Aldrich v Carpenter, (1893), 160 Mass. 166, 35 N. E. 456 

Colt v Root, (1821), 17 Mass. 229 

EXAMPLE 1: D, a ship-owner, told P that if P would pay a debt which D owed to a ship-chandler, D would repay P. There are only two parties to this contract and it therefore cannot be the promise to pay the debt of another. 

Perkins v Littlefield, (1962), 5 Allen 370

EXAMPLE 2: P furnished materials for a ship owned in part by D, and, to prevent P from libelling the ship, D orally promised P that he would pay the debt. Since D was a part owner of the ship, it was his own debt which he promised to pay and his purpose was to benefit himself by getting a release of his own property, although the incidental effect would be the payment of the debt of the other owners. The promise is enforceable, though oral. 

Fish v Thomas, (1856), 5 Gray 45

EXAMPLE 3: D took title to a portion of X's land but X 

remained in possession for a time thereafter, the taxes on all

the land being assessed to X. P, the collector of taxes, called the next month and found that D had finally taken possession of his portion. D told P that if P would have the tax apportioned, D would pay to P the portion of the tax which properly belonged to D. This is a promise by the debtor to pay his own debt and is not within the statute. 

Burr v Wilcox, (1866), 13 Allen 269 b. 

PROMISE TO PAY OUT OF DEBTOR'S FUNDS IN PROMISOR'S HANDS 

The promise must contemplate a payment by the promisor out of his own property or at least not out of the funds, property or estate of the debtor. 

Stebbins v Smith, (1826), 4 Pick. 97 

Towne v Grover, (1830), 9 Pick. 306

EXAMPLE: X contracted with D to build a house for D and 

X contracted with P for labor and materials but P refused te furnish anything except upon D's promise that D would pay X's bill out of the funds coming to X. D's promise is not within the statute. 

Eastbrook v Gebhart, 32 Ohio St. 415, Cincinnati Traction Co.vCole, (1919) 258 Fed.169, Costigan 1239 6. cited in

PROMISE TO DEBTOR TO DISCHARGE DEBT 

A promise to pay the debt of another must be made to the creditor and not to the debtor. Otherwise there are not three parties to the contract and hence there can be no contract of guaranty or suretyship, no promise to pay if the debtor does not pay, but instead there is an absolute original promise to pay the debt. 

Pike v Brown, (1851), 7 Cush. 133 

Weld v Nichols, (1836), 17 Pick. 538 

Howe v Walker, (1855), 4 Gray 318

EXAMPLE 1: In consideration of a promissory note given by D to P, P orally promised that he would pay D's debt to X. 

The consideration for the note is D's promise to the debtor which is not within the statute. 

Hubon v Park, (1875), 116 Mass. 541 

Perkins v Littlefield, (1862), 5 Allen 370 

EXAMPLE 2: An oral promise made by a grantee to a grant or of land, as part of the consideration for the conveyance, that the grantee would assume and pay taxes thereafter assessed on the land as of the first of May previous, is a promise to the debtor. It is not within the statute either as a contract for the sale of an interest in land or as a promise to answer for the debt of another. 

Preble v Baldwin, (1850),6 Cush.549,15 LRA (NS) 1090, note

EXAMPLE 3: An oral agreement, between the makers of a promissory note for money borrowed to replenish the reserve of a bank of which they were directors, as to the proportion of the note for which as among themselves, each would be

· responsible, is a promise to the debtors and not within the statute. 

Adamson v McKeon, (Iowa, 1929), 229 N.. 414, 65 A.L. R. 817 EXAMPLE 4: D, an owner of land, orally promised P that 

D would pay P for fertilizer sold and charged by P to X who was a tenant on D's land. This promise, being made to the creditor, is within the statute. 

Henderson v Skinner, (1928), 146 S.C.281,145 S.E.875,59 ALR 174 

If I owes a debt to P and D promises P, the creditor, to pay it, that is a promise to the creditor and is within the statute. But in the same case, should D, for a valid consideration, prise X, the debtor, to pay the debt to P and indemnify X from the payment, although one of the results of performance would be to pay the debt of X, yet it is not a promise to the creditor to pay the debt of another, but a promise to the debtor to pay his debt, and is not within the statute. 

Aldrich v Ames, (1857), 9 Gray 76, Costigan 1249 

Machinist v Green, (N.H., 1920),109 Atl.45, Costigan 1267 

Frohardt v Duff, (Iowa, 1912) 155 F.7.609,40 L.R.A.(N.S.)242

Johnson v Huffaker (1917)99 Kan.366,162 Pac.1150, LRA 1917D 872 

Williston on Contracts, section 460

Although, even if the promise is made to the creditor, it is not within the statute if the leading object of the promisor is to subserve some interest or purpose of his own. 

Alger v Scoville, (1854), 1 Gray 591 

7. ORED IT GIVEN TO PROMISOR 

Where goods are delivered to a third person, the test to decide whether the promisor is an original debtor or a guarantor, when the promise is made before the credit is given, is whether credit was given to the person receiving the goods. If it was, then the person promising to pay is a guarantor only, undertaking to pay another's debt. But if no credit was given to the person receiving the goods, then the promisor is himself debtor for goods sold to him and delivered to another person by his order, his promise is not to pay the debt of another, and a parol romine, being made on sufficient consideration, is a binding contract and is not within the tatute, Paine v Silva, (1898), 171 Mass. 276, 50 N. E. 1126 

Cahill v Bigelow, (1836), 18 Pick. 369 

Barrett v Mcliugh, (1880), 128 Mass. 165 

Bugbee v Kendricken, (1881), 130 Mass. 457 

Phelps v Stone, (1398), 172 Mass. 355, 52 N. E. 517 

Henderson v Skinner, (1928), 143 SC 281,143 SE 875,59 AIR 174 

Seo Weeks v Parsons, (1900), 176 Mass 5.70, 58 N. E. 157 

EXAMPLE 1: X, a contract01, was erecting a building for

D for whoin P was supplying brick under a contract with X. authorized D to pay such monthly sums as X should owe to P

- and D orally promised P that he would do so. P having given credit to X, the promise of D is collateral and within the statute.

O'Connell v Mt. Holyoke College (1899) 174 Mass.511,55 NE 460 

EXAMPLE 2: P had a contract with X to do mason work on houses being built by X on which D held a construction mortgage. P stopped work on becoming doubtful of X's ability to pay. D orally promised P that if P would continue work D would pay P the money as fast as it was due to P from X. P having given credit to X who was not released from his contract, the promise of D is collateral and within the statute. 

Ribock v Canner, (1914), 218 Mass. 5, 105 N. E. 462

Credit 

EXAMPLE 3: P and D orally agreed that all goods bought by X from P should be charged to and paid for by D. having been given to D, the promisor, and not to X, the promise of D is original and not within the statute. 

Dean v Tallman, (1870), 105 Mass. 443

EXAMPLE 4: X agreed to build a house for D and furnish all materials for a gross sum. X ordered the doors from P but P refused to deliver them, being doubtful of X's ability to pay.

D orally promised P that if P would deliver the doors to X, D would pay for them. Credit having been given to D, the promisor, and not to X, who received the goods, the promise is original and not within the statute. 

Walker v Hill, (1876), 119 Mass. 249

EXAMPLE 5: D was a large stockholder in a corporation which desired P to erect a building. D orally promised P that if P would erect the building, P would get his money. 

The promise is equivocal. P must ascertain at his peril to whom credit is to be given and the mere belief by P, without actual investigation, that D is to be responsible will not make D liable as on an original promise. 

Alexander v`Dove, (1918), 231 Mass. 362, 121 N. E. 74

Where services are rendered to a third person at the request of the promisor, the same rule applies. 

Ellis v Felt, (1910), 206 Mass. 472, 92 N. E. 702 

Downs v Perkin, (1911), 207 Mass. 409, 93 N. E. 645 

EXAMPLE 1: P, an innkeeper, supplied a dinner for a military company during a public celebration. The members supposed the dinner was to be provided for them but did not know who was to pay, and they refused to pay. D, their captain, orally promised P that he would pay for all. No credit having been given to D originally, his promise is collateral and within the statute. 

Tileston v Nettleton, (1828), 6 Pick. 509 

EXAMPLE 2: P contracted with D's father to take care of the father's rooms. After some services had been rendered, and not paid for, D orally promised P that if P would con-

8. CONSIDERATION FOR PROMISE 

8.

IN GENERAL 

skip 121

The question under the statute of frauds is not whether there is a sufficient consideration for the defendant's promise but whether that promise is to answer for the debt of another. The common law requires a consideration for every promise, oral or written, which is not under seal. 

The statute also requires that, if it is a promise to answer for the debt of another, it must be in writing. 

Barker v Thayer, (1915), 217 Mass. 13, 104 N. E. 572

Therefore, the mere fact that there is a valuable consideration for a promise to pay the debt of another does not take it out of the statute of frauds. 

Washington Co.v Freedman, (1928), 263 Mass.554,559,161 N.E.883 

But the statute does not apply where the promisor receives something from the promisee for his own benefit. 

Paul v Wilbur, (1905), 189 Mass. 48, 75 N. E. 63 

Curtis v Brown, (1850), 5 Cush. 488, 491 

Stratton v Hill, (1382), 134 Mass. 27

Alger v Scoville, (1854), 1 Gray 91 

Griffin v Cunningham, (1903),183 Mass.505,507,67 N.E.660

The decisive test is not the presence or nature of consideration but whether the promise is an original promise or a collateral one, that is, whether the promise is such that the promisor became, within the intention of the parties, a principal debtor primarily liable. 

Ribock v Canner, (1914), 218 Mass. 5, 105 N. E. 462 

Hurst v Goodman, (1910),68 7.Va.462,69 S.E.898,32 LRA(NS)590

EXAMPLE: X owed P on a promissory note. X, P and D made an oral agreement by which, in consideration of certain land to be conveyed by X to D, D promised P that he would pay the note of x. The agreement did not release the obligation on the note and there is no consideration moving from P the promisee to D the promisor for D's oral promise, hence D's promise is collateral and within the statute as being merely an oral promise to answer for the debt of X. 

Furbish v Goodnow, (1867), 98 Mass. 206 

Nelson v Boynton, (1841), 3 Met. 396

Overruling Perley v Spring, (1815), 12 Mass. 297 

b.

FORBEARANCE TO SUE OR TO ENFORCE LIEN 

If the principal and immediate object of the transaction is to benefit the promisor and not to pay the debt of another, forbearance to enforce a mortgage or other

Fears v Story, (1881), 131 Mass. 47 

Fish v Thomas, (1855), 5 Gray 45

C. 

RELEASE OR TRANSFER OF LIEN OR SECURITY 

It is well settled that it is not a sufficient ground to prevent the operation of the statute that the plaintiff has relinquished an advantage or given up some lien or claim in consequence of the defendant's promise if that advantage or relinquishment did not also directly enure to the benefit of the defendant. It is only when such relinquishment or surrender operates to transfer to the defendant the right, interest or advantage which the plaintiff gives up, or to create in the defendant some title or benefit derived from that which the other party surrenders, that the promise can be regarded as an original undertaking and not within the statute. 

Dexter v Blanchard, (1865), 11 Allen 365 

Carleton v Floyd, (1906), 192 Mass. 204, 78 N. E. 126

When, by the new promise, the old debt is extinguished, the promise is not within the statute. But where the original debt still subsists, and where the plaintiff has relinquished no interest or advantage which has enured to the benefit of the defendant it is not an original contract but a contract to pay another's debt and the promise must be in writing. 

Jepherson v Hunt, (1861), 2 Allen 471 

Call v Calef, (1840), 4 Cush. 388 

Lord v Davison, (1861), 3 Allen 151 

Walker v Penniman, (1857), 8 Gray 253 

Richardson v Robbins (1878), 124 Mass. 105 

Harburg Co. v Martin, (1902), 1 K. B. 778, Costigan 1258 

Crowley v Whittemore, (1926), 235 Mass. 99, 150 N. E. 880 

Distinguish Chapin v Lapham, (1838) 20 Pick.467,33 LRA 359, note

EXAMPLE 1: D was the owner of an equity of redemption in certain land which was subject to an overdue mortgage to P, made by D's grantor. D orally promised P that if F would not foreclose, D would pay the mortgage note owed by D's grantor within a reasonable time thereafter. The right which P relinquished transferred to D a freedom from foreclosure which he did not before possess, the promise is original and not within the statute. 

Manning v Anthony, (1911) 208 lass.399,94 NE 466,32 LRANS 1179 

EXAMPLE 2: X bought goods with funds advanced by D who took the bills of lading in his own name as security and indorsed them to A under an agreement that I was to sell the goods and account to D for the proceeds. P, the carrier, refused to deliver the goods to X until the freight was paid. 

D then orally promised P to pay the freight if P would deliver the goods to A. P's surrender of the goods to enured directly to D's benefit. There is a new and independent contract and D's promies is original and not within the statute. 

Wills v Brown, (1375), 118 Mass. 137

GD EXAMPLE 3: P, having a lien on a ship for a debt incurred by its former owner, refused to allow the ship to sail unless the debt was paid. D, the present owner, orally promised to pay the debt to P if P would discharge the lien. 

P's surrender of the lien enured directly to D's benefit. 

There is a new and independent contract and not a promise to answer for the debt of another, within the meaning of the statute.

Fears v Story, (1881), 131 Mass. 47 

EXAMPLE 4: D was the mortgagee of a ship for which the owners bought coal from P in Boston. The ship being about to sail, ono of its owners went into bankruptcy and P threatened to attach the ship. Dorally promised P that if

P would forbear to attach, D would pay the coal bill. The right which P relinquished transferred no right to D which he did not before possess, since P's claim was subject to 

D's mortgage, and D's promise is collateral. 

Ames v Foster, (1871), 106 Mass. 400, Costigan 1255

EXAMPLE 5: D's minor son, who was ill, owed a debt to P which D orally promised P that D would pay if P would not trouble the son about it. The original debt still subsists and P's forbearance to sue the son transferred no rights to 

D whose promise is within the statute. 

Dexter v Blanchard, (1865), 11 Allen 335 

EXAMPIE 6: Mrs. X, a guest at P's tavern, being in arrears for board, P refused to allow her to remove her baggage. D orally promised P that he would pay her bill if 

P would allow her to remove the baggage. The old debt is not extinguished and D's promise is within the statute. The court held that P had no lien yet the decision would have been the same if he had a lien since its surrender would not enure to the benefit of D. 

Hursh v Byers, 29 Mo. 469

EXAMPLE 7: X agreed to build a house for D and to buy materials therefor from P. When the house was partly built, X agreed to release D from the rest of the building contract, in consideration for which D orally promised P to pay for the materials bought by X from P. There is consideration for D's promise but it moves from X, not from P. The debt of X still subsists and P has relinquished nothing. The promise is within the statute. 

Curtis v Brown, (1850) 

1850), 5 Cush. 488 

Gill v Herrick, (1873), 111 Mass. 501 

Brightman v Hicks, (1871), 108 Mass. 246,15 LRA (NS) 1089, noto 9.

DISCHARGE OF ORIGINAL DEBTOR a. IN GENERAL. 

Even though there is an existing liability of a third person for which one, promisor undertakes to answer still the promise is not within the statute if the terms are

such that it effects an extinguishment of such liability. other words, the liability of the original debtor must continue in order for the promise to be within the statute. 

Nelson v Boynton, (1841), 3 Met. 396 In 

Eden v Chaffee, (1895), 180 Mass. 225, 35 N. E. 675 

Stowell v Gram, (1903), 184 ass. 562, 69 N. E. 342 

Jewett v Warriner, (1921), 237 Mass. 36, 129 . E. 296 

Pope Co. v Wheelwright, (1921), 240 Mass. 221, 133 N.E. 106

No written release or particular form of words is necessary to constitute a discharge, a mutual understanding of the parties being sufficient. 

27 C. J. 154

EXAMPLE 1: X, having agreed to build a house for D, bought lumber therefor from P. P threatened to sue X for the price and trustee D. D orally promised P that if P would release X from the debt, D would pay it. X was released. 

D's promise is original and not within the statute. 

Griffin v Cunningham, (1905), 183 Mass. 505, 67 N. E. 660

EXAMPLE 2: D's son, in the charcoal business, bought wood from P. The son sold his business to D who so notified P and orally requested P to transfer the son's debt to D and D would pay it.

The transfer was made, extinguishing the original debt and D's promise is not within the statute. 

Langdon v Hughes, (1871), 107 Mass. 202

EXAMPLE 3: X agreed to erect a building for D and en gaged P as a subcontractor. When P had partly completed his work, X gave P two notes which were not paid when due. D orally promised P that if P would finish out his subcontract with X, D would pay P all that X owed him. The promise of D is collateral unless P proves that he accepted D as sole debtor in substitution for X, amounting to a release of X from the debt. 

Slotnick v Smith, Trudeau v Poutre, (1925), 252 Mass. 303, 147 N. E. 727 (1896), 165 Mass. 81, 42 F. E. 508

EXAMPLE 4: X was indebted to P and agreed to pay him in labor. P told him he would notify him when he was ready for him to work. Before being so notified, X went to work for D and, while so employed, P and X went to D and P asked D if he would give X up. D refused but orally promised P that D would see X's debt paid or would pay the debt. There is no evidence that the debt of X to P was discharged and the promise of D is collateral. 

Stone v Symmes, (1836), 18 Pick. 467

EXAMPLE 5: X agreed to build a house for D and bought flooring from P. When part of the flooring had been delivered, P refused to deliver more. Then D orally promised 

P that if P would deliver the rest to X, D would pay for it. 

There is no evidence that X has been released from his debt and the promise of D is collateral. 

Miles v Driscoll, (1909), 201 Mass. 318, 87 N. E. 579

Gill v Herrick, Childs v Walker, (1873), 111 Mass. 501 (1861), 2 Allen 259 

Distinguish Dows v Swett, (1883), 134 Mass. 140 

EXAMPLE 6: P, an emancipated minor, was hired to do certain work by his father. After its completion, P and his father and D entered into an oral agreement by which P was to release his father from the debt and look to D for payment. The original debtor is discharged and the promise of 

D is original and not within the statute. 

Wood v Corcoran, (1861), 1 Allen 405 

b. NOVATION 

Where the creditor, the debtor and a third person who owes the debtor, mutually agree that such third person shall be substituted for the debtor and that the debtor shall be discharged from his debt to the creditor and the third person shall be discharged from his debt to the original debtor, the transaction constitutes a novation and is not within the statute for the reason that in such cases the promise is to pay the promisor's own debt and also because there is no subsisting debt of another to which the promise can be collateral, liability upon the promise being the only liability left by force of the novation. 

27 Č. J. 154 

Nelson v Boynton, (1541), 3 Met. 396 

Allen v Leonard, (1860), 16 Gray 202 

Caswell v Fellows, (1872), 110 Mass. 52 

Miles v Driscoll, (1909), 210 Mass. 318, 87 N. E. 579 

Carleton v Floyd, (1906), 192 Mass. 204, 78 N. E. 126 

Plummer v Greenwood, (1897), 169 Mass. 584, 48 N. E. 782 

Griffin v Cunningham, (1905), 183 Mass. 505, 67 N. E. 660 

EXAMPLE 1: X, an owner of land, employed P at a certain commission to find a customer for it. P found D for a customer whose offer was accepted by X. P, D and X entered into an oral agreement that X should be released from his debt to P and that P should look to D for the commission.. 

The effect of this agreement is to discharge X from his liability to P and to substitute D in his place. There is a novation, D's promise is original, and not within the statute. 

Curran v O'Donnell, (1920), 236 Mass. 357, 128 N. E. 408 

EXAMPLE 2: D contracted with X for mason work on D's houses. P was employed by X in the work. Before its completion, D, in consideration of P's doing other work for D without charge, orally promised P to pay P the balance which X should owe P and P accepted D as his debtor in place of X, to which X consented. D's promise is original and not within the statute. 

McNulty v Cruff, (1912), 211 Mass. 459, 98 N. E. 512 10.

PROMISE TO INDEMNIFY 

It is sometimes said that a mere promise of indemnity is not within the statute, but the statement is too

broad. It is clear, however, that a promise to indemnify made to the debtor is not within the statute. 

Hawes v Murphy, (1906), 191 Mass. 469, 78 N. E. 109 

Posten v Clem, (Ala., 1918), 78 So. 883, 1 A. L. R. 381 

EXAMPLE 1: P, at the request of D, and for a valuable consideration, signed a bail bond for X payable to the County, and D orally promised to indemnify P and save him harmless. The promise of D was made to P, the debtor on the bond, to pay the debtor's own debt. It is not a promise to the County to pay if the debtor does not, and hence is an original promise and not within the statute. 

Aldrich v Ames, (1857), 9 Gray 76, Costigan 1249

EXAMPLE 2: The promise of one surety to another to hold the other harmless upon their joint obligation, or to pay the same, is a promise to the debtor, is an original promise and not within the statute. 

Blake v Cole, (1839), 22 Pick. 97

EXAMPLE 3: P, the owner of a majority of the stock in a corporation, had indorsed several of the Company's notes. P sold and delivered all his stock to D, in return for which D deeded his farm to P and orally agreed to indemnify P against liability as indorser on the notes. The promise is an absolute promise, made to the debtor, and not a promise to the creditors to pay the notes if P did not. It is an original promise and not within the statute.

Alger v Scoville, (1854), 1 Gray 391 

Subsequently for

EXAMPLE 4: P conveyed land to D. 

valuable consideration, D orally promised P to indemnify him against all claims against P by reason of the wall on X's adjoining lot being set partly on the lot purchased by D. made use of the wall and X sued P and recovered judgment. 

D's promise, being made to the debtor P, is an original promise and not within the statute. 

Weld v Nichols, (1836), 17 Pick. 538

The sound rule is that the promise may or may not be within the statute dependent upon whether it is an original or collateral promise which is to be determined by the established tests, mainly by the leading object and purpose of the promisor, the person to whom credit was given, and the existence of a consideration beneficial to the promisor. 

27 C. J. 155 

Colt v Root, (1821), 17 Mass. 229 

Hartley v Sanford, 66 N.J.L. 627, 50 Atl. 454, 55 LRA 206, Cost, 1250

EXAMPLE: D held a mortgage for $125 on a horse belonging to X. X sold the horse to P and warranted it free from all encumbrances and D, in order to induce P to buy it, orally offered to accept $50 from P in consideration of D's assent to the sale and for D's guaranty to save P harmless from all loss by reason of any encumbrance thereon. Stratton v Hill, (1883), 134 Mass. 27 1 

11 ASSUMPTION OF MORTGAGES OR OTHER ENCUMBRANCES 

By the great weight of authority, an agreement by the purchaser of property with the vendor to pay, as part of the purchase price, an encumbrance to which the property is subject, is not a promise to answer for the debt of another. The the cry of the decisions is that the promise is a promise to pay the purchaser's own debt to the vendor and the fact that performance of the promise incidentally discharges the. indebtedness of the vendor is immaterial. 

Enos v Anderson, (1907), 40 Colo.395,93 Pac.475,15 LRANS 1087 

Creesy v Willis, (1893), 159 Mass. 249, 34 N. E. 265 

Goodwin v Gilbert, (1813), 9 Mass. 510 

Carr v Dooley, (1876), 113 Mass. 294 

Pike v. Brown, (1851), 7 Cush. 133 

Barker v Scudder, 56 Mo. 272 

Besshears v Rowe, 46 Mo. 502.

This is no more than a promise to pay the promisor's debt in a particular way. 

Wood v Moriarty, (1887), 15 R.1.518, 9 Atl.427,Costigan 639 

Preble v Baldwin, (1850), Cush.549,15 LRA (NS) 1090, note 

Brightman v Hicks, (1871), 103 Mass. 246,15 LRA (NS) 1089,note 

Distinguish Curtis v Brown, (1850), (1850), 5 Cush. 488 12.

DEL CREDERE AGENTS. 

A del credere commission is one under which the agent, in consideration of an additional payment, engages to insure to his principal, not only the solvency of the debtor, but also the punctual discharge of the debt. 

Although he is virtually a surety and the purchaser is the primary debtor yet the promise of the agent to guarantee the debt of the purchaser is made primarily for his own benefit and therefore his promise to his principal is not a promise to answer for the debt of another within the meaning of the statute and is enforceable though oral. 

Swan v Nesmith, (1828), 7 Pick. 220

His promise, though a typical guaranty, is withdrawn from the statute only because the transaction on both sides is concerned with a larger contract to which the guaranty is a mere incident. 

Wolff v Koppel, (1843), 5 Hill (N. Y.) 458, Costigan 1237

D. AGREEMENTS IN CONSIDERATION OF MARRIAGE 1. IN GENERAL 

The statute applies to all agreements, except mutual promises to marry, which are founded upon a consideration of marriage, either with the promisor or, with a third person, and renders stren unenforceable either at law or in equity. 

27 C. J. 126. 

Richardson v Richardson (1894)148 T11.563,36 N 603,26 LRA 305

EXAMPLE 1: An oral agreement, made before marriage and in consideration thereof, by which a husband promised to make a will in his wife's favor, is within the statute. 

Sughrue v Barlow, (1919), 233 Mass. 468, 124 N. E. 285 

EXAMPLE 2: An oral promise by a man that, in consideration of his intended marriage, he will convey certain lands to his wife upon their marriage is doubly within the statute, as a promise made in consideration of marriage and as a contract for the sale of land. 

Rowell v Barber, (1910), 142 Wis.304, 135 NW.937,27 LRANS 1140

EXAMPLE 3: A, while insolvent, orally promised his intendeä wife that he would give her certain bonds in consideration of their marriage. The bonds were delivered to her with the understanding that they should not belong to her until the marriage day. The promise is within the statute and the husband's creditors are entitled to the bonds. 

Deshon v Wood, (1888), 148 Mass. 132,19 N.E.1,1 L.R.A. 518

Agreements in consideration of marriage are to be distinguished from agreements made merely in contemplation or expectation of marriage. The former are within the statute while the latter are not. 

27 C. J. 127 

Larson v Johnson, (1890), 78 Wis. 300, 47 N. W. 615 

Bader v Hiscox, (Iowa, 1919), 174 N.W. 565, 10 A.L.R. 316

The statute does not apply to executed contracts. 

EXAMPLE: Judith gave Augustus a promissory note for money lent by him to her, secured by a mortgage of her real estate. Subsequently, she married him in consideration of his oral promise that the marriage would operate as a satisfaction of the note. The agreement having been completely executed by the marriage, the statute does not apply. 

Weld v Weld, (1905), 71 Kan.622,81 Pac.183, Costigan 1269

The statute requires a writing if the agreement is in any part executory. Marriage is not a part performance upon which a decree for specific performance of the rest of an executory agreement can be made. 

Hunt v Hunt, (1902), 171 N.Y.396,64 N.E.159,59 L.R.A.306 

Contra: Nowack v Berger, (1895), 133 Mo.24,34 SW 489,31 LRA 810 

A contract to marry is a purely personal contract which an executor cannot perform and which necessarily dies with the person so that no right of action for breach of promise to marry will survive against the executor except where special damage is alleged and proved. Since an oral agreement made in consideration of marriage is within the statute, therefore, if an oral agreement to marry is dependent thereon so that all the various promises constitute a single indivisible contract, the parties have

Chase v Fitz, (1882), 132 Mass. 359 

2. THE WRITING 

10 to

Debark 

1944 To satisfy the statute, the agreement or memorandum must, either in its own terms, or by reference to some other writing, express with reasonable certainty all the conditions and essential elements of the bargain. 

White v Bigelow, (1891), 154 Mass. 393, 29 N. E. 904 

Freeland v Ritz, (1891), 154 Mass. 257, 28 N. E. 226 

Smith v Munsel, (1920), 110 Atl. 12, Costigan 1343 

Frazer v Andrews, (1907), 134 Iowa 621,112 NW 92,11 LRANS 593 

EXAMPLE: A letter from a father to J. S., his prospective son-in-law, signifying his assent to the marriage of his daughter to J. S. and promising to give her 1500 pounds at the time of their marriage is a sufficient writing to satisfy the statute. 

Bird v Blosse, (1883), 2 Ventris 361, Costigan 1355

A fragment of a letter is not sufficient, at least in the absence of an allegation that the remainder has been lost, and of oral evidence setting out the contents of the entire letter or the substance of it. 

White v Bigelow, (1891), 134 ass. 593, 29 N. E. 904

Where the statute does not expressly require that an agent be authorized in writing to sign a binding memorandum, parol authority is sufficient, as in Massachusetts. 

Ulen v Kittredge, 1810), 7 Mass. 235

Woodworth v Franklin (1921)85 Okla.27,204 Pac.452,27 ALR 590 E. REAL PROPERTY AND INTERESTS THEREIN 1. IN GENERAL 

The language of the statute provides "No action shall be brought upon a contract for the sale of lands, tenements or hereditaments or of any interest in or concerning them" unless evidenced by a writing. The words "lands, tenements and hereditaments" signify real property. 

Rice v Woonsocket, (1920), 43 R. I. 305, 112 Atl. 175

A portion of the Massachusetts statute of frauds is contained in a separate chapter of the General Laws, entitled "Alienation of Land". 

G. L. c. 183

Cole v Hadley, (1894), 162 Mass. 579, 39 N. E. 279 

Drew v Wiswall, (1903), 193 Mass. 554, 67 N. E. 666 

Estabrook v Wilcox, (1917), 226 Mass. 156, 115 N. E. 233 

Sargent v Leonardi, (1916), 223 Mass. 556, 112 N. E. 633

An oral agreement, accompanying a lease of land, that a building to be erected by the tenant for his own use on the land will be paid for by the lessor at the end of the tenancy at its then value, is regarded as between the parties, not as a sale of an interest in land, though annexed thereto, but as a contract for work, labor and materials furnished and hence not within the statute. 

South Baltimore v uhlbach, 69 Md.395, 16 Atl.117,1 LRA 507 

b. BOUNDARY LINES 

As between adjoining land owners, a boundary line may be established by oral agreement and acquiescence. 

Tripp v Bagley, (Utah. 1928), 276 Fac. 912, 69 A.L.R. 1417 

Tietjen v Dobson, (Ga., 1930), 152 S.E. 222, 69 A.L.R. 1408 

Bemis v Bradley, (1927),126 Me.462,139 Atl.593,69 A.L.R.1399 

Waterman v Johnson, (1832), 13 Pick. 261

The reason for the rule is that the parties do not undertake to acquire and pass title to real estate. They simply, by agreement, fix and determine the situation and location of the thing which they already own; the purpose being, by something agreed on, to identify their several holdings and to make certain that which they regarded as uncertain. 

Standifer v Combs, (1919),184 Ky.708,212 S.W. 921, Costigan 1285

The presumption is that the line so established is the true line according to the original location of the lots. 

Sparhawk v Bullard, (1840), 1 fet. 95 

Liverpool Wharf v Prescott, (1863), 7 Allen 494 

Kellogg v Smith, (1851), 7 Cush. 375 

Stone v Clark, (1840), 1 Met. 378 

Beckman v Davidson, (1894), 162 Mass. 347, 39 N. E. 38 

Hooten v Comerford, (1890), 152 Mass. 591, 26 N. E. 407 

Teass v St. Albans, (1893),38 W.Va.1,17 S.E. 400,19 L.R.A.802 

Cf. Cornell v Jackson, (1845), 9 Met. 150

But in many states, including Massachusetts, such oral agreement is not binding unless followed by occupation according to the line adopted, adverse and under a claim of right, for twenty years or more, or until the fall of the bar of the statute of limitations. After such occupation and acquiescence, the oral agreement operates as an estoppel. 

Crawford v Roloson, (1926), 256 Mass. 331, 152 N. E. 319

A parol agreement to make a deed of land is within the statute. 

Dowling v McKenney, (1878), 124 Mass. 478 

Lavoie v Dube, (1918), 229 Mass. 87, 118 N. E. 179

Bruce v Meserve, (1917), 228 lass. 463, 117 N. E. 830 

Macurda v Fuller, (1916), 225 Mass. 341, 114 N. E. 366 

Downing v Brennan, (1919), 232 Mass. 535, 122 N. E. 729 

Cook v Doggett, (1861), 2 Allen 439

This includes an oral agreement to purchase land and then to convey it to another whether the purchaser is a mere agent for the other or not. 

McDonald v Conway, (1925), 254 Mass. 429, 150 N. E. 200 **Part performance will not take out of the statute a where parol agreement relating to an interest in land; "Only mase part is Payme Broney 

Kidder v Hunt, (1923), 1 Pick. 328 

Adams v Townsend, (1840), 1 Met. 483 

DeMontague v Bacharach, (1904),187 Mass. 128, 72 NE. 938 

White v McKnight, (1928), 146 S.C.59,143 S.E.552,59 ALR 1297 even though the whole purchase money is paid; 

Blodgett v Hildreth, (1870), 103 Mass. 484

Congdon v Fery, (1859), 13-Cray 3

A parol agreement to make a will of land is within the statute because a devisee comes within the legal definition of one who takes

Equitable as well as legal interests in land are embraced within the statute. 

Richards v Richards, (1857), 9 Gray 313 Dougherty

b. PAROL LEASES AND AGREEMENTS TO LEASE 

A parol lease or agreement to lease land is within the statute. 

G. L. c. 

G. L. c. 183, section 3 

Flanagan v Welch, (1914), 220 Mass. 183, 107 N.E. 979 

Mathews v Carlton, (1905), 189 Mass. 285, 75 N.E. 637 

Snider v Deban, (1925), 249 Mass. 59, 144 N. E. 69 

Podren v Macquarrie, (1919), 253 Mass. 127, 125 N. E. 335 

Emery v Boston Terminal Co., (1901),178 Mass.172,59 N.E. 763 

Chicago Co. v Davis, (1892), 142 111. 171,31 NE 438,15 LRA 754

C. OTHER AGREEMENTS 

An agreement to give or assign a mortgage of land is within the statute. 

Linsky v Exchange Trust, (1927), 260 less. 15, 156 N. E. 689

An option to rurchase land is not an agreement to pur- (chase or sell land and is not within the statute. 

Richanbach v Ruby, (1928), 127 Cr.512,271 Fac.600,61 ALR 1441

An agreement to pay taxes which are or may be assessed on land is not a contract for the sale of lands or of any in-

Collétival. 

means in addetore that is To something promised of

" terest therein and is not within the statute. 

Brackett v Evans, (1848), 1 Cush. 79 

Preble v Baldwin, (1850), 6 Cush.549, 15 L.R.A.(N.S.)1090,note

A parol agreement which is collateral to the written contract and on a distinct subject may be proved and is not within the statute. 

Durkin v Cobleigh, (1892), 156 ass. 108, 30 N. E. 474

Thus an oral agreement by the vendors of land requiring the land to be filled and that the vendors will pay for the filling is not within the statute. 

Page v Monks, (1855), 5 Gray 492 

Thus an oral agreement by a grantor that he will pay for building a sewer in the street is not within the statute. 

Carr v Dooley, (1876), 119 Mass. 294

It has always been held in this Commonwealth that, when all parts of the agreement which affect the obligation of either party to purchase or sell any interest in land have been fully performed, the remaining obligations are no longer within the statute although they may have been while the contract continued to be completely executory. 

Wetherbee v Potter, (1868), 99 Mass. 362

Thus a special promise to pay a pre-existing debt is not within the statute even though the origin of the debt was a sale of land because the contract sued on is not on consideration of the sale of lands or of any interest therein, but to pay a pre-existing debt. 

Dillingham v Runnels, (1808), 4 Mass. 399 

Nutting v Dickinson, (1864), 8 Allen 540 

Basford v Pearson, (1864), 9 Allen 387 

Wilkinson v Scott, (1821), 17 Mass. 249, 257

For other agreements not within the statute, see 

Peabody v Rice, (1873), 113 Mass. 31 

White v Maynard, (1872), 111 Mass. 250 

4. THE WRITING OR MEMORANDUM 

a.

IN GENERAL 

A memorandum, to satisfy the statute, must contain the terms of the agreement of which it is the memorandum. 

Sanjean v Miller, Kaufman v Lennox, (1924), 248 Mass. 288, 142 N. E. 799 (1928), 265 Mass. 487, 164 N. E. 450 

Oakman v Rogers, (1876), 120 Mass. 214

This means all the essential terms so that the court can ascertain the rights of the parties from the writing itself without resorting to oral testimony. 

Riley v Farnsworth, (1874), 116 Mass. 223

The substance of the contract must be expressed with reasonable certainty either by its own terms or by reference to some other deed, record or other matter from which it can be ascertained with like reasonable certainty. The statute.

- requires a note or memorandum of the contract, not a detail of all its particulars. 

Atwood v Cobb, (1834), 16 Pick. 227

The memorandum inay be written on separate papers provided they are all signed by the party to be charged or his agent or provided that such as are not signed are attached to or referred to in a signed paper. There must be some evidence that the papers were connected in the minds of the parties so that they adopted all the papers as indicating their purpose. 

Clark v Olejnik, (1921), 240 Mass. 215, 135 N. E. 197 

Nickerson v Weld, (1910), 204 Mass. 346, 90 N. E. 589 

Lee ▾ Butler, (1896), 167 Mass. 426, 46 N. E. 52

In general, the memorandum must identify the contracting parties. 

Irvmor Corp. v Rodeald (1930)253 NY 472,171 NE 747,70 ALR 192 

Lewis v Wood, (1891), 153 Mass.321,26 N.E.862,11 L.R.A. 142 

North Packing Co. v Lynch, (1907), 196 Mass. 204,81 N.E.891 

DesBrisay v Foss, (1928), 264 Mass. 102, 162 N. E. 4

But this is not necessary where the doctrine of undisclosed principal applies, that is, where the memorandum is signed by a person who is in fact an agent and is acting as such. Then the existence of the undisclosed principal may be proved by parol and he may sue or be sued on the contract. 

White v Dahlquist Co., (1901), 179 Mass. 427, 60 N. E. 791 

Williams v Bacon, (1854), 2 Gray 387 

Lerned v Johns, (1864), 9 Allen 419 

Gowen v Klous, (1869), 101 Mass. 449

The subject matter of the contract must be contained in the memorandum sufficient for purposes of identification when read in the light of all the circumstances of ownership of the property by the vendor. 

Bradley v Haven, (1911), 208 Mass. 300, 94 N. E. 268

But an indefinite reference is sufficient if the subject matter can be identified readily by oral evidence as the only subject matter which could satisfy the description. 

Mead v Parker, (1874), 115 Mass. 413 

Miller v Burt, (1907), 196 Mass. 395, 82 N. E. 39 

Desmarais v Taft, (1912), 210 ass. 560, 97 N. E. 96

EXAMPLE 1: A written memorandum disclosed an agreement for the sale of "a house and lot of land situated on Amity Street". There were several houses and lots on Amity Street but parol evidence is admissible to show that there was only one which the defendant had any right to convey and that the parties had been negotiating for its purchase and sale. 

Hurley v Brown, (1868), 98 Mass. 545 

Harrigan v Dodge, (1909), 200 Mass. 357, 86 N. E. 780 

Auerbach v Nelson, (1919), 2 Ch. 383, Costigan 1361

EXAMPLE 2: A written memorandum was made for the sale of "an estate on Congress Street owned by Sarah A. Hill," the memorandum being dated in Stoneham. Sarah A. Hill owned several estates on Congress Street in Stoneham. The memo-

randum is not sufficient. 

Doherty v Hill, (1887), 144 Mass.465,11 N.E.581,Cost. 1357

The memorandum need not be a formal document. Thus, a written offer, signed by the offerer or his agent, and containing all the terms of the offer, and accepted orally, is a sufficient memorandum to bind the offerer but this would not be true if the terms of the offer were altered by the oral acceptance. 

Wiessner v Ayer, (1900), 176 Mass. 425, 57 N. E. 672

The memorandum may be in the form of a letter; 

Walker v Walker, (1960), 175 Mass. 349, 56 N. E. 601 or in the form of a note or check given for the purchase price of land if it contains the essential terms of the contract. 

Little v Pearson, (1828), 7 Pick. 301 

Harper v Battle, (1920), 180 N.C.375,104 SE 658,20 A.L.R. 357

But a check or note which merely recites that it is given "as payment of land" is not a sufficient memorandum. 

Duteil v Mullins, (1921), 192 Ky. 616, 234 S. W. 192 

McKinnon v Mixon, (1901), 128 Ala. 612, 29 So. 690 b. both parties but agent.

THE SIGNING 

The memorandum need not be signed by only by the party to be charged or by his 

DesBrisay v Foss, (1928),264 Mass. 102, 109, 162 N. E. 4 

Lubin v Hedtler, (1925), 251 Lass.422, 146 N.E. 660 

Old Colony R.R. v Evans, (1856), 6 Gray 25 

Sanborn v Flagler, (1964), 9 Allen 474 

Pearlberg v Levisohn, (1920),182 N.Y.Supp.615, Costigan 1326 

Lezinsky v Hoffman, (1920),181 N.Y.Supp. 732, Costigan 1332 

Salmon Falls Co, v Goddard, (1852), 14 Howard 446, Cost. 1313 

Lee v Vaughn (1911)101 Ark.68,141 SW 496,37 LRANS 352,C.1335

The authority of an agent to convey land by deed must be in writing and by deed; for land can be conveyed only by deed and the power must be of as high dignity as the act to be performed under it. 

Johnson v Dodge, (1856), 17 Ill. 433, Costigan 1329

But the authority of an agent to sign an executory agreement to convey land need not itself be in writing but may be proved by parol. The latter is the common case of an auctioneer who is deemed the agent of both parties and whose memorandum, therefore, entered in his own book, is taken to be a memorandum in writing binding upon both parties because by them respectively authorized. 

Hawkins v Chace, (1837), 19 Pick. 502

The duration of an auctioneer's agency for the buyer is usually limited to the actual time of the auction. 'Gill v Bicknell, (1848), 2 Cush. 355,358 

Clark v Olijnik, (1921), 240 Mass. 215, 133 N. E. 197 

Kelly v Holbrook, (1906), 191 lass. 565, 77 N. E. 1037
