- An auctioneer is primarily the agent of the seller and, as to him, the auctioneer's authority is generally more extensive and may cover a time both before and after the sale. 

Frequently, he is expected to carry the transaction to the end and, in such cases, his authority extends beyond the auction sale and, unless it is sooner revoked, the auctioneer may bind the seller by a memorandum signed within a reasonable time after the sale. 

White v Dahlquist Co., (1901), 179 Mass. 427, 60 N. E. 791

But a memorandum by the seller's agent, broker or auctioneer, made after the seller has contracted personally to sell to another purchaser would be made too late if made after such agent had notice of the seller's own personal agreement of sale, even though the sale by the agent had been made first because the notice would terminate the agent's authority to make a memorandum of his sale. 

Giolotto v Dingolo, (1925), 251 Mass. 38, 146 N. E. 226 

Elliot v Barrett, (1886), 144 Mass. 25€, 10 N. E. 820

The general rule is that the memorandum may be signed at any time subsequent to the formation of the contract, at least before action is brought, except that, if signed by an agent, it must be signed while his agency continues. 

Lerned v Wannemacher, (1864), 9 Allen 412, 416 

Sanborn v Chamberlin, (1869), 101 Mass. 409, 41€

The memorandum need not be signed at the bottom, but if the party to be charged has inserted his name in any part of the paper in his own handwriting, it is sufficient. 

Hawkins v Chace, (1837), 19 Fick. 502 

Cabot v Haskins, (1825), 3 Fick. 83, 95

The signing by the defendant of his Christian name only is a sufficient signature. 

Walker v Walker, (1900), 175 Mass. 349, 56 N. E. 601 C.

THE CONSIDERATION 

The Massachusetts statute of frauds provides "The consideration of such promise, contract or agreement need not be set forth or expressed in the writing signed by the party to be charged therewith but may be proved by any legal evidence. ff 

G. L. c. 259, section 2 

Marcy v Marcy, (1864), 9 Allen 8, 10 

Fackard v Richardson, (1821), 17 Mass. 122 

Wetherbee v Potter, (1868), 99 Mass. 354, 3€2 

White v Dahlquist Co., (1901), 179 Mass. 427, 60 N. E. 791

Therefore, in this state, if the consideration furnished by the plaintiff for the promise of the defendant is inaccurately stated in the memorandum, this will not invalidate a memorandum which is otherwise sufficient and the defect may be remedied by parol. The only case in Massachusetts opposed to this conclusion is 

Grace v Denison, (1273), 114 Mass. 16 where the court does not mention this section of the statute and the inference is that it was overlooked. 

Hayes v Jackson, (1893), 159 Mass. 451,34 NE 683, Costigan 1351

Aproze pro CCIE Q.

37018 17 

BETTER D A TOVOU an SA avban 

CAD do& 

PO PER SPEC UFGETODE GATODOEL JO ALT

- This portion of the statute does not mean that a statement of the price of the land may be omitted from the memorandum in all cases. It means that the consideration for the promise sued on, the consideration furnished by the plaintiff, need not be contained in the memorandum. The defendant is sufficiently protected if all that he is to do is required to be in writing. The statute of frauds requiring a writing of all the promises of the defendant applies as much when the vendee is sued upon a contract for the sale of lands as when the vendor is sued on such a contract. 

Bogigian v Booklovers' Library, (1907), 193 Mass. 444, 79 NE769 

Howe v Walker, (1855), 4 Gray 318 

Morton v Dean, (1847) 13 Met. 385

On the other hand, if the writing is not a mere memorandum but a conplete written contract signed by all the parties thereto, parol evidence is not admissible to vary the consideration or any other of its essential terms. 

Pearlstein v Novitch, (1921), 239 Mass. 228, 131 N. E. 853

If one conveys land or other property to another pursuant to an oral agreement which the other party refuses to perform and cannot be compelled to perform because of the statute of frauds, the value of the property so conveyed can be recovered by the party conveying it. 

Massachusetts, Bar Examination, January, 1916, morning #9 

Cromwell v Norton, (1906), 193 Mass. 291, 79 N. E. 433 

Dixon v Lamson, (1922), 242 Mass. 129, 137, 136 N. E. 346 

Peabody v Fellows, (1900), 177 Mass. 290, 293, 58 N. E. 1019 

Miller v Roberts, (1897) 169 Mass. 134, 145, 47 N. E. 585 

Kelley v Thompson, (1902), 181 Mass. 122, 63 N. E. 332 

O'Grady v O'Grady, (1894), 162 Mass. 290, 38 N. E. 196 

Holbrook v Clapp, (1895), 165 Mess. 563 

165 Mass. 563, 43 N. E. 508 

Townsend v Townsend, (1843), 6 Met. 319 

Bacon v Parker, (1884), 137 Mass. 309

Recovery is allowed here, not as an indirect way of enforcing the contract, but on the ground of failure of consideration. If the defendant is ready and willing to perform, the fact that the contract is within the statute and that he could set it up if he chose to is immaterial. 

Twomey v Crowley, (1884), 137 Mss. 184

So, where a vendee enters upon land under a parol agreement to purchase which is not performed by reason of the vendor's refusal to convey, the vendee is entitled to recover the value of the improvements made by him on the premises while in possession under such parol agreement to the extent that they have enhanced the value of the land.

Carter v Carter, (N. C. 1921), 108 S. E. 765, 17 A. L. R. 945 

See McCullough v Finley, (1904), 69 Kan. 705, 77 Pac.696, Cost. 1276 It is not enough if the court can decide upon inspection of the writing that the consideration is sufficient in law; it is a question for the jury whether that consideration has been in fact performed or received.

Lent v Padelford, (1813), 10 Mass. 230

F. AGREEMENTS NOT TO BE PERFORMED WITHIN YEAR 1. TEXT AND PURCHASE OF STATUTE The statute provides: "No action shall be brought upon an agreement that is not to be performed within one year from the making thereof" unless evidenced in writing as therein provided.

The mischief intended to be guarded against is leaving the prof of the contract which is to rur beyond a year dependent on the memory and truthfulness o1 witnesses. 

Biest v Ver Steeg Co., (1902), 97 Mɔ. App. 137, 70 S.W. 1081

CONSTRUCTION OF STATUTE 

In determining when contracts come within this clause, courts have been governed by the words "not to be performed". They have treated these as negative words. There must be a negation of the right to perform a contract within a year to bring it within this clause. 

Arkansas R.R. v Whitley (1891) 54 Ark.199,15 SW 455,11 LRA 621

Therefore, the statute has no reference to promises which probably will not be performed within a year, however strong the probability may be.

Lapham v Whipple, (1844), 8 Met. 59 3. APPLICATION TO PARTICULAR AGREEMENTS a. AGREEMENTS DEFINITELY FIXING TIME (1) MORE THAN YEAR 

An agreement to do a definite thing for a definite term over one year is within the statute. 

Hill v Hooper, (1854), 1 Gray 131 

Cabot v Haskins (1825), 3 Pick.83 

Marcy v Marcy, (1864), 9 Allen 8 

Frary v Sterling, (1868), 99 Mass. 461 

De Montague v Bacharach, (1901), 181 Mass. 256, 63 N. E. 435

Thus, a contract to support another person for thirteen months is within the statute. 

Eiseman v Schneider, (1897), 60 N. J. L. 291, 37 Atl. 623 

(a) ONE YEAR TO BEGIN IN FUTURO 

A contract for a year's service, to be entered upon in the future, even the next day, must be evidenced by a writing. 

Dykema v Piano Co. (1922) 220 Mich.600, 190 NW 638,27 ALR 660 

Raymond v Phipps, (1913), 215 Mass. 559, 102 N.E. 905 

EXAMPLE 1: P leased premises to D in writing for one year. Before the lease expired, the parties orally agreed to renew the lease for another year on the same terms. The agreement is within the statute. 

Delano v Montague, (1849), 4 Cush. 42

EXAMPLE 2: A contract entered into about the middle of a certain month to serve as superintendent of a company for

- one year from the first day of that month is not within the statute. 

Franklin Co. v Taylor, (1887), 37 Kan. 435, 15 Pac, 586

A period of one year "from" a certain day excludes the day named when there is nothing to show the contrary, and the agreement is within the statute. 

Metropolitan Co. v Boutell, (1904), 185 Mass. 391, 70. NE 421

A contract for one year, including its date, is not within the statute. 

Sanborn v Fireman's Ins. Co., (1860), 16 Gray 448

If no time is stipulated for the beginning of a year's services, and there is nothing in the agreement to forbid its immediate commencement, the contract has been held not within the statute. 

Dykema v Piano Co. (1922), 220 Mich.600,190 NW 538,27 ALR 660 

Chase v Hinckley, (1905), 126 Wis. 75,105 NW 230,2 LRA (NS) 738 

(b) POSSIBILITY OF TERMINATION 

The statute looks to the performance and not to the defeat of the contract. In order that a contract shall not come within the statute, it must be such that it is capable of being performed fully within a year according to its terms and not merely terminated. Thus, if the death of the promisor within the year would merely prevent full performace of the agreement, it is within the statute. 

Washington Packer Co. v Sickles, (1865) 5 Wallace 580 

EXAMPLE: An agreement to employ a 15 year old boy until he was 21 and to pay to his father certain sums at stated times during that period is within the statute. Although by the death of the boy within a year, the services which were the consideration for the promise would, cease and the obligation on the promise to pay would be terminated, the contract would not have been performed in accordance with its terms. 

Hill v Hooper, (1854), 1 Gray 131

AGREEMENTS NOT DEFINITELY FIXING TIME (1) POSSIBILITY OF PERFORMANCE WITHIN YEAR (a) IN GERERAL 

An agreement to do a definite thing for an indefinite time is not within the statute. 

Carnig v Carr, 167 Mass. 544, 46 NE 117,35 LRA 512, Costigan 214 

Valley Co. v Wise, (1909), 93 Ark.1, 123 SW 768,26 LRA(NS) 403 

Scribner v Flagg Co (1900), 175 Mass. 536, 56 N.E. 603 

Elwell v State Co., (1918), 230 Mass. 248, 119 N. E. 794 

Drew v Wiswall, (1903), 183 Mass. 554, 67 N. E. 666 

Somerby v Buntin, (1875), 118 Mass. 279 

Kent v Kent, (1836), 18 Piok. 569

Thus, a contract to support another person for life is not within the statute. 

Eiseman v Schneider, (1897), 60 N. J. L. 291, 37 Atl. 623

(b) PERFORMANCE DEPENDENT ON CONTINGENCY (1) IN GENERAL 

No agreement is within this clause of the statute which is capable of being performed within a year or is of such a character that, by the happening of any contingency, it may be performed within a year.

Blake v Cole, (1839), 22 Pick. 97 

EXAMPLE: D, a real estate broker, in consideration of certain information given to him by P, another broker, orally promised that if D earned a commission by reason of such information, he would pay one-half of it to P. The agreement is not within the statute. 

Collins v Snow, (1914), 218 Mass. 542, 106 N. E. 148

The statute does not apply to agreements which may be performed within a year although they may, by possibility, extend beyond that period. If, by any possibility, the agreement is capable of being performed within a year, it is not within the statute, though it is probable that it will extend over a longer period, and though it does so in fact extend. Worthy 

Jones, (1858), 11 Gray 168

When it does not appear that a contract cannot, in any event, be performed within a year, then it is not within the statute, and need not be evidenced in writing. 

Warner v Texas Ry., (1896) 164 U. S. 418 

Rowland v Hacker. (1922), 243 Mass. 160; 137 N. E. 265 

Bolton v Van Heusen, (1924), 249 Mass. 503;144 N. E. 384 

Drew v Wiswall, (1903), 183 Mass. 554, 67 N. E. 666

EXAMPLE: An agreement by X to give to P one-half his property for her support in addition to agreed weekly payments, if she would take care of him so long as he needed her, is not within the statute if it is not to be executed by will. 

Dixon v Lamson, (1922), 242 Mass. 129, 136 N. E. 346

On the other hand, where there is no contingency upon the happening of which the agreement may be performed within a year, the agreement is within the statute. 

Williams v Pittsfield Co., (1927), 258 Mass. 65, 154 N. E. 572 

Freeman v Foss, (1887), 145 Mass. 361, 14 N. E. 141 

Frary v Sterling, (1868), 99 Mass. 461 

Lyons v King, (1846), 11 Met. 411

Thus a promise to marry after the lapse of one year is within the statute. 

Derby v Phelps, (1822), 2 N. H. 515, Costigan 1296

The contingency may be either express or implied. 

Trowbridge v Wetherbee, (1865), 11 Allen 361 

Somerby v Buntin, (1875), 118 Mass. 279

Lapham v Whipple, (1844), 8 Met. 59

CONTINGENCIES NOT WITHIN STATUTE 

EXAMPLE 1: A promise to pay a sum certain on the day of 

P's marriage. 

Blake v Cole, (1839), 22 Pick. 97 

EXAMPLE 2: A promise to marry within three years. 

Lewis v Tapman, (1900), 90 Ma. 294, 45 Atl. 459,47 LRA 385 

EXAMPLE 3: A promise to pay for services in selling property, payment to be made when the property shall be sold and paid for.

Seddon Rosenbaum, (Va., 1889) 3 L. R. A. 337 

Bartlett v Mystic River Corp., (1890), 151 Mass.433,24 N.E.780

EXAMPLE 4: A promise to support a husband and wife during their lives. 

Lavoie v Dube, (1917), 229 Mass. 87 118 N. E. 179 

Lyman v Lyman, (1882), 153 Mass. 414 

EXAMPLE 5: A promise by X as agent of D Company that the company would employ P for five years or so long as X should continue as agent of D Company. 

Roberts v Rockbotton Co., (1843), 7 Met. 46 

EXAMPLE 6: A promise by a Railroad to keep and maintain cattle-guards on each side of P's land so long as the railroad is operated over his land. 

Arkansas Ry.vWhitley, (1891), 54 Ark.199,15 SW 455,11 LRA 021

EXAMPLE 7: A promise by D to provide for P's support at 

D's death. 

Thomas v Armstrong, (1889), 86 Va. 323,10 S.E. 6,5 L.R.A. 529

EXAMPLE 8: A promise that D might cut certain trees on 

D's land at any time within ten years. 

Kent v Kent, (1836), 18 Pick. 569

EXAMPLE 9: A promise by D to pay a commission to P on all sales made for D by X 

Scribner v Flagg Co., (1900), 175 Mass. 536, 56 N.E. 603 

E. D. Hewins, Inc. v Marlboro Mills, 242 Moss.282,136 NE 159 

EXAMPLE 10: A promise to provide for the support and education of a boy of 14 until he reaches his majority. 

Wooldridge v Stern, (1890), 42 Fed. 311, 9 L.R.A. 129 

EXAMPLE 11: A promise by D to P to employ and support P's 

12 year old daughter until she is 18. 

Peters v Westborough, (1837), 19 Pick. 364 

EXAMPLE 12: A promise by D to P to give to P "permanent" employment at stipulated wages if P would give up his own business as an enameller and enter D's service in the same

Carnig v Carr. 167 Mass. 544. 46 I.E.117, 35 LRA 512, Cost. 214 

See Camion v B. & II. R. R., (1930). 269 Mess. 579, 169 N.E. 499 

(II) PERFORMANCE ON DEATH 

In general, an agreement which will be completely performed according to its terms if either party should die within the year is not within the statute. 

Doyle v Dixon, (1867), 97 Mss. 208, Costigan 1287

An oral contract to make a will is, by possibility, capable of being performed within a year and, where it related solely to personal property, was not originally within the statute of frauds. 

Wellington v Apthorp, (1887), 145 Mass. 69, 13 N. E. 10

But this rule is changed by a later statute which requires that an agreement to make a will must be in writing, even as to personal property, and that a mere memorandum is not sufficient. 

G. L. c. 259, section 5 

Sughrue v Barlow, (1919), 233 less. 468, 124 N. E. 285 

Young v Young, (1925), 251 Mass. 218, 146 N. E. 574 

Read v McKeague, (1925), 252 Mes. 162, 147 N. E. 585 

Emery v Burbank, (1895). 163 Mass. 326, 39 N. E. 1026 

Krell v Codman. (1390), 154 Mass. 454, 28 N. 3. 578 

Runyan v Van Iderstine, (1918), 230 Mass. 428, 124 N. E. 285 

(III) PERFORMANCE BY ONE PARTY 

In some states the statute applies only to contracts which are not to be performed by either party within a year; and therefore where the contract has been performed completely on one side within a year, it is not within the statute. 

Arkansas Ry. v Whitley(1891)54 Ark. 199, 15 SW 465. 11 LRA 621

In other states, including Massachusetts, it is held that ii the agreement is not to be performed by one party within a year, it is within the statute even though the other party has fully performed and has done so within the year. These states maintain that part performance is not performance within the meaning and intention of the statute and that an agreement which cannot be performed fully according to its terms within a year is within the statute even if the act or promise which is the consideration for it may be performed within the year or has been actually performed. 

Frary v Sterling, (1868). 99 Mass. 461 

Boydell v Drummond. 11 ast 142

EXALPLE 1: D, in consideration of a certain farm being immediately conveyed to him by P's father, promised to pay 

500 to P on his reaching 21, three years later. The promise is within the statute. 

Marcy v Marcy, (1864), 9 Allen 8

EXAMPLE 2: P gave a bill of sale of his milk route to I 

for a condideration of $600, payable $100 in cash and $500 by a note payable in t years. It was agreed as part of the contract that all : k bought thereafter by P from D should be credited to D on the note at the time of its maturity. The agreement is within the statute though partly performed within a year. 

Kelley v Thompson, (1899), 175 Mass. 427, 56 N. E. 713

AGREEMENTS INVOLVING PARTICULAR SUBJECT MATTER (1) AGREEMENTS TO REFRAIN FROM DOING SOMETHING 

An agreement not to do a definite thing for a definite term of over one year is not within, the statute. Such a contract is performed fully if the promisor abides by its terms so long as he is capable of doing or refraining. 

Somerby v Buntin, (1875), 118 Mass. 279 Thus, an agreement not hereafter to carry on a particular business at a particular place is not within the statute because, being only a personal engagement to forbear doing certain acts, not stipulating for anything beyond the promisor's life, and imposing no duties on his legal representatives, it would be performed fully if he died within a year. 

Worthy v Jores, (1858), 11 Cray 168

For, whether a man agrees not to do a thing for his life, or never to do it, or only not to do as for a certain number of years, it is in her form an agreement by which he does not promise that anything will be done after his death and the performance of which is therefore completed with his life. 

Doyle v Dixon, (1867), 97 Mass. 208, Costigan 1287 not 

EXAMPLE 1: A promise "not hereafter to engage in the staging or livery stable business in Southbridge" is within the statute. 

Lyon v King, (1846), 11 Met. 411 

EXAMPLE 2: A promise "not to engage in the grocery business in Chicopee for five years" is not within the statute. 

Doyle v Dixon, (1867), 97 Mass. 208, Costigan 1287 

EXAMPLE 3: A promise to give a bill of sale of a flour mill business and not to engage in the same business again in the same town, there being no agreement for a transfer of an interest in land, is not within the statute. 

Worthy v Jones, (1858), (1858), 11 Gray 168 

(2) OTHER AGREEMENTS 

The power to make completed oral contracts of insurance, as well as agreements to insure, is sustained by practically all the cases in the absence of charter

Sanford v Orient Ins. Co., (1899), 174 Mass.416,54 N.E. 883

An agreement to execute a written contract for advertising rights which is not to be performed within a year is within the statute. 

Union Car v Boston El. Ry., (1928), 26 F. (2nd.) 755, 58 ALR 1007 

d. RIGHTS AFTER PART PERFORMANCE BY ONE PARTY, THE OTHER REFUSING TO PERFORM 

After part performance by one party and repudiation by the other of an oral agreement which is unenforceable by reason of the statute of frauds, the first party has lost the privilege which he has paid for. The consideration upon which he made the payment or performance of labor or services or delivered the property has failed by the wilful act of the other party and he is entitled to recover the value of that which he has so paid, rendered or delivered. 

Williams v Bemis, (1871), 108 Mass. 91 

Basford v Pearson, (1864), 9 Allen 387

An action would lie for any article delivered or for payment for labor or services rendered upon such an agreement and under such circumstances. 

Sherburne v Fuller, (1809), 5 Mass. 133 

He may recover in quantum meruit or quantum valebant; 

Hill v Hooper, (1854), 1 Gray 131 

King v Welcome, (1855 5 Gray 41 

Freeman v Foss, (1887), 145 Mass. 361, 14 N. E. 141 or, if he had paid money to the other party, an action for money had and received will lie. 

Cook v Doggett, (1861), 2 Allen 439 

Kidder v Hunt, (1823), 1 Pick, 328 

De Montague v Jacharach, (1901), 181 Mass. 256, 63 N.E. 453

VIII. LEGALITY OF OBJECT WHAT AGREEMENTS ARE ILLEGAL 1. IN GENERAL 

Illegal contracts are usually void. If the act to be done does not amount to a crime at common law, a contract is not illegal unless the act is a civil wrong, or is prohibited by statute, or is contrary to public policy. 

13 C. J. 411

Illegality of contract, unless of such a nature as to shock the conscience, or to be inherently wrong, or unless patent upon the plaintiff's own case, usually must be pleaded. 

Nowell v Equitable Trust Co., (1924), 249 Mass. 585,144 N.E 749 

Reuter v Ballard, (1929), 267 Mass. 557, 166 N. E. 822

2. AGREEMENTS IN VIOLATION OF POSITIVE LAV IN GENERAL 

Ordinarily, no action will lie upon a contract made in violation of the common law or of a statute. 

Wheeler v Russell, (1821), 17 Mass. 258, 280 

Prescott v Battersby, (1876), 119 Mass. 285 

Smith v Arnold, (1871), 106 Mess. 269 

Sawyer v. Smith, (1872), 109 Mass. 220 

Libby v Downey, (1862), 5 Allen 299

AGREEMENTS IN VIOLATION OF COMMON LAW (1) WAGERING AND GAMING AGREEMENTS 

Wagering contracts are void in most states. They are void by the common law of Massachusetts and are also illegal as against public policy. 

Harvey v Merrill, (1889), 150 Mass. 1, 22 N. E. 49 

Haller v Bank, (1928) 263 Mass. 37, 160 N. E. 324 

Ferguson v Coleman, (1846), 3 Rich. L. (S.C.)99, Costigan 1131 

Gaming or gambling contracts are also illegal. 

D'Orio v Startup Co., (Utah, 1928), 266 Pac. 1037, 60 A.L.R.338

A prize offered to the winner of a race is not a wager, There is no and may be the subject of a valid agreement. wager by the parties, to be lost by one and taken by the other, but only the property of a third party offered for their competition. 

Wilkinson v Stitt, (1899), 175 Mass. 581,56 N.E. 830 

People v Fallon, (1897), 152 N.Y.12,46 N.E.296,37 L.R.A 227 

Hankins v Ottinger, (1896), 115 Cal.454,47 Pac.254,40 L.R.A 76

At common law, if the agreement is executory, either party to the wager may withdraw from it and recall the money deposited in the hands of the stakeholder. If the latter pays over the money to the winner after notice not to do so, he is personally responsible to the other party to the wager who has notified him not to make such payment. 

Bernard v Taylor, (1893) 23 Or.416,31 Pac.968,18 L.R.A.859 

Collamer v Day, (1829), 2 Vt. 144, Costigan 1120 

Love v Harvey, (1873), 114 Mass. 80

At common law, if the agreement is executed, and the stakeholder has paid the wager to the winner with the consent of the loser. are in/pari delicto. sharing over it because the pat 

Fisher Hildreth, (1875), 17 Mass. 558 Equally 

7 Jult. 

By statute, in Massachusetts, if the wagering contract is executed, the loser may recover the money or value of the goods wagered from the winner in contract; and, if he does not, within three months after such loss, payment or delivery, without covin or collusion, prosecute such action with effect, any other person may sue for and recover in tort treble the value thereof. 

G. L. o. 137, section 1

138A contract by an heir to convey, on the death of his ancestor, leaving the heir surviving, a certain undivided part of what shall come to the heir by descent, distribution or devise, is a fraud on the ancestor and illegal and void. 

Boynton v Hubbard, (1810), 7 Mass. 112

A secret agreement by a bankrupt to pay one creditor in full is fraudulent and illegal; 

Blasdel v Fowle, (1876), 120 Mass. 447 

Holcomb v Weaver, (1884), 136 Mass. 265 and also an agreement which amounts to a fraud upon the other subscribers to the contract. 

Nickerson v English, (1886), 142 Mass. 267, 8 N.E. 45 

Dole v Wooldredge, (1886), 142 Mass. 161, 7 N.E. 832 

It is

In this Commonwealth, a transfer of either real or personal property, made with a view to defraud the creditors of the grantor, in which fraudulent intent the grantee has participated, is illegal, but voidable and not void. good between the parties and voidable by the creditors at their election. If no creditors intervene, the conveyance stands; if creditors elect to affirm the transfer and receive the consideration, it is ratified and confirmed. Payment of the grantor's debts to the full value of the property purges the fraud. This doctrine applies both to executed and executory contracts. 

Harvey v Varney, (1867), 98 Mass. 118 

Traders' Bank v Steere, (1895), 165 Mess. 389, 53 N. E. 187 

(3) FIDUCIARY AND CONFIDENTIAL AGREEMENTS 

Any agreement having a direct tendency to induce a person to commit a breach of trust or confidence is illegal. 

Guernsey v Cook, (1876), 120 Mass. 501 

Cole v Boardman, (1877), 123 Mass. 548 

Allen v Hawks, (1832), 13 Pick. 9 

EXAMPLE.1:A broker, acting for both parties in effecting an exchange of property, can recover compensation from neither unless his double employment is known and assented to by both. 

Rice v Wood, (1873), 113 lass. 133 

EXAMPLE 2: An agent cannot, without the consent of his principal, become the purchaser for his own use and benefit of property which was entrusted to him to sell. 

Smith v Townsend, (1872), 109 Mass. 500 

EXAMPLE 5: X requested P to recommend to him a builder "that you can indorse in every way responsible and reliable who can erect a building for me cheaper" than certain other builders. P recommended D who orally promised to pay to Pa sum of money "for his trouble". D was employed and paid by 

X. P cannot recover from D on his promise. 

Holcomb v Weaver, (1883), 136 Mass. 265

(4) AGREEMENTS BY DIRECTORS AND STOCK- HOLDERS 

It is the duty of a director or stockholder of a corporation, in attondance at meetings of stockholders, to act fairly and in good faith. He is not justified in entering into any agreement so as to perpetrate a fraud upon another stockholder. 

Guernsey v Cook, (1876), 120 Mass. 501 

Woodruff v Wentworth, (1882), 133 lass. 309 

Van Slyke v Andrews, (Minn., 1920), 178 N. W. 959,12 ALR 1068 

Distinguish Almy v Orne, (1895), 165 Mass. 126, 42 N. E. 561 

EXAMPLE 1: If a stockholder in a corporation, all the shares of which are held by himself and two other persons, is the maker of a promissory note held by one of the other stockholders and, in consideration of a promise of such other stockholder to cancel the note, agrees to vote with such other stockholder to dispose of all the assets of the corporation without the consent of the third stockholder, the agreement is illegal and void and its performance is no defense to an action on the note. 

Palmbaum v Mogulsky, (1914), 217 Mass. 306, 104 N. E. 706

EXAMPLE 2: An agreement between certain stockholders to keep one of them in control so long as he did his best for the corporation is illegal for, although he might do his best, he might not be the best man for the position. 

Hellier v Achorn, (1926), 255 Mass.273,151 N.E.305,45 ALR 788 

(5) MAINTENANCE AND CHAMPERTY Maintenance is defined by Blackstone as "an officious intermeddling in a suit that in no way belongs to one, by maintaining or assisting either party with money or otherwise to prosecute or defend it".

Maintenance is illegal and against public policy, though many exceptions to the rule are recognized. Thus, it is not regarded as illegal if one person assist another because of near relationship, or if he himself has either a direct or in-

- direct interest in the subject matter, absolute. or contingent, or has reasonable ground to believe that he has such interest, although in fact he has none. 

Lathrop v Amherst Bank, (1845), 9 Met. 489 

Reed v Chase, (1921), 238 Mass. 83, 130 N. E. 257

Champerty is branch of maintenance. It is the unlawful maintenance of a suit in consideration of some bargain to have part of the thing in dispute or some profit out of it. 

Most of the cases of champertous agreements exist between lawyers and clients. 

Williams v Fowle, (1882), 132 Mass. 385 

Miller v Anderson, (1924), 185 Wis.163,196 N.W.869,34 ALR 1529

All maintenance was strictly prohibited by the early common law as having a manifest tendency to oppression, by encouraging and assisting persons to persist in suits which otherwise perhaps they would not venture upon. 

Thurston v Percival, (1823), 1 Pick. 415 

Smith v Weeks, (1925), 252 Mass. 244, 47 N. E. 676

In some states the expense of the litigation must be borne by the stranger but in Massachusetts, maintenance and champerty are deemed to be illegal, not for this reason, but because of the evils resulting from the officious intermeddling, and maintenance may exist in this state whether the litigation is upheld either by the money or the personal services of another. 

Lathrop v Amherst Bank, (1845), 9 let. 489 

Ackert v Barker, (1881), 131 Mass. 436 

Geer v Frank, (1899),179 111.570,55 NE 965,45 LRA 110, Cost.1133

It is of the essence of the offense that there should be a suit or antagonistic proceeding contemplated between the parties in which assistance is to be rendered in the litigation. 

Joy v letcalf, (1894), 161 Mass. 514, 37 N. E. 671 

Manning v Sprague, (1888),148 Mass.18,18 N.E.673,1 L.R.A.516

Thus, if an agreement looks solely to a compromise and termination of a controversy in which an attorney is employed to purchase property and is to be paid for his services out of the profits of the purchase, the agreement is not champertous. 

It is also essential that there should be an agreement for a sharing in the fruits of the litigation, in order for the contract to be champertous; 

Holdsworth v Healey, (1924), 249 Mass. 436, 144 N. E. 386 

Smith v Hartsell, (1908),150 N.C.71,63 SE 172,22 LRA (NS) 203 and also that such agreement for sharing shall be the only source of the plaintiff's compensation. 

Hadlock v Brooks, (1900), 178 Mass. 425, 59 N. E. 1009

EXAMPLE 1: An agreement, by which an attorney shall receive for his services 10% of whatever sum shall be recovered

- in a pending suit, is champertous.

Thurston v Percival, (1823), 1 Pick. 415 EXAMPLE 2: A written agreement by creditors, to employ a person to collect their claims for a compensation of one-half the amount collected by him, is champertous if it contemplates legal proceedings, although they are not mentioned in the agreement.

Lancy v Havender, (1888), 146 Mass. 615, 16 N. E. 464 EXAMPLE 3: An agreement that an attorney shall receive 

"very large and liberal fees" in event of success in a suit, for which he would have a right of action against the client personally, is lawful, and is inconsistent with a champertous agreement. 

Blaisdell v Ahern, (1887), 144 Mass. 393, 11 N. E. 681

Thus, it is lawful to agree that the attorney's fee shall be equal to a certain percentage of the sum recovered, since it is merely the amount of his compensation which is made dependent upon the sum to be recovered and the client is made personally liable to pay it. The fact that an attorney has a right to deduct his fee from the proceeds of the suit, before paying anything to his client, is immaterial. 

Taylor v Rosenberg, (1914), 219 Mass. 113, 106 N. E. 603

An agreement that an attorney undertaking to prosecute a suit shall have a share of the thing recovered is not, for that reason alone, champertous. The bargain, to be illegal, must have the further element that the attorney's services shall not constitute a debt due from the client and that his prospective share is to be the only compensation which the attorney will receive. If, in effect, he agreed to look for his compensation to that alone which might be recovered, and thus to make his pay depend upon his success, the bargain is champertous and void. But where the right to compensation is not confined to an interest in the thing recovered, but gives a right of action against the party, though pledging the avails of the suit or a part of them as security for payment, the agreement is not champertous. 

Hadlock v Brooks, (1900), 178 Mass. 425, 59 N. E. 1009 (1903), 184 Mass. 571, 69 N. E. 343 (1912), 213 Mass. 205, 99 N. E. 1095 (1916), 224 Mass. 159, 112 N. E. 629 (1914), 217 Mass. 9, 104 N. E. 468 

Gargano v Pope,

Delval v Gagnon, 

Bennett v Tighe,

Millen v Coakley, EXAMPLE: An agreement that an attorney shall have the first fifty dollars collected by him in enforcing a mechanic's lien is not champertous, where it does not appear to be anything more than a mode adopted for giving the attorney a lien on a portion of the debt claimed to be due, for his services and disbursements. 

Scott v Harmon, (1872), 109 Mass. 237

A band $10

It is competent for the client to say "I have no money and I cannot pay you until I get a verdict". If he stops there, if he simply makes it a question of the time of payment, it is perfectly competent. It is competent for the client to say, and for the lawyer to agree to it: "I will pay you, if you are successful, a quarter of what you recover", provided they do not add the further condition, "Nothing if you do not recover". In other words, if they make the payment of any fee at all contingent on the success of the suit, that is a champertous contract, but any other kind of a contract they may make. A contract between attorney and client, providing for the payment of a fee for legal services contingent upon the results obtained by the attorney, without more, is not illegal, but is enforceable. 

Hadlock v Brooks, (1900), 178 Mass. 425, 59 N. E. 1009

But where a matter of public policy is also involved, such as the continuance of the marriage relation, an attorney's fee made contingent upon his obtaining a divorce for his client has been held illegal and void. 

Re Sylvester, (1923), 195 Iowa 1529,192 N.W.442,30 ALR 180

Champerty is a personal defense, which is available only to the parties to the agreement or to their personal representatives, and to no others. 

Small v R. ., (1881), 55 Iowa 522, e N. W. 437 C.

AGREEMENTS IN VIOLATION OF STATUTES (1) IN GENERAL 

If a statute expressly prohibits the doing of an act, and imposes a penalty for doing it, a contract made in violation thereof is both illegal and void. 

Somers v Finance Corp., (1923), 245 Mass. 286, 139 N. E. 837 

Coughlin v Royal Indemnity Co., (1923), 244 Mass.317,138 NE 395 

Nourse v Pope, (1866), 13 Allen 87 

EXAMPLE: No action will lie on a promissory note, the consideration for which was a sale of shingles, not of the size prescribed by statute. 

Wheeler v Russell, (1821), 17 Mass. 253 

If a statute is enacted under the police power of the legislature, for the protection of a buyer of goods, which imposes a penalty for the doing of a certain act, without in express terms prohibiting the act, a contract made in violation of the statute is both illegal and void, because a penalty contained in a police power statute implies a prohibition. 

Stewartson v Lothrop, (1858), 12 Gray 52 

Huey v Passarelli, (1929), 267 Mass. 578, 166 N. E. 727 

Griffith v wells, (1846), 3 Denio (N.Y.) 226, Costigan 1203 

Bisbee v McAllen, (1888), 39 Minn. 143,39 N..299, Cost. 1205 

Randall v Tuell, 89 Me.443,36 Atl.910,38 LRA 143, Cost. 1202 

Libby v Downey, (1832), 5 Allen 299 

Distinguish Whitcomb v Boston Dairy, 218 lass.24,105 N.E.554

-EXAMPLE: A state statute required milk sold in cans to be sealed by the state sealer at a certain rate and imposed a penalty for violation. P sold milk in unsealed cans to D because the state sealer refused to seal them at the statute rate. The sale is impliedly prohibited and the contract is void. 

Miller v Post, (1861), 1 Allen 434

Statutes are to be read as a whole to ascertain the legislative purpose ană, unless it is necessary to hold the contract void in order to accomplish that purpose, the inference is that the statute was not intended to be prohibitory of the contract. 

Barriere v Depatie, (1914), 219 Mass. 33, 106 N. E. 572 

Larned v Andrews, (1871), 106 Mass. 435 

Frescott v Battersby, (1876), 119 Mass. 285 

Sawyer v Smith, (1872), 109 Mass. 220 

Faneuil Hall Bank v Brighton Bank, (1860), 16 Gray 534

A statute forbidding an act to be done, or enjoining the mode of doing it, may be prohibitory so as to make any contract made in violation of it void, or it may be directory only so that it does not invalidate a contract made in violation thereof. 

Bowditch v Insurance Co., (1886), 141 Mass. 292, 4 N. E. 798 

Springfield Bank v Merrick, (1817), 14 Mass. 322 

(2) SUNDAY AGREEMENTS 

In general, contracts made on the Lord's day are illegal, and no action can be based on such a contract either in law or equity, either to enforce its obligations or to secure its fruits. They

But such contracts are not altogether inoperative. may be executed by the parties and then the same principle, which leads courts to refuse to act when called upon to enforce them, will prevent the court from acting to relieve either party from the consequences of the illegal transaction. 

This may indirectly give effect to the executed illegal contract. The purpose of the rule, however, is not to give validity to the transaction but to deprive the parties of all right to have either enforcement or relief from their illegal contracts. In such cases, the defense of illegality prevails, not as a protection to the defendant, but as a disability to the plaintiff.

Upon this principle, possession acquired from an illegal transaction, or by a contract fully executed, will often avail the party holding it as a sufficient title. The transaction takes effect from the disability of the parties to assert any right to the contrary. The court does not give it effect but simply refuses its aid to undo what the parties have already done. 

Myers v Meinrath, (1869), 101 Mass. 366

Thus, a bona fide purchaser of property from one who bought on Sunday will get a sufficient title which cannot be impeached. 

Mann v United Motor Co., (1917),226 Mass.495, 116 N. E. 239

Sunday contracts were valid at early common law but are generally made invalid by statute. 

G. L. c. 136 

Pattee v Greely, (1847), 13 Met. 284 

Aldrich v Blackstone, (1880), 128 Mass. 148 

Com. v White, (1905), 190 Mass. 578, 77 N. E. 636 

Stone v Graves, (1887), 145 Mass. 353, 13 N. E. 906 

Jones v Brantley, (iss., 1920), 83 So. 802, 8 A.L.R. 1353 

Stewart v Thayer, 168 Mass. 519, 170 Mass. 560

Works of necessity and charity are expressly excepted from the prohibition of the statute. 835 

Davis v Somerville, (1880), 128 Mass. 594 

Com. v McCarthy, (1923), 244 Mass. 484, 138 N. E. 

Rhodes v Hope, (Ark., 1926), 286 S. W. 877, 47 A. 

Charleston Co. v Poulnot, 143 S.C.283, 141 SE 454, 60 ALR 750 

L. R. 1104

If chattels have been sold and delivered on the Lord's day, without payment of the price, the seller cannot recover either the price or the value of the goods; 

Cranson v Goss, (1871), 107 Mass. 439 

Ladd v Rogers, (1865), 11 Allen 209, as qualified by 

Myers v Meinrath, (1869), 101 Mass. 366 nor can he maintain an action for any deceit practised in procuring them. 

Cardoze v Swift, (1873), 113 Mass. 250 Import

If the evidence shows a complete cause of action independently of any participation of the plaintiff in an illegal transaction, he may recover. Thus, a note made and dated on 

Sunday but delivered on a week-day is valid. 

Stacy v Kemp, (1867), 97 Mass. 166 

Banca Italiana v Columbia Co., (1925), 252 Mass.552,148 NE 105

Thus, an offer made on Sunday may be validly accepted on a week-day. 

Tuckerman v Hinkley, (1864), 9 Allen 452 

Dickinson v Richmond, (1867), 97 Mass. 45

If an agreement for a sale of chattels was made on Sunday, (which is of itself void and incapable of ratification), and the chattels are delivered and accepted on the following day, with the purpose that they be sold and paid for, the seller may recover upon an implied contract what they are reasonably worth. 

Bradley v Rea, 14 allen 20, 103 Mass. 188 

And if the buyer expressly promises, on a week-day, to pay for such chattels, he will be liable on his express promise. winchell v Carey, (1874), 115 Mass. 560

In some states, including Massachusetts, a contract which, having been made on Sunday, is void as between the parties, cannot be ratified subsequently in any manner so as to give it validity. 

Stebbins v Peck, (1857), 8 Gray 553 

Day v McAllister, (1860), 15 Gray 435

Skinner Co. v Burke, (1919), 231 liass. 555, 121 N.E. 427

Kryzminski v Callahan, 213 Mass.207,100 NE 335,43 LRA (NS)140 

Contra:Hofgesang v Silver, (1930), 202 Ky.503,68 A.L.R.1481

While it cannot be ratified so as to give it legal effect from the beginning, it may be adopted subsequently without formality. 

Miles v Janvrin, (1909), 200 Mass. 514, 86 N.E. 785

If a contract is valid, the court will not treat as a nullity what was done on Sunday in performance of it. 

Gordon v Levine, (1907), 197 Mass.263,83 NE 861,15 LRA (NS) 243 

Leonard v Travis, (1863), 6 Allen 129 

Johnson v Willis, (1856), 7 Gray 164

Thus, a part payment, made on Sunday, of a valid note is effectual as a payment pro tanto but, if the note is barred by the statute of limitations, such payment will not lift the bar of the statute so as to make the note enforceable as to the unpaid balance. 

Clapp v Hale, (1873), 112 Mass. 368

In Massachusetts, it is expressly provided by statute that the provisions of the Sunday laws shall not constitute a defense to an action for a tort or injury suffered by a person on the Lord's day. 

G. L. c. 136, section 20

This statute changes the former common law rule in 

McGrath v Merwin, (1873), 112 Mass. 465 

See Tillock v Webb, (1868), 56 Me. 100, Costigan 1198

AGREEMENTS CONTRARY TO PUBLIC POLICY 

policy. 3. 

a. IN GENERAL 

A contract may be void as against public

Shattuck v Eastman, (1866), 12 Allen 369 

Kingsbury v Ellis, (1849), 4 Cush. 578 

Bills v Comstock, (1847), 12 Met. 468 

Frost v Belmont, (1863), 6 Allen 152 

Farrar v Barton, (1809), 5 Mass. 39. 

Fuller v Dame, (1836), 18 Pick. 472

If an agreement contemplates legitimate services only, it is not illegal merely because an agent employed unddr it might misconduct himself and use corrupt methods. 

Kerr v American Co., (1905), 188 Mass. 27, 73 N. E. 857 b. pervert or obstruct

Any agreement which tends to public justice is void. 

Seaman v Colley, (1901), 178 Mass. 478, 59 N. E. 1017 

Partridge v Hood, (1876), 120 Mass. 403 

Phippen v Stickney, (1841), 3 Met. 384

An agreement to pay extra fees to witnesses who have been duly summoned is not enforceable except as to such expert witnesses as may require time and labor in order to prepare to testify. The rule is also put upon the ground of lack of consideration for the promise. 

Thatcher v Darr, (Wyo.,1921), 199 Fac.938, 1€ A.L.R. 1442 

Keown v Verlin, (1925), 253 Mass.374,149 N.E.115,41 A.L.R.1319 

Barrus v Phaneuf, (1896), 166 Mass.123,44 N.E.141,32A.L.R.€19 

An agreement to procure "straw bail” for a person under arrest and, when this is obtained to have in lo. me bi..o state, is a fraud on the court, and a promise to pay money therefor cannot be enforced. 

Way v Greer, (1907), 196 Mass. 237, 81 N. E. 1002

An agreement by an insolvent debtor to pay some of his creditors a portion of their claims, in consideration that they will not oppose his discharge, is illegal and void. 

Dexter v Snow, (1853), 12 Cush. 594 

See Case v Gerrish, (1833), 15 Fick. 49

The threat of criminal prosecution, used to compel the giving of a promissory note, may constitute duress so as to make the note unenforceable, although the amount for which the note is given may be actually due from the maker. 

Taylor v Jaques, (1871), 106 Mass. 291

A note given to a corporation, to induce an officer thereof to use his influence with the United States Attorney to procure dismissal of an indictment against the maker of the note, is illegal. 

Tremont Trust Co. v Brand, (1922), 244 Mass. 421,138 N.E.564 (b)

COMPOUNDING OFFENSES 

Any act, which is punishable by law as a crime, is an offense against the public, and cannot lawfully be made the subject of a private agreement for the suppression of a criminal prosecution, except so far as it may be expressly authorized by statute. 

Partridge v Hood, (1876), 120 Mass. 403 

Eastman Co. v Vermont Co., (1920), 236 Mass.138,128 N.E. 177 

Jones v Dannenberg Co., (1900)112Ga. 426,37 SE 729,52 LRA 271 

Thus, the acceptance of a promissory note, as a reward for compounding a felony, makes the note illegal, void and unenforceable; 

Com. v Fease, (1819), 16 Mass. 91 

Gorham v Keyes, (1884), 137 Mass. 583 

Clark v Fomeroy, (1862), 4 Allen 534 and if the maker has paid the note, he cannot recover the money so paid. 

Worcester v Eaton, (1814), 11 Mass. 368 

Leonard v Travis, (1863), € Allen 129 

A promissory note, given to protect the maker from prosecution for a crime, is based upon an illegal considera-

tion, and is void, whether the crime referred to is a felony or a misdemeanor. 

Wolff v Perkins, (1925), 254 Mass. 10, 149 N. E. 691 

Atwood v Fisk, (1869), 101 Mass. 363 

Jones v Rice, (1836), 18 Pick. 440

Such an agreement will neither be enforced nor abrogated by a court of equity. The doctrine is founded on the public policy that the course of justice cannot be defeated for the benefit of an individual. It is subject to the one exception that, where the parties are not in pari delicto, then relief in equity may be granted to the party who is the less guilty. 

Berman v Coakley, (1922), 243 Mass. 348, 137 N. E. 667

By statute, in Massachusetts, if a person has been committed to jail under indictment or complaint for assault and battery or other misdemeanor for which he is liable in a civil action, and the person injured appears before the court and acknowledges in writing that he has received satisfaction for the injury, the court may, in its discretion, discharge the defendant. 

G. L. c. 276, section 55 

Com. v Dowdican, (1874), 115 Mass. 133 

Bryant v Rich's Grill, (1913), 216 Mass. 344, 103 N. E. 925 

(c) OUSTING JURISDICTION OF THE COURTS 

Parties cannot, by their consent, give jurisdiction to courts where the law has not given it, nor can they take away jurisdiction where the law has given it. 

Webber v Cambridgeport Bank, (1904), 186 Mass.314,71 NE 567 

Amesbury v Bowditch Ins. Co., (1856), 6 Gray 596

Thus, in general, a stipulation in a contract is void providing that no suit shall be brought thereon except in a certain county;

Nute v Hamilton Insurance Co., (1856), 6 Gray 174 

or except in the courts of a particular state. 

Nashua Co.v Hammermill Co.,223 Mass.8,111 NE 678, LRA 1916D 691

Hall v People's Insurance Co., (1856), 6 Gray 185 Home Insurance Co. v Morse, (1874), 20 Wallace (U. S.) 445 Distinguish Daley v People's Ass'n,178 Mass.13,59 N.E. 452 

Occasionally, such contractual stipulations have been respected when the circumstances seem to make them reasonable and their enforcement proper. 

Mittenthal v Mascagni, (1903), 183 Mass. 19, 66 N. E. 425 

Estes v Newton, (1922), 241 Mass. 229, 136 N. E. 643

The right to a trial by jury may be waived, and an agreement that the judgment of the lower court shall be final is a waiver of such right, and is legal. 

Palmer v Lavers, (1914), 218 Mass. 286, 105 N. E. 1000

A stipulation in an agreement that no suit shall be brought thereon, unless commenced within twelve months, is valid. 

Eliot Bank v Beal, (1886), 141 Mass. 566, 6 N. E. 742

13 old noder JadosgTSC olled. 78

The ultimate question as to whether a stipulation, purporting to limit the courts in which an action on the contract may be brought, shall be respected, pertains to the remedy, and so is governed by the law of the forum; hence, the validity of a stipulation according to the law of the place where the contract is made does not necessarily render it effective in another jurisdiction in which an action, contrary to its terms, is brought. 

Meacham v R. R., (1914), 211 N. Y. 346, 105 N. E. 653 

Parker v El Saieh, (1928), 107 Conn.545,141 Atl.884,59 ALR 1424 

State v Tazwell, (1928), 125 Or.528,266 Pac.258,59 ALR 1436

Important d) AGREEMENTS TO REFER TO ARBITRA- 

TION 

Controversies which might be the subject of a personal action at law, or of a suit in equity, may be submitted to the decision of one or more arbitrators, as provided by statute. 

G.L.c. 251, section 1 

Second Society v Ins. Co., (1915), 221 Mass.518,109 N.E. 384

Parties are not permitted to select their own tribunals for the settlement of civil controversies. The general rule is well settled in this Commonwealth that a general agreement to refer to arbitration will not be enforced in equity and will not be sustined as a bar to an action at law, or a suit in equity. 

Brocklehurst v arsch, (1916), 225 Moss. 3, 113 N. E. 646 

Norcross Bros. v Vose, (1908), 199 Mss. 81, 85 N. E. 468 

Noyes v Mc.rsh, (1877), 123 Mass. 286

The reason generally given is that such an agreement affects the remedy and, if enforced, would oust the courts of their jurisdiction. Another reason is that a submission to arbitration is a power, and revocable at any time before it is fully executed by an award made. A party will not be compelled to enter into a submission which he can forthwith revoke, and the bringing of an action amounts to a revocation. 

Reed v Washington Ins. Co., (1885), 138 Mass. 572

When the question is a preliminary one as to some subsidiary or ancillary matter which does not go to the root of the action, but is merely in aid of an action at law or suit in equity, such as the ascertainment of damages, an agreement for arbitration will be upheld. 

Wood v Humphrey, (1873), 114 Mass. 185 

Cobb v N. E. Ins. Co., (1856), 6 Gray 192 

White v Middlesex Ry., (1883), 135 Mass. 216

But an agreement to submit all disputes relating to the performance of an agreement, or questions of liability, to the final decision of a tribunal constituted by the parties themselves, is illegal. 

Pearl v Harris, (1876), 121 Mass. 390 

Deerfield v Arms, (1838), 20 Pick. 480 

Jones v Brown, (1898), 171 Mass. 318, 50 N. E. 648 

Cochrane v Forbes, (1926), 257 Mass. 135, 153 N. E. 566

Rosenblum v Springfield Co., (1922), 243 Mass. 111, 137 NE 357 

White v Abbott, (1905), 188 Mass. 99, 74 N. E. 305 

Hood v Hartshorn, (1868), 100 Mass. 117 

Vass v Wales, (1880), 129 Mass. 38

EXAMPLE: An agreement that arbitrators shall "hear the parties and determine whether or not there has been any violation of the agreement and what damage either party has sustained, and the decision of the arbitrators shall be final and binding on the parties" is void. 

Miles v Schmidt, (1896), 168 Mass. 339, 47 N. E. 115

There is no decision which prevents two persons from agreeing that a sum of money shall be payable on a contingency; but they cannot legally agree that, when it is payable, no action shall be maintained upon it. 

EXAMPLE 1: A provision in a lease that, in event of disagreement as to rental, the amount shall be left to arbitrators, is valid. 

Dana v Dana, (1927), 260 Mass. 460, 157 N. E. 623

EXAMPLE 2: A condition may be validly inserted in an insurance policy, providing for the submission of the question of the amount of loss to arbitration, and that no suit shall be brought until after such award has been made. 

Hutchinson v Ins. Co., (1890), 153 Mass. 143,26 NE 439, 10 LRA 558 

See Haley v Bellamy, (1884), 137 Mass. 357

EXAMPLE 3: A clause in an accident insurance policy that, in case of disagreement as to the liability of the company, it shall be determined by arbitrators, is illegal and void. 

Lewis v Accident Co., (1907) 194 Mass. 1,79 NE 802,17 LRANS 714 

Bauer v International Waste Co., (1909), 201 Mass. 197,87 NE 637 hr- There is doubt about the validity of any arbitration clause which would constitute one party to a dispute a member of a board of arbitration to pass upon his own claims. bitration implies the exercise of the judicial function. It is contrary to natural right and fundamental principles of the common law for one to judge his own cause. 

Patton v Babson's Inc., (1927), 259 Mass.424,156 N.E. 534

Brocklehurst v Marsch, (1916), 225 Mass. 3,113 N. E. 646 (e) AGREEMENTS PROMOTING OR RESTRAIN- ING MARRIAGE 

Agreements for promoting marriages, or for procuring marriage with a designated person, have been decried by the common law from an early date as against public policy, the public weal being thought to require freedom of choice of persons entering into matrimony, uninfluenced by any mercenary motives of others. 

Braum v Potter Co., (1930), 301 Pa.365,152 Atl.751,72 ALR 1109 

Fuller v Dame, (1836), 18 Pick. 472

Thus, marriage brokerage contracts are illegal and void. 

Duval v Wellman, (1891), 124 N. Y. 156, 26 N. E. 343

1

1 In general, contracts in restraint of marriage are also Illegal and void. This is true where the consideration is money or other personal property, no matter how brief the period of restraint.

Sterling v Sinnickson, (1820), 5 N.J.L.756, Costigan 1153 

Re Sylvester, (1923), 195 Iowa 1329, 192 N. W. 442,30 ALR 180 

See King v King, (1900), 63 Ohio St.363,59 NE 111, Cost.1154

In a transfer of real estate, however, a condition in general restraint of marriage is ordinarily void but a condition in partial restraint of marriage which relates to the time when, or the place where, or the person with whom a marriage may take place is valid if the condition is reasonable. 

8 R. C. L. 1119 

Randall v Marble, 69 Me. 310 

Gibbens v Gibbens, (1885), 140 Mass. 102, 3 N. E. 1 

Knight v Mahoney, (1890), 152 Mass. 523, 25 N. E. 971 

(f) AGREEMENTS FOR SEPARATION OR DIVORCE 

An antenuptial "companionate marriage" agreement which provides that, in event of separation of the parties after marriage the woman will accept a specified settlement in lieu of all claims for alimony, support, dower, or wife's or widow's rights, and will not contest any action for divorce is void as against public policy, as an attempt to evade the laws applicable to marriage relations. 

Re Duncan, (Colo., 1930), 285 Pac. 757, 70 h. L. R. 824

Agreements between a third person acting for either a husband or a wife, made while the spouses are living apart, or in instant contemplation of a separation which takes place immediately, and for the purpose of securing definite and fixed support for the wife from a trust fund, or a gross payment for her benefit, or a bond conditioned for her support, cr a division af property between them, have been held valid and enforced provided they were fairly made. 

Terkelsen v Peterson, (1913), 216 Mass. 531, 104 N. E. 351 

Bailey v Dillon, (1903), 186 Mass. 244, 71 NE 538,66 LRA 427 

Grime v Borden, (1895), 166 Mass. 198, 44 N. E. 216 895) 

Page v Trufant, (1806), 2 Mass. 159 

Fox v Davis, (1873), 113 Mass. 255

No agreement of separation between husband and wife can be valid in Massachusetts unless it includes a third person as trustee and party there to. 

Silverman v Silverman, (1886), 140 Mass. 560, 5 N. E. 639

A promise to pay money to a wife, if she will return and live with her husband, is void for lack of consideration, and illegal as against public policy. 

Merrill v Peaslee(1888)146 Mass.460,16 NE 271, Costigan 1159 

See Polson v Stewart, (1896), 167 Mass. 211, 45 N. E. 737 

See Cooper v Cooper, (1888), 147 Mass. 370, 17 N. E.892

An agreement to dissolve the marriage relation by a collusive divorce is void, illegal, and unenforceable in any part.

Wolkovisky v Rapaport, (1913), 216 Mass. 48, 102 N. E. 910 

(g) AGREEMENTS IN RESTRAINT OF TRADE aa. EARLY ENGLISH LAW 

In general, the law has always regarded monopolies as hostile to the rights and interests of the public. One method of obtaining them in early times was by a grant from the sovereign to a particuler individual of the sole right to exercise a particular trade. 

The mischiefs arising from the practice became so intolerable that it was suppressed by a clause in Magna Charta in 1415. 

Ano ther method was by private contracts in which one of the parties agreed not to engage in some specified trade or business. 

Taylor v Blanchard, (1866), 13 Allen 370 

Alger v Thacher, (1837), 19 Pick. 51

Originally, such private contracts were held invalid and illegal on the ground that they tend to deprive the party restrained of the means of earning a livelihood and deprive the community of the benefit of his free and unrestricted efforts in his chosen field of activity. 

Anchor Co. v Hawkes, (1898) 171 Mass.101,50 NE 509,41 LR A 

Norfolk Motor Co. v Grubb, Va.,1929),147 SE 214,63 ALR 310 

bb. THE MODERN DOCTRINE 

The general principal that arrangements in restraint of trade are not favored is still firmly established in the law, but the distinction was long ago taken between contracts involving a partial restraint of trade and contracts involving a general restraint of trade, the former being held valid, if not unreasonable, and the latter invalid. 

Anchor v Hawkes, (1898), 171 Mass. 101,50 NE 509,41 LRA 189 

Palmer v Stebbins, (1825), 3 Pick. 188 

Worthy v Jones, (1858), ii Gray 168

In the course of time, the strict doctrine of the early common law was relaxed in the English courts, and agreements in restraint of trade were classified under three heads: (1) Where the restraint was unlimited as to both time and space; (2) Where it was limited as to time, but unlimited as to space; (3) Where it was time. limited as to space, but unlimited as to In the first and second cases, the agreement was void; in the third, it was valid. These fixed rules were followed by both English and American courts until within a comparatively few years when a new view was introduced, making the validity of the agreement dependent on the reasonableness of the restraint. 

13 C. J. 468

It is now recognized that competition is not always beneficial, especially among natural monopolies, such as railroads. 189

The Sherman Anti-Trust Act, enacted by Congress in 1890, and its several amendments, and other federal statutes declaring contracts in restraint of trade to be illegal, apply only to interstate and foreign commerce, and with the Indian tribes. They have no effect on any other agree.nts made in restraint of trade, the latter being exclusively within the power of the states, either under the common law, or under statutes enacted by the states under their police power. 

Com. v Straus, (1906), 191 Mass.545,78 NE 136,11 ĪRA(NS) 968 

cc. REASONABLENESS OF RESTRAINT Contracts in restraint of trade are unlawful unless the restriction is reasonable in reference to the interests of the parties concerned and reasonable in reference to the interests of the public. 

Pierce v Woodward, (1828), 6 Pick. 206 

Pierce v Fuller, (1811), 8 Mass. 223 

New York Co. v Kidder Press, (1906), 192 Mass.391,78 NE 463

United States v Addystone Pipe Co., (1899), 175 U. S. 211 

The restriction may be by an express covenant not to engage in the same business within a reasonable space, or it may be implied. 

Boutelle v Smith, (1874), 116 Mass. 111

The cases usually arise when an established business has been sold, the seller's agreement to remain cut of the trade for the time being, being required as a part of the consideration, and as a means of protecting the good will with which he has expressly or impliedly agreed that he will not interfere. 

Edgecomb v Edmonston, (1926), 257 Mass. 12; 153 N. . 99 1. 

Contracts restraining freedom of employment can be enforced only when they are reasonable, and not wider than is necessary for the protection to which the employer is entitled, and when not injurious to the public interest. 

Handforth v Jackson, (1889), 150 Mass. 149, 22 N. E. 634 

Keith v Heywood Co., (1926), 255 Mass. 321, 151 N. E. 309 

Walker Co. v Westerman, (1928), 263 Mass. 235, 160 N. E. 801

Thus a contract of employment of a driver of a laundry wagon is valid by which he covenants that, after the termination of such employment, he will not solicit laundry work for two years in his employer's territory of Somerville and Medford; 

Boston Laundry v O'Reilly, (1925), 253 Mass. 94, 148 N. E. 373 or for three years in his employer's territory of Weymouth, Hingham and Braintree. 

Sherman v Pffefferkorn, (1922), 241 Mass. 468, 135 N. E. 568

EXAMPLE: A contract of employment by the manager of an undertaking business in Haverhill that, for ten years after his employment ceases, he will not engage in a similar business in Haverhill "and vicinity" is valid as to Haverhill, and is not unenforceable as matter of law as to the "vicinity" providing the meaning of the latter word can be determined by fixing the limits beyond Haverhill, if any, within which the employee, if engaged in such business, would be in competition with the employer.

Chandler, Gardner, Inc.v Reynolds (1924) 250 Mass. 309,145 NE 476

A contract is not necessarily in restraint of trade because unlimited in point of time. 

Smith v Brown, (1895), 164 Mass. 584, 42 N. E. 101

In 1895, the Massachusetts court said that, so far as it was aware, in every case in this Commonwealth, except one, where a contract in restraint of trade has been held valid, the restriction was limited as to space; 

Gamewell Co. v Crane, (1893), 160 Mass. 50,35 NE 98,22 LRA 673 the one exception being 

Morse Drill Co. v Morse, (1869), 103 Mass, 73 

EXAMPLE: A covenant, by the vendor of a business of manufacturing and dealing in bed quilts and comfortables, that he would not engage in a similar business for five years was held void because not limited as to space. 

Bishop v Palmer, (1888), 146 Mass. 469, 16 N. E. 299 

Taylor v Blanchard, (1866), 13 Allen 370 

See Dean v Emerson, (1869), 102 Mass. 480

But the modern tendency of the cases, which is now followed in Massachusetts, is that a covenant, even if unlimited both in time and space, is valid if it is coupled with the sale of a business, and is no more than is necessary to give to the purchaser what he has bought: 

Marshall Co. v New Marshall Co., (1909) 203 Mass. 410,89 NE 548 

Anchor Co. v Hawkes, (1898), 171 Mass. 101,50 NE 509,41 LRA 189 

United Shoe Co. v Kimball (1996),193 Mass. 351,79 N.E.790

Nordenfelät v Maxim Co., (1894), App. Cas. 535, Costigan 1108 

Diamond Match Co. v Roeber, (1887), 106 N.Y. 473,13 N.E. 419 and such restrictions as have been held void are deemed to go further than is reasonable to give full value to the property sold. 

Herreshoff v Boutineau, (1890), 17 R.1.3,19 Atl.712,8 LRA 469

Samuel Stores v Abrams, (Conn., 1919), 108 Atl.541,9 ALR 1450 

Conn.,

The good will of a business, including its trade name may be sold as a part, and it is frequently the most important part, of the business. If the agreement of sale of a business omits to mention that the good will is transferred, it will be presumed that it passes to the vendee with the other assets in the absence of contrary intention shown. 

Canadian Club Co. v Canadian Club Corp., 268 Mass. 561,168NE 106 

EXAMPLE: A sale by one partner to another of "all my right, title and interest in and to all and singular the partnership property, meaning thereby to sell and convey all my interest in the entire assets of said firm, wherever the same may be situated", includes a transfer of the good will. 

Hoxie v Chaney, (1887), 143 Mass. 592, 10 N. E. 713

The sale of the good will of a mercantile business is often nothing more than a sale of the custom or trade which appertains to the place where the vendor was then carrying on his business; in fact, good will is sometimes defined as the tendency of customers to resort to the old place. 

Marshall Co. v New Marshall Co., (1909), 203 Mass. 410,89 NE 548

AUTIOQ

- the But, in the sale of the good will of a profession, personal qualities of integrity, professional skill and ability attach to, and follow, the person and not the place. 

Foss v Roby, (1907), 195 Mass.292,81 N.E.199,10 LRA (NS) 1200 

Gilman v Dwight, (1859), 13 Gray 356 

Proctor v Hansel, (Iowa, 1928), 218 N. 255, 58 ALR 153

The sale of the good will within reasonable limits of space carries with it the implied covenant, as in other sales, that the vendor will not himself do anything to disturb or injure the vendee in the enjoyment of that which he has purchased. 

Angier v lebber, (186), 14 Allen 211 

Dwight v Hamilton, (1873), 113 Mass. 175

The sale of the stock in trade in a particular store, and of the good will, does not create, by mere implication and apart from express stipulation, an undertaking on the part of the vendor not to engage again in a similar business in some other place at a subsequent time. 

Hoxie v Chaney, (1887), 143 Mass. 592, 10 N. E. 713 

Bassett v Percival, (1862), 5 Allen 345

In fact, cases may be put where the sale of the good will would not prevent the vendor from setting up a similar business again in the same city or town. Thus, one who sells the good will of a small grocery store in the south end of Boston without doubt might set up a small grocery store in the north end of the same city. 

Marshall Co. v New Marshall Co., (1909), 203 Mass.410,69 NE 548 

In each case, where the good will of a business is sold, and the vendor sets up a competing business, it is a question of fact whether, having regard to the character of the business sold, and that set up, the new business does or does not derogate from the grant made by that sale. 

Old Corner Book Store v Upham, (1907), 194 Mass. 101,80 NE 228

Where one party has sold to another all his interest and good will in a certain business, and has agreed not to carry on the same business in the same place, a court of equity will prevent him by 'injunction from violating the agreement ho has made, if the vendee has not been guilty of laches. 

Ropes v Upton, (1877), 125 Mass. 258 

Smith v Brown, (1895), 164 Mass. 184, 42 N. E. 101

There can be no good will connected with the transfer of an illegal business. 

Joe Gouy Shong v Joe Chew Shee, (1926), 254 Mass. 366,150 NE 225 

Deaton v Lawson, (Wash.,1905), 82 Pac.879,2 L.R.A. (N.S.) 392

- competition in price, among themselves, is valid, if made to prevent ruinous competition, and not injurious to the public. 

Central Shade Roller Co. v Cushman, (1887), 143 Mass. 353,9NE629

Patents which are granted by government, for the purpose of giving a monopoly, do not offend the laws against restraint of trade.

Opinion of Justices, (1907), 193 Mass. 605, 81 N. E. 142 Morse Drill Co. v Morse, (1869), 103 Mass. 73 

The sale of a secret process of manufacture, which inoludes a covenant on the part of the vendor that he will communicate all necessary information to the vendee to enable him to use the process, and will not divulge the secret to any other person is not illegal as in restraint of trade, as the public has no rights in a secret. 

Vickery v Welch, (1837), 19 Pick. 523 

Taylor v Blanchard, (1866), 13 Allen 370 

Garst v Harris, (1900), 177 Mass. 72, 58 N. E. 174 

(h) INJURY TO PUBLIC SERVICE 

All agreements, the tendency of which is to injure the public service, are illegal. 

Alvord v Collins, (1838), 20 Pick. 418 

Bills v Comstock, (1847) 12 Met. 468

Kingsbury v Ellis, (1849), 4 Cush. 578 

Marshall v B. R. R. (1853), 18 Howard (U. S.) 314 

Kerr v American Co., (1905), 188 Mass, 27, 72 N 73 P, 857 

Noble v Mead-Morrison Co., (1921), 237 Mass. 5, 189 N. E. 669 

Quirk Milling Co. v R. R. (1906), 98 Minn. 22,107 WW 742, Costl143 

Old Dominion Co. v Hamilton, (Va., 1926),131 S.E.850,46 ALR 186

Thus an agreement for lobby services which includes the exercise of personal influence or importunities to members of the legislature or other official body, or for bribery or corruption, or for influencing them by any arguments, persuasions or inducements other than those which directly and legitimately bear upon the merits of the pending application or petition, are against public policy and void. 

Trist v Child, (1875), 21 Wallace (U. S.) 441 Frost v Belmont, (1863), 6 Allen 152 

Adams v East Boston Co., (1920), 236 Mass. 121,127 N.E. 628 

State v Okanogan Co., (1929), 153 Wash.399,280 Pac.31,67 ALR 668 

See Barry v Capen (1890), 151 Mass. 99,23 NE 735, 6 LRA 808

In general, an agreement by a public officer to render the services required of him for less than the compensation provided by law, is void as against public policy. If the compensation is fixed by positive law, and it is more than the services are worth, the party must pay it; if less, the officer must be content with it. Neither can resort to any other rule than the written law. 

Werner v Hillman Co. (1930)300 Pa.256,150 ATL.471,70 ALR 967 

Brophy v Marble, (1875), 118 Mass. 548

By the general rule, the salary of a public officer, which is not yet due, cannot be assigned.

Schwenk v Wyckoff, (N. J. Eg., 1890). 9 L. R. A. 221 

See Brackett v Blake, (1844), 7 Met. 335

But the assignment of the salary or wages of a private individual, to be earned in the future, either under an existing or a future contract of employment, is valid in most states, if the requirements of the statutes are complies with. 

G. L. c. 154, sections 5, 7

Citizens' Loan Association v R. R. (1907), 196 Mass. 528,82 NE.696 

A public officer is not entitled to a reward offered for making an arrest which it was his duty to make. 

Pool v Boston, (1849), 5 Cush. 219 

Greme Cour 

Hogan v Stophiet, (1899),179 111.150,53 NE 604, Costigan 360

This is true even though he makes the arrest while off duty, provided it is made by him in his capacity as an officer. 

Davies v Burns, (1862), 5 Allen 349

A common carrier cannot exempt itself by agreement from liability for its own negligence. do 

Fonseca v Cunard Co. (1891), 155 Mass.553,27 NE 665,12 LRA 340 (1) AGREEMENTS AFFECTING DUTIES TO- WARD THIRD PERSONS 

The law goes further than merely to annul contracts where the obvious and avowed purpose is to or to cause the doing of, unlawful acts. It avoids contracts and promises made with a view to place one under wrong influences, those which offer him a temptation to do that which may injuriously affect the rights and interests of third persons Thus, a person, having property, may make a will in favor of whom he pleases. A common friend may lawfully represent to him the expediency and fitness of making a bequest in favor of a particular individual, but any promise to pay another for soliciting a will in his favor would be void. 

Pike v Pike, (1929), 266 Mass.186,165 N.E.5, 61 A.L.R. 643 

Fuller v Dame, (1836), 18 Pick. 472, 481

By the great weight of authority, an agreement by an heir, in consideration of a present advance by his ancestor, not to contest his will, is valid and will be enforced. 

Re Cook, (1926), 244 Ń. Y. 63, 154 N. E.823, 55 A.L.R. 806 

See Seaman v Colley, (1901), 178 Mass. 478, 59 N.E. 1017

B. EFFECT OF ILLEGALITY 1. GENERAL RULES 

No court will consciously lend its aid to the enforcement of an illegal contract, whether the illegality is pleaded in defense or not.

Claflin v U. S. Credit Co., (1896) 165 Mass. 501, 43 NE 293 

The general principal is undoubted that courts of justice will not assist a person who has participated in a transaction, forbidden by statute, to assert rights growing out of it, or to relieve himself from the consequences of his

Snell v Dwight, (1876), 120 Mass. 9 

Perkins v Cummings, (1854), 2 Gray 258 

Nickel v Fox, (1928), 262 Mass. 170, 159, N. E. 541

For this reason, the law gives no remedy for a breach of a contract made on Sunday, or upon a gaming or wagering contract. 

Pelosi v Bugbee, (1914), 217 Mass. 579, 105 N. E. 222

The law, which prohibits the end, will not lend its aid in promoting the means designed to carry it into effect, and, in this respect, the law gives no countenance to the distinction between malum in se and malum prohibitum. 

EXAMPLE: If money was lent for the purpose of gaming, and was lent at the time when, and the place where both the lender and the borrower were gaming and playing at cards with each other, the lender cannot recover the loan from the borrower. 

White v Buss, (1849), 3 Cush. 448

Where the matters called for in the contract that render it illegal do not involve moral turpitude, but are merely mala prohibita, and the contract remains purely executory, it has been held in many cases that either party may disaffirm it on account of its illegality and recover back money or property that he has advanced under it. So long as it is entirely unexecuted in that part which the law forbids, there is a locus penitentiae, or opportunity of withdrawing. 

White v Franklin Bank, (1839), 22 Pick.181 

Love v Harvey, (1873), 114 Mass. 80 

Eastern Metal Co. v Webb Co., (1907), 195 Mass. 356,81 N.E.251 

West Springfield Ry. v Bodur tha, (1901), 181 Mass.583,64 NE 414 

Duane v Stamp Co., (1917), 227 Mass. 466, 116 N. E. 873

Where the matters called for in the contract that render it illegal and void are mala prohibita, forbidden by statute, and the contract has been executed and completed, the law will not allow either party to avoid its effect, or to recover back what he may have paid or parted with under the contract. 

Duffy v Gorman, (1852), 10 Cush 45

In such cases, on grounds of public policy, no aid will be given to a party who is himself at fault and the law leaves the parties where they have placed themselves, and without remedy against each other, provided they are in pari delicto. 

Huckins v Hunt, (1885), 138 Mass. 366, Costigan 1230 

Gibbs v Smith, (1874), 115 Mass. 592 

Dunham v Presby, (1876), 120 Mass. 285 

Curtis v Aspinwall (1873), 114 Mass. 187 

Jones v Henderson (1920), 225 S. W. 34, 20 A. L. R. 1471 

Howe v Chmielinski, (1921), 237 Mass. 532, 130 N. E. 56 

Brooks v Volunteer Harbor, 233 Mass. 168,123 NE 511,4 ALR 1086 

Lowell v R. R. (1839), 23 Pick. 24 

Distinguish Jewett v Butler, 159 Mass. 517, 34 NE 1087, Cost.1150

- The general rule is "In pari delicto, potior est conditio defendentis", where the parties are in equal wrong, the position of the defendant is the stronger. Its meaning is that the law leaves the parties exactly where they stand; not that it prefers the defendant to the plaintiff, but that it will not recognize a right of action founded on an illegal contract in favor of either party against the other. They must settle their own questions in such cases, without the aid of the courts, and equity follows the rules of law. 

Atwood v Fisk, (1869), 101 Mss. 363

Frost v Gage, (1862, 3 Allen 560 

Wyman v Fiske, (1861), 3 Allen 238 

Sampson v Shaw, (1869), 101 Mass. 145

EXAMPLE 1: A bill in equity will not lie to compel the surrender and cancellation of an overdue promissory note and a mortgage given to secure its payment, or the ground that the consideration therefor was a promise of the payee to forbear to prosecute for embezzlement. 

Atwood v Fisk, (1869), 101 Mass. 363 

EXAMPLE 2: If a creditor, arrested for embezzlement, agrees to compound his crime by authorizing the debtor to keep the money owed by him to the creditor, in consideration of the debtor's promise not to prosecute, a subsequent action by the creditor to recover the debt will not lie. 

Leonard v Travis, (1863), 6 Allen 129 

EXAMPLE 3: A conveyance of land, made in consideration of a composition of a felony, cannot be avoided by the grantor. 

Worcester v Eaton, (1814), 11 Mass. 368 

EXAMPLE 4: P hired a horse and wagon from D on Sunday for a pleasure drive. Upon his return, P delivered his watch to D as security for payment of the debt. Although the contract for hire was illegal, and D could not have recovered such a debt, neither can P recover his watch. 

King v Green, (1863), 6 Allen 139 

EXAMPLE 5: An action will not lie for the conversion of a chattel sold and delivered by the plaintiff to the defendant on the Lord's day, and retained by the defendant afterwards, notwithstanding the return by the plaintiff of the consideration and his demand for a corresponding return by the defendant. 

Myers v Meinrath, (1869), 101 Mass. 366 

No action lies for fraud on an executed contract void for illegality. 

Robeson v French, (1846), 12 Met. 24

Way v Foster, (1861), 1 Állen 408 2. EXCEPTIONS TO THE GENERAL RULE 

a.

WHERE THE PARTIES ARE NOT IN PARI DELICTO 

If the parties to an illegal contract are

- not in pari delicto, the law will grant relief against the contract in favor of the one who is the less at fault. 

Belding v Smythe, (1885), 138 Mass. 530 

Sampson v Shaw, (1869), 101 Mass. 145 

Lowell v R. R., (1839), 23 Pick. 24

Thus, under many liquor statutes, the buyer is treated as not being ir pari delicto with the seller. 

Adams v Goodnow, (1869), 101 Mass. 81

So, if one party has entered into an illegal contract, prohibited by statute, and has been induced to do so through the duress or undue influence of the other, the parties are not deemed to be in pari delicto. 

EXAMPLE: A promissory note, given by a father to prevent his son from being prosecuted for perjury, may be cancelled in equity. 

Bryant v Peck & Whipple Co., (1891), 154 Mass.460, 28 N.E. 678

WHERE PARTY COMPLAINING CAN ESTABLISH CASE WITHOUT RELYING ON ILLEGAL TRANSACTION 

No action will lie to recover a demand or a supposed claim for damages if, to establish it, the plaintiff requires aid from an illegal transaction, or is under the necessity of showing and depending in any degree upon an illegal agreement to which he himself has been a party. 

Dunham v Presby, (1876), 120 Mass. 285

But the fact that a person has violated the law is no bar to an action by him against a wrongdoer to whose wrongful act the plaintiff's illegal act has not contributed. He who violates the law must suffer its penalties but, in all other respects, he is under its protection and entitled to the benefit of its remedies.

Cumings v Perham, (1840), 1 Met. 555 

Ewings v Walker, (1857), 9 Gray 95

EXAMPLE 1: One of two persons engaged in trotting horses against each other for money, contrary to law, may maintain an action against the other for wilfully running him down. 

Welch v Wesson, (1856), 6 Gray 505

EXAMPLE 2: A person who hires a horse of its owner to drive it to a particular place, and drives it to another place, is liable in tort for conversion of the horse, although the contract of hiring was made on the Lord's day and, as both parties knew, for pleasure driving only, and therefore illegal and void. 

Hall v Corcoran, (1871), 107 Mass. 251, over-ruling 

Gregg v Wyman, (1849), 4 Cush. 522 

EXAMPLE 3: P, an unlicensed pedler, sold a ring on conditional sale, title to remain in the vendor until paid fcr. 

The buyer wrongfully pledged the ring to D, a pawnbroker, who took it in good faith, and subsequently refused to surrender it to P on demand. P may maintain an action in tort for con version against D. 

Pelosi v Bugbee, (1914), 217 Mass. 579, 105 N. E. 222
