3. CONSIDERATION OR PROMISE PARTLY ILLEGAL 

As a general rule, where a promise is made for one entire consideration, a part of which is fraudulent, immoral or unlawful, and there has been no apportionment made or means of apportionment furnished by the parties themselves, it is well settled that no action will lie upon the promise. 

If the bad part of the consideration is not severable from the good, the whole promise fails. 

Bishop v Palmer, (1888), 146 Mass. 469, 16 N. E. 299 

3ixby v Moore, 51 N. H. 402 

Holt v O'Brien, (1860), 15 Gray 311 

Perkins v Cummings, (1854), 2 Gray 258 

Moses v Copelof, (1919), 231 Mass. 513, 121 N. E. 508 

Stewart v Thayer, (1897), 168 Mass. 519, 47 N. E. 420

Eastern Metal Co. v Webb Co. (1907), 195 Mass.356,81 N.E. 251 

Boylston Co. v O'Neill, 231 Mass. 498,121 N.E. 411,2 A.L.R. 902

But if the contract is divisible, and capable of being separated into two or more distinct agreements, the legal portion can be enforced, the rest being treated as void. 

Dean v Emerson, (1869), 102 ass. 480 

Robinson v Green, (1841), 3 Met. 159 

Rand v Mather, (1853), il Gush. 1. 11 The question whether an agreement is entire or separable is primarily one of intention. But the general rule is that, where the part to be performed by one party consists of several distinct and separate items, and the price to be paid is apportioned to each item according to the value thereof, and not as one unit in a whole, or a part of a round sum, the contract will be regarded as severable. And this rule holds true even though the contract may be, in a sense, entire if what is to be paid is clearly and distinctly apportioned to the different items as such, and not to them as parts of one whole. 

Barlow Co. v Stone, (1908), 200 Mass. 158, 86 N. E. 356

Although an agreement may be indivisible, yet a promisee who has, on his part, promised a consideration which is wholly valid may, if he wishes, purge the agreement of its illegality by ignoring the illegal portion of the other's promise, and enforce only that portion which is lawful. 

Union Locomotive Co. v R. R., 35 N. J. L. 240 4. INTENTION 

In general, ignorance of the law is no excuse for making a contract in violation of that law. The rule does not apply where the performance of the agreement intended would, unknown to the parties, be illegal, but a legal method of performance is possible. 

13 C. J. 516 

Fox v Rogers, (1898), 171 Mass. 564, 50 N. . 1041 

Barry v Capen, (1889), 151 Mass. 99, 23 N. E. 755,6 LRA 808

Nor does the rule apply where the mistake is really one of fact, and not of law.

Stewart v Thayer, (1897), 168 Mass. 519, 47 N. E. 420 

Roys v Johnson, (1856), 7 Gray 162, Costigan 1209 

Stafford v Whitcomb, (1864), 8 Allen 518 

Emery v Kempton, (1854), 2 Gray 257 

Avery v Halsey, (1833), 14 Pick. 174 

Musson v Fales, (1820), 16 Mass. 332

The general rule is that a contract legal in itself is not rendered unenforceable by the mere fact that one of the parties thereto has knowledge of an intended purpose of the other party thereto, by means of the contract or subjectmatter thereof, to violate some law or public policy of some state. 

Tyler v Carlisle, (1887), 79 Me. 210, 9 Atl.356, Costigan 1211

The rule is contra in some jurisdictions, such rule being limited, however, in some of such jurisdictions to cases involving certain intended unlawful purposes, rather than unlawful purposes generally. 

Pearce v Brocks, (1866), L. R. 1 Exche. 213, Costigan 1212

Thus, in Massachusetts, a lease made with knowledge that the demised premises are to be used for the illegal sale of liquor, or for illegal gaming, cannot be made the basis for the recovery of rent. 

Codman v Hall, (1864), 9 Allen 335

Riley v Jordan, (1877), 122 Mass. 231 

Sherman v Wilder, (1371), 106 Mass. 537 

Garvey v McNulty, (1930), 270 Mass. 260, 170 N. E. 58 

See Musco v Torello, (1925), 102 Conn.346,128 Atl.645,42 ALR 1032

A sale of goods in another state, valid by the law of that state, will not be held void in Massachusetts from the bare fact of knowledge or belief of the vendor of the purchaser's intent to resell in this state in violation of law. 

McIntyre v Parks, (1841), 3 Met. 207 

Dater v Earl, (1855), 3 Gray 482

If the seller's knowledge is no higher than an uncommunicated inference as to what the buyer was likely to do, of course the buyer is free to change his mind and there has been no communicated desire of the seller to cooperate with the buyer's present intent but, on the contrary, an understood indifference to everything beyond an ordinary valid sale where made. 

Higgins v Fitzgerald, (1929), 266 Mass. 176, 164 N. E. 812

The difference of opinion as to whether a contract is invalidated by the mere knowledge by one of the parties of the other's unlawful intention does not extend to participation in such intention. Even courts which entertain the view that mere knowledge is insufficient agree that participation. in the unlawful intention renders the contract illegal. 

6 R. C. L. 697 

See State v Bank, (N.M., 1927), 257 Pac.993,53 A.L.R. 1356

The distinction is sound between a case where a seller simply has knowledge of the illegal design no more and where he makes a sale with a view to such design, and for the

- purpose of enabling the purchaser to effect it; for example, where a seller ships liquor in disguised form to escape dis covery. 

Adams v Couillard, (1869), 102 Mass. 167

Webster v Lunger, (1857), 8 Gray 584 Russell v De Grand, (1818), 15 Mass. 35 

Foster v Thurston, (1853), 11 Cush. 322

Gaylord v Soragen, 32 Vt. 110 

Graves v Johnson, 156 Mass. 211, 179 Mass. 53, Costigan 1218 

See Otis v Freeman, (1908), 199 Mass. 160, 85 N. E. 168

An exceptior to the general rule is that, when the intended purpose of one party is to commit a heinous crime, or crime of a serious nature, knowledge on the part of the other party of such purpose will render the contract unenforceable. 

Hanauer v Doane, (1870), 12 Wallace (U. S.) 342

C. CONFLICT OF LAWS 

The general rule is that if a contract is valid where made, it is deemed to be valid everywhere, and will sustain an action in the courts of the state whose laws do not permit such a contract. Even a contract expressly prohibited by a statute of the state in which suit is brought, if not in itself immoral, is not necessarily nor usually deemed sc invalid that the comity of the state, as administered by its courts, will refuse to entertain an action on such a contract by one of its own citizens abroad in a state the laws of which permit it. 

Milliken v Pratt, (1878), 125 Mass. 374 

M'Intyre v Parks, (1841), 3 Met. 207

Parsons v Trask, (1856), 7 Gray 473 Fonseca v Cunard S.S.Co.,153 Mass.553,27 NE 665,12 LRA 340 

Caine v Ry., (1923), 209 Ala.181,95 So.876,32 A.L.R. 793

EXAMPLE 1: In states where a greater interest is allowed than in Massachusetts, a contract securing a greater rate of interest, but agreeably to the law of the place where the contract was made, may be sued for in this state, where the plaintiff can recover the stipulated interest. 

Greenwood v Curtis, (1810), 6 Mass. 358, 377

EXAMPLE 2: A marriage celebrated in another state or country, if valid by the law of that state or country, is ordinarily valid in Massachusetts although the same might, by force of our laws. be. sutton warren, 1895) held invalid if contracted here.

EXAMPLE 3: A marriage which is prohibited in Massachu setts by statute may be valid here if celebrated elsewhere according to the law of the place, even if the parties are citizens and residents of this state, unless the legislature has clearly enacted that such marriage out of the state shall have no validity here. 

Com. v Lane, (1873), 113 Mass. 458

West Cambridge v Lexington, (1823), 1 Pick. 507

- The rule that a contract which is valid where made is valid everywhere is subject to exceptions. No state is bound to recognize and enforce a contract which is injurious to its own interests, or to the interests of its citizens, or that contravenes the established public policy of the state. 

Clark on Contracts (4th edition) 486 

This exception results from the consideration that the authority of the acts and contracts done in other states, as well as the laws by which they are regulated, are not, proprie vigore, of any efficacy beyond the territories of that state; and whatever effect is attributed to them elsewhere is from comity, and not of strict right. And every independent community will and ought to judge for itself how far that comity ought to extend. The reasonable limitation is that it shall not suffer prejudice by its comity. 

Story, Conflict of Laws, section 244 

In general, the lex loci contractus, or law of the place where a contract is made, governs its validity and interpretation. It determines the nature and legal quality of the act done, whether it constitutes a contract, what is its nature, validity, obligation and legal effect, and furnishes the rules for its construction and interpretation. 

Carnegie v Morrison, (1841), 2 Met. 381 

Jennings v Moore, (1905), 189 Mass. 197, 75 N. E. 214 

Hill v Chase, (1886), 143 Mass. 129, 9 N. E. 30 

Sondheim v Gilbert, (1889),117 Ind.71,18 N.E.687,5 L.R.A. 432 

Polson v Stewart, (1897), 167 Mass.211, 45 N.E.737,36 LRA 771

The rule of lex loci contractus is qualified by the lex solutionis, or law of the place where the contract is to be performed. If contracts are made with a view to performance in another state or country, they will be governed by the law of such state or country. 

Louis-Dreyfus v Paterson, (1930), 43 F(2nd) 824, 72 ALR 242 

Brockway v American Express, (1897), 168 Mass.257,47 NE 87 

Old Dominion Co. v Bigelow, (1909), 203 Mass.159,89 N.E. 193

Intention governs, and, in order to ascertain and determine the law by which a contract is to be interpreted and governed, we must look to its nature and character, the purpose for which it was made, and the objects intended to be accomplished by it. This is the only mode by which we can judge accurately of the intent of the parties. 

Penobscot R.R. v Bartlett, (1858),158 Lass.171,50 N. E. 626 

Greenlee v Hardin, (1930),157 Miss.229,127 So.777,71 ALR 741

The lex fori, or law of the place where the action is brought, governs the remedy. It controls the admission of evidence, prescribes the modes of proof, and the form of action by which a right is to be enforced. The right of a plaintiff to bring an action depends on the law of the forum. 

Hoadley v Northern Transportation Co., (1874), 115 Mass. 304 

American Co. v Ziegler, (1914), 216 Mass.287, 103 N. E. 909 

Drake v Rice, (1881), 130 Mass. 410 

Orr v Amory, (1814), 11 Mass. 25 

Lewis v Club Realty Co.,(1928), 264 Mass. 588, 163 N.E. 172

PO PLTU SU BOITOI (eb

Thus, the statute of limitations of the state or country where the action is brought, and the remedy is sought to be enforced, controls. If an action is brought in one state based upon a statute of another state, where the period fixed by the statute of the forum is shorter than that allowed by the lex loci contractus, the lex fori governs. 

Tieffenbrun v Flannery, (1930), 198 N.C.397, 68 A.L.R. 210 

See Cristilly v Warner, (1913), 87 Conn.461,51 L.R.A. (N.S.)415

At common law, the law of another state is a fact and must be proved like any other fact. The court could not take cognizance of the law of a foreign state in the absence of evidence. In the absence of such evidence, the case must be decided according to the common law of the forum because the common law of a sister state is presumed, in the absence of evidence, to be the same as the law of the forum. 

Parrott v Mexican Ry., (1911), 207 Mass. 184,34 LRA (NS) 261 

Frey Co. v Silk, (1923) (1923), 245 ass. 534, 140 N.E. 259

The common law of Massachusetts was changed by a statute in 1926, providing that "The courts shall take judicial notice of the law or the United States or of any state, territory or dependency thereof or of a foreign country whenever the same shall be material." IX.

Acts of 1926, chapter 168 

Lennon v Cohen, (1928), 264 Mass. 414,420, 163 N.E. 63

REALITY OF CONSENT A. IN GENERAL 

The general rule is that a contract is not binding if made as a result of mistake, misrepresentation, fraud, duress, undue influence, or mental incapacity, since there is only an apparent but not a real consent to be bound. B.

MISTAKE 

1. DEFINITION 

Mistake is that result of ignorance of law or fact which has misled a person to commit that which, if he had not been in error, he would not have done. The word may be used with accuracy to cover all forms of mental error, but it will be used here in its customary and more limited sense to describe what may be termed a mere, or a pure mistake, that is, mistake made without misrepresentation, fraud, duress or undue influence. 

2. EFFECT OF SIGNING WRITTEN INSTRUMENT a. IN GENERAL 

In general, a person must be presumed to have read and understood the papers which he signs. 

Wood v Accident Assoc., (1899), 174 Mass.217,54 N.E. 541 

Fonseca v Cunard S.S.Co., (1891), 153 Mass.553,27 N.E.665 

Farrell v Chandler, Gardner, (1925),252 Mass.341,147 N.E.822 

Trambly v Ricard, (1881), 130 ass. 259

(JORT)

- If he knows, or is charged with knowledge, of a written contract signed by him, it is not necessary that he should understand its legal effect. 

Soley & Sons v Jones, (1911), 208 Mass. 561, 95 N.E. 94 

Wheaton Co. v Boston, (1910), 204 Mass. 218, 90 N.E. 598

EXAMPLE: P, being about to be married, voluntarily conveyed $75,000 to D to hold in trust to pay to P the net income for her life and, upon her death without a will, to pay the rincipal sum to her children, if any. The deed of settlement reserved no power of revocation. If P did not understand that she could not revoke the deed unless a power of revocation was reserved therein, this is a mistake of law, for which no relief can be given. 

Taylor v Buttrick, (1896), 165 Mass. 547, 43 N. E. 507

If, by reason of mistake, a stipulation is omitted from or improperly included in a written contract which is voluntarily signed by the parties, there is no remedy at law. They have deliberately put their agreement in the form of a written contract which, if not ambiguous, cannot be varied or controlled by parol evidence. 

Loomer v Harlow, (1913), 214 Mass. 414, 102 N. E. 333

Thus, a written agreement for the sale of a business, entered into voluntarily and without false or fraudulent representations, which contains an inaccurate price due to a mistake in computation of the figures, is binding at law and, if it does not express the real intent of the parties, the only remedy is in equity to reform the contract in accordance with the true agreement. 

Farquhar v Farquhar, (1907), 194 Mass. 400, 80 N. E. 654 

b. FRAUD 

Any instrument may be impeached by proof of fraud and irposition in obtaining it and such fraud may be proved by the oral testimony of witnesses. 

Baker v Corey, (1837), 19 Pick. 49€ 

Brown v Grow, (1924), 249 Mass. 495, 144 N. E. 403 

3. MISTAKE OF ONE PARTY ONLY 

A mistake of one party only to a contract, which is not known to the other, is ordinarily no ground for relief, either in law or equity. 

Long v Athol, (1907), 196 Mass. 497,82 N.E.665,17 LRA (NS) 96 

Winston v Pittsfield, (1915), 211 Mass. 356, 108 N. E. 1038 

Shapira v Wildey Bank, (1913), 215 Mass. 498, 100 N.E. €19 

Cavanagh v Tyson, (1917), 227 Mass. 437, 116 N. E. 818 

Boyden v. Hill, (1908), 198 Mass. 477, 85 N. E. 413 

Hills v Snell, (1870), 104 Mass. 173 

EXAMPLE 1: A railroad freight agent, by mistake, quoted lower freight rates than he was authorized to quote to P who shipped goods in reliance thereon without knowledge of the error. There is a valid contract which the railroad is es-

- topped to deny. 

Borden v R. R., (1893), 113 N. C. 570, 18 S. E. 392

EXAMPIE 2: P delivered to D, a telegraph company, an offer to be sent to X, offering to sell lumber at a certain price. By mistake, D transmitted the offer at a lower price which X accepted in good faith. As against X, P is regarded as having sent the offer in the form transmitted by D, and is estopped to show the contrary. 

Ayer v. U. Tel. Co., (1887),79 Me.493,10 Atl.495, Cost.238

EXAMPLE 3: P made a very low bid to build a schoolhouse for D, due to a miscalculation, and D accepted in good faith. 

The contract is binding and cannot be rescinded in equity. 

Bowes Co. v Milton, (1926), 255 Mass. 228, 151 N. E. 116

But if one of the parties is aware of a material mistake made by the other, his acceptance is not made in good faith and there is no binding agreement. 

Hume v nibed totes, (1689), 182 U. 3. 406 

Shelton v Ellis, 70 Ga. 297

It is sometimes held that a contract containing a unilateral mistake can be rescinded in equity if the mistake is so grave that it would be unconscionable to enforce the contract as actually made, and if the other party can be put in statu quo. 

Hurst v National Co., (Fla., 1928), 117 So.792,59 A.L.R.807 

Western U. Tel.vChapelle, (Ark.,1929),21 SW(2nd) 964,66 ALR 195 

4. MUTUAL MISTAKE 

Where there is no agreement as to the identity of the subject-matter of the contract, there can be no contract. 

Kyle v Kavanagh, (1869), 103 Mass. 356 

Raffles v Wichelhaus, (1864), 2 H. & C. 906, Costigan 226 

O'Reilly's Case, (1927), 258 Mass. 205, 154 N. E. 851

Where a conveyance has been based upon such a misunderstanding, even though it may be innocent on both sides, equity in proper cases will grant relief. In order to be ground fær such relief, the misunderstanding must not have arisen from the voluntary negligence or failure to obtain reasonably accessible knowledge on the part of the complaining party, in the absence of fraud or duress. 

Dzuris v Pierce, (1913), 216 Mass. 132, 103 N. E. 296 

Kennedy v Poole, (1913), 213 Mass. 495, 100 N. E. 635 

Jeselsohn v Park Trust, (1922), 241 Mass. 388, 135 N. E. 315 

Martin v Jablonski, (1925), 253 Mass. 451, 149 N. E. 156

Likewise, if there has been no meeting of the minds owing to a mutual mistake as to the price, a contract can be set aside. 

Farquhar v Farquhar, (1907), 194 Mass. 400, 80 N. E. 654 

Necl v Lang, (1920), 236 Mass. 61, 127 N. E. 512 

Rupley v Daggett, 74 Ill. 351

But if there is a mutual mistake as to some quality or attribute of the subject-matter, this does not affect the validity of the contract unless there is a condition or warranty with regard thereto, and hence gives no right of rescission. The minds of the parties have met upon the specific thing to be transferred. 

Wood v Boynton, (1885), 64 Wis. 265, 25 N. W. 42 

Hecht v Batcheller, (1888), 147 Mass. 335, 17 N. E. 651 

Contra: Sherwood v Walker, 66 Mich. 568, 33 N. W. 919 5. 

MISTAKE OF LAW 

The general rule is that ignorange or mistake of fact in a contract will be ground for relief as between the parties, but ignorance or mistake of law will not. 

Atlas Shoe Co. v Bloom, (1911), 209 Mass. 563, 95 N. E. 952

But, in equity, the trend of judicial opinion in modern times is toward a modification of the old rule that relief will be given only as against mistakes of fact as distinguished from mistakes of law.

Thus, a mutual mistake that a contract did not bind one personally who signed it as administratrix has been held ground for reformation of the contract in equity whether the mistake be regarded as one of law or fact. 

Shearer v Pringle, (Wis., 1930),233 N. W. €33,71 ALR 1302 

See Eustis Co. v Saco Co., (1908), 198 Mass. 212, 84 NE 449

C. MISREPRESENTATION WITHOUT FRAUD 

The word "misrepresentation" may include both honest and fraudulent misrepresentation, but it is customary to employ it as meaning only honest misrepresentation, without fraud. In general, such misrepresentation has no effect upon a contract unless it amounts to a warranty, or unless a fiduciary or confidential relationship exists between the parties, or unless it is made recklessly, in which cases, it is regarded as fraudulent. 

Globe Co. v Wagner, 188 111.133,58 NE 970,52 LRA 649, Cost. 717 

Norrington v Wright, (1885), 115 U. S. 188, Costigan 897 

Bryant v Ocean Ins. Co., (1839), 22 Pick. 200 

Hills v Sughrue, 15 M. & W. 253

Pike v Fay, (1869), 101 Mass. 134 D. FRAUD 1. WHAT CONSTITUTES FRAUD a. IN GENERAL 

A false representation may be oral, in writing, by an act, or by an omission to act. In general, fraud includes all five elements of an action for deceit, viz: 

A false representation of a material fact; 

Made with knowledge of its falsity; (1) (2) (3) Made to one who is ignorant of its falsity; (4) Made with intent or at least a reasonable expe ctation that it would be acted upon by the one to whom it was made, to his damage;

ebe

- (5) And acted upon by auch person to his demage. 

Creed v Cripps, (vt., 1930), 152 Atl. 369, 71 A. L. R. 1287 

Brightman v Bates, (1900), 175 Mass. 105, 55 N. E. 809 

Harvey v Hunt, (1876), 119 Mass. 279 or

Fraud must relate to a present or pre-existing fact and cannot ordinarily be predicated upon unfulfilled 'promises, statements as to future events because a statement promissory in nature is not properly a representation but a contract. 

Dawe v Morris, (1889), 149 Mass. 188, 21 N. E. 313 

O'Dell v Appalachian Corp., (Va.,1929),149 SE 487,68 ALR 629 

Carlson Co. v Ley, (1929), 269 Mass. 472, 168 N. E. 812 

Kaplan v Suher, (1926) 254 Mass.180,150 NE 9,42 ALR 1142

Fraud will not be presumed, in the absence of evidence tending to prove its existence. 

Capen v Capen, N. T. (1920), 234 Mass. 355, 364, 125 . . 692

While it is true that a party has a right to select with whom he will contract and cannot have another person thrust upon him without his consent 

Boston Ice Co. v Potter, (1877), 123 Mass. 28 

Boulton v Jones, (1857), 2 H. & N. 564, Costigan 222 

Winchester v Howard, (1867), 97 Mass. 303 

Cundy v Iindsay, (1878), 5 App. Cas. 459 

Distinguish Stoddard v Hamm, (1880), 129 Mass. 383; nevertheless, if the negotiations are carried on in person, and one party intends at the time to contract with the specific individual before him, though the contract may be voidable for fraud as between the parties, it is valid as to third parties, even though the fraudulent party was acting under an assumed name and obtained credit by so doing. 

Edmunds v Merchants' Co., (1885), 135 Mass. 283 

Robertson v Coleman, (1886), 141 Mass. 231, 4 . E. 619

FAILURE TO DISCLOSE FACTS 

N.

In contracts of sale, caveat emptor is the general rule, and mere silence or failure to disclose known facts does not amount to fraud so as to entitle the other party to rescind the contract unless there is a duty to disclose such facts. 

James v Anderson, (Va.,1927),140 S.E.264,56 A.L.R.421

Laidlaw v Organ, (1817) 2 Wheaton, (U.S.) 178 

Coddingten v Goddard, (1860), 16 Gray 436 

Distinguish Prescott v Wright, (1855), 4 Gray 461 

Hogan v Wixted, (1885), 138 Mass. 270

Failure to communicate material facts which it is one's duty to disclose is actionable fraud. Aliud est tacere, aliud celare. Mere silence is one thing, active concealment is quite another. 

Minor v Sharon, (1875), 112 Mass. 477 

Kidney v Stoddard, (1843), 7 let. 252 

Dow v Sanborn, (1861), 3 Allen 181 

Van Houten v Morse, (1894), 162 Mass.414,38 NE 705, 26 LRA 430

D 

A fraudulent representation of a material fact may be waived by the party to whom it is made. 

EXAMPLE: A false statement to a lessee of the cost of coal for heating the premises, if material, is waived by the lessee's statement that it would have made no difference in his carrying out the agreement. 

Brockton Co. v Lee, (1929), 266 Mass. 560, 165 H. E. 873 

c. REPRESENTATION OF OPINION 

A statement of mere opinion is not actionable, whether true or false. 

Deming v Darling, (1889), 148 Mass. 504, 20 NE 107 2 LRA 743 

Brown v Pierce, (1918), 229 Mass. 44, 118 N. . 266 

Gordon y Parmelee, (1861), 2 Allen 212 

Pike v Fay, (1869), 101 Mass. 134 

Cf. Cooper v Lovering, (1870), 106 Mass. 77

In a matter of opinion, judgment or estimate, if one states as of his own knowledge a matter which is not susceptible of personal knowledge, if he in fact believes it, and it is not intended to deceive, it is not a fraud, although the matter thus stated is not in fact true. 

Tucker v White, (1878), 125 Mass. 344

But if a person states as of his own knowledge a fact which is susceptible of knowledge, recklessly, without knowing whether it is true or not, it is actionable fraud, if untrue, though he believed it to be true. The falsehood consists in stating that he knew the facts when he did not know them. 

Litchfield v Hutchinson, (1875), 117 Mass. 195 

French v Vining, (1869), 102 Mass. 132 

Hazard v Irwin, (1836), 18 Pick. 95 

Bates v Cashman, (1918), 230 Mass. 167, 119 N. . 663 

d. RELIANCE ON REPICSENTATION 

In order to enable a party to rescind a contract, the fraud or misrepresentation relied on must have in fact induced him to make the contract, and must be part of the same transaction. 

Harvey v Squire, (1914), 217 Mass. 411, 105 N. E. 555

There is a conflict of authority as to whether misrepresentations of the cost of property, or the price paid for it, are actionable. Some courts, including Massachusetts, hold that such statements merely relate to the value of property and are no more actionable than any other statements of value, in the absence of a warranty. They are mere traders' talk or puffing, and are no more than an indication as to the value of the property, on which the vendee has no right to rely, and in respect to which the rule of caveat emptor applies. 

Hemmer v Cooper, (1864), 8 Allen 334

Thus, representations by a vendor as to the value or cost of property to be sold, or as to offers for it made by others,

- even though false, are not representations upon which the purchaser ought to rely and are not sufficient to furnish any ground for action or defense. 

Cooper v Lovering, (1870), 106 Mass. 77

But the utmost limit of this rule has been reached in applying it to statements of the price paid by the person making them, and an action will lie for false and fraudulent representations by a third person as to the price paid by such third person for the property in question. 

Medbury v Watson, 

Watson, (1843), 6 Met. 246

Other courts hold that a representation as to the cost of property is to be regarded as regarded as a question of fact and not as a mere opinion as to its value and, therefore, if false, may constitute actionable fraud. 

My ass 

Dunlap v Pierce, (1929), 336 111.178, 168 ..277,66 ALR 181 not

The general rule undoubtedly is that one bargaining with another must use reasonable diligence to discover for himself facts obvious to an ordinary observer, of which the means of knowledge are equally available to both parties; and that, he fails to do this, he cannot maintain an action of deceit for the misrepresentation of them. 

Brady v Finn, (1894), 162 Mass. 260, 38 N.E. 506 

Brown v Leach, (1871), 107 Mass. 564 

Poland v Brownell, (1881), 131 Mass. 158 

Salem Rubber Co. v Adams, (1839), 23 Pick. 256 if 

Rothermel v Phillips, (1928),292 Pa.371,141 Atl.241,61 ALR 489 

Lilienthal v Suffolk Co.(1891)154 Mass.185, 28 NE 151,12 LRA 821 

Cf.Fourth Bank v Webb, (1930),131 Kan.167,290 Pac.1,71 ALR 619

But this requirement does not call for more than reasonable diligence; and distance or other slight circumstances have been held sufficient to warrant leaving the question to the jury. 

Whiting v Price, (1898), 172 Mass. 240, 51 N.E. 1084 

Holst v Stewart, (1894), 161 Mass. 516, 37 N.E. 755 

Jekshewitz v Groswald, (1929), 265 Mass. 413, 164 N.E. 605

And it is well settled that, regardless of the buyer's opportunity to investigate, he may assert the fraud or misrepresentation of the seller where the latter, by fraud or concealment, prevents him from investigating or ascertaining the facts. 

Hazard v Irwin, (1836), 18 Pick. 95 not void. 

2. EFFECT OF FRAUD 

An agreement induced by fraud is voidable and 

Granlund v Saraf, (1928), 263 Mass. 76, 160 N.E. 408

The injured party, after discovering the fraud, has the election to avoid the contract or to abide by it but the right to avoid must be exercised before the rights of third parties have intervened. 

Commissioner v Trust Co., 253 Mass.205,148 N.E. 609,41 ALR 668

Fuller v Dupont, (1903), 183 Mss. 596, 67 N. E. 662 

Sager v Raleigh Co., (Va., 1929), 150 S.E.244,66 A.L.R. 305

It is the ordinary rule that one who rescinds a contract for fraud must put the defendant in statu quo by returning what has been received under the contract. 

Lincoln Co. v James, (1927), 260 Mass. 541, 157 N. E. 698 

Shea ▾ Manhattan Co., (1916), 224 Mass. 112, 112 N. E. 631 

E. DURESS 

Duress is coercion, or personal restraint, or fear of personal injury or imprisonment. The common law divides duress into two classes; (1) Duress by unlawful imprisonment; and (2) Duress per minas, or by threats. 

13 C. J. 398 

Felton v Gregory, (1881), 130 Mass. 176 

Wilcox v Howland, (1839), 23 Pick. 167 

Grimes v Briggs, (1872), 110 Mass. 446 

Taylor v Jaques, (1871), 106 Mass. 291 

Emmons v Scudder, (1874), 115 Mass. 367

United Shoe Co. v La Chappelle, (1912), 212 Mass. 467,99 NE 299 

Duress is species cf fraud. It is sometimes described as the extreme undue influence but this is not entirely accurate because duress always operates through fear while undue influence operates either through fear or other motives. 

Woodbury v Woodbury, (1886), 141 Mass. 329, 5 N. E. 275

EXAMPLE 1: It is legal duress for a trustee to refuse to turn over property to a beneficiary who is rightfully entitled there to except upon condition of signing a release. 

Ingram v Lewis, (1930), 37 F (2nd) 259, 70 . L. R, 702 A. 

Lanigan v Scharton, (1921), 238 Mass. 468, 131 N. E. 223 

Butler v Gleason, (1913), 214 Mass. 248, 101 N. E. 371 

Manheim v Woods, (1913), 213 Mass. 537, 100 N. E. 747

EXAMPLE 2: It is undue influence where a testator signs a death-bed will, not from the motive of fear, but merely "in order to have a little quiet". 

Estes v Bridgforth; (1897), 114 Ala. 221, 21 So. 512

The early common law rule is that duress must be such as would overcome a person of ordinary courage, Morse v Woodworth, (1892), 155 Mass. 233,27 NE 1010 29 NE 525 

See Willett v Herrick, (1927), 258 Mass. 585,603,155 NE 589 but this rule has been criticised in Massachusetts where it has been held that if a party obtains a contract by creating a motive from which the other party ought to be free, and which in fact is, and is known to be sufficient to produce the result, it does not matter that the motive would not have prevailed with a differently constituted person. 

Silsbee v Webber, (1898), 171 Mass. 378, 50 N. E. 555 

Chandler v Sanger, (1874), 114 Mass. 364 

Anthony v Brown, (1913), 214 Mass. 439, 101 N. E. 1056

There must be duress of the person to whom the threats are made, and a man cannot avoid his obligation because of

the duress of another. 

Thus, the contract of a surety, if his own free act, is binding, and the duress of his principal cannot affect his free agency, or in any way control his action. 

Robinson v Gould, (1853), 11 Cush. 55

Thus, a bill of sale cannot be avoided on the ground of duress, by the seller's creditors, or by an officer attaching the goods in their behalf. 

Lewis v Bannister, (1860), 16 Gray 500

There are exceptions to this rule in the case of husband and wife, and in the case of parent and child, and each may avoid a contract if it were made to relieve the other from duress. 

Harris v Carmody, (1881), 131 Mass. 51

Thus, duress on the part of a husband in obtaining his wife's signature to a note is a good defense in an action against her, both as against him, and as against a holder with notice. 

Randolph Co. v Lewis (1928), 196 NC 51, 144 SE 545, 62 ALR 1474 

Stevens v Thissell, (1922), 240 Mass.541, 134 N. E. 398

But it is no defense to the wife if the holder took in good faith and in ignorance of such duress. 

Fairbanks v Snow, (1887), 145 Mass. 153, 13 N. E. 596 

Rochford v Atkins, (1913), 213 Mass. 463, 100 N. E. 640 

Duress ordinarily makes a contract voidable, and not void.

Rau v Von Zedlitz, (1882), 132 Mass. 164 

Hackett v King, (1863), (1863), 6 Allen 58 

Fisher v Shattuck, (1835), 17 Pick. 252 

Worcester v Eaton, (1816), 13 Mass. 371

However, where duress is so severe as to render the person on whom it is imposed a mere automaton, is void. 

13 C. J. 398 

Tilley v Damon, (1853) 11 Cush. 247 the contract 

Pierce v Brown, (1868), 7 Wallace (U. S. ) 205

F. UNDUE INFLUENCE 

Undue influence is any means employed to overcome the will without convincing the judgment. 

Ginter v Ginter, (Kan., 1909), 101 Pac. 634,22 LRA(NS) 1024 

Hoffman v Hoffman, (1906), 192 Mass. 416, 78 N. E. 492

It consists of acts which, though not fraudulent, amount to an abuse of the power which circumstances have given to the will of one individual over that of another. 

Holland, Jurisprudence, 264 

A person who exercises undue influence is usually one in whom confidence is reposed, or one in possession of real or apparent authority over the other, or one who is in a position, by reason of age or circumstances, to impose his will upon the other. 

Hawkes v Lackey, (1911), 207 Mass. 425, 93 N. E. 828 

Butler v Gleason, (1913), 214 Mass. 248, 101 N. E. 371

Shackleford v Shackleford, (Ark), 223 S. W. 561, 11 A.L.R. 730 

When confidence is reposed and accepted, the person trusted is liable for expressing dishonest opinions upon which the other party relies and acts to his damage, and he is also liable for concealing facts which, by reason of his relationship, he should disclose. The broad principle upon which the courts act in cases of this description is that, wherever there exists such a confidence, of whatever character that confidence may be, as enables the person in whom confidence or trust is reposed, to exert influence over the person trusting him, the court will not allow any transaction between the parties to stand unless there has been the fullest and fairest explanation and communication of every particular resting in the breast of the one who seeks to establish a contract with a person so trusting him. 

Reed v Little Co., (1926), 256 Mass. 442, 152 N. E. 918

Undue influence must destroy free agency and amount to mental or physical coercion such as one is too weak to resist. 

It follows that mere solicitation, persuasion or argument addressed to the judgment or affections of the other party, do not amount to undue influence. 

Whitcomb v Whitcomb, (1910), 205 Mass. 310, 91 N. E. 210

Weakness of mind is often a condition for the exercise of undue influence, but the full possession of one's faculties does not necessarily render one immune to such influence. 

There may be absent the strength of character to resist the overpowering will of another. 

Lyons v Elston, (1912), 211 Mass. 478, 98 N. L. 93

X. IN TERPRETATION OF CONTRACTS A. RULES OF EVIDENCE: 

ЖА 

FAROL EVIDENCE RULE STATEMENT OF FAROL EVIDENCE RULE 

The parol evidence rule is that oral evidence is not admissible to contradict, alter, add to, vary or control the terms of a written contract. 

137, 58 N. E. 162 

Violette v Rice, (1899), 173 Mass. 82, 53 N. E. 144 

Dean v Washburn Co., (1900), 177 Mass. 

Bergin v Williams, (1855), 3 Met. 486

Thus, a grantor cannot maintain an action of contract upon an oral statement made by his grantor, before delivery of the deed, that the land is free from encumbrances. 

Tucker v White, (1878), 125 Mass. 344

The rule, by its express terms, has no application to writings which are not contracts, for example, receipts, Macdonald v Dana, (1891), 154 Mass. 152, 27 N. E. 993 

Foster v Bank, (1924), 248 Mass. 279, 142 N. E. 767 or a written memorandum, North Packing Co. v Lynch, (1907), 196 Mass. 204, 81 N.E. 891 or charges in a book of accounts. 

Langdon v Hughes, (1871), 107 Mass. 272

EVIDENCE AS TO FACT OF AGREEMENT 

Paral evidence is admissible to show that a written contract was not in fact a valid contract either through incapacity of the parties, fraud, mistake, illegality of object or where the writing was not intended by the parties to embody the entire transaction and to constitute the sole evidence of the agreement; 

Danielson v Bank, (Wis.,1930),230 N. W. 83, 70 A.L.R. 746 

Western Union v Dittemore, (1928), 264 Mass.74, 161 N.E. 908 

Cohn v Dunn, (1930), 111 Conn.342,149 Atl.851,70 A.L.R. 740 

American Co. v Crawford, (1929), 31 F (2nd) 708,68 A.L.R.1246 

Pettey v Benoit, (1906), 193 Mass. 233, 79 N. E. 245 

Robinson v Mansfield, (1832), 13 Pick. 139 or where the writing never became operative as an obligation because of some unperformed condition not expressed in the writing, upon which the parties had made such obligation to depend. 

EXAMPLE: D signed and delivered to P a writing, agreeing to subscribe for a certain book, it being orally agreed that the writing was to have no binding effect until D should notify P whether he wanted the book. Parol evidence is admissible to show whether such notice was given. 

Mass. Society v Howard, (1920), 254 Mass. 483, 125 N.E. 605

EVIDENCE AS TO TERMS OF AGREEMENT 

3. &• 

TERMS COLLATERAL TO WRITTEN CONTRACT 

Parol evidence is admissible to prove additional terms which are collateral to, and independent of, a written contract. 

Durkin v Cobleigh, (1892)156 Mass. 108, 30 N.E. 474,17 LRA 270 

Potter v Grimm, (1915), 248 Pa.440,94 Atl.135,12 A.L.R. 349 

Glassman v Ficksman, (1921), 238 Mass. 580, 131 N. E. 316

Thus, an oral promise is admissible, made by the vendor's agent to the purchaser under a written contract of lots in an undeveloped tract, that if the promisee would purchase such lots, the vendor would develop the tract by building streets and sidewalks, and put in water, sewers, and electricity. 

Roof v Jerd, (1929),102 vt. 129, 146 Atl. 250,68 A.L.R. 235 

Tripp v Smith, (1901), 180 Mass. 122,61 N.E. 804 

Kimball v Bradford, (1357), 9 Gray 243 b.

AMBIGUOUS TERMS 

The construction of a written instrument, if distinct in its terms, is for the court, whether it is a written contract, or a writing relied on as evidencing a contract. 

Casavant v Sherman, (1912), 213 Mass. 23, 99 N. E. 475 

Nashua Co. v Chandler Co., (1896), 166 Mass.419,44 N.E. 348 

Bresky v Rosenberg, (1926), 256 Mass. 66, 152 N. E. 347 

Creighton v Elwell, (1923), 243 Mass.580, 137 N.E. 737 

Meegan v Hall, (1922), 241 Mass. 449, 135 N. E. 555 

Smith v Faulkner, (1853), 12 Gray 251 

Fowle v Bigelow, (1813), 10 ass. 379

ROATO A 37

COND 

Burnham v Allen, (1854), 1 Gray 496 

Short v Woodward, (1859), 13 Gray 86

Oral evidence is admissible to explain ambiguous terms. 

Simpson v Dix, (1881), 131 Mass. 179 

Thornell v Brockton, 1886), 141 Mass. 151, 6 N. E. 74 

Proctor v Hartigan, (1887), 143 Mass. 462 

143 Mass. 462, 9 N. E. 841 

Welch v Bombardieri, (1925), 252 Mass. 84, 147 N. E. 595 

New England Co. v Standard Co., (1896), 165 Mass.328,43 NE 112

If the terms of the trade, which are in writing, are plain, the parol evidence rule applies, but if they are not plain, the true meaning of them can be ascertained by evidence of the circumstances under which the writing was made. 

Whidden & Co. v Jordon, (1913), 215 Mass. 189, 102 N. E. 436

If the existence of the extraneous facts is ascertained, and is not in dispute the interpretation of the ambiguous contract, in the light of these circumstances, is for the court; 

Arcade Iron Co. Jenks, (1918), 229 Mass. 95, 118 N.E. 288 but if the extrinsic evidence is conflicting, it is a mixed question of law and fact, to be submitted to the jury. 

Keyser v Kemper, (Md., 1929), 146 Atl. 275, 65 A. L. R. 641

An ambiguity may be latent as well as patent, for example, in a description of land in a deed or mortgage. 

Detroit Ry. v Howland, (1929), 246 Mich. 318,224 NW 366,68 ALR 1

Where the contract is not ambiguous, the conduct of the parties, or the construction they may have adopted of its terms, cannot affect their rights or obligations thereunder. 

Wooldridge v Wolf, (1925), 254 Mass. 128, 140 N. E. 685

But where the language of a written contract is open to doubt, and the parties to it have adopted and acted upon a particular construction, such construction will have great weight with the court and will usually be adopted by it. 

Crowe v Bixby, (1921), 237 Mass. 249, 253, 129 N. E. 433 

Ovans v Castrucci, (1929), 267 Mass. 600, 605, 166 N. E. 922 

Tilden Co. v Densten Co., (1914), 216 Mass. 323,328,103 N.E 916 

Wit v Commercial Co., (1925), 253 Mass. 564, 572,149 N. E.609 

New York R. R. v Stoneman, (1919), 233 Mass. 258, 123 N.E. 679 

Jennings v Whitehead Co. (1885) 138 Mass. 594 

Waterman v Johnson, (1832), 13 Pick. 261

In such cases, there is no surer way to find out what the parties meant than to see what they have done. 

Pittsfield R. R. v B.& A. R. R. (1927), 260 Mass.390,157 NE 611 

EXAMPLE: D wrote a letter to P stating his understanding of an ambiguously worded written agreement previously made between them. D is bound by his statement and it will be taken to constitute the contract if relied on by P. 

Gould v Converse, (1923), 246 Mass. 185, 140 Ñ. E. 785

A contract is to be construed to give full effect to each of its provisions if it is susceptible of such construction. 

Dahlstom Co. v Evatt Co., (1926), 256 Mass.404,414, 52 N.E 715

A contract susceptible of two meanings, one lawful, the other unlawful, will be supported and not defeated, it being

presumed that the parties intended to act according to law. 

Eastman Co. v Vermont Co., (1920), 236 Mass. 138, 128 NE 177

But when the parties undertake to put their agreement in writing, and express its crucial terms by characters or symbols so illegible that the court cannot determine the significance of the paper, then no contract in writing has been made. 

Aradalou v R. R., C. (1916), 225 Mass. 235, 240, 114 N.E. 297

CUSTOM AND USAGE 

Oral evidence is often admissible to introduce a custom or usage into a contract in order to explain that the language, as applied to the subject-matter, was understood by the parties to have a special meaning, different from that which might be ordinarily attributed to it. 

Seccomb v Insurance Co., (1865), 10 Allen 305 

Ford Tirrell, (1857), 9 Gray 401 

Wit Realty Co. v Redmond, (1925), 251 Mass. 344, 146 N.E. 903.

Thus, the time of delivery, of credit, or of payment, where the contract is silent, may be shown by the universal course of dealing of the trade.

Custom has been defined as "that length of usage which has become law. It is a usage which has acquired the force of law. A general custom is the common law itself, or a part of it". 

Conahan v Fisher, (1919), 233 Mass. 234, 241, 124 N.E. 13

A custom, if general, is incorporated into and becomes a part of every contract to which it is applicable; if local, of every contract made by parties having knowledge of, or bound to know of its existence. 

Sawtelle v Drew, (1877), 122 Mass. 228 

Soutier v Kellerman, 18 Mo. 509 

James Elgar, Inc. v Newhall, (1920), 235 Mass. 373, 126 

N.E. 661 

Monotuck Silk Co. v Fair, (1873), 112 Mass. 354 

Dodge v Favor, (1860), 15 Gray 82

If the custom is general as applied to a particular transaction, especially where the defendant is a member of the trade, the presumption is that both parties knew of it and contracted accordingly. The question is one of fact for the jury under suitable instructions. In order to be admi ssible, the usage not only must be universal but must have become notorious by the long and uniform practice of those engaged in the trade at the place where the contract was made. 

Barrie v Quinby, (1910), 206 Mass. 259, 92 N. E. 451 

Procter v Atlantic Co., (1911), 208 Mass. 351, 94 N.E. 281 

Frey Co. v S. Silk, Inc., (1923), 245 Mass. 534, 542, 140

NE 259 

American Ry. Ex. v Mohawk Co., 250 Mass. 1, 144 NE 721, 35 

ALR 14 

Brickley v Wrenn, (1925), 252 Mass. 16, 146 N. E. 797 

Scudder v Bradbury, (1871), 106 Mass. 422

The proper office of a custom or usage in trade is to ascertain and explain the meaning and intention of the parties to a contract whethor writton or in parol, which

It does not go beyond this. 

Conahan v Fisher, (1919), 233 Mass.234,241, 124 N.. 13 

Puffer Mfg. Co. v Yeager, (1918), 230 Mass.557,120 N.3.97 

South Deerfield Co.v R.R., (1916), 222 Mass.555,111 N.E.367 

Hall v Paine, (1916), 224 Mass.62,112 E 153, LRA 1917C 737 

Roach v Lane, (1917), 226 Mass. 598, 116 N. E. 470

Therefore, a general custom, known to both parties, cannot be shown to overcome an unambiguous express contract. 

Johnson v Norcross Bros., (1911), 209 Mass. 445, 95 N.E.833 

King v Gannon, (1927), 261 Mass. 94,158 N..346,54 ALR 1215 

Gilbert v McGinnis, (1885),114 111.28,28 N.E.382,Costigan 923 

New York Ry. v Ayer, (1921), 239 Mass. 70,77,131 г.E.325 

Costa v Gorton Pew, (1922), 242 Mass. 294, 136 N. E. 100 

Menage v Rosenthal, (1900), 175 Mass. 358, 56 N. E. 579 

Cesana v Johnson, (1919), 232 Mass. 444, 122 N. E. 444 

Guild v Sampson, (1919), 252 Mass. 509, 122 N. . 712 

Follins v Dill, (1918), 229 Mass. 321, 118 N. E. 644 

Brown v Foster, (1873), 113 Mass. 136

There must be obscurity or ambiguity about the language employed, or the subject-matter involved, to open the door for explanation.

Perry v Mott Iron Works, (1911), 207 Mass.501,93 N.E.798 

Fosgate Co. v Spokane Union, (1928), 263 Mass. 15,160 NE 297

Likewise, a general custom cannot be recognized if contrary to any rule of law. 

Hedden v Roberts, (1883), 134 Mass. 38 

Cutter v Howe, (1877), 122 Mass. 541 

Cayzer v Taylor, (1857), 10 Gray 274 

Mansfield v Stoneham, (1860), 15 Gray 149 

Furber v Dane, (1909), 203 Mass. 108, 89 N. E. 227

Remington v Pattison, (1928), 264 Mass. 249, 162 N. E. 347 

Shute v Bills, (1906), 191 Mass.438 N.E.96,7 LRA (NS) 965

For example, the test of due care is not custom or usage, but what reasonable prudence would require under the circumstances. 

Dunagan v Appalachian Co., (1929),33 F(2nd) 876,68 ALR 1393 

Bailey v New Haven Co., (1871), 107 Mass. 496

Hinckley v Barnstable, (1872), 109 Mass. 126 

d. SUBSEQUENT ORAL AGREEMENTS 

The parol evidence rule excludes oral evidence of any prior or contemporaneous agreement to control the terms of a written contract. It does not mean that the parties cannot afterwards vary the written contract, whether sealed or unsealed, by. oral agreement.

C. 

Freedman v Gordon, (1915), 220 Mass. 324, 107 N. E. 982 

Roxbury Co. v Nute, (1919), 233 Mass. 112, 123 N. E. 391 

Koth v Board, (1927), 141 S. c. 448, 140 S. E. 99,55 ALR 682 

Martin v Maryin, (1925), 98 Vt.326,127 Atl.292,55 ALR 697 

Gouzoulas v Stock, (1916), 223 Mass. 537, 112 N. E. 221 

Leathe v Bullard, (1857), 8 Gray 545 

Munroe v Perkins, (1830), 9 Pick. 298

T2

EXAMPLE: A written building contract provided: "The con tractor will hold strictly to the design on the drawings, and no deviation can be made without written order from the architect." Notwithstanding this specification, the parties, by subsequent oral agreement, can modify, change or annul the written contract. 

Lynch v Culhane, (1922), 241 Mass. 219, 135 г. . 119 E. 

Sitkin v Smith, (Ariz., 1929), 276 Pac. 521, 66 1.L.R.645

The original consideration attaches to and supports the modified contract. 

Thomas v Barnes, (1892), 156 Mass. 581, 51 . E. 683

Many courts hold that a contract required by the statute of frauds to be evidenced in writing cannot be modified by subsequent oral agreement. 

Anderson v Anderson, (1924),128 Wash.324,223 Pac.323,38 ALR 202

EXAMPLE: A provision in a written contract for the sale of standing timber, giving a right of removal during a coming winter provided the cranter still owned the land, cannot be modified by parol so as to provide for removal at that time regardless of who then owned the property. 

Schaap v Wolf, (1021), 173 7is. 351,181 NW 214,17 ALR 7

Other courts, including Massachusetts, hold contra on the ground that the statute of frauds merely intends that the bargain may be made certain and does not undertake to regulate its performance. The statute does not say that such a contract shall not be varied by a subsequent oral agreement for a substituted performance. 

Cummings v Arnold, (1842), 3 Met. 486 

Hastings v Lovejoy, (1885), 140 Mass. 261, 2 N. E. 776 

e. FRAUD AND MISTAKE 

Since fraud vitiates any contract cr transaction into which it enters, the parol evidence rule does not apply where the issue is whether the contract was procured by fraud. 

Garley Bros. v Butler, (1927)170 Mirn.373,212 NW 602,56 ALR 1 

Wasserman v Cosmopolitan Trust, (1925),252 Mass.255,147 NE 742 

Cheraska v Ohanasian, 259 Mass.341,156 NE 715,52 ALR 1149 

Arnhold v National Co., (1927), 20 F (2nd) 364, 56 ALR 4 

McCusker v Geiger, (1907), 135 ass. 46, 80 N. E. 648

Cral evidence is also admissible in equity to show that a contract was entered into by mistake.

Page v Figgins, (1889), 150 Mass. 27, 22 E 63, 5 LRA 152 

Chute v Quincy, (1802), 156 Mass. 189, 30 F. . 550

B. RULES OF CONSTRUCTION 

1.

INTENTION OF PARTIES 

הד An agreement should receive that construction which will best effectuate the intention of the parties. 

Aldrich v 3ay State Co., (1904), 186 Mass. 489, 72 N.E. 52

-- 
--  All agreements, if possible, are to be construed so as to give them effect, and so as to be in harmony with law and justice. But this principle does not authorize the court to nake a new contract, or to bind parties to terms beyond the fair meaning of the language which they have used. 

Old Colony Ry.v Brockton Ry., (1914), 218 Mass.84, 105 N.E.866

Steady 

EXAMPLE 1: "Permanent" employment does not guarantee employment for life, but means merely that it shall continue for an indefinite time at the pleasure of the parties, and it may be terminated by hard times. 

Arentz v Morse Co., (1928), 249 NY 439,164 NE 342,62 ALR 231

EXAMPLE 2: A contract whereby D agreed to let P take ice from D's ice-houses during life cannot reasonably be construed as giving to the right to terminate P's rights by sale of the property, in the absence of express language to that effect. D could sell the premises but, if he did, he would be responsible to P in damages. 

Froctor v Union Coal Co., (1923), 245 Mass. 28,137 N.E.659

In Contracts are interpreted more strictly than wills. the drawing of a will, it is the testator's intention alone which it is sought to express, and greater liberality is allowed, in order to prevent his purpose from being defeated. 

In the case of contracts, entered into, it may be, after much consideration of their terms by the parties, and where the language used is presumed to have been chosen because it aptly describes the respective rights and liabilities of the parties, greater strictness is properly required. 

Davis v Ins. Co., (1912), 212 Mass.310,98 N.E.1043,41 LRANS 250

The intention is to be collected from the whole agreement and not merely from particular words in it; 

New England Co.v Laurel Mills, (1906), 190 Mass. 48,76 N.E.231 

Aurnhammer v Brotherhood Co., (1925), 250 Mass.563,146 N.7.47 

Manly v United Marine Co., (1812), 3 Mass. 85

Reed v Insurance Co., (1877), 95 U. S. 23 

Knower v Emerson, (1830), S Fick. 422 and, in contracts of doubtful meaning, it is to be gathered also from the subject-matter and the accompanying circumstances, so far as competent. 

Greene v Boston S.D.Co., (1926), 253 Hass.519,152 r.E.107 

Potter v Starratt, (1920), 235 Mass. 325, 126 . E. 656 N. 

Barrows v Fuller, (1925), 253 Mass. 79, 148 г. E. 574 

Morse v Boston, (1927), 260 Mass. 255, 250, 157 N. E. 523 

Brown v Fales, (1885), 139 Masc. 21, 29 N. E. 211 

Bray v Kettell, (1361), 1 Allen 80

There is no right to strike out a material term from a contract in order to uphold it. Where there are inconsistent provisions, the one requiring something to be done may be given greater consideration than the other which defeats a full performance; and, while repugnant words may sometimes be

- rejected, such a result is only to effect the eviden purpose of the entire instrument, and such words are not to be considered immaterial where they are susceptible of a construction with the rest of the contract and which gives effect to all its terms. 

Kennon v Shepard, (1920), 236 Mass. 57, 127 N. E. 426 

Morrill Co. v Boston Co., (1904), 186 Mass. 217, 71 N.E. 550 

Taber v Beaudette Co., (1928), 262 Mass. 99, 159 N. E. 505 

Ferguson v Union Ins. Co., (1904), 187 Mass. 8, 72 N. E. 358

Where there is an obvious omission, this will be supplied by the court, especially where there was evidence, admitted without objection, that such was the mutual intention of the parties. 

Pacific Co. v Toye, (1916), 224 Mass. 98, 112 . E. 653

In the absence of any express stipulation in the contract, the question is not what was the understanding of the parties in fast, but what ought to have been their understanding in view of what was caid and done under the circumstances in which they were placed. 

Higgins v Shepard, (1903), 182 Mass. 364, 65 N. E. 805 

Greenwood v Murdock, (1857), 9 Gray 20 

Forbush v Lombard, (1847), 18 Met. 109

Thus, when a statute gives special force and effect to a particular contract, parties who enter into such a contract are held to contemplate and assent to the force and effect attributed by law to that statute. 

Loranão v Gethro, (1917), 228 Mass.181,117 NE 135,1 ALR 1374 

Where the language of a contract is open to doubt, and the parties to it have adopted and acted upon a particular construction, such construction will be considered as of great weight by the court, and will usually be adopted by it. Mary of these cases are cases of ambiguity in boundaries or monuments in deeds. 

Stone v Clark, (1840), 1 Met. 378 

Lovejoy v Lovett, (1878), 124 Mass. 270 

Howard v Fessenden, (1867), 14 Allen 124 

2. MEANING OF WORDS 

In general, words are to be understood prima facie in their plain and literal meaning. 

Stony Brook R. R. v R. R., 260 Mass. 57,157 FE 607,53 AIR 730

If they are clear and free from ambiguity, the contract cannot be construed as meaning something different from what the parties intended as expressed by the language they saw fit to employ. 

Farber v Ins. Co., (1924), 250 Mass.250,145 NE 535,36 ALR 800 

Goldstein v D'Aroy, (1909), 1 Mass. 312, 87 N. E. 584 

Glynn voran, (1895), 174 lass. 253, 54 . E. 535

Thus, an agreement to sell land, and to give a good and sufficient deed of it, means a deed that will convey a good title to the land, and not one which is subject to an attach mert. 

New York R. R. v Butter, (Mass., June, 1981), Adv. Sheets 1541

- But technical words will be given a technical meaning, if so intended, and custom or usage may be shown to vary the usual meaning. 

Soutier v Kellerman, 18 Mo. 509

Every word and every phrase must be taken into consideration and be given a meaning and effect if reasonably possible. 

Heywood v Perrin, (1830), 10 Pick. 228 

3ray v Fickman, (1928), 263 Mass. 403,414, 161 N. E. 612

The construction of words in one part of a document often may be affected by the varied use of words in other parts of it, especially if the instrument appears to be carefully drawn with regard to the accurate use of language. 

Watts v Bruce, (1923), 245 Mass. 531, 159 N. 7. 650 

3. GRAMMATICAL CONSTRUCTION 

Obvious mistakes of grammar will be corrected. 

Atwood v Cobb, (1834), 16 Pick. 227 

Allen v Lowe Co., (1927), 261 Mass. 355, 158 г. E. 767

Punctuation is a most fallible standard by which tɔ interpret a writing. It may be disregarded entirely, or it may be resorted to as an aid to construction. 

Com. v Grant, (1909), 201 Mass. 458, 87 N. E. 895

A rcnoun must be referred to that antecedent to which the tenor of the instrument and the "rinciples of law require that it should relate, whether exactly according to the rules of syntax or not. 

Com. v. Call, (1839), 21 Pick. 515 

Oak Bluffs v Cottage City Co., (1920), 235 Mass. 18,126 NE 394

If there is a conflict between printed and written words, the latter will control. 

Oakbank Oil Co. v Love, (1918), H. L. 54, Costigan 216 

Yorston v Brown, (1901), 178 Mass. 103, 59 N. E. 654 

4. CONSTRUCTION AGAINST PARTY USING WORDS 

It is said that the rule that words will be construed most strongly against the party using them is to be resorted to only when all other rules of construction fail. 

Barney v lewcomb, (1851), 9 Cush. 46 

Taber v Beaudette Co., (1928), 262 lass. 99, 159 N. E. 505 

Koshland v Ins. Co., (1921),237 Mass.467,471,130 N.E. 41 

Tier v American Locomotive Co., (1915), 215 Mass.303,102 NE 48

The rule clearly does not apply in a promise to answer for the debt of another. 

Sherman Mulloy, (1869), 174 ass. 41, 54 1. E. 345 

Keith v/Thomas, (1929), 266 l'ass. 566, 571, 165 N. E. 679 

5. TIME IS OF THE ESSENCE OF A CONTRACT 

Parties have a right to make a stated time for performance the essence of a contract. Such an agreement is binding, and will be given effect by courts of equity as well as of law; I. 

Porter v Farrington, (1928), 262 Mass. 203, 159 N. . 530 if it is not waived, as it may be, by words or conduct.

EXAMPLE: If a written agreement is made for building a house, to be completed by a certain time, and, after it is made, the plan of the house is enlarged at the owner's suggestion so that it would take more time to build, yet no new stipulation was made as to the time of its completion, time thereby ceases to be of the essence of the contract. 

Palmer v Stockwell, (1857), 9 Gray 239 

At common law, time is always of the essence of a contract, and performance of every contract is required at the appointed time, unless the time is waived. This is often harsh and unjust for, although some time of performance by each party is usually named, for example, in any agreement for the sale of land, it is often not regarded by the parties as one of the essential terms of their contract. 

In equity, the mere designation of a date when a thing is. to be done does not result in making time of the essence of the contract; in fact, time is not ordinarily of the essence of a contract in equity. 

Schmidt v Reed, (1892), 132 г. Y. 108, 30 N. I. 373

But it will be rade so by clear manifestation of the intent of the parties in the contract itself, by subsequent notice from one party to the other, by laches in the party seeking to enforce it, by a change in the value of the property, or by other circumstances which would make a decree for specific performance inequitable. 

Barnard v Lee, (1867), 97 Mass. 92 

Norrington v Wright, (1885), 115 U. S. 188, Costigan 897 

Hazen v Warwick, (1926), 256 l'ass. 302, 152 r. E. 342 

Ames v Brooks, (1887), 143 Mass. 341, 9 N. E. 737 

Pickering v Greenwood, (1874), 114 Mass. 479 

Preferred Underwriters v R. R., (1923),243 Mass.457,137 № 500

Time may be an essential part of an agreement in equity, either by an express undertaking of the parties, or from the nature of the transaction, or from the subject-matter of the agreement. Thus, both in law and equity, time is of the essence of an option, and strict compliance with its conditions must be shown. 

Hunt v Bassett, (1929), 269 Mass. 298, 168 N. E. 783

Time becomes material where delay diminishes the value of the thing contracted for, or where the value of the property is subject to frequent fluctuation. 

Longley v Cotting, (1830), 9 Pick. 329 

Goldsmith v Guild, (1865), 10 Allen 239

But mere delay, provided it does not extend beyond the period of the statute of limitations, is not laches. Laches is delay that works to the disadvantage of another There is no hard and fast rule as to what constitutes laches. Each case depends upon its own peculiar facts. 

Calkins v Wire Hardware Co., (1929), 267 Mass.52,165 NE 889

If an action at law is not barred by the statute of limitations, the circumstances may be such that equity, independ-

6. LIQUIDATED DAMAGES AND PENALTIES 

The purpose of inserting a penalty clause in a contract is to secure the performance of the contract, but a provision for liquidated damages is inserted to provide damages, in lieu of specific performance. 

Dubinsky v Wells Bros., (1914), 218 Mass.232,105 N. E. 1004 

Morrison v Richardson, (1907), 194 Mass. 370, 80 N. E. 463 

Cochrane v Forbes, (1929), 267 Mass. 417, 166 N. C. 752 

Wallis v Wenham, (1910), 204 Mass. 83, 90 F. 3. 396

Liquidated damages are enforceable, but a penalty is regarded as a forfeiture from which the defaulting party can be relieved, and his liability will be limited to the actual damages sustained.

Penalties usually provide for the payment of a larger sum on the failure of a party to pay a less amount. The typical case is where the sum named secures several promises of varying importance, one or more of which is for the payment of much smaller sum of money. The use of the words "penalty" "liquidated damages", or "forfeiture" is not conclusive and in some cases, it may be apparent from the terms of the entire contract and from the subject-matter that the sum named is a penalty, though called liquidated damages; 

Winston v Pittsfield, (1915), 221 Mass. 356, 108 N. E. 1038 

Wheaton Co. v Boston, (1910), 204 Mass. 218, 226, 90 N. E.598 

Smith v Brown, (1895), 164 Mass. 584, 42 N. E. 101 

Cf. Smith v Bergengren, 153 Mass.236, 26 N.E.690,10 LRA 768 or that it is liquidated damages, though called a penalty or forfeiture. 

Guerin v Stacy, (1900), 175 Mass.595, 56 . E. 892

Courts do not look with favor upon penalties and for- 27587 feitures; 

Judkins v Charette, (1926), 255 Mass.76,151 NE 81,45 ALR 1 and it is said to be the tendency and preference of the law to regard a sum, stated to be payable if a contract is not fulfilled, as a penalty, and not as liquidated damages, because then it may be apportioned to the loss actually sustained. 

Wallis v Carpenter, (1866), 13 Allen 19

Nevertheless, the law always tries to find the real intention of the parties; 

Cushing v Drew, (1867), 97 Mass. 445 and, in so far as precedent permits, the proper course is to enforce contracts according to their plain meaning, and not to undertake to be wiser than the parties and, therefore, when parties say that a sum is payable as liquidated damages, in general, they will be taken to mean what they say, and will be held to their word. 

Kaplan v Gray, (1913), 215 Mass. 269, 102 N. E. 421 

Garst v Harris, (1900), 177 Mass. 72, 58 . E. 174

- EXAMPLE: In a contract not to carry on a particular business within specified limits of time and place, intenti en governs whether the sum named for non-performance is liquidated damages or a penalty, and if the seller binds himself to performance in the amount paid to him for the business, the amount has been held to be liquidated damages 

Robbins v Plant, (1927),174 Ark.639,297 SW 1027,59 ALR 1128 

Perkins v Lyman, (1814), 11 Mass. 76

The rules for determining whether a sum is a penalty or liquidated damages are as follows: 

1. If the damages for breach may be readily ascertained, (for example, where the damage is caused by a failure to pay money), and the sum named as damages is manifestly in excess of such amount, the court will regard the sum as having been intended to be named merely for security of performance, cr as a penalty, and will refuse to enforce it, and the party in such case can recover merely the actual damages sustained. 

Kemble v Farren, 6 Bingham 141 

2. If the damages for breach are difficult to ascertain, the court will treat the sum named as liquidated damages, from which there is no deduction, unless it is so manifestly in excess of the actual damage that the parties could not have fixed upon it except with a view to providing a penalty. 

Cushing v Drew, (1867), 97 Mass. 445 

Chase v Allen, (1859), 13 Gray 42 

Pierce v Fuller, (1811), 8 Mass. 223 

Keefe v Fairfield, (1903), 184 Mass. 354, 68 N. E. 342 

Atlantic Co. v Barnard, (1928), 264 Mass. 560, 163 N.E.167 

Putnam Co. v Mustakangas, (1920), 236 Mass.376,128 N.E.629

EXAMPLE: Where the amount named for breach of a contract is $9250, and the actual damages are ascertained to be $4500, the amount named in the contract is a penalty. 

Makletzova v Diaghileff, (1917), 227 Mass.100,116 N.E.231

The rest of the contract is not invalidated by a penalty dontained in it. Merely the provision for the penalty is void, and the plaintiff is entitled to recover actual damages only. 

Smith v Brown, (1895), 164 Mass.584,42 N.E.101

XI. 

OPERATION OF CONTRACTS A. PARTIES WHO MAY ENFORCE CONTRACTS 1. DOCTRINE THAT THIRD PERSON CANNOT SUE a. ENGLISH AND MASSACHUSETTS RULE 

There is great confusion in the cases on the right of a third person to maintain an action on a contract made for his benefit. 

Tweeddale v Tweeddale, 116 7is.517,93 NW 440, Costigan 631

The English rule, which is followed in several jurisdictions, including Massachusetts, appears to be that, notwithstanding a contract may have been intended for the benefit of a third person, he cannot enforce the promise if he is a stranger to the contract and to the consideration. 

Rockwood's Case, (1590), Cro. Eliz. 164, Costigan 637 

National Bank v Lodge, (1878),98 U.S. 123, Costigan 603 

Jefferson v Asch, (1894), 55 Minn.446,55 NW 604, 25 LRA 257 

Baxter v Camp, (1898), 71 Conn.245, 71 Atl.803,42 LRA 514

EXAMPLE 1: D promised X that if X would work for D, D 

would pay a sum of money to P. P cannot recover on D's promise to X, there being no privity of contract between P and D, and no consideration moving from P to D. 

Price v Easton, 4 B. & Ad. 433 

EXAMPLE 2: P and X were engaged to be married. Before the marriage, their fathers orally agreed between themselves that each should give a marriage portion to his child. After the marriage, it was agreed in writing between the fathers that P should have the power to sue both men on their respective promises. P cannot maintain an action against either parent on his promise. 

Tweddle v Atkinson, (1861), 1 Best & Smith 393, over-ruling 

Bourne v Mason, (1669), 1 Ventris 6, Costigan 591

In Massachusetts, while some of the early decisions permitted a third person to sue on a simple contract made for his benefit; 

Brewer v Dyer, (1851), 7 Cush. 337 

Cabot v Haskins, (1825), 3 Pick. 83 

Arnold v Lyman, (1821), 17 Mass. 400 

Carnegie v Morrison, (1841), 2 Met. 381, 401 the present rule is declared to be similar to the English rule, and that, ordinarily, such action cannot be maintained unless the promisor also made an express promise to the plaintiff, or unless the promisee was acting as agent for the plaintiff. 

Exchange Bank v Rice, (1871), 107 Mass. 37 

Clare v Hatch, (1902), 180 Mass.194,62 N.E.250 

Orenberg v Johnston, (1929), 269 Mass. 312, 168 N. E. 794 

New England Co.v Russell, (1918), 231 Mass.274,120 N.E.Ɛ52 

Saunders v Saunders, (1891), 154 Mass. 377, 28 N. E. 270 

Sampson Co. v Com., (1909), 202 Mass. 326, 88 N. E. 911 

Dahlstrom Co. v Evatt Co., 256 Mass. 404, 413, 152 N.E. 715 

Home Savings Bank v Mackintosh, (1881), 131 Mass. 489 

Distinguish Hicock v McKay, (1858), 12 Gray 218 

EXAMPLE 1: After D had made and delivered a promissory note to P, D's father conveyed land to P in consideration of P's promise, made to the father, that P would never sue D on the note. P's promise is no defense to an action on the note. 

Marston v Bigelow, (1889), (1889), 150 Mass.45,33 NE 71,5 LRA 43

EXAMPLE 2: X, having a contract for the delivery of goods to him by D, gave D a written order to deliver the goods to P, and D promised X that he would do so. P cannot maintain an

Rogers v Union Stone Co., (1881), 130 Mass. 581 b.

EXCEPTIONS (1) TRUSTS 

If a contract is of such a nature as to constitute the promisor a trustee for a third person, the promise is enforceable in equity by the beneficiary. A trust may be created by express declaration, or it may be implied. 

Boyden v Hill, (1908), 198 Mass. 477, 488, 85 N. E. 413

EXAMPLE: X transferred certain shares of stock to D to hold in trust for P. P can enforce the trust in equity, whether the agreement was oral or in writing. 

Chace v Chapin, (1881), 130 Mass. 128 

2) QUASI CONTRACTS 

Quasi-contracts are contracts created and implied by law, in order to prevent fraud and unjust enrichment. 

Carnegie v Morrison, (1841), 2 Met. 381 

Putnam v Field, (1870), 103 Mass. 556

If a defendant has in his hands money which, in equity and good conscience, belongs to the plaintiff, as where one person receives from another money or property as a fund from which certain creditors of the depositor are to be paid, and promises, either expressly, or by implication from his acceptance of the money or property without objection to the terms on which it is delivered to him, to pay such creditors, the persons for whose benefit such contract is made can maintain an action on the promise. 

Exchange Bank v Rice, (1871), 107 Mass. 37 

Mellen v Whipple, (1854), 1 Gray 317

Here the law creates the privity and enables the party who is to have the benefit of the promise to treat the consideration as moving from him. 

Perry v Swasey, (1853), 12 Cush. 36

The action for money had and received by the defendant to the plaintiff's use was encouraged and, to a great extent, brought into use by Lord Mansfield and, from his day to the present, has been constantly resorted to. It approaches nearer to a bill in equity than any other common-law action, It aims at the abstract justice of the case and looks solely to the inquiry whether the defendant holds money which, in equity and good conscience, ex aequo et bono, belongs to the plaintiff. Thus, where money has been paid to a defendant, the consideration for which wholly fails, or money which has been paid to him under a mistake of fact, the plaintiff can recover his money in this form of action. 

Claflin v Godfrey, (1838), 21 Pick. 1 (1816), 13 Mass. 216 

Hotchkiss v Judd, (1866), 12 Allen 447 

Woodward v Cowing,

entweb y hicwboot 61

- EXAMPLE: A debtor sent $1000 to D with instructions for 

D to keep $800 and to pay $200 over to P to pay a debt owing to P from the debtor. P can maintain an action at law against 

D on his implied promise to pay, for money had and received. 

Hall v Marston, (1822), 17 Mass. 574 

If one voluntarily accepts services rendered for his benefit, when he has the option whether to accept or reject them, a promise to pay for them sometimes may be inferred. 

Day v Caton, (1876), 119 Mass. 513

But one cannot, merely by erecting a house on the land of another, compel him to pay for it, even if the land is benefited by the erection of the structure. Such a promise cannot be implied even if the owner uses the structure thus erected as, while it remains upon his land, he is entitled to the proper use of his own land and all that is upon it. 

O'Connor v Hurley, (1888), 147 Mass.145, 16 N. E. 764 

3) AGENCY 

An apparent, but not a real exception, to the rule that a third person cannot sue on a contract made for his benefit, exists in a contract made by an agent for an undisclosed principal. Where the promisee is, in fact, acting as an agent for a third person, the principal is the real party to the contract and may sue in his own name on the promise made to his agent. 

Huntington v Knox, (1851), 7 Cush. 371 

Capitol Co.v Jimmie Gallagher, (1929), 268 Mass.321,167 NE 674

The circumstances of the parties respecting the naming of a child are so peculiar and the nearness of the relation so great that the inference may be drawn that the father is acting as agent for the child in making any contract as to giving him a name, so that the child may sue on a promise made to the father to pay a sum of money to the child in return for the privilege of naming the child. 

Gardner v Denison, 217 Mass.492,105 N.E.359,51 LRA (NS) 1108 

Eaton v Libbey, (1896), 165 Mass. 218, 42 N. E. 1127 

Wolford v Powers, (1882), 85 Ind. 294, Costigan 323 

(4) NOVATION 

The case of a novation is also an apparent exception only. Where a debtor and his creditor and a third person enter into an agreement by which the third person is to pay the debt to the creditor, and the debtor is released, there is a novation, and the creditor may sue the third person. In such a case, however, there is a contract between the creditor and the third person. 

13 C. J. 705 

Borden v Boardman, (1892), 157 Mass.410,32 NE 469, Cost.598 

(5) EXCEPTION BY STATUTE 

In accordance with the rule in most states, a Massachusetts statute provides that, "If a policy

DOCTRINE THAT THIRD PERSON CAN SUE 

2. 

a. GENERAL AMERICAN RULE 

The English rule is not adopted in most states. In these jurisdictions, the rule, stated in general terms, and leaving out limitations recognized in various states, is that a third person may enforce a promise made for his benefit, even though he is a stranger to the contract and the consideration. In some states, this rule is embodied in statutes providing that every action shall be prosecuted by the real party in interest. 

Wood v Moriarty, (1887), 15 R.I.518,9 At1.427, Costigan 63:0 

Buchanan v Tilden, (1899), 158 NY 109, 92 NE 724,44 LRA 170 

New Orleans v Magnier, (1861), 16 La.Ann.338, Costigan 615 

Smith v Pfluger, 126 Wis. 253, 105 NW 476,2 LRA (NS) 783

EXAMPLE 1: Holly lent $300 to D and D promised Holly to pay the same amount next day to P, to whom Holly owed $300. 

P can recover on D's promise made to Holly. 

Lawrence v Fox, (1859), 20 N. Y. 268, Costigan 592 

EXAMPLE 2: Clara Adams sued a telegraph company for failure to deliver the following message: "F. E. Adams, Athons. 

Clara, come quick. Rufe is dying. 0. M. Simmons." F. E.

Adams was the husband of Clara who was the sister of Rufe. 

Clara can maintain an action on this contract made for her benefit between Simmons and the company. 

Western Union v Adams, 75 Tex. 531, 12 S.W.857, 6 L.R.A.844 b.

LIMITS TO DOCTRINE (1) IN GENERAL 

Even under the general American rule, it is not every promise, made by one to anot her, from the performance of which a benefit may enure to a third person, that will give a right of action to the third person, he being neither privy to the contract nor to the consideration. 

The contract must have been made for his benefit, and he must be the party intended to be benefited. 

Fry v Ausman, 29 S.D.30,39 LRA (NS) 150, Costigan 611 

Kramer v Gardner, 104 Minn.370,116 N.W.925,22 LRA(NS) 492 

Tweeddale v Tweeddale,116 Wis.517,61 L.R.A.509, Costigan 631 

Seaver v Ransom, (1918),224 N.Y.233,120 N.E.639,2 A.L.R.1187

- 

EXAMPLE 1: D agreed to buy a partnership business and to pay all the debts of the partnership. The partnership creditors cannot maintain an action against D on this promise, which was not made for their benefit. 

Morgan v Randolph-Clowes,73 Conn.396, 47 Atl.658,51 LRA 653

EXAMPLE 2: The D water-works company breached its con tract with a city for which it had agreed to furnish an adequate water supply for all municipal purposes. In consequence of such breach, P's residence in the city was destroyed by fire. P cannot maintain an action against the company. 

House v Houston Co.,88 Tex.233,31 S.W.179,28 L.R.A.532

Contra: Paducah v Paducah Co.,89 Ky.340,12 S. W. 554,7 LRA 77 

Gorrell v Greensboro Co., 124 NC 328,32 SW 720, Cost.617 

(2) CONTRACTS UNDER SEAL 

Most states hold that a person cannot maintain an action on a sealed instrument to which he is not a party, even though it was made for his benefit. 

Huntington v Knox, (1851), 7 Cush. 371 

Henricus v Englert, (1893), 137 N.Y.488, 33 N.E. 550 

Case v Case, (1911), 203 H.Y.263,96 N.E.440, Costigan 625 

Cavanaugh Co.v Gaston, (1926), 255 Mess.587,152 NE 623,47 A'R 1 B.

PARTIES AGAINST WHOM CONTRACTS MAY BE ENFORCED 

1. 

ASSIGNMENT OF RIGHTS UNDER CONTRACTS 

a.

DEFINITION OF ASSIGNMENT 

An assignment is a transfer to another of the whole of any property real or personal, in possession or in action, or of any estate or right therein. It is most frequent ly used to describe the transfer of non-negotiable choses in action. 

5 C. J. 836 

b. EARLY COMMON LAW RULE 

At early common-law, most choses in action were not assignable unless the debtor assented to the assignment and promised to pay the assignee; 

Hay v Green, (1853), 12 Cush. 282 

Hunt v Mann, (1882), 132 Mass. 53 although the rule did not apply to negotiable instruments. 

Coolidge v Ruggles, (1819), 15 Mass. 387 

Skinner v Somes, (1817), 14 Mass. 107

But subsequently, even though there was no express promise by the debtor to pay the assignee, the law gave to the assignee the right to maintain an action on the assignment in the name of the assignor, or of his executor or administrator. 

Walker v Brooks, (1878), 125 Mass. 241 

Jenkins v Eliot, (1906), 192 Mass. 474, 78 N. E. 431 

Portuguese Bank v Telles, (1916), 242 U. S. 7, Costigan 577 

Sickles v Lauman, (Iowa, 1918), 169 N. W. 670, 4 A.L.R.1073

Seeck v Jakel, (1914), 71 Or. 35, 141 Pac. 211, LRA 1915A 679 

Ebel v Piehl, (1903),134 Mich.64,95 NW 1004, Costigan 546

In order to constitute an assignment, two things must concur: 

1. The assignor must by some significant act express his intention that the assignee shall have the debt or right in question and, according to the nature and circumstances of the case, deliver to the assignee, or to some person for his use, the security, if there be one, the bond, deed, note or written agreement upon which the debt or chose in action arises. 

2. The transfer shall be of the whole and entire debt cr obligation in which the chose in action consists and, as far as practicable, place the assignee in the condition of the assignor, so as to enable the assignee to recover the full debt due and to give a valid discharge to the party liable. 

Palmer v Merrill, (1850), 6 Cush. 282 C.

MODERN RULE 

Today, many states have a rule similar to that of Massachusetts, where it is provided by statute thɛt "The assignee of a non-negotiable chose in aetion which has been assigned in writing may maintain an action thereon in his own name but subject to all defences and rights of counterclaim, recoupment or set-off to which the defendant would have been entitled had the action been brought in the name of the assignor." 

G. L. c. 231, sections 5 6 ? 

Com. v Market Co., (1925), 250 Mass. 449, 146 N. E. 29 

Bowen v R. R., (1909), 202 Mass. 263, 88 N. E. 781 

See Carye v Guillow, (1870), 105 Mass. 18

Under this statute, the assignee cannot sue in his own name unless the assignment is in writing. 

Pritchard v Upham's Theatre, (1921), 238 Mass. 441, 131 NE 70

The right of an assignee of a chose in action to bring an action depends on the law of the forum; 

Levenbaum v Hanover Trust Co.,(1925),253 Mass.19,148 NE 227 and he gets no greater rights than the assignor had. 

Boston S.D.Co.v Adams, 224 Mass.442, 113 NE 277, LRA 1916F 488

Ordinarily, no particular form of assignment is necessary; 

Row v Dawson, (1749), 1 Vesey, Senior 331, Costigan 511 and, except as provided by statute, it may be by parol if there is a valuable consideration, and a delivery of the thing assigned;

Jones v Witter, (1816), 13 Mass. 304 

Dunn v Snell, (1819), 15 Mass. 481, 485 

Hooker v Eagle Bank, (1864), 30 H. Y. 83, Costigan 519 or it may be without consideration, if delivered with intention to make a gift. 

Lee v Magrath, (1882), L. R. 10 (Ireland) 45, Costigan 541
