If an assignee has an equitable right or interest in a debt or other property, he can enforce it in a court of equity; 

Walker v Brooks, (1878), 125 ass. 241 but equity will not decree specific performance of a contract for personal services. 

Chambers v Davis, (1922), 128 Miss.613,91 So.346,22 ALR 114 

d. ASSIGNMENT CF WAGES 

In Massachusetts, by statute, no assignment of wages or salary to be earned in the future to secure a loan of less than $300 shall be valid against an employer of the person making the assignment until such assignment is accepted in writing by the employer, and recorded with the clerk of the town where the assignor resides, nor unless it states that $10 per week as earned of the wages or salary so assigned is exempt and unless it is in substantially the form prescribed by the statute. The written consent of the wife, if any, of the assignor must be attached to the assignment, and a copy of the assignment must be delivered by the assignee to the assignor. 

G. I. c. 154 

Hellen v Boston, (1907), 194 ass. 579, 80 N. E. 603 

Jump v Bernier, (1915), 221 lass. 241, 108 N. E. 1027 

Gilman v Raymond, (1920), 235 Mass. 234, 127 N. E. 794 

Hall v Boston Co., (1911), 207 Mass. 328, 93 N. E. 640 

Citizens' Assoc.v R.R.,196 Mass.528,82 NE 696,14 LRA (NS) 1025 

See generally: Weed v Jewett, (1841), 2 Met. 608 

Rodijkeit v Andrews, 74 Ohio 104, 77 NE 747, 5 LRA (NS) 564 

Chicago R. R. v Provolt,42 Colo. 103,93 Pac.1126,16 LRA (NS)507

This statute has been held to be constitutional; 

Mutual Co. v Martell, (1911), 222 U. S. 225, affirming 

Mutual Co. v Martell, 197 Mass. 388, 86 N.E.916,43 LRA (NS)746 

McCallum v Simplex Co., (1908), 197 Mass. 388, 83 N.E. 1108 although, in Indiana, a statute has been held to be constitutional which prohibits entirely the assignment of future earnings. 

Internat'l Co.v Weissinger, 160 Ind.349,65 N.E.521,65 LRA 599

Money to be earned subsequent to the assignment, under a new or different contract of employment, is not assignable. 

Herbert Bronson, (1878), 125 Mass. 475 

Twiss v Cheever (1861), 2 Allen 40 

Eagan v Luby, (1882), 133 Mass. 543

A valid assignment of wages will remain in force, notwithstanding the discharge of the debtor in bankruptcy, until it is determined by the statutory limitation of time, by act of the parties, or by operation of law. 

Raulins v Levi, (1919), 232 Mass. 42, 121 N. E. 500

TV V GOUDA OL 

02

2. 

ASSIGNMENT OF LIABILITIES UNDER CONTRACTS a. IN GENERAL 

The question whether a contract is assignable is one which illustrates the effect of a general custom in the application of the common law to changing conditions in the affairs of men. At bottom, it is a matter of the intention of the parties. In the days of primitive business, when contracts were simple in nature and small in scope, the personal element of the contracting parties was a more important factor than it is in the complicated, extensive and impersonal transactions of the present time. 

New England Works v Morris, (1917), 226 Mass. 246, 115 NE 335 

One has the right to select and determine with whom he will contract and cannot have another person thrust upon him without his consent. 

Boston Ice Co. v Potter, (1874), 123 Mass. 28, Costigan 574 

Watts Co. V Am. Bond Co.,(1929),267 Mass.541,555,162 NE 4 

Perry v Hayes, (1913), 215 Mass. 296, 102 N. E. 318

Today, nevertheless, an agreement to deliver goods may be assigned by the person to whom they are to be delivered, there is nothing in the terms of the contract, whether by requiring something afterwards to be done by him, or by some other stipulation, which manifests the intention of the parties that the contract shall not be assignable; 

Arkansas Co. v Belden, (1888), 127 U. S. 379, Costigan 569 and the assignability of choses in action generally is now the rule and non-assignability the exception. 

9 C. J. 850 

Flinner v McVay, 37 1ont. 306, 96 Pac. 340, 19 LRA (NS) 879

The test as to whether a contract is assignable is whether the element of delectus personae is present or not. 

A contract for services which require a peculiar personal skill or experience, such as an agreement to paint a picture, or to write a book, is not assignable. 

Cole v Handasyde, (1910), (England), S.C. 68, Costigan 559 

Woodley v Bond, (1872), 66 N. C. 396, Costigan 582 if When rights arising out of a contract are coupled with liabilities or obligations to be performed by the contractor, and involve such a relation of personal confidence that it must have been intended that the rights should be exercised and the obligations performed by him alone, the contract, including both his rights and obligations, cannot be assigned without the consent of the other parties to the original contract. If such consent is given, the transaction is sometimes referred to as an assignment but it is not an assignment in any true sense of the word but is rather a novation. 

Kemp v Baerselman, (1906),2 K.B.604,2 B.R.C.436,Costigan 565 

Wakefield v Surety Co., (1911), 209 Mass. 173, 95 N. E. 350 

Rappleye v Racine Co., (1890), 79 Iowa 220,44 NW 363,7 LRA 139 

Derby v Sanford, (1852), 9 Cush. 263

- One has a right to the benefit he anticipates from the character, credit and substance of the party with whom he contracts. Thus, a debtor cannot transfer his obligation to pay to another without the consent of the creditor. But a creditor may make a valid assignment of a mere right to receive money from his debtor.

In the case of contracts with manufacturers, the delectus personae is not so clear. It may well be that the article is of such a character and quality, and the reputation of the manufacturer is such, that when one contracts for an article from him, it is implied that it is to be made by him alone. 

Some of these contracts, therefore, are assignable, and some are not.

Swarts v Narragansett Co., (1904), 26 R. I. 388, 59 Atl. 77 

EXAMPLE: A contract, made by a retail druggist who reposed confidence in the skill and judgment of a manufacturer to design, manufacture and install the fixtures and equipment in a drug-store, is not assignable by the manufacturer. 

New England Works v Morris, (1917), 226 Mass. 246,115 NE 315 

So with contracts generally for the sale of goods. The terms of the contract may call for a supply of goods to which the particular seller is to do something, or as to which he is to satisfy himself in some particular manner, so that the contract calls for personal services, as well as for the delivery of goods and it is not assignable. But if the contract calls for an article, and specifies that it is to be of a certain standard or description, or like a certain sample, and nothing further is said, the whole element of delectus personae is gone, and the contract is assignable, for it may be fulfilled equally well by any one. 

Atlantic Ry.v Atlantic Co.,147 NC 568,61 SE 185, 23 LRA (NS)23 

Tolhurst v Portland Mfrs.,H.L., (1903),A.C.414, Costigan 562 

Larue v Groezinger, (1890), 84 Cal. 281, 24 Pac. 42

Although no court has put it that way, a study of the decisions indicates that the question of assignability depends, not upon the provisions of the contract, but up on whether its enforcement by the assignee will render it more onerous upon the other party. It follows that the same contract which might be characterized as non-assignable in one case may, under other circumstances, be held assignable. For example, proof of the financial responsibility of the assignee would go far toward minimizing the objection arising from a provision for extension of credit. The question of assignability, therefore, is not to be determined solely by a study of the provisions of the contract itself but the extraneous circumstances of the case are to be taken into account also, the real inquiry being whether the enforcement of the contract under the changed circumstances will require the other party to do something more than he intended to undertake. 

Crane Co. v Terminal Co., (Mä., 1925), 128 Atl.280,39 ALR 1184

ILLUSTRATIONS 

Building and construction contracts, which usually require the labor and attention of a number of men, are generally held to be assignable by the contractor unless it appears that the agreement was made because of the knowledge, experience, or pecuniary ability of the contractor or that, for any other reason, he was especially fitted to carry it out, or unless it involved some other feature of a personal nature so that the personal performance of the contractor was the inducement to and the essence of the contract. 

Johnson v Vickers, 139 Wis.145, 120 NW 837,21 LRANS 359,C.530

Contracts for repairs are ordinarily assignable. 

EXAMPLE: The X Co. leased certain railway cars to a coal merchant for seven years, the X Co. agreeing to keep the cars in repair. Within a year, the Co. dissolved and assigned its lease to P but the merchant refused to pay rent to P. The contract is assignable.

British Waggon Co. v Lea, (1880), 5 Q. B. D. 149,Costigan 554 

Thus, the

A debt may be assigned by a creditor though it is uncertain in amount or payable upon a contingency but it must be actual or possible debt due or to become due. assignment of an unliquidated balance is good. 

Gibson v Cooke, (1858), 20 Pick. 15 

Mulhall v Quinn, (1854), 1 Gray 105 

Brackett v Blake, (1844), 7 Met. 385 

Crocker v Whitney, (1815), 10 Mass. 316 

O'Connell v Worcester, (1916), 225 Mass.159,114 N.E. 201

Other illustrations of contracts which have been held to be assignable include an option, if it is not for personal services; 

Sloan v Williams, (1891), 138 111.45, 27 NE 531, 12 LRA 496 

Paper Co. v Safepack Mills, (1921), 239 Mass.114,131 NE 288 a contract to clean the streets; 

Devlin v Mayor, 63 N. Y. 8 to deliver cord wood; 

Atlantic Ry.v Atlantic Co.,147 SC 368,61 SE 185,23 LRANS 223 to sell land; 

Simmons v Zimmerman, (1904), 144 Cal. 256, 79 Pac. 451 a sale of goods at sea; 

Hodges v Harris, (1828), 6 Pick. 360 and a right of action for a tort occasioning an injury to property. 

Delval v Gagnon, (1912), 213 Mass. 203, 99 N. E. 1095

Among those contracts which have been held not to be assignable are a right of action for purely personal injuries; 

Delval v Gagnon, (1912), 213 Mass. 203, 99 N. E. 1095 a right of action for malicious abuse of process; 

Titcomb v Bay State Co., (1923), 254 Mass.599,150 N.E.874 a mere naked right to set aside a contract on the ground of fraud;

Mulready v Pheeny, (1925), 252 Mass. 379, 148 N. E. 132 and an assignment of book accounts to come into existence in the future in connection with an established business. 

Taylor v Barton Co., 228 Mass. 126,117 N.E.43, LRA 1918A 124 

c. 

PARTIAL ASSIGNMENT 

At law, a total assignment of a debt or other chose in action by a creditor is valid, but a partial assignment is void without the consent of the debtor. 

Tripp v Brownell, (1853), 12 Cush. 376, 382 

Bullard v Randall, (1854), 1 Gray €05

The reason is that a debtor is not to have his responsibilities so far varied from the terms of his original contract as to subject him to several suits and distinct demands on the part of several persons when his contract was one and entire. This would be a hardship on the debtor. 

Andrews v St. Alphonsus Soc'y., (1919), 233 Mass. 20,123i NE 103

In equity, a partial assignment may be valid without the consent of the debtor, creating a trust in favor of the assignee, and an equitable lien upon the fund. 

Campbell Co. v Holehan, (Cal., 1920), 192 Fac.121, Costigan 536 

Warren v Bank, (1893), 149 I11.9, 38 N.E. 122,25 L.R.A.746 

Richardson v White, (1896), 167 Mass. 58, 44 N. E. 1072 

Kingsbury v Burrill, (1890), 151 Mass. 199, 24 N. E. 36

The reason is that, in equity, the equitable rights of an assignee can be enforced in a single action in the name of the original debtor, and for the whole amount due, with the assignor, assignee and the debtor as parties, so that little or no hardship is imposed upon the debtor. 

Gibson v Cooke, (1838), 20 Pick. 15 

Gleaton v Bank, (Ga. 1929), 149 S. E. 438

EXAMPLE: A contractor, while building a municipal school house, borrowed $575 from P and assigned to P $600 of the money due or to become due to the contractor from the city. P can maintain an action in equity on the assignment. 

James v Newton, (1886), 142 Mass. 36€, 8 N. E. 122 

d. NOTICE OF ASSIGNMENT 

As between assignor and assignee, the assignment is complete without any notice to the debtor. 

Jennings v Whitney, (1916), 224 Mass. 138, 112 N. E. 655 

Allyn v Allyn, (1891), 154 Mass. 570, 28 N. E. 779 

Kimball v Leland, (1872), 110 Mass. 325

The assignor, by his assignment, gives authority to the assignee to use his name in any legal proceedings which may become necessary to give full effect to the assignment, the assignor becomes trustee of the assignee, and the assignment conveys the right which the assignor then possesses in the thing assigned.

But as between assignee and debtor, the assignment does not become operative until the time of notice to the debtor and does not change the rights of the debtor against the as-

signor as they exist at the time of the notice. 

American Bridge Co. v Boston, (1909), 202 Mass. 374, 88 NE 1089 Thus, if the debtor should pay his debt to the original creditor before the debtor has been notified to pay the assignee, he will be protected against a second payment to the assignee. 

Brice v Bannister, (1878), L.R. 3 Q. B. D. 569, Costigan 548

After notice of the assignment has been given to the debtor, neither payment by him to the assignor, nor a release or discharge given by the assignor to the debtor, can affect the right of the assignee to prosecute his claim. 

Riley v Taber, (1857), 9 Gray 372 

Eastman v Wright, (1828), 6 Pick. 316 

St. Johns v Charles, (1870), 105 Mass. 262 

St. Andrew v Manchaug Co., (1883), 134 Mass. 42 

Staples v Somerville, (1900), 176 Mass. 237 

176 Mass. 237, 57 N. E. 380 

City Bank v Wilson, (1906), 193 Mass. 164, 79 N. E. 246 

Goshen Bank v Bingham, (1890), 118 N. Y. 349, 23 N. E. 180 

Miller v Tharel, (1876), 75 N. C. 148, Costigan 514 

EXAMPLE: Frances delivered a bond to Sarah, promising to pay her 75 pounds. Sarah assigned the bond to John as security for a debt and John notified Frances of the assignment. 

Subsequently, Frances paid the amount of the bond to Sarah who gave her a release. The payment and release were void as a fraud upon John, and Frances must pay the debt a second time, this time to John. 

Legh v Legh, (1799), 1 B. & P. 447, Costigan 510 

0. PRIORITY AS BETWEEN ASSIGNEES (1) ENGLISH RULE 

Under the English rule, which is followed in our federal courts and in many American states, the assignee who first gives notice to the debtor will have the preference, even if he is a subsequent assignee. 

Judson v Corcoran, (1854), 17 Howard 612 

Re Phillips, (1903), 205 Pa. 515, 66 L. R. A. 760 

Graham Co. v Pembroke, 124 Cal. 117, 56 Pac. 627, 44 L.R.A. 632 This rule proceeds upon the theory that personal property passes by delivery of possession, and that if one who acquires the right to take possession does not do so, he is responsible for the consequences. A chose in action does not admit of tangible, actual possession, but the assignee who first gives notice to the debtor does that which is tantamount to obtaining possession, by placing the debtor under an obligation to treat the debt or fund as the property of such assignee; and if an assignee omits to give notice, he is guilty of the same neglect as he who leaves a personal chattel in the actual possession and under the absolute control of another person. 

In re Leterman Co., (1919), 260 Fed. 543, Costigan 531 

See Adamson v Paonessa, (1919), 180 Cal. 157, 179 Pac. 880 

Washington v Bank, 147 Mich. 571, 111 N.W. 349, 11 LRA (NS) 471

(2) MASSACHUSETTS RULE 

Under the Massachusetts rule, which is followed in some American states, an assignment of a chose in action is complete upon the mutual assent of the assignor and assignee, and does not gain additional validity as against third persons by notice to the debtor. The assignment which is prior in point of time is prior in point of law. Qui prior est tempore, potior est jure.

The reason is that after an assignor has once assigned his interest in a claim, there remains nothing for him to assign again and, therefore, notwithstanding a subsequent assignee bona fide takes an assignment, he thereby acquires nothing.

An attaching creditor cannot stand on any better footing than his debtor and, if the assignment is not fraudulent as to creditors, and a creditor attaches any property of his debtor, it must be attached subject to all lawfully existing liens created by the debtor. Consequently, if the debtor has no equitable interest in a chose in action, the creditor cannot acquire any by his attachment. Therefore, the want of notice to the debtor will not defeat an assignee's interest in this debt in favor of a subsequent attaching areditor, and the same rule applies as to a subsequent assignee. 

Thayer v Daniels, (1873), 113 Mass. 129 

Kingman v Perkins, (1870), 105 Mass. 111 

Dix v Cobb, (1808), 4 less. 508 

Putnam v Story, (1882), 132 Moss. 205, 211 

Rabinowitz v Benk, (1920), 235 Mass. 102, 126 N. E. 289 

Herman v Ins. Co., (1914), 218 Iss. 181, 105 N. E. 450 

Mussachusetts Bar Examination, January, 1908, morning #9

It follows that a prior assignee does not lose his priority over the fund or debt assigned by the fact that a subsequent assignee first gave notice of his assignment to the debtor, if the prior assinec also gave notice to the .. debtor at any time before the debtor actually paid over the money. 

White v Wiley, 14 Ind. 496 

(3) EXCEPTION BY STATUTE 

By a Massachusetts statute, the rule in Thayer v Deniels does not apply to assignments of future wages. The statute provides that no assignment of future wages shall be binding upon an employer until a cory of the assignment is delivered to the employer by the assignee. 

Hence, if an employee makes an assignment of future wages to one who does not give the statutory notice thereof to the employer until after the employer has received the statutory notice from another of a second assignment of the wages of the employee, the earlier assignment is void by virtue of the statute, G. L. 154, section 4 

Hall v Boston Glass Co., (1911), 207 Mass. 328, 93 N. E. 640

ASSIGNMENT BY OPERATION OF LAW (1) FOUR CLASSES 

Rights and liabilities arising out of contract are some times assigned by operation of law in the transfer of an interest in land, or in the case of marriage, or bankruptcy, or death.

Assignments of real estate, and in case of bankruptcy, are treated in separate courses, and will be omitted here. 

Assignment by marriage is of little importance today. 

Under the early common law, it was not unusual for a lady of title, who was heavily in debt, to marry a condemned criminal just prior to his execution. By such marriage, her debts became her husband's, and her creditors were left without remedy. But under the Married Woman's Acts, a husban is no longer liable for his wife's debts, contracted by her before marriage. 

G. L. c. 209, section 8 

(2) ASSIGNMENT BY DEATH 

In general, one's rights and liabilities under a contract will pass, upon his death, by operation of law, to his executor or administrator, unless they are of a personal nature, whether his personal representatives are referred to in the contract or not. 

Mendenhall v Davis, (Wash.,1909),100 Fac.33€, 21 LRA (NS) 914 

Dumont v Heighton, 14 Ariz.25,123 Fac.30€, 39 LRA (NS) 1187 

Harrison v Conlan, (1865), 10 Allen 85 

Martin v Hunt, (1861), 1 Allen 418

Thus, a power of sale contained in a mortgage, or any other power coupled with an interest, is not revoked by death. 

Varnum v Meserve, (1864), 8 Allen 158 

Hunt v Rousmanier, (1823), 8 Theaton 174, 206 

Mulloney v Black, (1923), 244 Mass. 391, 138 N.E. 584

Where a party agrees to do that which does not necessarily require him to perform in person that which he may, by assignment or otherwise, employ others to do, we may fairly infer, unless it is otherwise expressed, that a mere personal relation is not contemplated, and that the contract is assignable before or after his death. 

Lacy v Gettman, (1890), 119 N. Y. 109, € L. R. A. 728 

Jordan v Dobbins, (1877), 122 Mass. 168

Stebbins v Palmer, (1822), 1 Pick. 71 

White v Allen, (1882), 133 Mass. 423 Hab 

Marvel v Phillips, (1894),162 Mass.399,38 NE 1117,26 LRA 416 

Hyland v ich, (1889), 150 Mass.112,22 NE 765, € LRA 383 

Cf. Hughes v Gross,166 Mass. 61, 43 N.E.1031, 32 L.R.A. 620

The line of demarcation between a personal contract, which is terminated by death, and one which the personal representatives of the deceased are required to fulfill, is not very clearly defined, for obvious reasons. Two elements which enter into the making of a contract, the intention, and

DE POR SUP 

(1. ombyok oppera po (8) 857 700 adnemelu C TEV 

VITA'SG FRY STE 

-Unicaa ofder -3fer B! ད་དུ NIO (21)AHI

- the understanding of the parties, are not subject to any fixed standard of weights and measures. They are invisible and intangible things, variable with time and place, and unde terminable by any constant or set formula. Hence, no hard and fast rule can be established for their ascertainment. 

Some contracts, to be sure, are not difficult of classification. Those of a strictly personal nature, involving particular personal skill or taste, such as contract to write a book, give a concert, carve a piece of statuary, or to marry, are personal contracts and die with the person. Executors and administrators are unable to perform contracts of such a personal nature. But, ordinarily, contracts which do not fall within this exception, come under the general rule, and death does not excuse performance. 

MacDonald v O'Shea, 58 Wash. 169, 108 Pac.436, Costigan 583

It is also true that a contract may involve matters of such a nature as to render the performance of them incompatible with the settlement of the decedent's estate and so inconsistent with the duties of an executor er administrator that, in the absence of an express provision to the contrary, the parties may be presumed to have intended its dissolution at their deaths. The whole question is one of construction and depends upon the intention of the parties. 

Dickinson v Callahan, (1852),19 Pa. St., Costigan 585, note

C. SEVERAL; JOINT, AND JOINT AND SEVERAL CONTRACTS 

1. 

DISTINCTION BETWEEN RIGHTS AND OBLIGATIONS 

A right may belong to two or more individuals severally, or jointly, but not to two or more jointly and severally. An obligation may be imposed on two or more persons severally, or jointly, or jointly and severally. 

2. SEVERAL CONTRACTS a. PROMISORS 

Two or more persons may bind themselves severally for the same obligation, so that the promisee is entitled to claim the performance against each of them separately. 

Lurton v Gilliam, (1839), 2 I11. 177, Costigan 1210 

Colt v Learned, (1875), 118 Mass. 380

By a Massachusetts statute, "all or any persons severally liable upon written contracts, including bills of exchange and promissory notes, may be joined in one action". 

G. L. c. 231, section 4

A principal and a surety have contracts different in their nature and terms and have a several obligation. 

Wallis v Carpenter, (1866), 13 Allen 19

The signers of a subscription paper are severally, and not jointly, liable. 136 

Hall v Thayer, (1846), 12 Met. 130, Carter v Carter, (1833), 14 Pick. 424

Robertson v March, (1841), 4 I11. 198, Costigan 651

b. PROMISEES

If a cover ant is made with several persons that a distinct sum of money shall be paid to each, a separate duty arises to each. It is the same as if a separate and distinct contract had been entered into with each separately. Dach one may bring separate actions. are several promisees. They 

Emmeluth v Assoc., (1890), 122 NY 130, 25 NE 234,9 LRA 704 

3. JOINT CONTRACTS a. DEFINITION 

A joint contract is one in which the contractors are jointly bound to perform the promise or obligation, or entitled to receive the benefit of such promise or obligation. Thus, a joint contract may include joint promisors, or joint promise es, or both joint promisors and joint promisees.

Omit b. PROMISORS (1) IN GENERAL 

In general, joint promisors should be sued jointly, but, if one of two or more joint promisors is sued, he is liable to a several juigment unless he pleads the non-joinder by filing a demurrer or an answer in abatement. 

G. L. c. 231, section 20 

G. L. c. 260, section 17 

Ward v Johnson, (1816), 15 Mass. 148 

Bartlett v Robbins, (1842), 5 Met. 184 

Dennett v Chick, (Maine,1823), 2 Greenleaf 191, Costigan 671

By immemorial practice, founded on necessity, and embodied in a Massachusetts statute, if an action of contract is brought against several defendants, some of whom cannot be served, by reason of their absence from the state, the action may proceed against those who are duly served. But when a judgment is taken for this reason against less than the whole number of joint contractors, an action on the same contract may be maintained afterwards against any of those not served, in like manner as if the contract had been joint and several. 

G. L. c. 227, section 15 

Stone v Wainwright, (1888), 147 Mass. 201, 17 N. E. 301

At common law, if one of two or more joint obligors died before an action had been instituted on the obligation, the debt was extinguished against his representative, and the surviving obligors were alone chargeable. 

Pickersgill v Lahens, (1872), 15 Tall. 140, Costigan 660

But this rule may beaf8gated in jurisdictions, like Massachusetts, in which a statute provides that "If one of two or more persons who are indebted upon a joint contract, or upon a judgment founded on such contract, dies, his estate shall be liable there for as if the contract had been joint and

G. L. c. 197, section 8 

Nadstanek v Trask, (129),130 Or.669, 261 Pad 840,67 ALR 599 

Lee v Blodget, (1913), 214 Mass. 374, 102 № 3. 67 

New Haven Co. v Hayden, (1876), 119 Mass. Sel 

Curtis v Mansfield, (1853), 11 Cush. 152 

Burnside v Merrick, (1842), 4 Met. 537 

Cowley v Patch, (1376), 120 Mass. 137

Another Massachusetts statute provides that if any of the several plaintiffs or defendant s in a personal action, the cause of which survives, dies before final judgment, the action may be prosecuted by the surviving plaintiff, or against the surviving defendant, as the case may be. 

G. L. c. 228, section 7

But the survivor and the executor of the deceased contractor could not be held in a joint action. 

Armstrong Mills v Oakes, (1924), 249 Mass. 397, 144 N. E. 81 

2) CONTRIBUTION 

If a judgment is obtained against joint or joint and several contractors, and one of them pays the entire judgment, he has a right of contribution from the athers. 

Chaffee ▾ Jones, Bachelder v Fiske, (1837), 19 Pick. 260 (1821), 17 Mass. 464 

(3) RELEASE OF ONE JOINT DEBTOR 

A release to one joint debtor releases all unless the instrument is drawn so as to show a contrary intention, as where a reservation of rights against other parties is inserted for that purpose, or the instrument is put in the form of a covenant not to sue. The reason is that there is one debt and, if that is discharged, the creuitor has no further cla im. 

Hale v Spaulding, (1888), 145 lass. 482, 14 N. E. 534 

Connecticut Co. v Oldendorff, (1896), 73 Fed. 88, Costigan 674 

Pettigrew Co. v Harmon, (1885), 45 Ark. 290, Costigan 677 

Cf. American Co. v stone, (1928), 27 F. (2nd) 8, 66 A.L.R.202

No particular form of words is necessary to constitute a valid release. An accord and satisfaction will so operate. 

Osborn v R. R., (1886), 140 Mess.549,5 N.E.486, Costigan 680

But a mere receipt is not sufficient. 

Gold Medal Co. v Harris, (1878), 124 Mass. 206

Apart from the statute above quoted, (G. 

L. c. 228, section 7), joint promisees should sue jointly, and a release given by one is binding upon all. 

Myrick v Dame, (1852), 9 Cush. 248 

Osborn v R. R., (1886), 140 Mass.549,5 NE 486, Costigan 680

4. JOINT AND SEVERAL CONTRACTS PROMISORS 

a.

In a joint and several contract, there is one more contract than there are promisors. 

13 C. J. 376 the 

If three persons jointly and severally contract, creditor may treat the contract as joint or several, at his election. He may join all in one action, or sue each severally. But he cannot, except in one instance, sue two of the three, because that is treating the contract neither as joint or several.

Tuttle v Cooper, (1830), 10 Pick. 281 

Bangor Bank v Treat, (1823), 6 Greenleaf 207, Costigan 673 

See generally; Langlie v Loge, (1930), 59 ND 399,71 ALR 373

If one of the three is dead, however, and that fact is averred in the declaration, the surviving two may be joined. 

Maine v Chandler, (1887), 79 Me.172, 8 Atl.553, Costigan 655

The liability of heirs and distributees on an agreement to pay from the estate an amount as attorney's fees, in the absence of a contrary stipulation, or circumstances requiring a different inference, would be joint and several. 

Coram v Davis, 

(1914), 216 Mass. 448, 456, 102 N. E. 1027 PROMISEES 

As stated previously, promisees may be joint or several, but they cannot be joint and several. 5.

CONSTRUCTION OF SUCH CONTRACTS 

Whenever an obligation is undertaken by two or more persons, it is the general presumption of the law that it is a joint obligation. Words of express joinder are not necessary for this purpose; but, on the other hand, there should be words of severance to create a several responsibility. 

Alpaugh v Wood, (1891), 53 NJL 638, 23 Atl.261, Costigan 649 

EXAMPLE 1: If a stenographer is engaged to take evidence by one of two defendants in a suit, and renders service to both, believing reasonably that a joint contract is intended, she can maintain a joint action against both, although the party hiring her intended the contract to be several, each to pay one-half, of which intention she was ignorant. 

Knowlton v Parsons, (1908), 198 Mass. 459, 84 N. E. 798

EXAMPLE 2: A contract by two persons with a boat-builder to pay him a certain sum, "each his one-half", for building a boat for them, is a several, and not a joint obligation. 

Costigan v Lunt, (1870), 104 Mass. 217

The liability of promisors as several, joint, or joint and several, is to be governed by intent. If, by any means, the court can construe this from the words of the instrument it will be done. If this cannot be done, then the circum-

13 C. J. 578 

EXAMPLE: A promissory note reading, "I promise to pay" etc., and subscribed by two or more persons, is a joint and several note. 

Hemmenway v Stone, (1810), 7 Mass. 58

The rights of promisees are to be determined by their interests in the contract. Thus, when the legal interest in a covenant is a joint partnership interest, the covenant, and the cause of action thereon, is joint although the covenant of the promisors in its terms, is several, or joint and several. 

Capen v Barrows, (1854), 1 Gray 376

XII. 

PERFORMANCE OF CONTRACTS 

A.

NECESSITY OF PERFORMANCE 1. DIPENDENT AND INDEPENDENT COVENANTS 

Tre question whether stipulations in a contract are dependent, so that neither party can call upon the other to perform unless he is willing and able to perform himself, or whether they are independent, so that the remedy for nonperformance is by action only, depends on the sense of the contract as a whole, and the intention of the parties as thus ascertained. 

Griggs v Moors, (1897), 168 Mass. 354, 47 . E. 128 N. 

Howland v Leach, (1831), 11 Pick. 151, 154 

Where mutual covenants go to the whole consideration on both sides, they are dependent covenants. 

Makin v Watkinson, 2970), L. 3. 6. 25, Costigan 704 

Bryne v Dorey, (1915, 221 Mass. 595, 109 r. 3. 146 

Beecher v Conradt, (1855), 13 . Y. 108, Costigan 854 

Gosline v Prince, (1922), 241 Mass. 550, 135 N. E. 686 

Diebold Co. v Morse, (1917), 226 Mass. 342, 115 N. E. 431 

Audette v L'Union St. Joseph, (1901), 175 Mass.113,59 N.E.668 

Morse v Moore, 83 Me.473, 22 Atl.562,15 LRA 224, Costigan 811

But where they go only to a part of the consideration, and a breach may be paid for in damages, the covenants are independent, and the defendant has a remedy on his covenant, and cannot plead it as a condition precedent. In covenants which are independent, either party may enforce his remedy for a breach whether his own cover art be performed or not. 

Knight v New England Worsted Co., (1848), 2 Cush. 271 

Grant v Johnson, (1851), 5 . Y. 247, Costigan 850 

Bettini v Gye, (1876), 1 Q. B. D. 183, Costigan 780

Boone v Eyre, (1776), 1 Henry Blackstone 273, Costigan 698 

Mass. Society v Russell, (1918), 229 Mass. 524, 118 N.E.652 N.

EXAMPLE: A promises to build a house on B's land, to dig a well thereon, and to place a pump in the well. B promises to furnish all materials for the house and pump. If B fails to supply these materials, it would release A from his covenants to build the house, and to put in the pump. But A's stipulation to dig the well is an independent covenant. It goes to only a small part of the consideration, does not depend upon any prior performance by B, and a breach by A would give B a remedy by an action on the contract for damages. 

Knight v New England Worsted Co., (1848), 2 Cush. 271 

Boston Blower Co. v Brown, (1883), 149 Mass. 421,21 г.7.

But in covenants which are dependent, an averment of performance on the part of the plaintiff is necessary in a declaration for a breach by the defendant. 

Taber v rye, (1831), 12 Pick. 105 

Johnson v Reed, (1812), 9 Mass. 78 

Cadwell v Blake, (1856), 5 Gray 402 

Gray v Gardner, (1821), 17 Mass. 188 

Culson v Blanchard, (1861), 1 Allen 420 

Hyde v Boston & Barre Co., (1833), 21 Pick. 90 883 

Richardson v Boston Chemical Laboratory, (1845), 9 Met. 42 

Ellen v Topp, (1351), 6 Exche. 424, Costigan 776

The order in which things are to be done is a very helpful test in determining whether mutual covenants are dependent or independent. If the whole is to be performed on one side before anything is to be done on the other, the covenants are dependent, and performance is a condition precedent. But if something is to be done on one side before the whole can be performed on the other, then they are independent. Thus, if a day is appointed for the payment of money, or for the performance of some other covenant, and it is to happen, or may happen, before the covenants of the other party are to be performed, the covenants are independent. The same contract may embrace some stipulations which are dependent and some independent. 

Kane v Hood, (1832), 13 Pick. 281 

Dana v King, (1824), 2 Pick. 155 

Swan v Drury, (1839), 22 Piek. 485 

Badger v Titcomb, (1934), 15 Pick. 409 

Couch v Ingersoll, (1824), 2 Tick. 292 

Stephenson v Cady, (1875), 117 Mass. 6 

Gardiner v Corson, (1819), 15 Mass. 500 

Mill Dam Foundry v Hovey, (1839), 21 Pick. 417 

Pead v Trull, (1890), 173 Mass. 450, 53 1. E. 901 

Morton v Lamb, (1757), 7 T. R. 125, Costigan 766 

Dey v Dox, (1832), 9 Wendell 129, Costigan 772 

International Co. v Martin, (1915), 221 Mass.1,108 N.E. 469 

EXAMPLE: P agreed to work for D for one year for a certain sum to be paid monthly. The performance of a year's service is independent of the obligation to pay monthly, but the obligation to pay a month's wages is dependent upon the

performance of a month's service. 

Thite v Atkins, (1851), 8 Cush. 370

Where concurrent acts are to be performed by the parties at the same time, the party suing for damages for nonperformance by the other party is only required to aver that he was ready and willing to perform the agreement on his part, and that the defendant was requested to perform the agreement on his part, but refused or neglected to do so. 

Tinney v Ashley, (1834), 15 Pick. 546, 552 

2. EXCUSES FOR NONPERFORMANCE a. IMPOSSIBILITY OF PERFORMANCE 

Where the contract is to do a thing which is possible in itself, or where it is conditioned on any event which happens, the promisor will be liable for a breach the reof, notwithstanding it was beyond his power to perform it, for it was his own fault to run the risk of undertaking to perform an impossibility, when he might have provided against it by his contract. And, therefore, in such cases, the performance is not excused by the occurrence of an inevitable accident, or other contingency, although it was not foreseen by, or within the control the party. 

Anderson v May, (1892), 50 Minn. 280, 52 N.W.530, Costigan 105 

Beacon Tool Co. v Mfg. Co., (1925), 252 Mass.88,147 N.E.572

Hills v Sughrue, 15 M. & W. 253 

He should provide in his contract for an excuse from such contingencies, for example, by a clause in a lease providing for suspension or abatement of rent in case of fire until the premises are made fit for habitation, or the lease terminated at the option of the lessor. It

The court can construe a contract in writing which the parties have made, but it cannot make a contract for them. is only when an unanticipated event happens which was not in contemplation of the parties at the inception of the contract, and upon which the continued existence of the contract must depend, that, upon the happening of the event, the contract is dissolved, and the promisor is relieved from further performance. 

John Soley v Jones, (1911), 208 Mass. 561, 95 N. E. 94 b.

SUBSEQUENT IMPOSSIBILITY (1) IN GENERAL 

The general rule is that impossibility of performance arising subsequent to the contract does not discharge the promisor, even though he was not at fault. 

St. John v St. John, (1916), 223 Mass. 137, 111 N. E. 719

It is the duty of one who proposes to enter into a building contract to determine whether it is possible to do the work before entering into the engagement, or to insist upon some stipulation covering the matter. If, without a special

agreement upon that point, he makes a general contract without fraud or mutual mistake, he has bound himself to do the work. 

If it turns out that he has bound himself to do something which is impossible or impracticable, he cannot, for that reason, refuse to go forward. Having made his contract, he must fulfill it or bear the consequences. 

Magnan Co. v Fuller, (1916), 222 Mass. 530, 111 N. E. 399

He will not be discharged, in the absence of a stipulation covering the matter, if the building is destroyed before completion by an accidental fire, by lightning or tornado, or if the work is made impossible or impracticable by reason of the softness or any other latent character of the sɔil, or other unforeseen difficulties. 

Stees v Leonard, (1874), 20 Minn. 494, Costigan 1011 

Rowe v Peabody, (1911), 207 Mass.226,93 NE 604, Costigan 1045 

Hodgkins v Currier, (1919), 232 Mass.528,122 N.E.653 

Stuart v Cambridge, (1878), 125 Mass. 102 

Wareham Bank v Burt, (1862), 5 Allen 113 

Ingle v Jones, (1864), 2 Wallace 1 

Tilden v Tilden, (1859), 13 Gray 103 

Weed v Draper, (1870), (1870), 104 Mass. 28 

Distinguish Butterfield v Byron, 153 Mass.517,12 L.R.A.571

In the absence of stipulation, a tenant is liable for rent if a building on leased land is destroyed by fire. 

Fowler v Bott, (1809), 6 Mass. 63 

Kramer v Cook,

Davis v Alden, 

(1856), 7 Gray 553 (1854) (1854), 2 Gray 309 

Roberts v Lynn Ice Co., (1905), 187 Mass.402,73 N.E.523

But a lease of rooms in a building in which other rooms are leased to different tenants gives the lessee no interest in the land, and is terminated by the total destruction of the building by fire. 

Stockwell v Hunter, (1846), 11 Met. 448, Costigan 1032, note 

Shawmut Bank v Boston, (1875), 118 Mass. 125

If a lessee covenants to keep the leased premises in repair and, at the termination of the lease, to surrender them in as good condition as they were at the date of the lease, and the buildings are destroyed by fire during the term without fault of the tenant, he will be bound to rebuild them, in the absence of a fire clause in the lease. 

Phillips v Stevens, (1819), 16 Mass. 238 

United States Co.v Parsons, (Miss.,1927),112 So.469,53 ALR 88

If premises are leased for carrying on a liquor business, which business is lawful at the time the lease is entered into, but is made unlawful subsequent ly, the tenant is bound to continue to pay rent, in the absence of a provision to the contrary in the lease. 

Imbeschied v Lerner, (1922), 241 Mass. 199, 22 A.L.R.819 

Robbins v McCabe, (1921), 239 Mass. 275

Contra: Heart v Brewing Co., 121 Tenn. 69, 19 LRA (NS) 964

Swerd v danell :erland

If one contracts to furnish labor and materials and construet a chattel or build a house on the land of another, the general rule is that he will not be excused from performance by the destruction of the chattel or building, without his fault, before the time fixed for its delivery. 

Adams v Nichols, (1837), 19 Pick. 275 

Wells v Calnan, (1871), 107 Mass. 514 

Distinguish. Butterfield v Byron, 153 Muss. 517,12 L.R.A.571 

(2) EVENT NOT REASONABLY WITHIN CONTEMPLATION OF PARTIES 

Where the event is of such a character that it cannot reasonably be supposed to have been in the contemplation of the parties when the contract was made, they will not be held bound by general words which; though large enough to include, were not used with reference to the possibility of the particular event which afterwards happens. It is on this principle that an act of God is sometimes said to excuse the breach of a contract. This is an inaccurate expression because, where it is an answer to a complaint of an alleged breach of contract that the thing done or left undone was so by the act of God, what is meant is that it was not within the contract 

Baily v De Crespigny, (1869), L. R. 4 Q.B.180, Costigan 1067

The subsequent outbreak of war does not excuse perform anco of a contract absolute in its terms. 

Geipel v Smith, (1872), L. R. 7 & B.404, Costigan 1087 

(3) ALTERNATIVE PROMISES 

The fact that one part of an alternative promise has become impossible of fulfillment does not relieve the promisor from performance of the other, whether the impossibility is due to the act of the promisor or not. 

Acker v Rittenberg, (1926), 255 Mass. 599, 152 N.E. 87 

Frothingham v Seymour, (1876), 121 Mass. 409 

Drake v White, (1875), 117 Mass. 10 

c. DEATH OR DISABILITY OF PERSON 

If the contract is of such a nature as to admit only of personal performance, there is an implied condition in the contract that death or disability which makes personal preformance impossible will excuse performance. 

Eliot Bank v Beal, (1886), 141 Mass. 566, 6 N. E. 742 

Marvel v Phillips, 162 Mass. 399, 38 NE 1117 26 LRA 416 

Stearns v Blevins, (1928), 262 Mass. 577, 160 N. E. 417 

Browne v Fairhall, (1913), 213 Mass. 290, 100 N. E. 556 

Willington v West Boylston, (1826), 1 Pick. 101

Harrington v Iron Works, (1875), 119 Mass. 82 

Fuller v Brown, (1846), 11 Met. 440

The question whether the illness of one party will excuse the other from performance turns upon whether the resulting breach goes to the essence of the contract. If the illness is slight and temporary, and does not go to the root of the contract, it will not prevent the enforcement of the contract,

Poussard v Spiers, (1876), 1 Q. B. D. 410, Costigan 784 

Contracts for the cultivation of the soil, or a lease for years, are not generally terminated by the death of the tenant before the expiration of the term. 

California Co. v Lopez, (Cal.,1929),279 Pac.664,64 ALR 1412 

Israel v Beal, (1930), 270 Mass.61,169 N.E.777,68 ALR 588 

d. DESTRUCTION OF SUBJECT MATTER 

Where, from the nature of the contract, it appears that the parties must have contemplated the continued existence of some particular specified thing as the foundation of what was to be done, then, in the absence of any warranty that the thing shall exist, the contract is to be construed, not as a positive contract, but as subject to an implied condition that the parties shall be excused in case, before breach, performance becomes impossible from the accidental perishing of the thing without the fault of either party. 7 

Butterfield Byron, (1891), 153 Mass.517,12 L.R.A. 571 

Taylor v Caldwell, (1865), 3 Best & Smith 826, Costigan 1028 

Hawkes v Kehoe, (1907), 193 Mass. 419, 79 N. E. 766 

Rowe v Peabody, (1911), 207 Mass. 226, 93 N. E. 604 

Evans v R. R.;

Smith v R. R.: 

Lord v Wheeler, Adams v Foster, (1372), 111 Mass. 142 (1866), 12 Allen 531 (1854), 1 Gray 282 (1849), 5 Cush. 156

Nugent v Smith, 1 C. P. D. 441 

Dexter v Norton, 47 N. Y. 62

In such case, if work has been done by the contractor before such destruction, he may recover on a quantum meruit and a quantum alebant for labor and materials furnished. 

Cleary Sohier, (1876), 120 Mass, 210 

Angus Scully, (1900),176 Mass.357,57 N.E.674,49 A.L.R. 562

So if property, real or personal, is destroyed by fire, the loss falls upon the owner at the time. And if an owner of a house agrees to convey it on payment of the price and before full payment and delivery of the deed, the house is accidentally destroyed by fire, the vendor cannot recover or retain any part of the purchase money. 

Thompson v Gould, (1838), 20 Pick. 134 

Wells v Calnan, (1871), 107 Mass. 517

Matousek v Galligan, (Neb., 1920), 178 N.W.510,12 A.L.R. 1270 

An agreement to sell a crop from a specified piece of land is excused if, without fault, there is no crop. 

Howell v Coupland, (1876), 1 Q. B. D. 258, Costigan 1032 

But an agreement to sell a specified quantity of produce is not excused by the fact that the seller expected to fulfill the contract with the crop of particular land and that crop, without his fault, is a failure. 

Anderson v May, 50 Minn.280,17 LRA 555, Costigan 1035 

Sunseri v Garcia, (1929), 298 Pa. 249, 148 Atl.81,67 ALR 1428

e. LEGAL IMPOSSIBILITY 

Where one covenants to do an act which it is lawful to do, and a law is enacted afterwards which prevents him from doing it, performance of the act is excused. 

Trammell v Vaughan, (1900), 158 Mo. 214, 51 LRA 854, Cost.1060

Legal impossibility may arise through any one of the three departments of government, the legislative, executive or judicial. 

Dewey v School, (1880), 43 Mich. 480, 5 N.W.646, Costigan 1085 

Gregg School v Hinshaw, (Ind.,1921), 132 NE 586, 17 ALR 1222 

Smith v Stoughton, (1904), 185 Mass. 329, 70 N.E. 195 

Semmes v Ins. Co., (1871), 13 Wall. 158, Costigan 708 

Bayliss v Fettyplace, (1811), 7 Mass. 325

People v Insurance Co., 91 N. Y. 174 

Lakeman v Pollard, 53 Me. 463 

Hughes v Wamsutta Mills, (1865), 11 Allen 201 

See Leopold v Salkey, (1878), 89 Ill.412, Costigan 789 

f. PREVENTION EY ADVERSE FARF TY 

The law implies that a party to a contract will do nothing himself to make it impossible for him to perform his contract and, if he does, such impossibility of performance is no defense to him. And if he prevents the other party from performing, the latter is excused from performance, as where one agrees to marry another, and marries a third person. 

Newcomb v Brackett, (1819), 16 Mass. 161 

Proctor v Coal Co., (1923), 243 Mass. 428, 137 N. E. 659 

Synge v Synge, (1894), 1 Q. B. 466, Costigan 967 

Short v Stone, 8 Q. B. 358 

g. ANTICIPATORY BREACH (1) IN GENERAL 

If a contract is to be performed at a future time, and the party bound to performance announces prior to the time his intention not to perform it, the promisee, if he pleases, may treat the notice of intention as inoperative, await the time when the contract is to be executed, and then hold the other party responsible for all the consequences of nonperformance. But, in that case, he keeps the contract alive for the benefit of the other party as well as his own. 

He remains subject to all his own obligations under it, and enables the other party not only to complete the contract, if so advised, notwithstanding his previous repudiation of it, but also to take advantage of any supervening circumstance which would justify him in declining to complete it. 

Frost v Knight, (1872), L. R. 7 Exch. 111, Costigan 962 

Barber Co. v Baking Co., 273 Fa. 90,116 Atl. 677,27 ALR 1227 

Bu-vi-bar Corp. v Krow, (1930), 40 F (2nd) 488, €9 ALR 1295 

Zuck v M'Clure, (1881), 98 Pa. St. 541, Costigan 968 

Kadish v Young, (1883), 108 111. 170, Costigan 1000 

Martin v Meles, (1901), 179 Mass. 114, 60 N. E. 397 

Cort v Gee, (1851), 17 Q. B. 127, Costigan 95€ 

Avery v Bowden, 5 El. & Bl. 714

(2) GENERAL AMERICAN RULE 

On the other hand, according to the English and general American rule, the promisee may, if he thinks proper, treat the repudiation of the other party as a wrongful putting an end to the contract and may at once bring his action for breach of it. This is called the doctrine of anticipatory breach. 

Roehm v Horst, (1900), 178 U. S. 1, Costigan 986 

3) MASSACHUSETTS RULE 

Massachusetts and Nebraska reject the doctrine of anticipatory breach and, in these states, if a contract is wholly executory, an absolute refusal to perform it, before performance is due by the terms of the contract, is not a present breach for which any action can be maintained. The rule seems to be that, in order to charge one in damages for breach of an executory personal contract, the other party must show a refusal or neglect to perform at a time when and under conditions such that he is or might be entitled to require performance. 

Daniels v Newton, (1074), 114 Mass. 530 

Tirrell v Anderson, (1323), 244 Mass. 200, 138 N. E. 569 

Carstens v MoDonald, (1894), 38 Neb. 858, 57 N. W. 757

There is an exception to the Massachusetts rule where a party disables himself to perform his contract by some voluntary act inconsistent with an intention to perform it. 

He is then guilty of a breach of his stipulation, and is liable to be sued before the day arrives. 

Buttrick v Holden, (1851) (1851), 3 Cush. 253

But there seems no good reason for applying this principle to cases of involuntary disability of a party to perform his stipulations which he may remove previous to the time appointed for their performance. 

Heard v Bowers, (1839), 23 Pick. 455 Thus,

An absolute refusal to perform a contract after the time and under the conditions in which the plaintiff is entitled to require performance is a breach of contract even though the contract is, by its terms, to continue in the future. in an action for a breach of contract to support the plaintiff during his life, if the contract is regarded as still subsisting, the damages are assessed up to the date of the writ. 

But if the breach has been such that the plaintiff has the right to treat the contract as absolutely and finally broken by the defendant, and he elects so to treat it, the damages are assessed as of a tetal breach of an entire contract. 

Parker v Russell, (1882), 133 Mass. 74, Costigan 979

Vitagraph v Park Theatre, (1924), 249 

Jewett v Brooke, (1883), 134 Mass. 505 

3. TENDER OF PERFORMANCE

willingness, accompanied with an ability, on the part of one of the parties, to do the acts which the contract requires him to perform, provided the other will concurrently do the things which he is required by it to do, and a notice by the former to the latter of such readiness. 

Cook v Doggett, (1861), (1861), 2 Allen 439 A tendor is not good unless made exactly in accordance with the terms of the contract. 

Lamb v Lathrop, (1834), 13 Wendell 95, Costigan 750 

Weld v Eliot Bank, (1893), 158 Mass. 339, 33 N. E. 519 Thus, an offer to deliver a greater or less quantity of goods than the contract calls for is not a good tender. 

Rommel v Wingate, (1869), 103 Mass. 327

But a tender of an excessive amount of money is good if no change is asked in return.

The tender must be made by the person who is under obligation, or his agent, to the party entitled to receive it or his agent. 

McIniffe v Wheelock, (1854), 1 Gray 600

It must be unconditional. If a tender of money is accompanied by any qualifying words, or with a demand for anything to be done by the party to whom the tender is made, beyond the mere receipt of the money tendered, it will avoid the tender. 

Richardson v Boston Laboratory, (1845), 9 let. 42

Thus a tender of money is not valid if made on condition that the creditor shall give a release, or a receipt in full of all demands. 

Loring v Cooke, (1825), 3 Pick. 48 

Thayer v Brackett, (1815), 12 Mass. 450 b.

TIME AND AIVER OF TINDER 

The 

A Massachusetts statute provides: payment or tender of payment of the whole amount due on a contract for the payment of money after it is due and payable and before action is commenced shall, if pleaded, have the same effect as if made at the time provided in the contract. 

Such payment or tender may also be made after action has been commenced and at least four days before the return day of the writ, with costs to the time of payment or tender. The tender last mentioned may be made to the plaintiff or his attorney in the action, and, if not accepted, the defendant may avail himself of the tender in defence in like manner as if made before the commencement of the action, bringing into court the amount so tendered." 

G. L. c. 233, section 12 

Roberts v White, (1888), 146 ass. 256, 15 N. E. 658 

National Co. v Standard Co., (1902), 181 Hass. 275,63 N.E. 900 

Where one would otherwise be required to make a tender, and the person to whom it is made expressly declares that he will not accept it, as a general rule, and especially in case of a chattel, the formality of a specific tender is waived.

Hazard v Loring, (1852), 10 Cush. 267

Knight v Abbott, 30 Vt. 557 

Thayer v Turner, (1844), 8 Met. 550 

Borden v Borden, (1809), 5 Mass. 67 

Howland v Leach, (1831), 11 Pick. 151 

Carpenter v Holcomb, (1870), 105 Mass. 280 

See Southworth v Smith, (1851), 7 Cush. 391

C. 

OPERATION AND EFFECT OF TENDER 

An offer to do something promised, if refused by the other party, will discharge the person making the tender from the obligation of his contract. An offer to pay money, if refused by the other party, will not discharge the person making the tender from the obligation of his debt, but it will stop the running of interest, prevent the other party from recovering more than the amount tendered and, in an action by the other party, will entitle the other party to recover the costs of his defense. 

26 R. C. L. 652 4.

PAYMENT &. IN GENERAL 

The giving of counterfeit money is not payment, though delivered in good faith and without fraud. 

Young v Adams, (1810), 6 Mass. 182

The mere sending and receipt of a check does not have the effect of payment. It must also appear either expressly or impliedly that the check was accepted in payment. The immediate return of a check by the payee would show that it was not accepted in payment. 

Dutton v Bennett, (1928), 262 Mass. 39, 159 N. E. 524 

Taylor v Wilson, (1846), 11 Met. 44

So, where money is deposited in a bank, the checks of the depositor will not transfer the debt or title to the debt to the payee without the bank's consent. 

Allen v Puritan Co., (1912), 211 Mass. 409, 07 N. E. 916

In Massachusetts, the law will presume that the iving of a promissory note for a simple contract debt is payment of the debt. The presumption relates to the intention of the parties, and is not conclusive. A variety of facts have been held sufficient to rebut the presumption and, in several cases, it is held that where the debt is a note secured by mortgage, the renewal of the note is not to be presumed a payment, so as to discharge the mortgage. 

Taft v Boyd, (1866), 13 Allen 84 

Dodge v Emerson, (1881), 131 Mass. 467 

Giligian v Truck Co., (1928), 265 lass. 51, 163 N. E. 651 

b. PAYMENT BY MAIL 

In the transmission of money by mail, there is a distinction between the relation of creditor and debtor, and that of principal and agent. A debtor is bound to pay his creditor personally, or his agent. The burden of

-- 
- proof to show a payment is not sustained, therefore, by proof that a letter, containing the requisite amount, was duly deposited in the mail. The debtor must also show that the creditor authorized this mode of remittance, either by express assent or direction, or by a usage or course of dealing from which such assent or direction may be fairly inferred. 

If this can be shown, then the transmission is at the risk of the creditor; otherwise, it lies upon the debtor. 

Gurney v Howe, (1857), 9 Gray 404 

Crane v Pratt, (1859), 12 Gray 348

But an agent, employed to collect a debt and remit the proceeds, is bound only to use ordinary and reasonable skill and diligence, either in collecting the amount, or in sending it to his principal, except so far as his discretion is limited by positive instructions. 

Buell v Chapin, (1668), 99 Mass. 594

C. 

APPLICATION OF PAYMENTS 

The rules as to the applications of payments, where there are several debts, are these: 

First: The debtor may, if he chooses, appropriate the payment, and designate the debt to which it shall be applied. 

Second: If he omits to do so, the creditor may make the application. 

Henry Bill Co. v Utley, (1892), 155 Mass. 367, 29 N. E. 635 

Third: If the creditor makes the application, he may do so against any of the debts which are not illegal, including those which are within the statute of frauds. 

Haynes v Nice, (1868), 100 Mass. 327

He may also apply it to a debt barred by the statute of limitations but such an application will not have the effect to take the debt out of the operation of the statute. It seems to be regarded as a mere permission of law to the creditor thus to apply it, and not an intentional payment on that account which is necessary to involve the admission of the whole debt, and the implied renewal of the promise to pay it. The debtor is not presumed to have intended to renew a promise which is no longer legally binding on him, although he has put it in his creditor's power to satisfy pro tanto a claim upon which he had lost his legal remedy. 

Pond v Williams, (1854), 1 Gray 630 

Fourth: Where there are several ascertained and admitted debts, none of which is barred by the statute, and a payment is made without an application of it by the debtor, that payment, when applied by the creditor, has all the effect upon the debt to which it is applied, that it would have if it had been made by the debtor expressly on account of it. 

And it is not material that the creditor makes no application of the sum at the time it is paid to him. He has a right to make such application at any time and, when it is made, it

Ramsay varner, (1867), 97 Mass. 8 

Fifth: If neither party makes any special application of the Sayment to a particular debt, but both concur in the entry of the payment upon general account, the law infers an intention on the part of both that it shall be applied to the debt of the earliest date, at least if such debt is not bared by the statute of limitations. 

Crompton v Pratt, (1870), 105 Mass. 255 

5. WAIVER OF PERFORMANCE 

You ті The parties to a contract may not only modify it by a subsequent agreement, but may waive its terms. 

Gallner v Babcock, (1921), 237 Mass. 265, 129 N. E. 431 

King v Gillett, (1840), 7 M. & W. 55, Costigan 1388

A waiver is an intentional relinquishment of a known right under a contract. It may be express of implied, and be manifested either by words or acts, but all the attendant facts taken together must amount to such relinquishment in order that a waiver may exist. N. 

Boyden v Hill, (1908), 198 Mass. 477, 85 . E. 413 

Wood v Blanchard, (1912), 212 Mass. 53, 98 N. E. 616 

Thayer v Wadsworth, (1837), 19 Pick. 349

In general, waiver of performance by a party who is entitled to insist on performance is tantamount to performance. 

Wilson v Renner, (1914), 85 N. J. L. 340, 89 Atl. 758

Whether there has been a waiver, especially when it is to be implied from numerous facts, is usually a question of fact. 

Suburban Co. v Brown, (1921), 237 Mass. 166, 129 N. E. 291

The party claiming the waiver must prove it. To take all that one can get by way of part performance of a contract is not necessarily a waiver as matter of law. 

Boyden v Hill, (1908), 198 Mass. 477, 85 I. E. 413 

Garfield Coal Co. v R. R., (1896 166 Mass. 119, 44 N. E. 119

The doctrine of waiver rests on the ground that the party to be bound knows of the change and intentionally assents to a modification or abandonment of his legd rights which are to be affected, and thereafter the parties proceed upon the substituted or new agreement thereby created. 

Metropolitan Co. v Boutell, (1904), 185 Mass. 391, 70 N. E. 421 

Lamson v Goodnow Co., (1873), 112 ass. 387

Thus, the parties may waive the right to treat the nonperformance in the original time as a breach. 

Taxi Service Co. v Gulf Co., (1925), 252 Mass. 314,147 NE 863

The acceptance of work as substantial performance of a contract, notwithstanding known omissions to do that which was required, is a waiver. 

Allen v Burns, (1909), 201 Mass. 74, 87 F. E. 194

Wiley v Athol, (1890), 150 Mass. 426,23 NE 311,6 LRA 342

Thus, while a defendant undoubtedly could recoup damages for defects from incompleted work and for any loss which he suffered by the plaintiff's delay, in building a house for the defendant, yet he could waive not only full performance but also whatever loss has been sustained, and accept the building as and when it was left by the plaintiff. And if he unqualifiedly accepted the work furnished by the plaintiff as being a sufficient compliance with the terms of the contract, he would thereby waive any failure of the plaintiff to perform fully the contract in accordance with its terms 

Hooper v Cuneo, (1917), 227 Mass. 37, 116 N. E. 237

B. SUFFICIENCY OF PERFORMANCE 1. SATISFACTION OF PARTY a. FANCY, TASTE OR JUDGMENT 

If one agrees to perform a contract to the satisfaction of another, a distinction is made in some states between cases where the fancy, taste, sensibility or judgment of the promisor are involved, and cases where only operative fitness or mechanical utility is concerned. 

Gerisch v Herold, (1912), 82 N. J. L. 605, 83 Atl. 892

If one has undertaken to do or furnish something to the personal satisfaction of a particuler person as a condition precedent to his right to exact performance from the other party, it is not for any one else to decide whether a refusal to accept is or is not reasonable. Although the compensation of the plaintiff may thus be dependent upon the caprice of another who unreasonably refuses to accept the articles manufactured or the work done, yet he cannot be relieved from the contract into which he has voluntarily entered. In modern business, many eager sellers are willing to take this risk with unwilling purchasers, especially where taste is involved. 

In such cases, if the customer is not personally satisfied the other party can recover nothing in any form of action. 

White v Randall, (1891), 153 Mass. 394, 26 N. E. 1071 

Weinstein v Miller, (1924), 249 Mass. 516, 144 N. E. 387 

Dubinsky v Wells Bros., (1914), 218 Mass. 232, 105 N.E. 1004

Thus, one who agrees to make a suit of clothes, mould a bust, or paint a portrait to the satisfaction of another may not unreasonably be expected to be bound by the opinion of his employer, honestly entertained. 

Brown v Foster, (1873), 113 Mass. 136 

Zaleski v Clark, (1876), 44 Conn. 218 

Gibson v Cranage, (1876) 39 Mich. 49 

b. OPERATIVE FITNESS OR MECHANICAL UTILITY 

The same rule is also generally applied to cases of operative fitness or mechanical utility provided the contract expressly stipulates that performance shall be to the personal satisfaction of the promisor;

971

McCarren v McNulty, (1856), 7 Gray 139 

Williams v Standard Co., (1899), 173 Mass.356,53 N.E. 862 but some states hold, in this class of cases, that reasonable satisfaction is all that a party is entitled to, regardless of the terms of the contract.

In most states, including Massachusetts, the question is one of intention of the parties, as derived from the language and nature of the contract. But, if a contract provides that the work shall be done in a satisfactory manner, and involves a question of operative fitness or mechanical utility of the article furnished or work done, and there is no express provision that it shall be done to the personal satisfaction of the other party, the contract is fully performed if donc in such a way that a reasonable man ought to be satisfied. In such cases, that which the law will say a party ought in reason to be satisfied with, that the law will say he is satisfied with and a simple allegation of dissatisfaction, without some good reason for it, will be disregarded. 

Fechteler v Whittemore, (1910),205 Mass.6,91 N.E. 155 

Duplex Boiler Co.v Garden, (1886), 101 N.Y.387,4 N.E.749 

MacDonald v Kavanaugh, (1927), 259 Mass.439,156 N.E.740 

Hunt v Boston El. Ry., (1908), 199 Mass.200, 85 N.E. 446 

Bottini v Addonizio, (1927), 261 Mass.456,158 N.E. 846 

Feldman v Desantis, (1927), 260 Mass.173, 156 N.E. 868 

Lockwood Co. v Mason, (1903), 183 Mass.25, 66 N.E. 420 

Cashman v Proctor, (1908), 209 Mass. 272, 86 N.E. 284 

Noyes v Assoc., (1906), 190 Mass.171, 76 N. E. 665 

Webber v Bank, (1904), 186 Mass. 314,71 N. E. 567 

See Doll v Noble, (1889), 116 N.Y.230,22 N.E.406,5 L.R.A.554 .

When the consideration is of such a nature that its value will be lost to the plaintiff, either wholly or in great part, unless paid for, a just hesitation must be felt, and clear language required, before deciding that payment is left to the will or even to the idiosyncracies of the interested party. 

In doubtful cases, courts have been inclined to construe agreements to perform work to the satisfaction of another as agreements to do the thing in such a way as reasonably ought to satisfy the defendant. 

Hawkins v Graham, (1889), (1889), 149 Mass. 284, 21 N.E. 312 

Handy v Bliss, (1910), 204 Mass. 513, 90 N.E. 864

C. BAD FAITH 

If the contract, fairly construed, is one for the personal satisfaction of the promisor, this means that his judgment is to be sole and exclusive only if justly entertained. 

Chandler, Gardner v Reynolds, 250 Mass. 309, 145 N. E. 746 

Then, the question is not whether the promisor, as a reasonable man, ought to have been satisfied but whether, if not satisfied, his dissatisfaction was real, and not a mere pretence or subterfuge to escape liability. 

Weinstein v Miller, (1924), 249 Mass. 516, 144 N. E. 387 

Edgecomb v Edmonston, (1926), 257 Mass. 12, 153 Mass. 99

(1800) & CICA JOS

Where the purchaser is, in fact, satisfied but fraudulently and in bad faith declares that he is not satisfied, the contract has been fully performed by the vendor and the purchaser is bound to accept the article. 

Duplex Boiler Co. v Garden, (1886), 101 N. Y. 387, 4 N.E.749 

2. APPROVAL OF THIRD PERSON 

Where the parties have made the certificate of an architect or other third person as to the performance of the work a condition precedent to payment, the general rule is that such certificate must be produced or its absence satisfactorily explained before recovery is permitted for the work done. 

Smith v Briggs, 3 Denio (N.Y.) 74

Atkins v Barnstable County, (1867), 97 Mass. 428 

3. STRICT AND SUBSTANTIAL PERFORMANCE a STRICT PERFORMANCE 

It is a general rule that an express contract excludes an implied one covering the same subject; and that therefore, when one breaks a contract for an entire price, he cannot recover on the express contract because he has not performed it, nor on a quantum meruit because his failure to complete his agreement prevents his recovery. 

Norrington v Wright, (1885), 115 U. S. 188, Costigan 897 

Jewett v Warriner, (1921), 237 Mass. 36, 129 N. E. 296

A wilful default, even though it does not go to the essence of the contract, bars recovery. 

Olmstead v Beale, (1837), 19 Pick. 528 

Mark v Stuart-Howland Co., (1917) 226 Mass. 35,115 N.E.42 

Stark v Parker, (1824), 2 Pick. 267 the labar

But if the services or materials furnished were accepted by the other party, with full knowledge of the breach party furnishing them may recover on a quantum meruit a quantum valebant. oh materia to

What 

I are worth 

Bee Printing Co. v Hichborn, (1862), 4 Allen 63 b.

SUBSTANTIAL PERFORMANCE (1) BUILDING CONTRACTS 

While a strict, literal performance of a contract as a condition precedent to recovery on the contract is generally upheld in contracts relating to chattels, an exception to the rule is generally recognized in building contracts, for the reason that structures erected on land cannot usually be removed without substantial damage to or destruction of the building. In such contracts, a substantial performance of the contract, made with honest intention to complete the building as agreed, will generally give a right of recovery on the contract. 

Nolan v Whitney, (1882), 88 N.Y. 648, Costigan 741 

Morris v Hokosona, (1914), 26 Colo. 251, 143 Pac. 826 

Jacob v Kent, (1921), 230 N. Y. 239, 23 ALR 1429, Costigan 743

- In practical effect, this rule would seem to prevail in Massachusetts where, although the form of action is on a quantum meruit, and not on the contract, the measure of damages is the same as though the action were on the contract. 

Williston on Contracts, section 1482 

Burke v Coyne, (1903),188 Mass..401,,74,F. E..942 

Bowen v Kimbell, (1909), 203 Mass. 364, 89 N. E. 542 

Hayward v Leonard, (1828) 7 Pick. 181, is the leading case in Massachusetts. This case held that when a party has entered into a special building contract to perform work for another, and to furnish materials, and the work is done and the materials furnished in good faith, but not in the manner stipulated for in the contract so that he cannot recover the price agreed by an action on that contract, yet, nevertheless the work and materials are of some value and benefit to the other party, the contractor may recover on a quantum meruit for the work done, and on a quantum valebant for the materials. 

It has been further said that, in this Commonwealth, a plaintiff who has substantially performed a building contract except in some comparatively slight deviations, or in some unessential variations, may recover on a count in quantum meruit if he can show an attempt to perform it with such an "approximation to complete performance that the owner obtained substantially what was called for by the contract, although it may not be the same in every particular, and although there may be omissions and imperfections on account of which there should be a deduction from the contract price." 

Lynch v Culhane, (1922), 241 Mass. 219, 135 N.E. 119 

Gleason v Smith, (1852), 9 Cush. 484

If the defects are easily remediable, without taking down and reconstructing any substantial portion of the building, the amount of deduction from the contract price to which the owner is entitled is the expense of making the work conform to contract requirements. 

Veazie v Hosmer (1858), 11 Gray 396 

Gillis v Cobe, (1901), 177 Mass. 584, 59 N. E. 455 

Smith v Proprietors, (1829), 8 Pick. 178 

Snow v Ware, (1847), 13 Met. 42

If the defects are of such a character that they could not easily have been remedied so as to make the work correspond exactly to the specifications, the measure of damages is the amount which the building is worth less, buy reason of the defect, than it would have been if constructed in entire conformity to the contract. 

Hayward v Leonard, (1828), 7 Pick. 181 

Pelatowski v Black, (1913), 213 Mass. 428, 100 N. E. 831 

Cullen v Sears, (1873), 112 Mass. 299 

Gleason v Smith, (1852), 9 Cush. 484

Under the rule of Hayward v Leonard, the true measure of recovery is the sum stipulated in the agreement, less the damages sustained by the failure strictly to perform.

Hammaker v Schleigh, (Md., 1929), 147 Atl. 790,65 A.L.R.1285

The rule applies only where there is an honest intention to go by the contract. 

Mark v Stuart-Howland Co., (1917), 226 Mass. 35,115 N.E. 42 

Cardell v Eridge, (1864), 9 Allen 355 

Bassett Sanborn, (1851), 9 Cush. 5 8 

Blood v Wilson, (1886), 141 Mass. 25

The question whether there was a substantial performance of the contract is to be determined in reference to the entire contract, and what was done or omitted under it. 

Bowen v Kimbell, (1909), 203 Mass. 364, 89 N. E. 542 He

One who has done work under a special contract, and resorts to a recovery under the rule of Hayward v Leonard, recovers on the ground, and only on the ground, that the result of his work is of some benefit to the defendant. comes into court admitting that he has not done what he agreed to do, and that he cannot hold the defendant on his promise to pay him the contract price. More than that, he admits that the part which he has failed to perform is one that so far goes to the essence of the contract that it is a condition precedent to a recovery by him on the contract for, if the part which he agreed to perform, and did not perform, was of slight importance, it is not a condition precedent, he can recover the contract price without performing it, and the only advantage which the defendant can take of it is by way of recoupment, or by a cross-action in which the burden is on him, the defendant, to prove the damage he has suffered from its nonperformance. 

Gillis v Cobe, (1901), 177 Hass. 584, 59 N. E. 455

EXAMPLE: If a landowner secured, by his contract, the erection of a building for 2000, worth 3000, and P, in erecting the building, fails to comply with the contract in matters going to the essence of the contract, and the building, erected, as it is erected, is worth $2500, P is not entitled to recover 2500. All that he is entitled to recover is twentyfive thirtieths of 2000. 

Gillis v Cobe, (1901), 177 Mass. 584, 59 N. E. 455

If a contractor resorts to a recovery under the rule of Hayward v Leonard because, being in default of performance of the contract or, what is the same thing, because, being unable to prove that he did perform the contract, he has no rights under it, he has not the same right to recover for the value of the work done and materials furnished by him that a person has who has done work and furnished materials as he had been requested to do. In the latter case, it is immaterial whether work is of any value to the defendant or not. (1900), 176 ass. 357, 57 N. E. 674 the result of his 

Angus v Scully,

In the former case, if a contractor has erected a building under a special contract, though not in accordance with its terms, and the building is of no value to the landowner,

CI. 1,600. 250. 20 ONGEPTOU

-- 
- the contractor can recover nothing. 

Taft v Montague, (1817), 14 Mass. 282

If the owner unjustifiably breaks a special contract for the erection of a building under such circumstances that the contractor is justified in abandoning it before completion, the contractor may recover the full value of what he has supplied, whether services or materials. 

Ford v Burchard, (1881), 130 Mass. 424

Any intentional departure or wilful default by the contractor in the performance of a substantial stipulation of a contract is, in itself, such bad faith as bars recovery, regardless of the presence or absence of an intent to gain or obtain some advantage thereby. 

Smedley v Walden, (1923), 246 Mass. 393, 141 N. E. 281 

Cobb v Library Bureau, (1929), 268 Mass. 311, 167 N. E. 765 

Eastman v Steadman, (1929), 289 Mass. 250, 269 Mass. 250, 168 N. E. 745 

Douglas v Lowell, (1907), 194 Mass. 268, 80 N. E. 510 

Divito v Uto, (1925), 253 Mass. 239, 148 N. E. 456 

(2) OTHER CONTRACTS 

In some states, the doctrine of substantial performance is extended to certain cases which are analogous to building contracts. 

Meincke v Falk, (1384), 61 is. 623, 21 N. W. 785 

Dyer v Lintz, (1903), 76 N. J. L. 204, 68 Atl. 908 

Drew v Goodhue, (1902), 74 Vt. 436, 52 Atl. 971 

3) DISTINCTION FROM SATISFACTION OF PARTY 

The doctrine of substantial performance should not be confused with the doctrine of personal satisfaction of the promisor. Under the former rule, the form of action is quasi-contractual in some states, including Massachusetts, and it is no bar to the plaintiff's recovery that there has not been full performance, in the absence of the plaintiff's wilful default. Under the latter rule, the form of action is on the express contract, and a plaintiff who has undertaken to do or furnish something to the personal satisfaction of a particular person must perform his contract according to its terms, or else be barred from any recovery. XIII. A.

TERMINATION AND RESCISSION OF CONTRACTS ABROGATION BY AGREEMENT 

An executary bilateral contract may be cancelled by mutual consent of the parties. They may agree to abrogate it so that both will be discharged from performance. The agreement of each to annul is a sufficient onsileration for an abandonment. 

Hanson v Wittenberg, (1910), 205 Mass. 319, 91 N. E. 383

This mutual rescission, or abrogation by agreement, of a wholly executory contract is a right distinct from the right to rescind a contract after the time for performance has arrived. 

13 C. J. 600

- B.

RESCISSION FOR INVALIDITY OF CONTRACT, OR FOR WRONG OR DEFAULT OF ADVERSE PARTY GROUNDS FOR RESCISSION IN GENERAL 

Invalidity of assent due to fraud, undue influence, duress or mistake is perhaps the most common ground on which parties seek to rescind their contracts. A rescission may also be claimed for other matters going to the substance of the contract, for failure of consideration, or for breach or failure to perform. 

13 C. J. 610 

Harlow v Putnam, (1878), 124 Mass. 553 

Trask v Vinson, (1838), 20 Pick. 105 

Rice v Goddard, (1833), 14 Pick. 293 

Chemical Co. v Howard, (1889), 148 Mass. 352, 2 L.R.A. 168 

b. FRAUD 

In many cases of fraud, which includes duress, a contract may be rescinded by the party defrauded, and the consideration recovered. But, before that can be done, the plaintiff must return to the defendant that which he received under the contract. If, instead of so doing, he elects to seek his remedy by a suit for damages caused by the fraud, he may recover all his damages in that suit but he the reby so far affirms the contract that he cannot recover back the consideration. On the other hand, if he rescinds the contract for the fraud, he cannot sue for breach of contract. 

Stevers v Pierce, (1890), 151 Mass. 207, 23 N. E. 1006 

Lefevre v Chamberlain, (1917), 228 Mass. 294, 117 N. E. 327 

Kimball v Cunningham, (1808), 4 ass. 502 

Merriam v Wolcott, (1861), 3 Allen 258 

Conner v Henderson, (1818), 15 Mass. 319 C.

NONPERFORMANCE OR BREACH 

A wrongful and entire failure by the defendant to perform the contract authorizes its rescission by the plaintiff, and entitles him to recover the amount paid by him, if any, upon a count for money had and received. 

Simmons v Lawrence Co., (1882), 133 Mass. 298

He is not bound to affirm the contract by an action for damages suffered by reason of the defendant's nonperformance. 

Cohen v Wintman, (1920), 230 Mass. 471, 128 N. 3. 778 or 

Where a breach goes to the very essence of a contract, where a party utterly refuses to proceed further with it, the innocent party may, on the one hand, consent to a rescission, and release the party who has repudiated it or, on the other hand, he may hold the guilty party to the contract, and sue for damages, He may bring this action at once, even before the expiration of the time the contract was to run, and recover full damages. 

But this right to regard the contract as wholly repudiated and to bring an action for full damages does not arise
