upon every breach of contract. A breach may be so slight as not to go to the essence of the contract, in which case, the only remedy. of the injured party may be an action for damages for that breach. A familiar illustration of that principle is found in actions on building contracts. 

White Co. v Remick Co., (1908), 198 Mass. 41, 84 N. E. 113

So long and so far as the contract remains executory, and before breach, it may be annulled by agreement of all parties. But when it has been broken, and a right of action has accrued, the debt or damages can only be released for a consideration; and even so far as it remains executory, it may be said that the agreement to annul on one side may be taken as the consideration for the agreement to annul on the other side. 

Collyer v Moulton, (1868), 9 R. I. 90, Costigan 1389 

Griffin v Cunningham, (1903), 183 Mass. 505, 67 N. E. 660 

Alle v Thurber, (1889), 149 Mass. 271, 21 N. E. 312 

Cutter v Cochrane, (1874), 116 Mass. 408 

Rollins v Marsh, (1830), 128 Mass. 116

Thus, if one or two joint debtors on a promissory not e pays a part thereof, and the creditor agrees to look to the other for the balance, there is no consideration for such promise, and the creditor is not pound thereby. 

Smith v Bartholomew, (1840), 1 Met. 276 If one partner goes out of a firm, and another comes in, the debts of the old firm may, by the consent of all three parties, the creditors, the old firm, and the new firm, be transferred to the new firm. Their mutual consent to the substitution may be established by circumstances as well as by words. 

Kirtley v Galbo Co., (1923), 244 Mass. 179, 138 1. E. 326 

Stowell v Gram, (1904), 184 ass. 562, 69 N. E. 342

Thus, when a new firm agrees to assume the debts of an old one, the circumstance that a creditor of both firms, with knowledge of such agreement, presents a bill to the new firm for debts due from both firms will support an inference of assent on the part of the creditor to the agreement. 

Shaw v McCregory, (1870), 105 ass. 96

If a contract is in process of performance, and a seller refuses to fill further orders except at an increased price which the buyer accepts, prima facie such substituted agreement takes the place of the original contract as to everything remaining unperformed, and the buyer cannot hold the seller for breach of the original agreement. 

Rogers v Rogers, (1885), 139 Mass. 440, 1 N. E. 122 

Juniper Co. v Nelson, (Va., 1922), 112 S.E.564, 24 A.L.R.247 

See Sherman v Buffinton, (1917), 228 Mass.139,117 F.E. 33

The general rule, where the original contract is within the statute of frauds, is that, though an absolute rescission may be oral, a substituted agreement must be evidenced in writing. 

Noble v Ward, (1866), 2 Exche. 135, Costigan 1364

- The Massachusetts rule is contra and is that either an absolute rescission or a substituted agreement may be oral. 

Stearns v Hall, (1851), 9 Cush. 31 

Cummings v Arnold, (1842), 3 Met. 486 

2. RESTORATION OF STATUS QUO 

Two cardinal rules are laid down as to the right to rescind:

First, The plaintiff must ordinarily return all he received under the void contract;

Second, Both parties must be restored to the condition in which they were before the contract was made. 

If it is impossible that the parties be restored to their original positions, the general rule is that the contract cannot be rescinded, and the remedy may be for damages resulting from the breach. 

Marston v Singapore Co., (1895),163 Mass. 296, 39 N.E. 1113 

Gassett v Glazier, (1896), 165 Mass. 472, 43 N.E. 193 

Barrie v Earle, (1886), 145 Mass. 1, 8 N.E. 639 

EXAMPLE: P and D contracted that P should have the privilege of conducting a restaurant in D's bar-room for two years. After enjoying this privilege for ten months, P was turned out by D in violation of the agreement. P cannot rescind the contract and recover the rental payments because he cannot return the benefits which he has received. 

DeMontague v Bacharach, (1902), 181 Mass. 256, 63 N.E. 435

The exception is that, when a party seeking to rescind a contract on the ground of fraud acts without unnecessary delay, and restores or offers to restore that which he has received, it is no defense that the wrongdoer has, by his own act, made a full restoration impossible, or has entered into obligations with others. 

O'Shea v Vaughn, (1909), 201 Lass. 412,422, 87 N.E. 616

If the consideration returned by the party seeking to rescind is diminished in value by natural causes, or in the natural and proper use of it, he may still return it as, in such case, the contract being rescinded, such diminution is the loss of the original owner. Res perit domino. 

But, if it be injured by his own negligence, he cannot return it, and his right to rescind the contract is gone. If the property is absolutely worthless, it need not be returned; but this rule has been held so strictly that articles which are of the slightest value, or the loss of which may be a disadvantage in any way, must be returned, even if they have no intrinsic or market value, such as casks containing worthless lime, or sacks which have been on bales of wool. 

Snow v Alley, (1887), 144 Lass. 564, 11 N.E. 764 

Perley v Balch, (1839), 23 Pick. 283

XIV. 

DISCHARGE OF CONTRACTS 

A. 

MODES OF DISCHARGE 

A contract may be discharged in five ways, (1) by agreement; (2) by performance; (3) by impossibility of performance, in some cases; (4) by breach; (5) by operation of law. All of these methods have been referred to previously, and only the two latter will be dealt with specifically here. 

B. DISCHARGE BY BREACH 

A contract may be discharged by breach in three ways, (1) by a party renouncing his liabilities under it; (2) by his own act making it impossible for him to fulfill his liabilities under it; and (3) by his totally or partially failing to perform his promise. 

Lincoln v Gay, (1895), 164 Mass. 537, 42 N.E. 95

C. DISCHARGE. BY OPERATION OF LAW 1. IN GENERAL 

Contracts are sometimes discharged by operation of law in case of (1) merger; (2) discharge in bankruptcy or insolvency; and (3) alteration of written instruments. 2. 

MERGER 

If an oral contract is entered into and, before any material part of it has been performed, it is reduced to writing, it is often said that the oral contract is merged in the written contract. 

Bergin v Williams, (1885), 138 Mass. 544 

Hayden v Shaw, (1906), 191 Mass. 533, 544, 78 N.E. 110

But this is, in effect, a statement, in different form, of the parol evidence rule. 

13 C. J. 598

As far as the doctrine of merger is concerned, agreements at common law ranked as follows: First, simple contracts, oral or written; second, sealed instruments and specialties; third, contracts of record and judgments. Class 2 merged Class 1, and Class 3 merged both Classes 1 and 2. 

Clark on Contracts, (4th edition), 660 3.

DISCHARGE IN BANKRUPTCY OR INSOLVENCY 

This form of discharge does not actually discharge the contract itself but rather operates as a bar to an action on it. It is wholly a matter of statutory regulation, is to be studied in the subject of Bankruptcy, and, therefore, will be omitted hero. 

Citizens' Assoc. v K.R., (1907),196 Mass.528,14 LRANS 1025 4.

ALTERATION OF WRITTEN INSTRUMENTS a. MATERIAL ALTERATIONS 

A material change or alteration of an instrument is one which causes it to speak a language different

If the instrument is negotiable, a Massachusetts statute provides: "Where a negotiable instrument is materially altered without the assent of all parties liable there on it is avoided, except as against a party who has himself made, authorized or assented to the alteration, and subsequent endorsers. But when an instrument has been materially altered and is in the hands of a holder in due course, not a party to the alteration, he may enforce payment thereof according to its original tenor." 

G. L. c. 107, section 147

The reason that a material alteration avoids a written instrument are two: first, that no man shall be permitted, on grounds of public policy, to take the chance of committing a fraud, without running any risk of losing by the event, when it is detected; the other, that the identity of the instrument is destroyed. 

Lee v Butler, (1897), 167 Mass. 426, 46 N. E. 52

C. BURDEN OF PROOF 

If an alteration of an instrument can be shown, the burden of proving it immaterial is on the party relying on the instrument.
